On the interplay among maximum principles, compact support principles and Keller-Osserman conditions on manifolds.
Bruno Bianchini Luciano Mari Patrizia Pucci Marco Rigoli

Abstract
1 1 1 Mathematic subject classification 2010 : primary 35R01, 35B50, 35B53, 58J65, 53C42; secondary 58J05, 35B08, 35B45, 35J08, 35R45. Keywords : maximum principle, Omori-Yau, compact support principle, Keller-Osserman, minimal graph, mean curvature operator, soliton. This paper is about the influence of Geometry on the qualitative behaviour of solutions of quasilinear PDEs on Riemannian manifolds. Motivated by examples arising, among others, from the theory of submanifolds, we study classes of coercive differential inequalities of the form div ( φ ⁡ ( | ∇ u | ) | ∇ u | ∇ u ) ≥ b ( x ) f ( u ) l ( | ∇ u | ) (respectively, ≤ or = ) \mathrm{div}\left(\frac{\varphi(|\nabla u|)}{|\nabla u|}\nabla u\right)\geq b(x)f(u)l(|\nabla u|)\qquad\text{(respectively, $\leq$ or $=$)} on domains of a manifold M M , for suitable φ , b , f , l \varphi,b,f,l , with emphasis on mean curvature type operators. We investigate the validity of strong maximum principles, compact support principles and Liouville type theorems; in particular, the goal is to identify sharp thresholds, involving curvatures or volume growth of geodesic balls in M M , to guarantee the above properties under appropriate Keller-Osserman type conditions, and to discuss the geometric reasons behind the existence of such thresholds. The paper also aims to give a unified view of recent results in the literature. The bridge with Geometry is realized by studying the validity of weak and strong maximum principles at infinity, in the spirit of Omori-Yau’s Hessian and Laplacian principles and subsequent improvements.

Via Vanvitelli 1, I-06123 Perugia (Italy)

E-mail address: patrizia.pucci@unipg.it

Università degli Studi di Milano

Via Saldini 50, I-20133 Milano (Italy)

E-mail address: marco.rigoli55@gmail.com

1
Introduction

The study of quasilinear differential inequalities of the type

\mathrm{div}\mathcal{A}(x,u,\nabla u)\geq\mathcal{B}(x,u,\nabla u)

(1.1)

on Euclidean space
\mathbb{R}^{m}
is a classical subject, and a great deal of work has been devoted to the analysis of the qualitative properties of solutions. The literature is vast, and we restrict ourselves to the special case

\mathcal{B}(x,u,\nabla u)=b(x)f(u)l(|\nabla u|),

(1.2)

for continuous
b,f,l
. With no claim of completeness, we quote
[
9
,
37
,
49
,
50
,
54
,
57
,
61
,
86
,
98
,
124
]
, and for similar inequalities in the sub-Riemannian setting of Carnot groups,
[
22
,
23
,
38
,
21
,
89
,
2
]
. The results in the references above will be related to those in our work in a more precise way in due course in the article.

Motivated by geometrical and physical problems, there has recently been an increasing interest in the study of some classes of quasilinear PDEs on complete Riemannian manifolds. As it is well known, the behaviour of their solutions strongly depends on the underlying space. A typical example is the minimal (hyper-)surface equation, which admits no non-constant positive solutions on
\mathbb{R}^{m}
while plenty of bounded solutions exist in the hyperbolic space
\mathbb{H}^{m}
. To the best of our knowledge, only a few authors have analyzed the influence of geometry on the behaviour of solutions of (
1.1
), (
1.2
) in a general setting, for instance see
[
120
,
95
,
89
,
3
]
, leaving however the picture still fragmentary, especially in case where
l
in (
1.2
) is a non-constant function. As one of the main purposes of the present work, we aim to give a detailed account of how geometry comes into play at the global level. Nevertheless, many interesting questions and problems remain open.

From now on, we let
(M,\langle\,,\,\rangle)
be a Riemannian manifold of dimension
m\geq 2
. We shall assume throughout the paper that
M
is non-compact. To avoid excessive technicalities, while still keeping a good amount of generality, we study the following subclass of (
1.1
): we consider a quasilinear operator
\Delta_{\varphi}
, called the
\varphi
-Laplacian, weakly defined by

\Delta_{\varphi}u=\mathrm{div}\left(\frac{\varphi(|\nabla u|)}{|\nabla u|}\nabla u\right),

where we assume

\varphi\in C(\mathbb{R}^{+}_{0}),\quad\varphi(0)=0,\quad\varphi(t)>0\ \text{ on }\,\mathbb{R}^{+};

(1.3)

hereafter,
\mathbb{R}^{+}_{0}=[0,\infty)
and
\mathbb{R}^{+}=(0,\infty)
. Different choices of
\varphi
give rise to well-known, geometrically relevant operators, for instance

-

the
p
-Laplacian
\Delta_{p}
,
p>1
, where
\varphi(t)=t^{p-1}
;

-

the mean curvature operator, describing the mean curvature of the graph hypersurface
\{(x,v(x)):x\in M\}
into the Riemannian product
M\times\mathbb{R}
. In this case,
\varphi(t)=t(1+t^{2})^{-1/2}
;

-

the operator of exponentially harmonic functions, where
\varphi(t)=t\exp\big(t^{2}\big)
, considered for instance in
[
45
]
;

-

the operator associated to
\varphi(t)=t^{p-1}+t^{q-1}
,
1<q<p
, that appears in quantum physics, see
[
11
]
;

and many more.
We focus our attention on the differential inequalities

\begin{array}[]{rll}(P_{\geq})&\quad\Delta_{\varphi}u\geq b(x)f(u)l(|\nabla u|)\\[5.69046pt]
(P_{\leq})&\quad\Delta_{\varphi}u\leq b(x)f(u)l(|\nabla u|)\\[5.69046pt]
(P_{=})&\quad\Delta_{\varphi}u=b(x)f(u)l(|\nabla u|)&\\[5.69046pt]
\end{array}

(1.4)

in a
connected
open set, that is, a domain
\Omega\subset M
. Typically, we do not require
\Omega
relatively compact, it might coincide with
M
or with an end of
M
, in other words, with a non relatively compact connected component of
M\backslash K
, for some compact set
K
.

Throughout the paper we fix the basic assumptions on
b,f,l
:

\begin{array}[]{l}b\in C(M),\qquad b>0\ \text{ on }\,M,\\[2.84544pt]
f\in C(\mathbb{R}),\\[2.84544pt]
l\in C(\mathbb{R}_{0}^{+}),\qquad l>0\ \text{on }\,\mathbb{R}^{+}.\end{array}

(1.5)

Because of the positivity of
b
and
l
, if
f\geq 0
the problems in (
1.4
) are called completely coercive in the recent literature (see
[
49
,
50
,
40
]
). Obviously, solutions of (
1.4
) are considered in the weak sense and, in view of geometric applications, we confine ourselves to locally Lipschitz or
C^{1}
solutions. It should be stressed that relaxing their regularity class is by no means a trivial or just a technical issue. For instance, under our requirements on
\varphi,b,l
, we are not aware of the validity of weak Harnack inequalities for (
1.4
), and solutions may not be even locally bounded.

Definition 1.1
.

A function
u:\Omega\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is a
C^{1}
solution
(respectively,
\mathrm{Lip}_{\mathrm{loc}}
solution
) of
(P_{\geq})
in (
1.4
) if
u\in C^{1}(\Omega)
(resp.,
u\in\mathrm{Lip}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\Omega)
) and satisfies
(P_{\geq})
in the weak sense, that is,

-\int_{\Omega}\frac{\varphi(|\nabla u|)}{|\nabla u|}\langle\nabla u,\nabla\psi\rangle\geq\int_{\Omega}b(x)f(u)l(|\nabla u|)\psi\qquad\text{for each }\psi\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\Omega),\ \psi\geq 0,

where integration is performed with respect to the Riemannian measure. The analogous statement, with the reverse inequality, defines
C^{1}
and
\mathrm{Lip}_{\mathrm{loc}}
solutions of
(P_{\leq})
.

1.1
Bernstein type theorems

The original motivation for the present paper was the investigation of Bernstein type theorems for graphs in general ambient spaces. The classical Bernstein theorem states that entire minimal graphs in
\mathbb{R}^{m+1}
, described by solutions
u:\mathbb{R}^{m}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
of the minimal hypersurface equation

\mathrm{div}\left(\frac{\nabla u}{\sqrt{1+|\nabla u|^{2}}}\right)=0\qquad\text{on }\,\mathbb{R}^{m},

are affine functions if
m\leq 7
. Its solution and the proof of the sharpness of the dimension restriction favoured the flourishing of Geometric Analysis (cf.
[
59
]
). Needless to say, the possible existence of a similar result for global graph hypersurfaces in different ambient spaces
\bar{M}^{m+1}
heavily depends on the geometry of
\bar{M}
. Suppose that
(\bar{M}^{m+1},(\,,\,))
has a nowhere-vanishing conformal vector field
X
and a distinguished slice
(M,\langle\,,\,\rangle)
orthogonal to the flow lines of
X
. Typical examples include the warped product structures

\begin{array}[]{lll}(1)&\bar{M}=\mathbb{R}\times_{h}M,&\quad\text{with metric}\qquad(\,,\,)=\mathrm{d}s^{2}+h(s)^{2}\langle\,,\,\rangle,\quad\text{and}\\[5.69046pt]
(2)&\bar{M}=M\times_{h}\mathbb{R},&\quad\text{with metric}\qquad(\,,\,)=\langle\,,\,\rangle+h(x)^{2}\mathrm{d}s^{2},\end{array}

(1.6)

for positive
h\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
and
h\in C^{\infty}(M)
, respectively. In the first case,
X=h(s)\partial_{s}
is a conformal field with geodesic flow lines, while in the second
X=\partial_{s}
is Killing, and the distance between the two flow lines equals the distance between their projections on any slice
\{s=\mathrm{const}\}
. For this reason, in case
(2)
the flow lines of
X
are called equidistant curves.

Example 1.2
.

Consider the upper half-space model of the hyperbolic space:

\mathbb{H}^{m+1}=\Big\{(x_{0},x)\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{m}\ \ :\ \ x_{0}>0\Big\}

with metric

(\,,\,)=\frac{1}{x_{0}^{2}}\Big(\mathrm{d}x_{0}^{2}+\langle\,,\,\rangle_{\mathbb{R}^{m}}\Big).

With the change of variables
s=-\log x_{0}
we express
(\,,\,)
as the warped product of type
1

\mathbb{H}^{m+1}=\mathbb{R}\times_{e^{s}}\mathbb{R}^{m},\qquad(\,,\,)=\mathrm{d}s^{2}+e^{2s}\langle\,,\,\rangle_{\mathbb{R}^{m}},

whose slices
\{s=\mathrm{const}\}
are called horospheres. Similarly, we can view
\mathbb{H}^{m+1}
as a different warped product of type
1
, along totally umbilical hyperspheres:

\mathbb{H}^{m+1}=\mathbb{R}\times_{\cosh s}\mathbb{H}^{m},\qquad(\,,\,)=\mathrm{d}s^{2}+\cosh^{2}s\langle\,,\,\rangle_{\mathbb{H}^{m}}.

(1.7)

On the other hand,
\mathbb{H}^{m+1}
admits a warped product of type
2
via equidistant curves, of the type

\mathbb{H}^{m+1}=\mathbb{H}^{m}\times_{\cosh\rho}\mathbb{R},\qquad(\,,\,)=\langle\,,\,\rangle_{\mathbb{H}^{m}}+\cosh^{2}\big(r(x)\big)\mathrm{d}s^{2},

(1.8)

where
r:\mathbb{H}^{m}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is the distance from a fixed origin in
\mathbb{H}^{m}
. This corresponds, in the upper half-space model, to the fibration of
\mathbb{H}^{m+1}
via euclidean lines orthogonal to the totally geodesic hypersphere
\{x_{0}^{2}+|x|^{2}=1\}
.

Given a function
v:M\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
, one can consider the graph

\Sigma^{m}=\Big\{(s,x)\in\mathbb{R}\times M,\ \ s=v(x)\Big\},

that according to whether
\bar{M}
is of type
1
or
2
we call, respectively, geodesic or equidistant graph. In the setting of the hyperbolic space, M.P. Do Carmo and H.B. Lawson proved in
[
43
]
the following beautiful result:

Theorem 1.3
.

Let
\Sigma^{m}\rightarrow\mathbb{H}^{m+1}
be the geodesic graph of a function
v
over a horosphere or a hypersphere
M
. Suppose that
\Sigma
has constant mean curvature
H\in[-1,1]
. More precisely,

(i)

If
M
is a horosphere, then
H=\pm 1
and
\Sigma
is a horosphere;

(ii)

If
M
is a hypersphere, then
H\in(-1,1)
and
\Sigma
is a hypersphere.

Note that, differently from the Euclidean case, no restriction appears on
m
. The result applies in fact to the much more general setting of properly embedded hypersurfaces, but its proof, relying on the moving plane method, seems difficult to adapt to graphs with variable mean curvature. This originates our search for alternative arguments and motivates the study of (
1.4
): indeed, as we shall see in a moment, the prescribed mean curvature equation for
\Sigma
leads to the study of inequalities
(P_{\geq}),(P_{\leq})
and
(P_{=})
. We let
\Phi_{t}
be the flow of
X
and, for convenience, we express the prescribed mean curvature equation in terms of the function
u(x)=t(v(x))
. Let
\nabla
be the connection on
(M,\langle\,,\,\rangle)
.

Geodesic graphs.
In this case, the flow parameter
t
satisfies

t=\int_{0}^{s}\frac{\mathrm{d}\sigma}{h(\sigma)},\qquad t:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow t(\mathbb{R})=I.

(1.9)

Set
\lambda(t)=h\big(s(t)\big)
. If
H
is the normalized mean curvature of
\Sigma
with respect to the upward-pointing unit normal

\nu=\frac{1}{\lambda(u)\sqrt{1+|\nabla u|^{2}}}\Big(\partial_{t}-(\Phi_{u})_{*}\nabla u\Big),

(1.10)

then a computation in
[
36
]
shows that
u:M\rightarrow I
solves

\mathrm{div}\left(\frac{\nabla u}{\sqrt{1+|\nabla u|^{2}}}\right)=m\lambda(u)H+m\frac{\lambda_{t}(u)}{\lambda(u)}\frac{1}{\sqrt{1+|\nabla u|^{2}}}\qquad\text{on }\,M,

(1.11)

where
\lambda_{t}
is the derivative of
\lambda
with respect to
t
.

Equidistant graphs.
In this case, the flow parameter is
t=s
. Having defined the normal direction

\nu=\frac{1}{h\sqrt{1+h^{2}|\nabla u|^{2}}}\Big(\partial_{t}-h^{2}(\Phi_{u})_{*}\nabla u\Big),

(1.12)

a computation in
[
35
]
shows that
u:M\rightarrow I=\mathbb{R}
solves

\mathrm{div}\left(\frac{h\nabla u}{\sqrt{1+h^{2}|\nabla u|^{2}}}\right)=mH-\left\langle\frac{h\nabla u}{\sqrt{1+h^{2}|\nabla u|^{2}}},\frac{\nabla h}{h}\right\rangle\qquad\text{on }\,M.

(1.13)

If we consider the conformal deformation

\overline{\langle\,,\,\rangle}=h^{-2}\langle\,,\,\rangle,

and we denote with
\|\cdot\|,\,\bar{\nabla}
and
\overline{\mathrm{div}}
, respectively, the norm, connection and divergence in the metric
\overline{\langle\,,\,\rangle}
, then (
1.13
) is equivalent to

\overline{\mathrm{div}}_{h}\left(\frac{\bar{\nabla}u}{\sqrt{1+\|\bar{\nabla}u\|^{2}}}\right)=mHh^{2}\qquad\text{on }\,\big(M,\overline{\langle\,,\,\rangle}\big),

(1.14)

where

\overline{\mathrm{div}}_{h}Y=h^{m-1}\overline{\mathrm{div}}\big(h^{1-m}Y\big)

is a weighted divergence.

For suitable choices of
H
including the minimal case
H=0
, equations (
1.11
) and (
1.14
) can be put into the form
(P_{=})
in (
1.4
) (in the second case, with a weight in the driving differential operator). A further interesting example is that of mean curvature flow (MCF) solitons. We recall that a family of hypersurfaces
f_{t}:\Sigma^{m}\rightarrow\bar{M}^{m+1}
moving by mean curvature flow

\partial_{t}f_{t}=\overrightarrow{H}(f_{t})

(with
\overrightarrow{H}
the unnormalized mean curvature vector) is said to be a mean curvature soliton if there exists a conformal vector field
Y
on
\bar{M}
such that
f_{t}(M)=\Psi_{\tau(t)}(f_{0}(M))
, where
\Psi_{\tau}
is the flow of
Y
and
\tau(t)
is a time reparametrization. Equivalently, a soliton satisfies the identity

\overrightarrow{H}=Y^{\perp},

where
\perp
is the orthogonal projection on the normal bundle. Solitons in
\mathbb{R}^{m+1}
with respect to the homothetically shrinking and to the translating vector fields
Y
give rise, respectively, to classical self-shrinkers and self-translators, that model the singularities developed under the MCF (cf.
[
91
]
). Bernstein type theorems for shrinkers that are graphs over
\mathbb{R}^{m}
have been proved in
[
46
,
152
]
, and for translators in
[
10
]
. Although solitons in more general ambient spaces can no longer describe the blow-up of a singularity, nevertheless they are still relevant since they act as barries for the MCF evolution. Suppose that
\Sigma
is the graph of
u:M\rightarrow I
along the flow lines of a conformal field
X
, and note that
\overrightarrow{H}=mH\nu
. If the soliton field coincides with
\pm X
(
=\pm\partial_{t}
),

(1)

for geodesic graphs, by (
1.10
) equation (
1.11
) specifies to

\mathrm{div}\left(\frac{\nabla u}{\sqrt{1+|\nabla u|^{2}}}\right)=\left[\frac{m\lambda_{t}(u)\pm\lambda^{3}(u)}{\lambda(u)}\right]\frac{1}{\sqrt{1+|\nabla u|^{2}}};

(1.15)

(2)

for equidistant graphs, by (
1.12
) equation (
1.14
) becomes

\overline{\mathrm{div}}_{h}\left(\frac{\bar{\nabla}u}{\sqrt{1+\|\bar{\nabla}u\|^{2}}}\right)=\pm h(x)^{3}\frac{1}{\sqrt{1+\|\bar{\nabla}u\|^{2}}}\qquad\text{on }\,\big(M,\overline{\langle\,,\,\rangle}\big).

(1.16)

1.2
Main properties under investigation

We make a preliminary observation. Suppose that
f
has at least a zero on
\mathbb{R}
: then, by the translation invariance of (
1.4
) with respect to
u
, without loss of generality we can assume that
f(0)=0
. The function
u\equiv 0
is then a solution of
(P_{=})
, and the reduction principle in
[
40
]
(or Lemma
4.6
, see also
[
85
]
and the appendix of
[
2
]
) guarantees that

\begin{array}[]{c}u_{+}=\max\{u,0\}\quad\text{solves $(P_{\geq})$ weakly on $\Omega$.}\\[2.84544pt]
\end{array}

Therefore, when
f
has a zero, without loss of generality we can restrict ourselves to investigate
(P_{\geq})
under the further assumption
f(0)=0
and, if
u>0
somewhere, we can also suppose
u\geq 0
on
\Omega
.

Definition 1.4
.

We say that:

•

the
compact support principle
(shortly,
(\mathrm{CSP})
) holds for
(P_{\geq})
if each non-negative
C^{1}
solution of
(P_{\geq})
on an end
\Omega
of
M
, satisfying
u(x)\rightarrow 0
as
x\rightarrow\infty
in
\Omega
, has compact support, that is,
u\equiv 0
outside some compact set;

•

the
finite maximum principle
(shortly,
(\mathrm{FMP})
) holds for
(P_{\leq})
on the domain
\Omega\subset M
if any non-negative
C^{1}
solution of
(P_{\leq})
for which
u(x_{0})=0
at some
x_{0}\in\Omega
, satisfies
u\equiv 0
on
\Omega
;

•

the
strong Liouville property
(shortly,
(\mathrm{SL})
) holds for
(P_{\geq})
if there exist no non-negative, non-constant
C^{1}
solutions of
(P_{\geq})
on all of
M
.

•

the
Liouville property
(shortly,
(\mathrm{L})
) holds for
(P_{\geq})
if there exist no non-negative, non-constant,
bounded

\mathrm{Lip}_{\mathrm{loc}}
solutions of
(P_{\geq})
on all of
M
.

Remark 1.5
.

Besides the regularity required on
u
, we emphasize that the only difference between properties
(\mathrm{L})
and
(\mathrm{SL})
is that, in
(\mathrm{L})
, we require that the solution of
(P_{\geq})
be
a-priori
bounded.

Remark 1.6

(
Constant solutions
)
.

It is clear, by the properties of
b,f,l
in (
1.5
), that a constant
u=u^{*}
solves
(P_{\geq})
if and only if

\begin{array}[]{l}\displaystyle l(0)=0,\quad\text{independently of $f$,}\ \text{ or }\\[8.5359pt]
\displaystyle l(0)>0,\quad f(u^{*})\leq 0.\end{array}

Therefore, in what follows we will always concentrate on
non-constant
solutions.

Note that
(\mathrm{FMP})
is of a local nature, and thus its validity should not depend on the considered manifold. On the other hand,
(\mathrm{CSP})
,
(\mathrm{L})
and
(\mathrm{SL})
are
global
properties, and for this reason they are expected to depend on the geometry at infinity of
M
and not only on the structure of the operator related to
\varphi,b,f,l
. More precisely, the next scheme summarizes what occurs in general:

\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}\text{geometric conditions}\\[2.84544pt]
\text{related both to $b$}\\[2.84544pt]
\text{and to $\varphi,f,l$}\end{array}\right\}\quad+\quad\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}\text{condition on}\\[2.84544pt]
\text{$\varphi,f,l$}\end{array}\right\}\quad\Longrightarrow\quad\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}\text{\text{either $(\mathrm{SL})$,}}\\[2.84544pt]
\text{or $(\mathrm{L})$,}\\[2.84544pt]
\text{or $(\mathrm{CSP})$}\end{array}\right\}.

(1.17)

We now describe the requirements on
\varphi,f,l
needed in order to possibly obtain
(\mathrm{SL})
or
(\mathrm{CSP})
, and next we will consider the role of geometry and of
b
. We assume

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\displaystyle\mbox{$\varphi\in C^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{+})$, $\qquad\varphi^{\prime}>0$ on $\mathbb{R}^{+}$,}\\[8.5359pt]
\displaystyle\frac{t\varphi^{\prime}(t)}{l(t)}\in L^{1}(0^{+}).\end{array}\right.

(1.18)

Then, the function

K(t)=\int_{0}^{t}\frac{s\varphi^{\prime}(s)}{l(s)}\mathrm{d}s

(1.19)

realizes a homeomorphism of
\mathbb{R}^{+}_{0}
onto its image
[0,K_{\infty})
, with inverse
K^{-1}:[0,K_{\infty})\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{+}_{0}
. Unless otherwise specified, we set

F(t)=\int_{0}^{t}f(s)\mathrm{d}s.

(1.20)

To deal with
(\mathrm{FMP})
and
(\mathrm{CSP})
, we further suppose that

f\geq 0\quad\text{ on some $\ [0,\eta_{0})$, $\ \eta_{0}>0$}.

(1.21)

The validity of
(\mathrm{FMP})
and
(\mathrm{CSP})
is related to the next integrability requirement:

\frac{1}{K^{-1}\circ F}\in L^{1}(0^{+}).

(KO
0
)

More precisely,
(\mathrm{FMP})
depends on the failure of (
KO0
) while
(\mathrm{CSP})
on its validity. Regarding
(\mathrm{SL})
, the relevant condition becomes an integrability at infinity, that to be expressed needs the further assumption

\displaystyle\frac{t\varphi^{\prime}(t)}{l(t)}\not\in L^{1}(\infty),

(1.22)

in order for
K^{-1}
to be defined on
\mathbb{R}^{+}_{0}
(i.e.
K_{\infty}=\infty
). If we now suppose that

f\geq 0\ \text{ on $\mathbb{R}^{+}$,}

(1.23)

then
(\mathrm{SL})
depends on the requirement

\frac{1}{K^{-1}\circ F}\in L^{1}(\infty).

(KO
∞
)

If
l\equiv 1
,
K
coincides with the function

H(t)=t\varphi(t)-\int_{0}^{t}\varphi(s)\mathrm{d}s,\qquad t\geq 0,

(1.24)

that represents the pre-Legendre trasform of

\Phi(t)=\int_{0}^{t}\varphi(s)\mathrm{d}s,

and in this case we recover the necessary and sufficient conditions for
(\mathrm{CSP})
,
(\mathrm{FMP})
and
(\mathrm{SL})
thoroughly investigated in
[
100
,
122
,
123
,
125
]
on
\mathbb{R}^{m}
, see also the references therein. In the case of the
p
-Laplacian where
\varphi(t)=t^{p-1}
, and for
l\equiv 1
, (
KO0
) and (
KO∞
) take, respectively, the well-known form

\frac{1}{F^{1/p}}\in L^{1}(0^{+}),\qquad\frac{1}{F^{1/p}}\in L^{1}(\infty).

(1.25)

Condition (
KO∞
) originated from the works of J.B. Keller and R. Osserman
[
80
,
104
]
for the prototype case

\Delta u\geq f(u)

(1.26)

on
\mathbb{R}^{m}
: in particular, Osserman introduced (
KO∞
) in his investigation on the conformal type of a Riemann surface. For convenience, in what follows we name both (
KO0
) and (
KO∞
) the
Keller-Osserman conditions
. To our knowledge, the study of the relations between Keller-Osserman conditions and the geometry of
M
initiated with the influential paper
[
28
]
by S.Y. Cheng and S.T. Yau, for the semilinear example (
1.26
). An important feature of (
KO0
) and (
KO∞
) to notice is their independence on the underlying space and on the weight
b
. The way geometry relates to the Keller-Osserman conditions in order to give
(\mathrm{SL})
and
(\mathrm{CSP})
is one of the primary concerns of the present work, and will be expressed in terms of sharp curvature or volume growth bounds on
M
, and sharp estimates for
b
. In this respect, in many instances even when
l
is constant such interplay is still partially unclear.

The bridge between geometry and the properties in Definition
1.4
is provided, at least in this paper, by the validity of the weak and strong maximum principles at infinity, that we now define:

Definition 1.7
.

Assume (
1.3
) and fix
b,l
satisfying (
1.5
). We say that

•

(bl)^{-1}\Delta_{\varphi}
satisfies the
weak maximum principle at infinity
, shortly,
(\mathrm{WMP}_{\infty})
, if for each non-constant
u\in\mathrm{Lip}_{\mathrm{loc}}(M)
such that
u^{*}=\sup_{M}u<\infty
, and for each
\eta<u^{*}
,

\inf_{\Omega_{\eta}}\Big\{\Big(b(x)l(|\nabla u|)\Big)^{-1}\Delta_{\varphi}u\Big\}\leq 0,

where

\Omega_{\eta}=\{x\in M\,:\,u(x)>\eta\}

and the inequality has to be intended in the following sense: if
u
solves

\Delta_{\varphi}u\geq Kb(x)l(|\nabla u|)\qquad\text{on }\,\Omega_{\eta},

(1.27)

for some
K\in\mathbb{R}
, then necessarily
K\leq 0
.

•

(bl)^{-1}\Delta_{\varphi}
satisfies the
strong maximum principle at infinity
, shortly,
(\mathrm{SMP}_{\infty})
, if for each non-constant
u\in C^{1}(M)
such that
u^{*}=\sup_{M}u<\infty
, and for each
\eta<u^{*}
,
\varepsilon>0
,

\Omega_{\eta,\varepsilon}=\{x\in M:u(x)>\eta,\,\,|\nabla u(x)|<\varepsilon\}\qquad\text{is non-empty,}

(1.28)

and

\inf_{\Omega_{\eta,\varepsilon}}\Big\{\Big(b(x)l(|\nabla u|)\Big)^{-1}\Delta_{\varphi}u\Big\}\leq 0,

where, again, the inequality has to be intended in the way explained above.

Remark 1.8
.

Condition
\Omega_{\eta,\varepsilon}\neq\emptyset
in (
1.28
) is not automatic: for example, consider the function
u(x)=\exp(-|x|)
on

\mathbb{R}^{m}\backslash\{0\}
, for which
|\nabla u|\rightarrow 1
when
u\rightarrow u^{*}=1
. However, it is easy to see that

\Omega_{\eta,\varepsilon}
is always non-empty if
M
is complete; this can be seen as a consequence of I. Ekeland quasiminimum principle
2
2

2

Here is a quick geometrical reasoning. By contradiction, suppose that
|\nabla u|\geq\varepsilon
on
\Omega_{\eta}
,
and take any maximal flow line
\gamma
of
X=\nabla u/|\nabla u|
starting from some
x\in\Omega_{\eta}

(it might be locally non-unique since
X
is just continuous, but it exists by Peano theorem and
\gamma
is
defined on
\mathbb{R}^{+}
since
X
is bounded). From
\gamma(\mathbb{R}^{+})\subset\Omega_{\eta}
, integrating along
\gamma

would imply
u^{*}=\infty
, against our assumptions.
.

As we shall see in a short while,
(\mathrm{WMP}_{\infty})
and
(\mathrm{SMP}_{\infty})
hold under mild geometric assumptions, involving the Ricci curvature or the volume growth of geodesic balls. Moreover,
(\mathrm{WMP}_{\infty})
is equivalent to
(\mathrm{L})
for each
f
with
f(0)=0
and
f>0
on
\mathbb{R}^{+}
. Both principles relate to (
KO0
) and (
KO∞
) to guarantee, respectively,
(\mathrm{CSP})
and
(\mathrm{SL})
. Hereafter, we denote with
r(x)
the distance of
x
from a fixed subset
\mathcal{O}\subset M
that we call an origin. The origin may be a point (in this case, we denote it by
o
) or a relatively compact, open set with smooth boundary. It is known that
r
is smooth on an open, dense subset
\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{O}}\subset M\backslash\mathcal{O}
, and we denote as usual
\mathrm{cut}(\mathcal{O})=M\backslash(\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{O}}\cup\mathcal{O})
. To better describe our results and properly place them in the literature, we separately comment on each of the properties in Definitions
1.4
and
1.7
.

1.3
The finite maximum principle
(\mathrm{FMP})

Beyond the basic requirements (
1.3
) and (
1.5
), assume

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\displaystyle\mbox{$\varphi\in C^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{+})$, $\qquad\varphi^{\prime}>0$ on $\mathbb{R}^{+}$,}\\[8.5359pt]
\displaystyle\frac{t\varphi^{\prime}(t)}{l(t)}\in L^{1}(0^{+}).\end{array}\right.

(1.29)

We construct
F
and
K
respectively as in (
1.19
) and (
1.20
). If
f>0
in a right neighbourhood of zero, the validity of
(\mathrm{FMP})
turns out to depend on the next non-integrability requirement:

\frac{1}{K^{-1}\circ F}\not\in L^{1}(0^{+}).

(
\neg
KO
0
)

If
l\equiv 1
, then
K
coincides with the function

H(t)=t\varphi(t)-\int_{0}^{t}\varphi(s)\mathrm{d}s,\qquad t\geq 0,

(1.30)

and
f
is non-decreasing and positive, in
[
122
,
125
]
property
(\mathrm{FMP})
is shown to be equivalent to (

¬

KO0
), see also Chapter 5 and Theorem 1.1.1 of
[
123
]
. We presently extend such a characterization to the case of a non-constant function
l
. The literature on the finite maximum principle for quasilinear inequalities is fairly intricate, with contributions from a number of different mathematicians. A detailed and commented account of previous works can be found in
[
123
, p. 125]
and in
[
122
]
. To introduce our main result, we begin with

Definition 1.9
.

We say that a function
h:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is
C
-increasing on
[a,b]
for some constant
C\geq 1
if

\sup_{a\leq s\leq t}h(s)\leq Ch(t).

Clearly,
1
-increasiness corresponds to
h
being non-decreasing, but on the other hand
C
-increasiness allows oscillations of
h
.

Theorem 1.10
.

Let
M
be a Riemannian manifold, and assume that
\varphi,b,f,l
satisfy (
1.3
), (
1.5
) and (
1.29
). Suppose further that

-

f(0)l(0)=0
;

-

f
is non-negative and
C
-increasing on
(0,\eta_{0})
, for some
\eta_{0}>0
;

-

l
is
C
-increasing on
(0,\xi_{0})
, for some
\xi_{0}>0
.

Then,
(\mathrm{FMP})
holds for non-negative solutions
u\in C^{1}(\Omega)
of
(P_{\leq})
on a domain
\Omega\subset M
if and only if either

f\equiv 0\qquad\text{on }\,[0,\eta_{0}),

(1.31)

or

f>0\quad\text{on }\,(0,\eta_{0}),\quad\text{and}\qquad\frac{1}{K^{-1}\circ F}\not\in L^{1}(0^{+}).

(1.32)

Remark 1.11
.

For the sake of clarity, in
[
122
]
no differentiability of
\varphi
is needed: indeed,
\varphi^{\prime}
does not appear in the definition of
H
, and the authors just require
\varphi
to be strictly increasing. However, the presence of a possibly only continuous function
l
forces us to increase the regularity of
\varphi
to be able to define
K
.

Example 1.12
.

Observe that Theorem
1.10
applies to the inequality

\Delta_{p}u\leq u^{\omega}|\nabla u|^{q},

(1.33)

with
\omega\geq 0
,
q\in[0,p)
, to guarantee that
(\mathrm{FMP})
holds if and only if

\omega+q\geq p-1.

The proof of Theorem
1.10
follows the standard method to show Hopf type lemmas, that is, it relies on the construction of suitable radial solutions of
(P_{\geq})
defined on annuli (see
[
122
,
123
]
). However, the study of the related ODE is, for nontrivial gradient terms
l
, considerably more involved than that in
[
123
]
. This calls for a detailed investigation of singular Dirichlet and mixed Dirichlet-Neumann problems for quasilinear ODEs, accomplished in Section
3
. The results therein have independent interest, and are central in many of the main theorems of the present paper.

For the prototype inequality
\Delta u\leq f(u)
, very recently in
[
118
]
the authors succeeded to prove Theorem
1.10

without requiring the
C
-monotonicity of
f
. It is likely that the same is possible also for
(P_{\leq})
in our generality, and so we propose the following

Problem 1
.

Is it possible to prove Theorem
1.10
without requiring the
C
-monotonicity of
f
and
l
? Or, at least, keeping the
C
-monotonicity of just one of them?

1.4
Strong and weak maximum principles at infinity

We start describing the origin of properties
(\mathrm{SMP}_{\infty})
and
(\mathrm{WMP}_{\infty})
, and for simplicity we restrict to the case
b\equiv 1
,
l\equiv 1
and
\Delta_{\varphi}=\Delta
, the Laplace-Beltrami operator. In this case, when
u\in C^{2}(M)
,
(\mathrm{SMP}_{\infty})
and
(\mathrm{WMP}_{\infty})
can
equivalently be restated as the existence of a sequence of points
\{x_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\subset M
such that

\displaystyle u(x_{k})>u^{*}-\frac{1}{k},\qquad\Delta u(x_{k})<\frac{1}{k},\qquad|\nabla u|(x_{k})<\frac{1}{k}

(1.34)

for
(\mathrm{SMP}_{\infty})
to hold, and

\displaystyle u(x_{k})>u^{*}-\frac{1}{k},\qquad\Delta u(x_{k})<\frac{1}{k}

for
(\mathrm{WMP}_{\infty})
to hold. In this case,
(\mathrm{SMP}_{\infty})
is called the Omori-Yau principle, in view of the pioneering papers by H. Omori
[
103
]
and S.T. Yau (cf.
[
156
]
and
[
28
]
, the second with S.Y. Cheng). It proved to be a fundamental tool in investigating geometric problems (see
[
3
]
and the references therein). Omori in
[
103
]
realized that the validity of
(\mathrm{SMP}_{\infty})
is not granted on a generic Riemannian manifold, although it is sufficient that
M
enjoys very mild requirements. For example, by combining works of
[
30
,
128
,
110
,
19
]
, see
[
3
, Thm. 2.4]
,
\Delta
satisfies
(\mathrm{SMP}_{\infty})
whenever

\mathrm{Ric}(\nabla r,\nabla r)\geq-G(r)\qquad\text{on }\,\mathcal{D}_{o},

(1.35)

r(x)
being the distance function from some origin
o
, and
G\in C^{1}(\mathbb{R})
has the following properties:

G\geq 0,\quad G^{\prime}\geq 0,\quad\frac{1}{\sqrt{G}}\not\in L^{1}(\infty).

(1.36)

Clearly, in (
1.35
) and (
1.36
) what really matters is the growth of
G
at infinity. A borderline example is given, for instance, by
G(t)\asymp 1+t^{2}
. This is a particular case of a general criterion discovered in
[
128
,
110
]
, granting the validity of
(\mathrm{SMP}_{\infty})
provided that
M
supports a function satisfying

\begin{array}[]{l}w\in C^{2}(M\backslash K)\ \text{ for some compact $K$},\\[5.69046pt]
w(x)\rightarrow+\infty\ \text{ as }\,x\ \text{ diverges in $M$},\\[5.69046pt]
|\nabla w|\leq\sqrt{G(w)},\qquad\Delta w\leq\sqrt{G(w)}\qquad\text{on }\,M\backslash K,\end{array}

(1.37)

where
G
meets the requirements in (
1.36
). For reasons that will be soon justified, we call
w
a
strong Khas’minskii
potential. To the best of our knowledge, this is essentially the only effective known condition, and
w
is often explicitly given not exclusively via curvature bounds like (
1.35
), but also by the geometrical nature of the problem at hand. This is the case, for instance, of immersed submanifolds, where
w
depends on extrinsic data, and of generic Ricci soliton structures, see
[
3
]
.

When the operator is nonlinear and non-homogeneous, instead of a single function
w
we need a family of Khas’minskii potentials, see Section
6
below and Chapter 3 in
[
3
]
. For
b^{-1}\Delta_{\varphi}
,
(\mathrm{SMP}_{\infty})
has been studied in
[
110
, Sec. 6]
and
[
119
, Thm 1.1]
, and again a family of potentials of strong Khas’minskii type is exhibited to ensure
(\mathrm{SMP}_{\infty})
under an appropriate Ricci curvature bound. The construction of the potential in these papers is hand-made and appears not easily generalizable to cover the case of a non-constant
l
. Therefore, although our present strategy to prove
(\mathrm{SMP}_{\infty})
for
(bl)^{-1}\Delta_{\varphi}
is still based on finding a strong Khas’minskii potential family, the construction of the latter relies on a different approach involving the study of the maximal domain of existence and the asymptotic behaviour of solutions of a singular two-points boundary ODE problem, see Sections
3
and
6.1
.

For the convenience of the reader, we summarize the assumptions of
\varphi,l
in the following:

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\displaystyle\varphi\in C(\mathbb{R}^{+}_{0})\cap C^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{+}),\qquad\varphi(0)=0,\qquad\varphi^{\prime}>0\ \text{ on }\,\mathbb{R}^{+};\\[8.5359pt]
l\in C(\mathbb{R}_{0}^{+}),\qquad l>0\ \text{on }\,\mathbb{R}^{+};\\[8.5359pt]
\displaystyle\frac{t\varphi^{\prime}(t)}{l(t)}\in L^{1}(0^{+}).\end{array}\right.

(1.38)

We shall also require the growth conditions

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\displaystyle l(t)\geq C_{1}\frac{\varphi(t)}{t^{\chi}}&\qquad\text{on $(0,1]$, for some $C_{1}>0$, $\chi\geq 0$};\\[11.38092pt]
\displaystyle\varphi(t)\leq C_{2}t^{p-1}&\qquad\text{on $[0,1]$, for some $C_{2}>0$, $p>1$.}\end{array}\right.

(1.39)

Remark 1.13
.

Since
l
is continuous up to zero, if
\varphi(t)\asymp t^{p-1}
near
t=0
the first condition in (
1.39
) forces the upper bound
\chi\leq p-1
. For example, in the
p
-Laplacian case where
\varphi(t)=t^{p-1}
, chosen
l(t)=t^{q}
, the first in (
1.39
) holds if and only if
q\in[0,p-1]
. Furthermore, to recover the case
l
constant the best choice of
\chi
is

\chi=p-1;

the choice
\chi=0
represents the borderline case of strong gradient dependence
l(t)\asymp\varphi(t)
near
t=0
. The latter often needs a special care to be treated.

We express our main result in terms of a sharp condition on the Ricci tensor.

Theorem 1.14
.

Let
M
be a complete
m
-dimensional manifold such that, for some fixed origin
o\in M
, the distance
r(x)
from
o
satisfies

\mathrm{Ric}(\nabla r,\nabla r)\geq-(m-1)\kappa^{2}\big(1+r^{2}\big)^{\alpha/2}\qquad\text{on }\,\mathcal{D}_{o},

(1.40)

for some
\kappa\geq 0
,
\alpha\geq-2
. Let
l
and
\varphi
satisfy (
1.38
) and (
1.39
). Consider
0<b\in C(M)
such that

b(x)\geq C\big(1+r(x)\big)^{-\mu}\qquad\text{on }\,M,

for some constants
C>0
,
\mu\in\mathbb{R}
. If

\mu\leq\chi-\frac{\alpha}{2}\quad\text{and either}\quad\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\alpha\geq-2\quad\text{and}\quad\chi>0,\quad\text{or}\\[5.69046pt]
\alpha=-2,\quad\chi=0\quad\text{and}\quad\bar{\kappa}\leq\frac{p-1}{m-1},\end{array}\right.

(1.41)

with
\bar{\kappa}=\frac{1}{2}\big(1+\sqrt{1+4\kappa^{2}}\big)
, then
(bl)^{-1}\Delta_{\varphi}
satisfies
(\mathrm{SMP}_{\infty})
.

In particular, the Euclidean space
M=\mathbb{R}^{m}
is recovered by choosing
\kappa=0
,
\alpha=-2
, while, to deal with the hyperbolic space
\mathbb{H}^{m}
of constant sectional curvature
-1
we choose
\kappa=1
,
\alpha=0
. Even for these model manifolds, Theorem
1.14
, in the above generality on
b
and
l
, is new. As an example, Corollary
6.7
in Section
6.1
expresses the result for the mean curvature operator both in
\mathbb{R}^{m}
and in
\mathbb{H}^{m}
.

Often in geometry,
(\mathrm{SMP}_{\infty})
is used to infer a-priori estimates, and consequently Liouville type theorems, for solutions of PDEs. As an example of this type of application, in Corollary
6.8
we establish sharp estimates for bounded above solutions of differential inequalities of the form

\Delta_{p}u\geq b(x)f(u)|\nabla u|^{q}-\bar{b}(x)\bar{f}(u)|\nabla u|^{\bar{q}}.

(1.42)

A prototype example is given by

\Delta_{p}u\geq f(u)-c|\nabla u|^{q},\qquad\text{with $q>0$, $c>0$,}

that in the semilinear case appear as value functions of stochastic control problems (cf.
[
84
,
126
]
). Existence and nonexistence of entire solutions have been investigated in a series of papers, notably
[
83
,
60
,
56
,
52
,
95
]
.

Next, we turn our attention to
(\mathrm{WMP}_{\infty})
, which has been introduced in
[
108
]
following the authors’ observation that, in many geometric applications, the gradient condition in (
1.34
) was unnecessary. It has various advantages with respect to
(\mathrm{SMP}_{\infty})
: first, it can be stated for
u\in W^{1,p}_{\mathrm{loc}}(M)
,
p\geq 1
, which is a natural regularity class for solutions of
(P_{\geq})
; second, the absence of the gradient bound allows to directly use the weak formulation together with refined integral estimates, to obtain sharp criteria for
(\mathrm{WMP}_{\infty})
that just depend on the volume growth of geodesic balls
B_{r}
, a requirement implied, but not equivalent, to (
1.40
). This approach will be described in more detail below.

Remark 1.15
.

It is important to observe that there exist manifolds satisfying
(\mathrm{WMP}_{\infty})
but
not

(\mathrm{SMP}_{\infty})
, hence the two principle are different. Counterexamples are very easy to construct in the setting of incomplete manifolds (indeed,
\mathbb{R}^{m}\backslash\{0\}
satisfies
(\mathrm{WMP}_{\infty})
but not
(\mathrm{SMP}_{\infty})
, see
[
110
]
), and a nice example in the complete case appeared very recently in
[
20
]
.

First, we introduce the following characterizazion improving on
[
108
,
112
,
96
]
. Despite the simplicity of the proof, the equivalences stressed below are particularly useful in geometric applications.

Proposition 1.16
.

Let
\varphi
and
b,f,l
satisfy respectively (
1.3
) and (
1.5
). Then, the following properties are equivalent:

(i)

(bl)^{-1}\Delta_{\varphi}
satisfies
(\mathrm{WMP}_{\infty})
;

(ii)

(\mathrm{L})
holds for some (equivalently, every)
f
with

f(0)=0,\qquad f>0\qquad\text{on }\,\mathbb{R}^{+};

(iii)

each
u\in\mathrm{Lip}_{\mathrm{loc}}(M)
solving
(P_{\geq})
on
M
and bounded above satisfies
f(u^{*})\leq 0
.

It should be stressed that, by a generalization of work of R.Z. Khas’minskii
[
78
]
(see
[
63
]
for a nice exposition),
(\mathrm{L})
with the choice
f(t)=\lambda t
and
\lambda>0
is related to the theory of the (minimal) Brownian motion on
M
, and indeed equivalent to the stochastic completeness of
M
, that is, the infinite lifetime of a.e. Brownian path. Exploiting this last equivalence, A. GrigorYan in
[
63
, Thm. 9.1]
found the weakest known geometric condition on a complete
M
for
\Delta
to satisfy
(\mathrm{L})
with
f(t)=\lambda t
and
\lambda>0
, that is,

\frac{r}{\log\mathrm{vol}(B_{r})}\not\in L^{1}(\infty).

However, the beautiful method of proof in
[
63
]
relies on the linearity of the Laplace-Beltrami operator. Hence, the search for similar volume conditions for general
\Delta_{\varphi}
calls for different ideas, developed in a series of works
[
77
,
109
,
134
,
135
]
and refined in
[
110
,
95
]
. Our contributions are contained in Theorems
5.4
and
5.15
below.

Remark 1.17
.

A characterization similar to that of Proposition
1.16
holds for
(\mathrm{L})
when
f\equiv 0
in a right neighbourhood of zero. In fact, by
[
112
, Thm. A]
(for
\Delta_{p}
) and
[
110
]
(general
\Delta_{\varphi}
), for these
f
’s property
(\mathrm{L})
is equivalent to the parabolicity of
\Delta_{\varphi}
, see also
[
96
]
. For the
p
-Laplace operator, parabolicity is more often introduced via capacity estimates. In this respect, we refer to
[
63
]
for
p=2
, and
[
147
,
146
,
68
]
for a general
p
.

Remark 1.18
.

Khas’minskii introduced a sufficient condition for
M
to be stochastically complete in terms of the existence of
w
satisfying all of the properties in (
1.37
) but that on the gradient, with
G(t)=\lambda t
,
\lambda>0
, see
[
78
,
63
]
. This justifies the name strong Khas’minskii condition given to (
1.37
). It should be observed that, for a large class of operators including some geometrically relevant fully nonlinear ones, appropriate Khas’minskii conditions turn out to be equivalent to suitably defined maximum principles at infinity, see
[
96
]
and the recent
[
93
]
.

To introduce a special case of our main Theorem
5.4
, observe that (
1.40
) implies, via the Bishop-Gromov comparison theorem, the following estimates

\begin{array}[]{ll}\displaystyle\limsup_{r\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\log\mathrm{vol}(B_{r})}{r^{1+\alpha/2}}<\infty&\quad\text{if }\,\alpha>-2,\\[14.22636pt]
\displaystyle\limsup_{r\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\log\mathrm{vol}(B_{r})}{\log r}\leq(m-1)\bar{\kappa}+1&\quad\text{if }\,\alpha>-2,\end{array}

(1.43)

with

\bar{k}=\frac{1}{2}\big(1+\sqrt{1+4\kappa^{2}}\big).

Regarding our assumptions on
\varphi
and
l
, differently from (
1.38
) we now require the milder

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\displaystyle\varphi\in C(\mathbb{R}^{+}_{0}),\qquad\varphi(0)=0,\qquad\varphi>0\ \text{ on }\,\mathbb{R}^{+};\\[8.5359pt]
l\in C(\mathbb{R}_{0}^{+}),\qquad l>0\ \text{on }\,\mathbb{R}^{+}.\end{array}\right.

(1.44)

We also need the next growth conditions, to be compared with those in (
1.39
).

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\displaystyle l(t)\geq C_{1}\frac{\varphi(t)}{t^{\chi}}&\qquad\text{on $\mathbb{R}^{+}$, for some $C_{1}>0$, $\chi\geq 0$};\\[11.38092pt]
\displaystyle\varphi(t)\leq C_{2}t^{p-1}&\qquad\text{on $[0,1]$, for some $C_{2}>0$, $p>1$;}\\[8.5359pt]
\displaystyle\varphi(t)\leq\bar{C}_{2}t^{\bar{p}-1}&\qquad\text{on $[1,\infty)$, for some $\bar{C}_{2}>0$, $\bar{p}>1$.}\end{array}\right.

(1.45)

The use of different upper bounds for
\varphi(t)
related to its behaviour near
t=0
and
t=\infty
is crucial to obtain sharp results in the setting, for instance, of the mean curvature operator. We are now ready to state

Theorem 1.19
.

Let
M
be a complete
m
-dimensional manifold. Fix
\alpha\geq-2
and suppose that

\begin{array}[]{ll}\displaystyle\liminf_{r\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\log\mathrm{vol}(B_{r})}{r^{1+\alpha/2}}=V_{\infty}<\infty&\quad\text{if }\,\alpha>-2;\\[11.38092pt]
\displaystyle\liminf_{r\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\log\mathrm{vol}(B_{r})}{\log r}=V_{\infty}<\infty&\quad\text{if }\,\alpha=-2.\\[11.38092pt]
\end{array}

(1.46)

Let
\varphi
and
l
satisfy (
1.44
) and (
1.45
), and consider
0<b\in C(M)
such that

b(x)\geq C\big(1+r(x)\big)^{-\mu}\qquad\text{on }\,M,

for some constants
C>0
,
\mu\in\mathbb{R}
. Suppose:

\mu\leq\chi-\frac{\alpha}{2}\quad\text{and either}\quad\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\alpha\geq-2,\quad\chi>0,\quad\text{or}\\[5.69046pt]
\alpha\geq-2,\quad\chi=0,\quad\mu<-\frac{\alpha}{2},\quad\text{or }\\[5.69046pt]
\alpha>-2,\quad\chi=0,\quad\mu=-\frac{\alpha}{2},\quad V_{\infty}=0,\quad\text{or }\\[5.69046pt]
\alpha=-2,\quad\chi=0,\quad\mu=-\frac{\alpha}{2},\quad V_{\infty}\leq p.\end{array}\right.

(1.47)

Then,
(bl)^{-1}\Delta_{\varphi}
satisfies
(\mathrm{WMP}_{\infty})
.

Remark 1.20
.

As underlined in Remark
1.13
,
p
and
\bar{p}
are implicitly related to bounds on
\chi
via (
1.45
). However, we feel remarkable that
p,\bar{p}
do not appear in conditions (
1.47
), apart from the last borderline case. A detailed discussion follows the statement of Theorem
5.4
in Section
5
.

Suitable counterexamples show the sharpness of Theorem
5.4
, and consequently of Theorem
1.19
, with respect to each parameter involved. In particular, the restrictions in (
1.47
) are sharp.

Conjecture 1.21
.

In the setting of Theorem
1.14
, the full
(\mathrm{SMP}_{\infty})
holds if the range (
1.41
) is replaced by (
1.47
).

To better appreciate the range of applicability of Theorem
1.19
, we state as a direct corollary the following extension of Do Carmo-Lawson’s Theorem
1.3
in the minimal setting. The result is a particular case of Theorems
5.17
and
5.18
below.

Theorem 1.22
.

Let
M
be a complete manifold, and consider the warped product
\bar{M}=\mathbb{R}\times_{h}M
, with warping function
h
satisfying either

(i)

h
is strictly convex and
h^{-1}\in L^{1}(-\infty)\cap L^{1}(+\infty)
, or

(ii)

h^{\prime}>0
on
\mathbb{R}
,
h^{\prime}(s)\geq C
for
s>>1
and
h^{-1}\in L^{1}(+\infty)
.

If

\liminf_{r\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\log\mathrm{vol}(B_{r})}{r^{2}}<\infty,

then

under
(i)
, the only entire minimal graph is the constant
u=s_{0}
, with
s_{0}
the unique minimum of
h
.

under
(ii)
, there exists no entire minimal graph.

The corresponding statement for variable mean curvature will be given in Theorem
6.11
, under the validity of
(\mathrm{SMP}_{\infty})
. It should be noted that, besides bounded solutions, in Theorem
5.4
we can also consider solutions
u
of

\Delta_{\varphi}u\geq b(x)f(u)l(|\nabla u|)\qquad\text{on }\,\Omega_{\eta}=\{u>\eta\}

with a controlled growth at infinity.
Indeed, under appropriate assumptions, the theorem guarantees both
u^{*}<\infty
and
f(u^{*})\leq 0
. The result is a significant improvement of
[
95
, Thm 5.1]
and
[
2
, Thm. 2.1]
; it applies, for instance, to differential inequalities with borderline gradient dependence of the type

\Delta_{\varphi}u\geq b(x)f(u)\varphi(|\nabla u|)\qquad\text{on }\,M,

to ensure that, under mild assumptions, any solution that grows polynomially is bounded from above and satisfies
f(u^{*})\leq 0
. Recall that, by definition,
u
grows polynomially if there exists
\sigma\geq 0
such that

|u(x)|=O\big(r(x)^{\sigma}\big)\qquad\text{as }\,r(x)\rightarrow\infty.

The reader that is interested in such borderline examples can see Corollary
5.8
, as well as Theorem
5.11
in the particular setting of the mean curvature operator. We report here the following application to entire vertical self-translators of the mean curvature flow:

Theorem 1.23
.

Let
(M^{m},\langle\,,\,\rangle)
be a complete manifold and consider the product
\bar{M}^{m+1}=\mathbb{R}\times M
. Fix
0\leq\sigma\leq 2
and suppose that either

\begin{array}[]{ll}\sigma<2&\quad\displaystyle\text{and}\qquad\liminf_{r\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\log\mathrm{vol}(B_{r})}{r^{2-\sigma}}<\infty,\qquad\text{or}\\[14.22636pt]
\sigma=2&\quad\displaystyle\text{and}\qquad\liminf_{r\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\log\mathrm{vol}(B_{r})}{\log r}<\infty.\end{array}

(1.48)

Then, there exist no entire graph
\Sigma\subset\bar{M}
of
v:M\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
which is a self-translator for the MCF with respect to the vertical direction
\partial_{s}
and satisfies

|v(x)|=o\big(r(x)^{\sigma}\big)\qquad\text{as }\,r(x)\rightarrow\infty.

(1.49)

Remark 1.24
.

Specializing Theorem
1.23
in Euclidean space
\mathbb{R}^{m+1}
, there is no entire graph
v:\mathbb{R}^{m}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
over the horizontal
\mathbb{R}^{m}
which is a self-translator in the vertical direction and satisfies
v=o(r^{2})
as
r\rightarrow\infty
. The result is sharp, since the bowl soliton in
\mathbb{R}^{m+1}
(cf.
[
5
]
and
[
31
, Lem. 2.2]
) and the non-rotational manifolds in
[
151
]
for
m\geq 3
are examples of rotationally symmetric, entire (convex) graphs which translate vertically by MCF and have order of growth
r^{2}
.

Other applications for entire self-translators in
\mathbb{R}^{m+1}
(not necessarily vertical) and for entire self-expanders will be given in Theorems
5.20
and
5.23
, respectively.

Results in the spirit of Corollary
5.8
below, with a dependence on the gradient, appear in
[
49
,
50
]
on Euclidean space
\mathbb{R}^{m}
, and will be compared with ours in Section
5
. In a manifold setting, due to the possible lack of a polynomial bound for the growth of the volume of geodesic balls, the integral methods in
[
49
,
50
,
40
,
38
,
24
]
are, in most of the cases, not sufficient to get sharp conclusions. Indeed, even in the polynomial setting of Euclidean space, Theorem
5.4
complements and in some cases improves on the existing literature.

We conclude with the next observations: in view of the volume estimates (
1.43
) that follow from the Ricci bound (
1.40
), Theorems
1.14
and
1.19
hold precisely for the same range of
\alpha,\mu,\chi
(aside from some borderline cases covered by (
1.47
) but not by (
1.41
)). In view of this, the following problem seems interesting to us:

Problem 2
.

Prove or disprove by exhibiting a counterexample (in a complete manifold), the validity of
(\mathrm{SMP}_{\infty})
in the assumptions of Theorem
1.19
.

The strong Liouville property
(\mathrm{SL})

In the literature, the validity of
(\mathrm{SL})
has been mainly investigated
by means of two different approaches: radialization techniques and refined comparison theorems

[
95
,
124
,
120
,
21
]
, or integral estimates, in the
spirit of the work of E. Mitidieri and S.I. Pohozaev
[
98
]
, see
[
38
,
49
,
50
,
40
,
142
]
.
Assume the validity of (
1.29
) and (
1.22
), in order for the function
K
in (
1.19
) to realize a homeomorphism of
\mathbb{R}^{+}_{0}
onto itself. Suppose that

f>0\qquad\text{on }\,(T,\infty),\quad\text{for some }\,T\geq 0,

and set

F(t)=\int_{T}^{t}f(s)\mathrm{d}s.

(1.50)

Under these assumptions, the proof of the validity of
(\mathrm{SL})
via radialization techniques relies on the construction of suitable blowing-up radial supersolutions, explicitly related to the Keller-Osserman condition

\frac{1}{K^{-1}\circ F}\in L^{1}(\infty).

(
\mathrm{KO}_{\infty}
)

As far as we know, (

KO
∞

) first appeared for nontrivial
l
in the work of R. Redheffer
[
129
]
(Corollary 1 therein) for the inequality
\Delta u\geq f(u)l(|\nabla u|)
. Since then, it has been systematically studied by various authors. Among them, for nontrivial
l
we quote

-

[
25
]
(for the 1-dimensional problem),
[
9
,
61
,
53
]
(when
\Delta_{\varphi}
is the mean curvature operator) and
[
97
,
57
,
53
]
(when
\Delta_{\varphi}
is the
p
-Laplacian);

-

in a sub-Riemannian setting,
[
89
,
23
,
21
,
22
,
2
]
.

For further generalizations to quasilinear inequalities, possibly with singular or degenerate weights, we refer to
[
37
,
39
,
54
,
98
]
.

We first discuss the necessity of (
KO∞
) for
(\mathrm{SL})
, and recall the following

Definition 1.25
.

A point
o\in M
is said to be a
pole
if the exponential map
\exp_{o}:T_{o}M\thickapprox\mathbb{R}^{m}\rightarrow M
is a diffeomorphism.

It can be proved that
o
is a pole for
M
if and only if the distance function
r(x)=\mathrm{dist}(x,o)
is smooth outside of
o
, see
[
106
]
and the references therein. The radial sectional curvature
K_{\mathrm{rad}}
is, by definition, the sectional curvature restricted to planes containing
\nabla r
. As a particular case of Theorem
8.4
below, we obtain

Theorem 1.26

(
Necessity of (
KO∞
)
)
.

Let
M^{m}
be a complete Riemannian manifold with a pole
o\in M
, and assume that

K_{\mathrm{rad}}(x)\leq-\frac{1}{4r(x)^{2}}\qquad\text{for }\,x\in M\backslash\{o\}.

(1.51)

Let
\varphi,b,f,l
satisfy (
1.38
), (
1.22
), (
1.5
) and

f(0)=0,\qquad f>0\ \text{ and $C$-increasing on }\,\mathbb{R}^{+}.

Then, (
KO∞
) is necessary for the validity of
(\mathrm{SL})
.

Remark 1.27
.

Note that, with the notation
K_{\mathrm{rad}}(x)\leq G(r(x))
for some
G\in C(\mathbb{R}^{+}_{0})
, we mean that the sectional curvature satisfies

K(X\wedge\nabla r)(x)\leq-G\big(r(x)\big)

(1.52)

for each
x\in M\backslash\{o\}
and
X\perp\nabla r(x)
,
|X|=1
, where
X\wedge\nabla r
is the
2
-plane spanned by
X
and
\nabla r
and
K
is the sectional curvature.

Inequality (
1.51
) is a mild requirement just needed to ensure that the model to be compared to
M
is complete and the volume of its geodesic spheres increases. It allows to apply Theorem
1.26
to all
Cartan-Hadamard manifolds
, namely complete, simply-connected manifolds with non-positive sectional curvature, a class that includes both the Euclidean and the hyperbolic space. This might suggest that (
1.51
) be just a technical assumption (although not easy to remove) and thus, loosely speaking, that geometry does not to affect implication
(\mathrm{SL})

\Rightarrow
(
KO∞
).

On the contrary, the sufficiency of (
KO∞
) heavily depends on the validity of maximum principles at infinity. To investigate the interplay, it is worth to consider
(\mathrm{SL})
as the combination of two properties:

-

an
L^{\infty}
-estimate
for non-negative solutions of
(P_{\geq})
;

-

property
(\mathrm{L})
for bounded, non-negative solutions of
(P_{\geq})
.

Note that (

KO
∞

) plays a role in the first property, while, by Proposition (
1.16
), the second property is equivalent to
(\mathrm{WMP}_{\infty})
provided that
f(0)=0
and
f>0
on
\mathbb{R}^{+}
. As a first result, Theorem
8.6
below relates directly
(\mathrm{SMP}_{\infty})
to
(\mathrm{SL})
, by showing that for some classes of operators, notably including the
p
-Laplacian with constant
b
, and for general
f
with
f>0
on
\mathbb{R}^{+}
,

\text{\eqref{KO}}\quad+\quad\text{$(\mathrm{SMP}_{\infty})$}\quad\Longrightarrow\quad\text{$(\mathrm{SL})$}.

(1.53)

However, for more general operators, in particular for non-homogeneous ones, such a simple relation is currently unknown. Nevertheless, for large classes of functions
\varphi,b,f,l
, we can guarantee
(\mathrm{SL})
by coupling (
KO∞
) with the lower Ricci curvature bounds considered in Theorem
1.14
, the latter being sharp for the validity of
(\mathrm{SMP}_{\infty})
. This is the content of Theorems
8.20
and
8.21
below, dealing respectively with the case
\chi>0
and
\chi=0
, that should be considered the main results of Subsection
8.2
. We refer therein for the statements in full generality, and quote the following corollary for the mean curvature operator:

Theorem 1.28
.

Let
M^{m}
be complete and assume that

\mathrm{Ric}(\nabla r,\nabla r)\geq-(m-1)\kappa^{2}\big(1+r^{2}\big)^{\alpha/2}\qquad\text{on }\,\mathcal{D}_{o},

(1.54)

for some
\kappa\geq 0
,
\alpha\geq-2
and some origin
o
. Let
b,f,l
satisfying (
1.5
) and

\begin{array}[]{l}\displaystyle b(x)\geq C_{1}\big(1+r(x)\big)^{-\mu}\qquad\text{on }\,M,\\[8.5359pt]
f(0)=0,\quad\text{$f>0\ \ $ and $C$-increasing on }\,\mathbb{R}^{+},\\[8.5359pt]
\displaystyle l(t)\geq C_{1}\frac{t^{1-\chi}}{\sqrt{1+t^{2}}}\qquad\text{on }\,\mathbb{R}^{+},\end{array}

for some constants
C,C_{1}>0
,
\mu\in\mathbb{R}
,
\chi\in(0,1]
with

\mu\leq\chi-\frac{\alpha}{2}.

Then, under the validity of the Keller-Osserman condition

F^{-\frac{1}{\chi+1}}\in L^{1}(\infty)

(1.55)

with
F
as in (
1.50
),
(\mathrm{SL})
holds for
C^{1}
solutions of

\mathrm{div}\left(\frac{\nabla u}{\sqrt{1+|\nabla u|^{2}}}\right)\geq b(x)f(u)l(|\nabla u|)\qquad\text{on }\,M.

(1.56)

Suitable counterexamples will show the sharpness of (
1.55
), that seems to be new even in the Euclidean and hyperbolic space setting, corresponding, respectively, to
\alpha=-2
and
\alpha=0
.

Remark 1.29
.

If
\chi=0
and no Keller-Osserman condition is assumed, a Liouville theorem that well matches with the above result can be found in Theorem
5.11
below.

When
f(t)
is a power of
t
, say
f(t)\asymp t^{\omega}
, (
1.55
) becomes
\omega>\chi
. In this case, we will prove
(\mathrm{SL})
under a mere volume growth requirement. More precisely, we have the following

Theorem 1.30
.

Assume that the conditions in Theorem
1.19
are satisfied, with the second and third of (
1.45
) replaced by

\varphi(t)\leq C_{2}t^{p-1}\quad\text{for }\,t\in\mathbb{R}^{+},

(1.57)

for some
p>1
,
C>0
. Let
f\in C(\mathbb{R})
satisfy

f(t)\geq C_{2}t^{\omega}\qquad\text{for some $C>0$ and each }\,t\geq 1.

If
\omega>\chi
, then any non-constant
u\in\mathrm{Lip}_{\mathrm{loc}}(M)
solution of
(P_{\geq})
on
M
is bounded from above and satisfies
f(u^{*})\leq 0
. In particular, if
f>0
on
\mathbb{R}^{+}
,
(\mathrm{SL})
holds for
\mathrm{Lip}_{\mathrm{loc}}
solutions.

Remark 1.31
.

If
\varphi\in C^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{+})
and
t\varphi^{\prime}(t)\asymp\varphi(t)
as
t\rightarrow\infty
, like for instance in the case of the
p
-Laplacian, a direct check shows that
\omega>\chi
in the above theorem imply (

KO
∞

), and it is equivalent to it whenever, instead of (
1.44
),
l
satisfies

l(t)t^{\chi}\asymp\varphi(t)\qquad\text{as }\,t\rightarrow\infty.

The argument of the proof of Theorem
1.30
, though close in spirit to that of Theorem
1.19
, uses a different combination of integral estimates. Nevertheless, unlike
[
98
,
38
,
49
,
50
,
40
]
which treat similar results in
\mathbb{R}^{m}
, our method has again the advantage to work in settings where the volume growth of geodesic balls is not polynomial. This requires an iterative procedure originally due to
[
109
,
110
]
, of independent interest, but the appearance of the function
l
also requires a careful mixing with techniques in
[
50
]
.

To describe the range of applicability of Theorem
1.30
, we consider the capillarity equation

\mathrm{div}\left(\frac{\nabla u}{\sqrt{1+|\nabla u|^{2}}}\right)=\kappa(x)u\qquad\text{on }\,M,

(1.58)

modelling a graphical interface in
M\times\mathbb{R}
whose mean curvature is proportional to the height of the graph via the non-homogeneous coefficient
\kappa(x)
. Then, we have

Theorem 1.32
.

Suppose that
M
is complete and that

\kappa(x)\geq C\big(1+r(x)\big)^{-\mu}\qquad\text{on }\,M,

(1.59)

for some constants
C>0
and
\mu<2
. If there exists
\varepsilon>0
such that

\liminf_{r\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\log\mathrm{vol}B_{r}}{r^{2-\varepsilon-\mu}}<\infty,

(1.60)

then the only solution of the capillarity equation (
1.58
) on
M
is
u\equiv 0
.

To the best of our knowledge, Theorem
8.39
seems to us to be the first result considering entire solutions of (
8.115
) in a manifold setting, in particular allowing that the volume of geodesic balls grows faster than polynomially. Nevertheless, it is of interest even for Euclidean space, guaranteeing
u\equiv 0
whenever
\mu<2
. To our knowledge, when
\kappa>0
is constant the vanishing of
u
on
\mathbb{R}^{m}
solving (
1.58
) was first obtained in
[
144
,
100
]
with no growth assumptions on
u
(cf. also
[
123
, Thm. 8.1.3]
for
u
growing polynomially). The methods in
[
144
,
100
]
are different from one another; in particular, the one in
[
144
]
has later been extended in
[
141
]
to more general inequalities, and Theorem
1.32
is shown to hold on
\mathbb{R}^{m}
but only for
\mu<1
. Recently, in
[
49
]
the authors were able to achieve the sharp bound
\mu<2
for solutions on
\mathbb{R}^{m}
. In Section
8.5.2
, we will describe in more detail the relationship between our result and the ones in
[
49
]
, and we will improve on
[
144
,
100
,
123
,
141
,
49
]
for a class of equations including (
1.58
). We stress that none of the methods therein easily adapt to manifolds just satisfying (
1.60
).

Remark 1.33
.

Theorem
1.32
has a curious and unexpected feature: although (
1.58
) does not contain a gradient term, the “artificial” inclusion of a suitable
l(|\nabla u|)
in the right-hand side of (
1.58
) is the key to prove the corollary as a consequence of the Keller-Osserman condition
\omega>\chi
in Theorem
1.30
(note that
\omega=1
for (
1.58
)). This is in striking contrast with previous results for equation

\mathrm{div}\left(\frac{\nabla u}{\sqrt{1+|\nabla u|^{2}}}\right)=b(x)f(u),

(1.61)

in a manifold setting: for instance, to obtain the vanishing of
u
in (
1.61
) when
f(t)t\geq C|t|^{\omega+1}
on
\mathbb{R}
, Theorem 4.8 in
[
110
]
needs inequality
\omega>1
, which does not hold for (
1.58
). Loosely speaking, inserting a suitable gradient term enables us to weaken the requirement in the Keller-Osserman condition up to include the capillarity equation.

Observing that the volume growth conditions in Theorem
1.30
coincide with those in Theorem
1.19
for the validity of
(\mathrm{WMP}_{\infty})
, one might wonder whether (
1.53
) can be weakened to

\text{\eqref{KO}}\quad+\quad(\mathrm{WMP}_{\infty})\quad\Longrightarrow\quad(\mathrm{SL}).

(1.62)

In Example
8.12
below, we will show that
(\mathrm{WMP}_{\infty})
is
not
sufficient, and the full strength of
(\mathrm{SMP}_{\infty})
is needed. The counterexample is, however, on an incomplete manifold, and suggests the following

Problem 3
.

Prove or disprove: if
M
is a complete manifold, then the implication (
1.62
) holds at least for a subclass of operators
\Delta_{\varphi}
and
b,f,l
.

1.5
The compact support principle
(\mathrm{CSP})

Unlike that on
(\mathrm{L})
and
(\mathrm{SL})
, the literature on
(\mathrm{CSP})
is not so extensive. The subject initiated with the seminal paper by R. Redheffer
[
131
]
, and received a renewed interest in the last 15 years starting from
[
125
]
, see also
[
64
,
117
,
51
]
and the monograph
[
123
]
. However, all of these works consider the problem in the setting of Euclidean space, and to our knowledge just
[
120
,
132
,
136
]
analyze the role played by the geometry of the manifold. As we shall see, the link between geometry and
(\mathrm{CSP})
does not depend on the validity of a maximum principles at infinity: to explain which geometric conditions are to be expected, we first comment on the following result in
[
120
, Thm. 1.1]
:

Theorem 1.34

(
[
120
]
)
.

Let
M
be a complete manifold, and let
r
be the distance from a fixed origin
o
. Assume (
1.3
) and that
\varphi
is strictly increasing on
\mathbb{R}^{+}
. Let
f\in C(\mathbb{R})
satisfy

\quad f(0)=0,\qquad f>0\quad\text{and non-decreasing on some }\,[0,\eta_{0}),\ \ \eta_{0}>0.

(1.63)

Then, in order for
(\mathrm{CSP})
to hold for
(P_{\geq})
with
b(x)=1
,
l(t)=1
it is necessary that

\frac{1}{H^{-1}\circ F}\in L^{1}(0^{+}),

(1.64)

where
F
and
H
are defined in (
1.20
) and (
1.30
). Viceversa, (
1.64
) is also
sufficient for
(\mathrm{CSP})
provided that

\inf_{M}\Delta r>-\infty

(1.65)

holds in the weak sense.

As a matter of fact, as we prove in Proposition
9.6
in Appendix A, (
1.65
) forces the origin
o
to be a pole for
M
, in particular
r
shall be smooth outside of
M\backslash\{o\}
. With the aid of the Hessian comparison theorem, (
1.65
) holds for a large class of manifolds including Cartan-Hadamard ones, such as the Euclidean and hyperbolic spaces. On the other hand, the topological restriction imposed by the existence of a pole is binding, and it would be desirable to remove it. However, already in
[
120
]
the authors realized that a condition like (
1.65
) or some other extra assumption needs necessarily to be included. Their example, reported below, is illustrative.

Example 1.35
.

Consider the radially symmetric model

M=(\mathbb{R}^{m},\mathrm{d}s_{g}^{2}),\qquad\mathrm{d}s_{g}^{2}=\mathrm{d}r^{2}+g(r)^{2}(\,,\,)_{1},

where
(\,,\,)_{1}
is the standard round metric on the unit sphere and
0<g\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{+})
satisfy
g(r)=r
for
r\in[0,1]
and
g(r)=\exp\{-r^{\alpha}\}
for
r\geq 2
, for some
\alpha>2
. Clearly
M
is a manifold with pole
o\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
. Then, for each
\omega\in(0,1)
the function

u(r)=r^{-\beta},\qquad\beta\in\left(0,\frac{\alpha-2}{1-\omega}\right]

solves
\Delta u\geq Cu^{\omega}
on the end
\Omega=M\backslash B_{R}
for
R
large enough. Although (
1.64
) holds,
u
clearly contradicts
(\mathrm{CSP})
. Note that in this case
\Delta r=-(m-1)\alpha r^{\alpha-1}
, hence (
1.65
) is violated.

A more elaborated example along these lines will be given in Section 7 below. The construction shows that, in sharp contrast with
(\mathrm{SL})
and
(\mathrm{L})
, what matters in this case is that
M
should not possess ends shrinking too rapidly at infinity. This is the content of our first contribution to
(\mathrm{CSP})
. We begin defining a weaker notion of a pole, to allow a nontrivial topology of
M
.

Definition 1.36
.

Let
\mathcal{O}\subset M
be a relatively compact, open set with smooth boundary in
M
. We say that
\mathcal{O}
is
a pole of
M
if the normal exponential map
\exp^{\perp}:T\mathcal{O}^{\perp}\rightarrow M\backslash\mathcal{O}
realizes a diffeomorphism.

Here,
T\mathcal{O}^{\perp}
is the subset of the normal bundle of
\partial\mathcal{O}
consisting of vectors pointing outward from
\mathcal{O}
. The case of a point
o
being a pole can easily be recovered by choosing
\mathcal{O}=B_{\varepsilon}(o)
for
\varepsilon
small enough. Let
r
be the distance function from
\cal O
, which is therefore smooth on
M\backslash\mathcal{O}
. Denote with
\operatorname{II}_{-\nabla r}
the second fundamental form of
\partial\cal O
with respect to the inward unit normal
-\nabla r
, and let
B_{R}(\mathcal{O})=\{x\in M:0<r(x)<R\}
.

We assume that the radial sectional curvature
K_{\mathrm{rad}}
satisfies

K_{\mathrm{rad}}\leq-\kappa^{2}(1+r)^{\alpha}\qquad\text{on }\,M\backslash\cal O,

(1.66)

for some
\kappa\geq 0
,
\alpha\geq-2
.

Beyond the standard requirements (
1.3
) and (
1.5
) we assume (
1.29
), in order for
K
to be defined, and the following condition corresponding to (
1.63
):

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}f\ \text{ is positive and $C$-increasing on $(0,\eta_{0})$, for some $\eta_{0}>0$.}\\[5.69046pt]
l\ \text{ is $C$-increasing and locally Lipschitz on $(0,\xi_{0})$, for some $\xi_{0}>0$.}\\[5.69046pt]
f(0)l(0)=0.\end{array}\right.

(1.67)

Set
F
as in (
1.20
). We first address the necessity of the Keller-Osserman condition

\frac{1}{K^{-1}\circ F}\in L^{1}(0^{+}).

(
\mathrm{KO}_{0}
)

In analogy with Theorem
1.34
, we see that there is no geometric obstruction, at least on manifolds with a pole.

Theorem 1.37

(
Necessity of (
KO0
)
)
.

Let
M
be a manifold with a pole
\mathcal{O}
. Assume (
1.3
), (
1.5
), (
1.29
) and (
1.67
). Then, (
KO0
) is necessary for the validity of
(\mathrm{CSP})
for
(P_{\geq})
.

The proof relies on the construction of a suitable radial solution of a Dirichlet problem at infinity for singular ODEs, of independent interest. As for the sufficiency part, geometry enters into play, and the statement is considerably more elaborated. We state the following corollary of our main result, Theorem
7.13
in Section
7
, that considers
\varphi,l
of polynomial type near
t=0
.

Theorem 1.38
.

Let
(M,\langle\,,\,\rangle)
be a manifold with a pole
\mathcal{O}
such that (
1.66
) holds for some
\alpha\geq-2
,
\kappa\geq 0
. Suppose

\operatorname{II}_{-\nabla r}\geq-C_{\alpha,\kappa}\langle\,,\,\rangle\qquad\text{on }\,T\partial\cal O,

(1.68)

with

\begin{array}[]{l}C_{\alpha,\kappa}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\kappa&\quad\text{if }\,\alpha\geq 0\ \text{or }\ \kappa=0,\\[5.69046pt]
\left[\frac{\alpha+\sqrt{\alpha^{2}+16\kappa^{2}}}{4}\right]&\quad\text{otherwise}.\end{array}\right.\end{array}

(1.69)

Consider
\varphi,b,f,l
satisfying (
1.3
), (
1.5
), (
1.29
) and (
1.67
). Fix
\chi,\mu\in\mathbb{R}
with

\chi>0,\qquad\mu\leq\chi-\frac{\alpha}{2}

(1.70)

and assume that

\begin{array}[]{ll}l(t)\asymp t^{1-\chi}\varphi^{\prime}(t)&\quad\text{for }\,t\in(0,1),\\[5.69046pt]
b(x)\geq C_{1}\big(1+r(x)\big)^{-\mu}&\quad\text{for }\,r(x)\geq r_{0},\end{array}

(1.71)

for some constant
C_{1}>0
. If there exists a constant
c_{F}\geq 1
such that

F(t)^{\frac{\chi}{\chi+1}}\leq c_{F}f(t)\qquad\text{for each }\,t\in(0,\eta_{0}),

(1.72)

then,

\text{$(\mathrm{CSP})$ holds for $(P_{\geq})$}\qquad\Longleftrightarrow\qquad\eqref{KO_zero}.

(1.73)

Remark 1.39
.

By (
1.71
), (
KO0
) is equivalent to

F^{-\frac{1}{\chi+1}}\in L^{1}(0^{+}).

Note also that the
C
-increasing property of
l
implies that
t^{1-\chi}l(t)
is
C
-increasing too, possibly with a different
C
, and this forces a bound on the vanishing of
l
near
t=0
. For instance, if

\varphi^{\prime}(t)\asymp t^{p-2},\qquad f(t)\asymp t^{\omega}\qquad\text{as }\,t\rightarrow 0,

for some
p>1
,
\omega>0
, then (
1.71
) holds with
l

C
-increasing if and only if
\chi\leq p-1
, (
1.72
) is satisfied whenever
\omega\leq\chi
, and (
KO0
) is equivalent to
\omega<\chi
.

The bound (
1.68
) means, roughly speaking, that
\partial\mathcal{O}
should not be too concave in the inward direction. In particular, if
\kappa=0
, (
1.68
) requires
\mathcal{O}
to have a convex boundary. By choosing
\mathcal{O}=B_{\varepsilon}(o)
, the theorem applies to Cartan-Hadamard manifolds.

Example 1.40
.

Another relevant example to which Theorem
1.38
applies is that of hyperbolic manifolds with finite volume. It is known by the thick-thin decomposition (see Theorem D.3.3 and Proposition D.3.12 in
[
12
]
) that a manifold
M^{m}
with sectional curvature
-1
and finite volume decomposes as the disjoint union
\mathcal{O}\cup\Omega_{1}\cup\ldots\cup\Omega_{s}
, where
\mathcal{O}
is a smooth, relatively compact open set and, for each
j
,
\Omega_{j}
is a non-compact cusp end isometric to the warped product
\mathbb{R}^{+}_{0}\times_{e^{-r}}N^{m-1}
, for some compact flat manifold
(N,g_{N})
, with metric
\mathrm{d}r^{2}+e^{-2r}g_{N}
. Therefore,
\mathcal{O}
is a pole of
M
,
r
is the distance from
\mathcal{O}
and a direct computation gives

\operatorname{II}_{-\nabla r}=-\langle\,,\,\rangle\qquad\text{on }\,T\partial\cal O,

precisely the borderline case in (
1.68
).

Remark 1.41
.

Observe that the bound
\mu\leq\chi-\frac{\alpha}{2}
in (
1.70
) is the same as that in (
1.41
) and (
1.47
) for the validity of, respectively,
(\mathrm{SMP}_{\infty})
and
(\mathrm{WMP}_{\infty})
. Example
7.4
below shows that it cannot be weakened (see the range (
7.24
) of the parameter there).

Because of the presence of nontrivial
b,l
, the proof of Theorem
1.38
is technically considerably more demanding than that of Theorem
1.34
, and calls for a few extra-assumptions guaranteeing certain mild “homogeneity properties”, which accounts for conditions (
1.71
). However, the underlying principle is the same and relies on the explicit construction of a radial, compactly supported,
C^{1}
solution of

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\Delta_{\varphi}w\leq b(x)f(w)l(|\nabla w|)\qquad\text{on }\,M\backslash B_{R}(\mathcal{O}),\\[5.69046pt]
w\geq 0\qquad\text{on }\,M\backslash B_{R}(\mathcal{O}),\\[5.69046pt]
w\equiv 0\qquad\text{on }\,M\backslash B_{R_{1}}(\mathcal{O}),\end{array}\right.

(1.74)

for some
R_{1}>R
, via a direct use of (
KO0
). We provide two variants of the construction, that work under a mildly different set of assumptions: one of them is quite involved and closely related to that in
[
132
]
(which seems to be the only reference investigating
(\mathrm{CSP})
with a gradient term
l
), while the other one is new and considerably simpler.

Athough sharp, Theorem
1.38
still requires the presence of a pole in order to apply the Laplacian comparison theorem from below and deduce, from the combination of (
1.66
) and (
1.68
), the lower bound

\Delta r\geq-Cr^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}\qquad\text{for }\,r\geq 1,

and some constant
C>0
, which is the weighted version of (
1.65
). For the relevant case of the
p
-Laplace operator, we introduce a different radialization method, that we believe to be of independent interest. It is based on smooth functions replacing the distance from
\mathcal{O}
, and called for this reason
fake distances
. The method does not require the existence of a pole. Suppose that
M
is a complete manifold satisfying

\mathrm{Ric}\geq-(m-1)\kappa^{2}\langle\,,\,\rangle\qquad\text{on }\,M,

(1.75)

for some
\kappa\geq 0
, and let
v_{\kappa}(r)
be the volume of a geodesic sphere of radius
\kappa
in the space form of constant sectional curvature
-\kappa^{2}
(i.e.
\mathbb{R}^{m}
for
\kappa=0
or the hyperbolic space
\mathbb{H}^{m}_{\kappa}
of curvature
-\kappa^{2}
for
\kappa>0
). We restrict here to the case

p\in(1,m],

the complementary case
p>m
being slightly different and discussed in Section
2
. Assume

v_{\kappa}^{-\frac{1}{p-1}}\in L^{1}(\infty)

(which always holds if
\kappa>0
, while it requires
p<m
if
\kappa=0
). Assume that
\Delta_{p}
is not
p
-parabolic on
M
, equivalently, that for each fixed origin
o
there exists a minimal positive Green kernel
\mathcal{G}_{p}(x,o)
for
\Delta_{p}
with pole at
o
. Define the fake distance
\varrho:M\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
implicitly via the equation

\mathcal{G}_{p}(x,o)=\int_{\varrho(x)}^{\infty}\frac{\mathrm{d}s}{v_{\kappa}(s)^{\frac{1}{p-1}}}.

In the literature, the fake distance modelled on the Green kernel of the Laplace Beltrami operator has been used with great success to study the geometry in the large of manifolds with non-negative Ricci curvature, see for instance the works of Cheeger-Colding
[
27
]
, Colding-Minicozzi
[
33
]
and Colding
[
32
]
, together with the references therein. On the contrary, in a quasilinear setting and especially for the purposes of the present paper, its use seems to be new. In Proposition
2.3
we prove that, if
\kappa>0
, then
|\nabla\varrho|\leq C
outside of a neighbourhood of
o
, and by direct computation

\Delta_{p}\varrho=\frac{v_{\kappa}^{\prime}(\varrho)}{v_{\kappa}(\varrho)}|\nabla\varrho|^{p}.

Consequently, for each diffeomorphism
\psi:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
we obtain

\Delta_{p}\psi(\varrho)=v_{\kappa}^{-1}\big(v_{\kappa}|\psi^{\prime}|^{p-2}\psi^{\prime}\big)^{\prime}|\nabla\varrho|^{p}.

(1.76)

Since
v_{\kappa}^{-1}\big(v_{\kappa}|\psi^{\prime}|^{p-2}\psi^{\prime}\big)^{\prime}
is the expression of the
p
-Laplacian of a radial function in the model of curvature
-\kappa^{2}
, (
1.76
) enables us to construct solutions of (
1.74
) by radializing with respect to
\varrho
instead of
r
. However, to be able to conclude the validity of this property
(\mathrm{CSP})
we shall need
\varrho
to be an exhaustion function, equivalently, we need the vanishing of
\mathcal{G}_{p}
as
x
diverges. Sufficient conditions for the validity of this property follow, for instance, from the validity of Sobolev inequalities on
M
, and will be investigated in Section
2
. The striking advantage with respect to
r
is that
\varrho
is smooth and
p
-subharmonic, hence a bound of the type in (
1.65
) is automatically satisfied. We apply these ideas to obtain a characterization of
(\mathrm{CSP})
on a class of manifolds satisfying (
1.75
), which is a particular case of Theorem
7.25
and is somehow complementary to Theorem
1.38
. Here is the statement of the result.

Theorem 1.42
.

Let
(M,\langle\,,\,\rangle)
be a complete
m
-dimensional Riemannian manifold with

\mathrm{Ric}\geq-(m-1)\kappa^{2}\langle\,,\,\rangle\qquad\text{on }\,M,

(1.77)

and assume that, for some
p\in(1,2]
and
\nu>p
, the Sobolev inequality

\left(\int|\psi|^{\frac{\nu p}{\nu-p}}\right)^{\frac{\nu-p}{\nu}}\leq S_{p,\nu}\int|\nabla\psi|^{p}

(1.78)

holds for each
\psi\in\mathrm{Lip}_{c}(M)
. Fix
\chi\in(0,p-1]
. Let
f\in C(\mathbb{R})
satisfy

f(0)=0,\qquad f>0\ \ \text{ on }\,(0,\eta_{0}),\qquad\text{$f$ is $C$-increasing on }\,(0,\eta_{0}),

for some
\eta_{0}>0
. Define
F
as in (
1.20
), and furthermore suppose

F(t)^{\frac{\chi}{\chi+1}}\leq c_{F}f(t)\quad\text{on }\,(0,\eta_{0}),

(1.79)

for some
c_{F}>0
. Let
\Omega
an end of
M
. Then,
(\mathrm{CSP})
holds for solutions of

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\Delta_{p}u\geq f(u)|\nabla u|^{p-1-\chi}\qquad\text{on }\,\Omega\\[5.69046pt]
\displaystyle u\geq 0,\qquad\lim_{x\in\Omega,\,x\rightarrow\infty}u(x)=0\end{array}\right.

(1.80)

if and only if

F^{-\frac{1}{\chi+1}}\in L^{1}(0^{+}).

(1.81)

Example 1.43
.

Conditions for the validity of (
1.78
) will be given in Section
2
, see Examples
2.8
,
2.9
and
2.10
. In particular, we stress that (
1.78
) holds with
\nu=m
if
M
is (complete and) minimally immersed in a Cartan-Hadamard ambient space
N
. By Gauss equations, in this setting Theorem
1.42
can be applied provided that the minimal immersion
M\rightarrow N
has bounded second fundamental tensor in order to satisfy (
1.77
).

We stress that Theorem
7.25
below allows a dependence on the function
b
, which is required to be bounded from below in terms of a decaying function of
\varrho
. However, it is difficult to bound
\varrho
from
below
with the more manageable
r
. A particular case when we have effective estimates is for manifolds with
\mathrm{Ric}\geq 0
in the semilinear case
p=2
(although this last restriction seems to be merely technical), where there is also no need for the Sobolev inequality (
1.78
). The reader is referred to Theorem
7.27
below for the precise statement.

Moreover, observe that in Theorem
1.42
we require
p\in(1,2]
, that is, that the
p
-Laplacian be non-degenerate. This condition is technical, and is necessary to apply the comparison theorems that are currently available in the literature. For this reason, we feel interesting to investigate the following

Problem 4
.

Prove or disprove the validity of Theorem
1.42
(or, more generally, Theorems
7.25
and
7.27
below) in the full range
p\in(1,\infty)
.

When
l
is constant a further fake distance, recently constructed in
[
13
]
, allows to improve on Theorems
1.42
and
7.27
when
u
solves

\Delta u\geq(1+r)^{-\mu}f(u),

by reducing the geometric conditions to the only

\mathrm{Ric}\geq-(m-1)\kappa^{2}\big(1+r^{2}\big)^{\alpha/2}\langle\,,\,\rangle,

for some
\kappa>0
and
\alpha\in(-2,2]
. The threshold
\alpha=2
is sharp and related to a probabilistic requirement called the Feller property, cf.
[
8
,
114
]
. More details on this issue are given in Subsection
7.2.4
.

1.6
More general inequalities

The techniques discussed in the present paper are also effective to investigate more general inequalities of the type

\Delta_{\varphi}u\geq b(x)f(u)l(|\nabla u|)-\bar{b}(x)\bar{f}(u)\bar{l}(|\nabla u|).

(1.82)

This class includes relevant geometric examples, such as (
1.11
) when the graph is neither minimal nor a MCF soliton. Another important prototype inequality is

\Delta_{p}u\geq f(u)-c|\nabla u|^{q},\qquad\text{with $q>0$, $c>0$,}

that in the semilinear case appears (with the equality sign) as value functions of stochastic control problems (cf.
[
84
,
126
]
). Existence and nonexistence of entire solutions have been investigated in a series of papers, notably

-

[
83
,
60
]
, where the authors consider solutions of

\Delta u\pm\bar{b}(x)|\nabla u|^{q}=b(x)u^{\gamma}\qquad\text{on }\,\mathbb{R}^{m},

(1.83)

for
q,\gamma\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
and non-negative
b,\bar{b}
that are allowed to vanish in a controlled (yet very general) way.

-

[
56
,
95
]
, that concern quasilinear analogues of (
1.83
) when the driving operator is, respectively, a weighted
p
-Laplacian and a general
\varphi
-Laplacian in a manifold setting. Although related, their techniques differ from those in
[
83
,
60
]
.

-

[
52
]
, that considers a fully nonlinear version of (
1.83
) of the type

\mathcal{M}[u]\geq f(u)\pm g(|\nabla u|),

where the driving operator
\mathcal{M}
is uniformly elliptic. Their results are relevant also in the semilinear setting.

Apart from some special cases, the existence-nonexistence problem for such inequalities is far from being completely understood, and many interesting questions are still unanswered even in the Euclidean setting. For instance, none of the references considers the interplay of the weights and nonlinearities in the generality of (
1.82
). To keep the paper at a reasonable length, if still possible, we decided not to make attempts to deal with (
1.82
) and we leave it to future research.

2
Radialization and fake distances

The proof of some of our main results, for instance the
(\mathrm{CSP})
, relies on the construction of a suitable radial solution of
(P_{\geq})
or
(P_{\leq})
to be compared with a given one. For convenience, hereafter we extend
\varphi
to an odd function on all of
\mathbb{R}
by setting

\varphi(s)=-\varphi(-s)\qquad\text{for each }\,s<0.

(2.1)

Suppose that
w\in C^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{+}_{0})
satisfies
\varphi(w^{\prime})\in C^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{+}_{0})
. If
u(x)=w(r(x))
, where
r(x)
is the distance from a fixed origin
\mathcal{O}
(a point, or a relatively compact open set with smooth boundary), then

\Delta_{\varphi}u=\mathrm{div}\left(\frac{\varphi(|w^{\prime}|)}{|w^{\prime}|}w^{\prime}\nabla r\right)=\mathrm{div}\big(\varphi(w^{\prime})\nabla r\big)=\big(\varphi(w^{\prime})\big)^{\prime}+\varphi(w^{\prime})\Delta r,

therefore
u
solves, say,
(P_{\geq})
if and only if

\big(\varphi(w^{\prime})\big)^{\prime}+\varphi(w^{\prime})\Delta r\,\geq\,b(x)f(w)l(|w^{\prime}|).

(2.2)

Take
0<g\in C^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+}_{0})
and a model manifold
