b(x)\leq a_{1}
on
B_{2R}(o)
. Let
C\geq 1
be the constant defining the
C
-increasing property of
f
. Define
\wp(t)=v_{g_{\kappa}}(2R-t)
,
a(t)=a_{1}
,
T_{0}=3R/2
and
T=R
. We claim that we can suitably reduce
R
, and choose
\eta\in(0,\eta_{0})
small enough, in such a way that

\frac{\wp_{1}}{\wp_{0}}\varphi\left(\frac{\eta}{R}\right)+4C\Theta(R)f_{\eta}l_{\xi}<\varphi(\xi),

(4.10)

where
f_{\eta}
,
l_{\xi}
,
\wp_{0}
,
\wp_{1}
and
\Theta
are defined as in (
3.6
). Indeed, since
v_{g_{\kappa}}^{\prime}\geq 0
, by definition
\wp_{1}=v_{g_{\kappa}}(2R)
,
\wp_{0}=v_{g_{\kappa}}(R/2)
, and thus
\wp_{1}/\wp_{0}\rightarrow 4^{m}
and
\Theta(R)\rightarrow 0
as
R\rightarrow 0
. Hence, we can first reduce
R
to guarantee

4C\Theta(R)f_{\eta_{0}}l_{\xi}<\frac{\varphi(\xi)}{2},

and then choose
\eta\in(0,\eta_{0})
small enough to satisfy (
4.10
).
Applying Theorem
3.1
, there exists a solution
z(t)
of

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\big(\wp(t)\varphi(z_{t})\big)_{t}=2C\wp a_{1}f(z)l(z_{t})\qquad\text{on }\,(0,R),\\[5.69046pt]
z(0)=0,\qquad z(R)=\eta,\end{array}\right.

(4.11)

where the subscript
t
indicates differentiation with respect to
t
. Furthermore, combining Theorem
3.1
and Proposition
3.7
(note that here
\wp^{\prime}<0
), the solution
z
satisfies the following properties:

\begin{gathered}z>0\ \text{ on }\,(0,R],\qquad z_{t}>0\ \text{ on }\,[0,R],\qquad\|z_{t}\|_{\infty}<\xi.\end{gathered}

(4.12)

Taking into account that we have extended
\varphi
on
\mathbb{R}
in such a way that
\varphi(-s)=-\varphi(s)
, the function
w(r)=z(2R-r)
satisfies

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\big[v_{g}\varphi(w^{\prime})\big]^{\prime}=2Cv_{g}a_{1}f(w)l(|w^{\prime}|)\qquad\text{on }\,\left(R,2R\right),\\[5.69046pt]
w(2R)=0,\quad w(R)=\eta,\quad w^{\prime}<0\ \text{ on }\,\left[R,2R\right].\end{array}\right.

(4.13)

Define
v(x)=w(r(x))
. Using (
4.9
), together with
\varphi(w^{\prime})<0
and (
4.13
), we deduce that
v
solves

\displaystyle\displaystyle\Delta_{\varphi}v

\displaystyle=\displaystyle\big(\varphi(w^{\prime})\big)^{\prime}+\varphi(w^{\prime})\Delta r\geq v_{g}^{-1}(r)\big[v_{g}(r)\varphi(w^{\prime})\big]^{\prime}

(4.14)

\displaystyle=v_{g}(r)^{-1}\big(2Cv_{g}(r)a_{1}f(w)l(|w^{\prime}|)\geq 2Cb(x)f(v)l(|\nabla v|)

on the annulus
E_{R}(o)=B_{2R}(o)\backslash\overline{B_{R}(o)}
. Moreover, denoting with
\nu
the outward pointing unit normal from
\partial B_{2R}(o)
,

\langle\nabla v,\nu\rangle=w^{\prime}(2R)<0\qquad\text{on }\,\partial B_{2R}(o).

(4.15)

Following E. Hopf’s argument, we now prove that
u
solving (
4.8
) is identically zero provided that
u(x_{0})=0
at some
x_{0}
. Suppose by contradiction that this is not the case, and let
\Omega_{+}=\{x\in\Omega:u(x)>0\}
. Choose
x_{1}\in\Omega_{+}
in such a way that
\mathrm{dist}(x_{1},\partial\Omega_{+})>\mathrm{dist}(x_{1},\partial\Omega)
, and let
B(x_{1})\subset\Omega_{+}
be the largest ball contained in
\Omega_{+}
. Then,
u>0
in
B(x_{1})
, while
u(\bar{x})=0
for some
\bar{x}\in\partial B(x_{1})\cap\Omega
. Clearly
\nabla u(\bar{x})=0
, since
\bar{x}
is an absolute minimum for
u
. Take a unit speed minimizing geodesic
\gamma:[0,\mathrm{dist}(x_{1},\bar{x})]\rightarrow\Omega
from
x_{1}
to
\bar{x}
. Up to choosing the arclength parameter
s
sufficiently close to
\mathrm{dist}(x_{1},\bar{x})
and setting
2R=\mathrm{dist}(x_{1},\bar{x})-s
, the closure of the ball
B_{2R}(o)
centered at
o=\gamma(s)
does not intersect
\mathrm{cut}(o)
, and
B_{2R}(o)\subset B(x_{1})
, with
\bar{x}\in\partial B_{2R}(o)
. We consider the function
v
constructed above on
E_{R}(o)\subset B_{2R}(o)
, with
\eta
small enough to satisfy (
4.10
) and also

\eta<\inf_{\partial B_{R}(o)}u.

(4.16)

We claim that
u\geq v
on
E_{R}(o)
. Otherwise, suppose that

\displaystyle\max_{E_{R}(o)}(v-u)=\bar{\delta}>0,

and let
\Gamma
be the set of maximum points of
v-u
. Note that
\Gamma\Subset E_{R}(o)
and that
\nabla u=\nabla v
for each
x\in\Gamma
. For
\delta\in(0,\bar{\delta})
, set
U_{\delta}=\{v-u>\delta\}
. By construction, there exists
\varepsilon>0
such that
\varepsilon\leq|\nabla v|\leq 1
on
E_{R}(o)
, and since
l>0
on
\mathbb{R}^{+}
we deduce that the quotient
l(|\nabla u|)/l(|\nabla v|)
is continuous on
E_{R}(o)
and equal to
1
on
\Gamma
. A compactness argument shows that, for
\delta
sufficiently close to
\bar{\delta}
,
l(|\nabla u|)/l(|\nabla v|)\leq 2
on
U_{\delta}
. Taking into account that the
C
-increasing property of
f
implies
f(v)\geq C^{-1}f(u)
on
U_{\delta}
, we deduce

\Delta_{\varphi}u\leq b(x)f(u)l(|\nabla u|)\leq 2Cb(x)f(v)l(|\nabla v|)\leq\Delta_{\varphi}v\qquad\text{on }\,U_{\delta}.

From
v=u+\delta
on
\partial U_{\delta}
, by the comparison Proposition
4.1
we get
v\leq u+\delta
on
U_{\delta}
, contradicting the very definition of
U_{\delta}
. Hence,
v\leq u
on
E_{R}(o)
, and in particular
\langle\nabla(u-v),\nu\rangle\leq 0
at
\bar{x}
, which is impossible by (
4.15
) and by the fact that
\nabla u(\bar{x})=0
. This contradiction concludes the proof of the sufficiency part.

Necessity of conditions (
4.6
) and (
4.7
)
.

Suppose the failure of both (
4.6
) and (
4.7
), or equivalently (recall that
f
is
C
-increasing) the validity of

f>0\quad\text{on }\,(0,\eta_{0}),\qquad\frac{1}{K^{-1}\circ F}\in L^{1}(0^{+}).

(KO
0
)

For each
o\in M
, we shall now construct on
B_{2R}(o)
(with
R
as in the beginning of the proof) a
C^{1}
non-negative, nonzero solution
u
of
(P_{\leq})
with
u=0
on
\overline{B_{R}(o)}
, contradicting
(\mathrm{FMP})
. Set
T_{0}=2R
,
T=R
,

\wp(t)=v_{g_{\kappa}}(t+R),\quad a(t)=\inf_{B_{2R}(o)\backslash B_{R}(o)}b,

where
v_{g_{\kappa}}(r)
is the volume of a geodesic sphere of radius
r
in a model of curvature
-\kappa^{2}
, as defined at the beginning of the proof. Since
f(0)l(0)=0
, we can choose
\eta
small enough to satisfy (
3.8
) in Theorem
3.1
, whence there exists
w\in C^{1}([0,T])
non-decreasing and solving

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\displaystyle\big[\wp\varphi(w^{\prime})\big]^{\prime}=\wp af(w)l(w^{\prime})\qquad\text{on }\,(0,T),\\[5.69046pt]
0\leq w\leq\eta,\quad w^{\prime}\geq 0\qquad\text{on }\,[0,T],\\[5.69046pt]
w(0)=0,\qquad w(T)=\eta.\end{array}\right.

(4.17)

Up to reducing
\eta
further, we can apply Proposition
3.9
to deduce that
w^{\prime}(0)=0
. Set
u(x)=w\big(r(x)-R\big)
. By the Laplacian comparison theorem and since
w^{\prime}\geq 0
, on
B_{2R}(o)\backslash B_{R}(o)
it holds

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\Delta_{\varphi}u&=&\displaystyle\big(\varphi(w^{\prime})\big)^{\prime}+\varphi(w^{\prime})\Delta r\leq\displaystyle\big(\varphi(w^{\prime})\big)^{\prime}+\varphi(w^{\prime})\frac{v_{g_{\kappa}}^{\prime}(r)}{v_{g_{\kappa}}(r)}\\[8.5359pt]
&\leq&\displaystyle\Big(\wp^{-1}\big[\wp\varphi(w^{\prime})\big]^{\prime}\Big)_{r(x)-R}\leq af(w)l(w^{\prime})\\[8.5359pt]
&\leq&b(x)f(u)l(|\nabla u|).\end{array}

Extending
u
to be zero on
B_{R}(o)
defines a nonzero,
C^{1}
-solution of
(P_{\leq})
on all of
B_{2R}(o)
, which clearly violates
(\mathrm{FMP})
.
∎

Remark 4.9
.

The function
u
in the proof of the necessity part, defined on
B_{2R}(o)
and vanishing identically on a smaller ball
B_{R}(o)
, is an example of a
dead core
(super)solution. For a thorough investigation of dead core problems, we refer the reader to
[
123
]
and the references therein. We mention that
u
can even be constructed to be positive on
B_{2R}(o)\backslash\overline{B_{R}(o)}
. Indeed, it is enough to replace the solution
w
of (
4.17
) with the supersolution
z
defined in the proof of Proposition
3.9
, which is known to be positive on
(0,T]
.

Remark 4.10
.

With a similar technique, one could consider the
(\mathrm{FMP})
for more general equations of the type

\Delta_{\varphi}u\leq b(x)f(u)l(|\nabla u|)+\bar{b}(x)\bar{f}(u)\bar{l}(|\nabla u|),

for suitable
\bar{b},\bar{f},\bar{l}
. The prototype case

\Delta_{p}u\leq f(u)+|\nabla u|^{q},\qquad\text{with }\,p>1

has been considered in
[
123
, Thm. 5.4.1]
and
[
125
]
when
q\geq p-1
, and in
[
51
]
for
q<p-1
.

5
Weak maximum principle, a-priori estimates and Liouville’s property

5.1
The equivalence between
(\mathrm{WMP}_{\infty})
and
(\mathrm{L})

In this section, we prove a more general version of Proposition
1.16
, that describes the relationship between
(\mathrm{WMP}_{\infty})
and the Liouville property
(\mathrm{L})
. We begin by introducing another form of the weak maximum principle.

Definition 5.1
.

Assume (
1.3
) and fix
b,l
satisfying (
1.5
). We say that

•

the
open weak maximum principle

(\mathrm{OWMP}_{\infty})
holds for
(bl)^{-1}\Delta_{\varphi}
if, for each
f\in C(\mathbb{R})
, for each open set
\Omega\subset M
with
\partial\Omega\neq\emptyset
and for each
u\in C(\overline{\Omega})\cap\mathrm{Lip}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\Omega)
solving

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\Delta_{\varphi}u\geq b(x)f(u)l(|\nabla u|)\qquad\text{on }\,\Omega,\\[5.69046pt]
\sup_{\Omega}u<\infty,\end{array}\right.

(5.1)

we have that

\text{either}\quad\sup_{\Omega}u=\sup_{\partial\Omega}u,\qquad\text{or}\quad f\big(\sup_{\Omega}u\big)\leq 0.

(5.2)

The open weak maximum principle at infinity has been introduced in
[
4
]
in the study of immersed submanifolds of warped product ambient spaces, and parallels Ahlfors’ definition of parabolicity. For a detailed investigation and an extensive bibliography, we refer to Chapters 3 and 4 of
[
2
]
.

Remark 5.2
.

The recent
[
93
]
contains a different approach to maximum principles at infinity in the spirit of
(\mathrm{OWMP}_{\infty})
(called there
the Ahlfors property
), which is based on viscosity theory and enables to consider classes of fully nonlinear operators of geometric interest. A systematic approach via viscosity theory is very well suited to treat weak and strong maximum principles in a unified way, and especially to investigate principles like the classical Ekeland or
(\mathrm{SMP}_{\infty})
, where a gradient condition on
u
appears.

Proposition 5.3
.

Let
\varphi
and
b,f,l
satisfy respectively the assumptions in (
1.5
), (
1.3
). Then, the following properties are equivalent:

(i)

(bl)^{-1}\Delta_{\varphi}
satisfies
(\mathrm{WMP}_{\infty})
;

(ii)

(\mathrm{L})
holds for some (equivalently, every)
f
satisfying

f(0)=0,\qquad f>0\quad\text{on }\,\mathbb{R}^{+};

(iii)

each non-constant
u\in\mathrm{Lip}_{\mathrm{loc}}(M)
solving
(P_{\geq})
on
M
and bounded above satisfies
f(u^{*})\leq 0
, with
u^{*}=\sup_{M}u
;

(iv)

(bl)^{-1}\Delta_{\varphi}
satisfies
(\mathrm{OWMP}_{\infty})
.

Proof.

We prove the chain of implications
(i)\Rightarrow(iii)\Rightarrow(ii)\Rightarrow(i)
and then
(iv)\Leftrightarrow(i)
. When the “some-every” alternative occurs, we always assume the weaker property and prove the stronger.

(i)\Rightarrow(iii)
.

Let
u\in\mathrm{Lip}_{\mathrm{loc}}(M)
be a non-constant solution of
(P_{\geq})
that is bounded from above, and assume by contradiction that
f(u^{*})=2K>0
. By continuity, there exists
\eta<u^{*}
sufficiently close to
u^{*}
such that
f(u)\geq K
on
\Omega_{\eta}=\{u>\eta\}
, thus

\Delta_{\varphi}u\geq Kb(x)l(|\nabla u|)\qquad\text{on }\,\Omega_{\eta}.

The definition of
(\mathrm{WMP}_{\infty})
then implies
K\leq 0
, contradiction.

(iii)\Rightarrow(ii)
.

Let
u\in\mathrm{Lip}_{\mathrm{loc}}(M)
be a non-constant, bounded, non-negative solution of
(P_{\geq})
. Then
f(u^{*})\leq 0
by
(iii)
. However, from
f>0
on
\mathbb{R}^{+}
we get
u^{*}\equiv 0
, that is,
u\equiv 0
is constant, contradiction.

(ii)\Rightarrow(i)
.

Let us consider a problem
(P_{\geq})
with
f>0
on
\mathbb{R}^{+}
,
f(0)=0
for which
(\mathrm{L})
holds. Suppose by contradiction that
(i)
is not satisfied, that is, there exists a non-constant
u\in\mathrm{Lip}_{\mathrm{loc}}(M)
with

\Delta_{\varphi}u\geq Kb(x)l(|\nabla u|)\qquad\text{on some }\,\Omega_{\bar{\eta}},

for some
K>0
and
\bar{\eta}<u^{*}
. Since
f(0)=0
, we can choose
\eta\in(\bar{\eta},u^{*})
in such a way that
f<K
on
(0,u^{*}-\eta)
. Hence,
u_{\eta}=u-\eta
solves

\Delta_{\varphi}u_{\eta}\geq Kb(x)l(|\nabla u_{\eta}|)\geq b(x)f(u_{\eta})l(|\nabla u_{\eta}|)\qquad\text{on }\Omega_{\eta}.

Thanks to Lemma
4.6
,
w=\max\{u_{\eta},0\}
is a
non-constant, non-negative, bounded solution of
\Delta_{\varphi}w\geq b(x)f(w)l(|\nabla w|)
on
M
, contradicting property
(\mathrm{L})
for such an
f
.

(i)\Rightarrow(iv)
.

If
u
solves (
5.1
) but none of the properties in (
5.2
) holds, then by continuity we can choose
\eta\in(\sup_{\partial\Omega}u,\sup_{\Omega}u)
such that
f(u)\geq K>0
on
\{u>\eta\}
. Thus,
u
solves
\Delta_{\varphi}u\geq Kb(x)l(|\nabla u|)
on
\{u>\eta\}\subset\Omega
, contradicting
(\mathrm{WMP}_{\infty})
.

(iv)\Rightarrow(i)
.

Assuming that
(\mathrm{WMP}_{\infty})
does not hold, take a non-constant
u
which is bounded above and solves
\Delta_{\varphi}u\geq Kb(x)l(|\nabla u|)
on some set
\Omega_{\eta}=\{u>\eta\}
, for some
K>0
. If
\eta
is close enough to
u^{*}
,
\partial\Omega_{\eta}\neq\emptyset
, thus clearly
u
contradicts
(\mathrm{OWMP}_{\infty})
on
\Omega_{\eta}
with the choice
f=K
.

∎

5.2
Volume growth and
(\mathrm{WMP}_{\infty})

In this section, we explore geometric conditions that ensure the validity of
(\mathrm{WMP}_{\infty})
, in the form given by
(iii)
of Proposition
1.16
, that is, each non-constant
u\in\mathrm{Lip}_{\mathrm{loc}}(M)
solving
(P_{\geq})
and bounded above satisfies
f(u^{*})\leq 0
. Here, as usual,
u^{*}=\sup_{M}u
, and similarly we will use the notation
u_{*}=\inf_{M}u
.

When
l\equiv 1
, the problem has been tackled in a series of papers
[
77
,
134
,
109
,
110
]
by means of integral methods, and in particular we refer to
[
110
]
for a thorough discussion. Since then, in a manifolds setting, the first results that we are aware of allowing a nontrivial
l
appeared in
[
95
]
, where
l
is assumed to be
C
-increasing (in fact, polynomial in Thm. 5.1 therein). In particular, the relevant case when
l
can vanish both as
t\rightarrow 0^{+}
and as
t\rightarrow\infty
seems to be still open even in the Euclidean space, although it has been recently considered for Carnot groups in
[
2
]
. It is a remarkable feature that the results quoted above ensure
f(u^{*})\leq 0
not only when
u
is a-priori bounded above, but also when
u
does not grow too fast at infinity, in the sense that in this case
u^{*}
is also shown to be finite. This is a natural condition, and its origin is related to the growth of an explicit Khas’minskii-type potential for the operator considered, see Section 4 of
[
110
]
for more information. Related interesting results on
\mathbb{R}^{m}
can be found in
[
49
,
50
,
124
,
38
]
and will be described in more detail later.

The next theorem improves on
[
95
, Thm 5.1]
and
[
2
, Thm. 2.1]
. Throughout this section, we assume the following growth conditions:

\begin{array}[]{ll}\text{there exist constants $p,\bar{p}>1$, $C,\bar{C}>0$ such that }\\[5.69046pt]
\varphi(t)\leq Ct^{p-1}\ \text{ on }\,[0,1],\qquad\varphi(t)\leq\bar{C}t^{\bar{p}-1}\quad\text{on }\,[1,\infty).\end{array}

(5.3)

If
p=\bar{p}
, (
5.3
) is called the
weak
p
-coercivity
of
\Delta_{\varphi}
in
[
38
]
. We will explain in depth the different role played by
p
and
\bar{p}
for the validity of
(\mathrm{WMP}_{\infty})
.

Theorem 5.4
.

Let
M
be a complete Riemannian manifold, and consider
\varphi,b,f
and
l
meeting the requirements in (
1.5
), (
1.3
) and the bounds (
5.3
) for some
p,\bar{p}>1
. Assume that, for some
\mu,\chi\in\mathbb{R}
satisfying

\chi\geq 0,\qquad\mu\leq\chi+1,

(5.4)

the following inequalities hold:

\begin{array}[]{ll}b(x)\geq C\big(1+r(x)\big)^{-\mu}&\quad\text{on }\,M,\\[5.69046pt]
f(t)\geq C&\quad\text{for }\,t\,\gg 1\\[5.69046pt]
\displaystyle l(t)\geq C\frac{\varphi(t)}{t^{\chi}}&\quad\text{on }\,\mathbb{R}^{+},\end{array}

(5.5)

for some constant
C>0
. Let
u\in\mathrm{Lip}_{\mathrm{loc}}(M)
be a non-constant, weak solution of
(P_{\geq})
such that either

(i)

u
is bounded above and one of the following properties hold:

\begin{array}[]{lll}\mu<\chi+1&\text{and}&\displaystyle\qquad\liminf_{r\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\log\mathrm{vol}(B_{r})}{r^{\chi+1-\mu}}<\infty\quad(=0\,\text{ if }\,\chi=0);\\[11.38092pt]
\mu=\chi+1&\text{and}&\displaystyle\qquad\liminf_{r\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\log\mathrm{vol}(B_{r})}{\log r}<\infty\quad(\leq p\,\text{ if }\,\chi=0).\end{array}

(5.6)

(ii)

u
satisfies

u_{+}(x)=o\big(r(x)^{\sigma}\big)\qquad\text{as }\,r(x)\rightarrow\infty,

(5.7)

for some
\sigma>0
such that

\chi\sigma\leq\chi+1-\mu,

(5.8)

and one of the following properties hold:

\begin{array}[]{ll}\chi\sigma<\chi+1-\mu,&\quad\displaystyle\liminf_{r\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\log\mathrm{vol}(B_{r})}{r^{\chi+1-\mu-\chi\sigma}}<\infty\quad(=0\,\text{ if }\,\chi=0);\\[11.38092pt]
\chi\sigma=\chi+1-\mu,\ \ \ \chi>0,&\quad\displaystyle\liminf_{r\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\log\mathrm{vol}(B_{r})}{\log r}<\infty;\\[11.38092pt]
\chi\sigma=\chi+1-\mu,&\quad\displaystyle\liminf_{r\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\log\mathrm{vol}(B_{r})}{\log r}\leq\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}p-\sigma(p-1)&\text{if }\,\sigma\leq 1,\\[2.84544pt]
\bar{p}-\sigma(\bar{p}-1)&\text{if }\,\sigma>1.\end{array}\right.\end{array}

(5.9)

Then,
u
is bounded above on
M
and
f(u^{*})\leq 0
. If moreover
u
satisfies
(P_{=})
,

tf(t)\geq C|t|\qquad\text{for }\,|t|>>1

(5.10)

and either
(i)
or
(ii)
holds both for
u_{+}
and for
u_{-}
, then

u\in L^{\infty}(M)\qquad\text{and}\qquad f(u^{*})\leq 0\leq f(u_{*}).

(5.11)

Remark 5.5
.

In the third case of (
5.9
), that is, when
\chi\sigma=\chi+1-\mu
and the volume growth of
B_{r}
is suitably small with respect to
p,\bar{p},\sigma
, the result still holds under the weaker assumption

u_{+}(x)=O\big(r(x)^{\sigma}\big)\qquad\text{as }\,r(x)\rightarrow\infty.

(5.12)

As a consequence of Theorem
5.4
, we deduce Theorem
1.19
of the Introduction.

Proof of Theorem
1.19
.

Consider a non-constant solution
u\in\mathrm{Lip}_{\mathrm{loc}}(M)
with
u^{*}<\infty
of
\Delta_{\varphi}u\geq Kb(x)l(|\nabla u|)
on some upper level set
\Omega_{\eta}=\{x\in M\ :\ u(x)>\eta\}
. We shall prove that
K\leq 0
. Suppose that this is not the case. By adding a constant to
u
, we can suppose that
\eta<0
but sufficiently near to
0
so that
\Omega_{0}=\{x\in M\ :\ u(x)>0\}\not=\emptyset
. Choose
f\in C(\mathbb{R})
such that
0\leq f\leq K
,
f(0)=0
and
f(t)=K
for
t>u^{*}/2
. Then,
u
solves

\Delta_{\varphi}u\geq b(x)f(u)l(|\nabla u|)

(5.13)

on
\Omega_{\eta}\supset\Omega_{0}
, and with the aid of Lemma
4.6
we can assume that
u\geq 0
solves (
5.13
) on the whole of
M
. Moreover,
f(u^{*})=K
. To reach a contradiction, we just need to check the requirements to apply Theorem
5.4
, case
(i)
and conclude
f(u^{*})\leq 0
. This, by Proposition
1.16
, implies the
(\mathrm{WMP}_{\infty})
.

First, observe that
\mu\leq\chi-\alpha/2
in (
1.47
) can be rewritten as

1+\frac{\alpha}{2}\leq\chi+1-\mu,

(5.14)

and from
\alpha\geq-2
it implies
\mu\leq\chi+1
. We exhamine the validity of (
5.6
). If
\alpha>-2
and
\chi>0
, then
\mu<\chi+1
by (
5.14
) and the volume assumption (
1.46
) imply the first in (
5.6
). If
\alpha=-2
and
\chi>0
, then again by (
1.46
) both of (
5.6
) are met, respectively, when
\mu<\chi+1
and
\mu=\chi+1
. Suppose now that
\chi=0
and
\mu<\chi-\alpha/2
. Then,
\mu<\chi+1
and, for each
\alpha\geq-2
, the strict inequality in (
5.14
) coupled with (
1.46
) guarantees the first in (
5.6
). If
\chi=0
and
\mu=\chi-\alpha/2
, according to whether
\alpha>-2
or
\alpha=-2
the requirement
V_{\infty}=0
, respectively
V_{\infty}\leq p
, in (
1.47
) is precisely what is needed to deduce the validity of (
5.6
), concluding the proof.
∎

It is worth to postpone the proof of Theorem
5.4
and comment on various aspects of its statement.

-

p,\bar{p}
play no explicit role in (
5.4
). However, a bound on
\chi
in terms of
p
alone is hidden, in some cases, in the requirement
l(t)\geq\varphi(t)/t^{\chi}
on
\mathbb{R}^{+}
: indeed, if
\varphi(t)\asymp t^{p-1}
near
t=0
, the continuity of
l
at zero forces

\chi\leq p-1.

-

For
l\equiv 1
, that is, when no gradient appears, the third in (
5.5
) forces
\varphi(t)\leq C^{-1}t^{\chi}
on
\mathbb{R}^{+}
. If we suppose that
\varphi(t)\asymp t^{p-1}
on
[0,1]
and
\varphi(t)\asymp t^{\bar{p}-1}
on
[1,\infty)
, the above theorem can be applied provided

\bar{p}-1\leq\chi\leq p-1.

Therefore, when the operator is the
p
-Laplacian operator, the gradientless case
l\equiv 1
is recovered with the choice
\chi=p-1
. On the other hand, for the mean curvature operator,
p\leq 2
and
\bar{p}>1
can be chosen arbitrarily close to
1
, and the gradientless case
l\equiv 1
is recovered for any choice of
\chi\in[0,1]
. For a fixed
\mu
, in case
(i)
or in
(ii)
with
\sigma\leq 1
, it is evident that the choice
\chi=1
,
p=2
gives the best result, while in case
(ii)
for
\sigma>1
the best choice is
\chi=0
and
\bar{p}
approaching
1
.

-

The second in (
5.9
) is the only place where
p
and
\bar{p}
appear. Its validity forces an upper bound for
\sigma
, since the right-hand side in the second of (
5.9
) is non-negative if and only if

\sigma\leq\frac{\bar{p}}{\bar{p}-1}.

-

The third in (
5.9
) supports and makes rigorous the next idea: in the sublinear range
\sigma<1
, the region where
|\nabla u|
is close to zero should be, somehow, larger than the one where
|\nabla u|
is big, and consequently the growth of
\varphi
on
[1,\infty)
(if still polynomial) might be neglectable with respect to the behaviour of
\varphi
on
[0,1]
. If
\sigma>1
the situation reverses, and now the main contribution should be given by
\varphi
on
[1,\infty)
.

We feel interesting and a bit surprising that the method to prove Theorem
5.4
is able to detect, in some sense, the size of the regions where
|\nabla u|
is small or large. In particular, if
u
is bounded above this might suggest that the above proof could be refined to show that, under the same assumptions, the
strong
maximum principle
(\mathrm{SMP}_{\infty})
is true, see Problem
2
in the Introduction.

As we will show at the end of the section, Theorem
5.4
is sharp in the following sense: under the validity of the range of the parameters
\chi,\mu
in (
5.4
), for almost each condition on
\sigma
and
\mathrm{vol}(B_{r})
we are able to find a non-constant solution of
(P_{\geq})
with
f\equiv 1
satisfying all the remaining assumptions but the chosen one.

If
f
has a unique zero, from (
5.11
) we deduce a Liouville type theorem for slowly growing solutions
u
of
(P_{=})
, that fits very well with some results obtained, in the Euclidean setting, by Farina and Serrin in
[
49
,
50
]
. For the sake of comparison, we state their theorems renaming their parameters to agree with our notation:

Theorem 5.6
.

[
50
, Thm. 11 and 12]

On Euclidean space, consider
\varphi,b,f
and
l
meeting the requirements in (
1.5
), (
1.3
) and the bounds (
5.3
) for some
p=\bar{p}>1
. Assume that, for some
\mu,\chi,\omega\in\mathbb{R}
satisfying

0<\omega\leq\chi\leq p-1,

the following inequalities hold:

\begin{array}[]{ll}b(x)\geq C\big(1+r(x)\big)^{-\mu}&\quad\text{on }\,\mathbb{R}^{m},\\[5.69046pt]
tf(t)\geq C|t|^{\omega+1}&\quad\text{for }\,t\in\mathbb{R},\\[5.69046pt]
\displaystyle l(t)\geq Ct^{p-1-\chi}&\quad\text{on }\,\mathbb{R}^{+},\end{array}

(5.15)

for some constant
C>0
. Then, a solution
u\in\mathrm{Lip}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{m})
of
(P_{=})
satisfying

|u(x)|=O\big(r(x)^{\sigma}\big)\qquad\text{as }\,r(x)\rightarrow\infty,

for some
\sigma>0
, is constant provided one of the following cases occur:

(i)

\omega<\chi
,
\mu<\chi+1
,

(p-1)(1+\chi-\mu)\geq(p-m)(\chi-\omega)\qquad\text{and}\qquad\sigma\in\left(0,\frac{\chi+1-\mu}{\chi-\omega}\right);

(5.16)

(ii)

\omega<\chi
,
m<p
,

(p-1)(1+\chi-\mu)\leq(p-m)(\chi-\omega)\qquad\text{and}\qquad\sigma\in\left(0,\frac{p-m}{p-1}\right);

(5.17)

(iii)

\omega=\chi
,
\mu<\chi+1
, independently of
\sigma>0
.

(iv)

\omega=\chi
,
m<p
,

\mu\geq\chi+1\qquad\text{and}\qquad\sigma\in\left(0,\frac{p-m}{p-1}\right).

Remark 5.7
.

Note that the bounds in
(i)
and
(ii)
well match when equality holds in the first of (
5.16
) and (
5.17
). If
l\equiv 1
, that is,
\chi=p-1
,
(i)
and
(iii)
have been proved, respectively, in Theorems B and A in
[
49
]
, and their sharpness is discussed in Examples 5 and 4, Section 11 therein. It is interesting to observe that the first in (
5.16
) is not required in
[
49
, Thm. B]
, but appears in discussing the sharpness of
(i)
. More precisely, Example 5 in
[
49
]
stresses that the conclusion of Theorem
5.6
in case
(i)
does not hold when
\sigma=\frac{\chi+1-\mu}{\chi-\omega}
in (
5.16
), provided that the first in (
5.16
) is strengthened to

(p-1)(1+\chi-\mu)>(p-m)(\chi-\omega)\qquad\text{with }\,\chi=p-1.

(5.18)

On the contrary, perhaps surprisingly, the conclusion of
(ii)
still holds for
\sigma=\frac{p-m}{p-1}
, see the next Corollary
5.8
. This is, clearly, not in contradiction with
[
49
]
in view of the incompatibility of (
5.18
) with (
5.17
), see also the discussion
[
124
, p. 677]
. Further results for solutions of
(P_{\geq})
which are a-priori bounded or vanishing at infinity are given in Theorems D,E,F in
[
49
]
and Theorems 1 and 2 in
[
141
]
. Inspection shows that they fit very well with the case when
u
is bounded above in Theorem
5.4
.

First, we compare Theorem
5.4
with case
(i)
in Theorem
5.6
, and we therefore assume
M=\mathbb{R}^{m}
,
p=\bar{p}
in (
5.3
),
0<\chi\leq p-1
and
\mu<\chi+1
. It is apparent that, for each
\omega>0
, condition
tf(t)\geq C|t|^{\omega+1}
implies both (
5.10
) and
tf(t)>0
on
\mathbb{R}^{+}
. Theorem
5.4
then gives the constancy of solutions of
(P_{=})
on
\mathbb{R}^{m}
under the assumption

u(x)=o\big(r(x)^{\sigma}\big)\qquad\text{as }\,r(x)\rightarrow\infty\qquad\text{and}\qquad\sigma\in\left[0,\frac{\chi+1-\mu}{\chi}\right),

(5.19)

for any dimension
m
and any
p>1
. The upper bound for
\sigma
is smaller than the one in (
5.16
), as a counterpart of the stronger requirement
tf(t)\geq C|t|^{\omega+1}
, but (
5.16
) converges to (
5.19
) as
\omega\rightarrow 0^{+}
. Moreover, the first in (
5.16
) is not needed, in accordance with
[
49
, Thm. B]
and
[
141
, Thm 2
(i)
]
. Next, we investigate the relationship with
(iii),(iv)
in Theorem
5.6
, where
\omega=\chi
is assumed. Since in our case
\omega=0
,
(iii)
and
(iv)
should be compared with case
\chi=0
of Theorem
5.4
. Observe that
\chi=0
includes an interesting class of borderline inequalities such as

\Delta_{p}u\geq b(x)f(u)|\nabla u|^{p-1},

(5.20)

for which we have the next corollary; since, for
\chi=0
, any
\sigma>0
satisfies (
5.8
), when
\mu<1
we obtain a Liouville theorem for solutions
u
with polynomial growth (i.e. satisfying
|u|=O(r^{\sigma})
as
r\rightarrow\infty
for some
\sigma>0
).

Corollary 5.8
.

Let
M
be a complete Riemannian manifold, and consider
\varphi,b,f
meeting the requirements in (
1.5
), (
1.3
) and the bounds (
5.3
) for some
p,\bar{p}>1
. Assume that, for some
\mu\leq 1
, the following inequalities hold:

\begin{array}[]{ll}b(x)\geq C\big(1+r(x)\big)^{-\mu}&\quad\text{on }\,M,\\[5.69046pt]
f(t)\geq C&\quad\text{for }\,t\gg 1\end{array}

(5.21)

and for some constant
C>0
. Let
u\in\mathrm{Lip}_{\mathrm{loc}}(M)
be a non-constant, weak solution of

\Delta_{\varphi}u\geq b(x)f(u)\varphi(|\nabla u|)\qquad\text{on }\,M.

(5.22)

Suppose that
u_{+}(x)=O\big(r(x)^{\sigma}\big)
, for some
\sigma>0
, and that

\begin{array}[]{rlll}(1)&\quad\mu<1,&\displaystyle\qquad\liminf_{r\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\log\mathrm{vol}(B_{r})}{r^{1-\mu}}=0,&\quad\text{ and }\,\sigma>0,\ \text{ or}\\[11.38092pt]
(2)&\quad\mu=1,&\displaystyle\qquad\liminf_{r\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\log\mathrm{vol}(B_{r})}{\log r}=d_{0},&\quad\text{ and }\,0<\sigma\leq\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\frac{p-d_{0}}{p-1},&\text{if }\,d_{0}\geq 1,\\[8.5359pt]
\frac{\bar{p}-d_{0}}{\bar{p}-1}&\text{if }\,d_{0}<1.\end{array}\right.\end{array}

(5.23)

Then,
u
is bounded above on
M
and
f(u^{*})\leq 0
. If moreover
u
satisfies
(P_{=})
,

tf(t)\geq C|t|\qquad\text{for }\,|t|>>1

(5.24)

and either
(i)
or
(ii)
holds for
u_{+}
and for
u_{-}
, then

u\in L^{\infty}(M)\qquad\text{and}\qquad f(u^{*})\leq 0\leq f(u_{*}).

(5.25)

Proof of Corollary
5.8
, assuming Theorem
5.4
.

It is enough to choose
\chi=0
and
\sigma>0
in Theorem
5.4
. An algebraic manipulation shows that the condition on
\sigma
appearing in
(2)
of (
5.23
) is equivalent to

d_{0}\leq\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}p-\sigma(p-1)&\text{if }\,\sigma\leq 1;\\[5.69046pt]
\bar{p}-\sigma(\bar{p}-1)&\text{if }\,\sigma>1.\end{array}\right.

While Theorem
5.4
requires
u_{+}=o(r^{\sigma})
, when
\mu>1
no problem arise as we can enlarge
\sigma
a bit to match this last requirement. On the other hand, if
\mu=1
, thanks to Remark
5.5
we can still reach the conclusion in Theorem
5.4
when
u_{+}=O(r^{\sigma})
. In particular, the upper bound for
\sigma
in (
5.23
) can be achieved.
∎

Remark 5.9
.

Comparing with
(iii),(iv)
in Theorem
5.6
, we readily see that (
5.23
) fits very well with their assumptions, and we can also capture the case
\sigma=\frac{p-m}{p-1}
. On the other hand, it should be remarked that our result is restricted to
\mu\leq 1
.

Remark 5.10
.

In the Euclidean setting
M=\mathbb{R}^{m}
and for
p=\bar{p}\geq m
, other interesting Liouville theorems for slowly growing solutions of
(P_{=})
can be found in
[
124
, Thm. 1.1]
and
[
50
, Thm. 10]
, where the case
tf(t)\geq 0
(
\equiv 0
in the second) is considered. There, the authors obtain the constancy of solutions of
(P_{=})
on
\mathbb{R}^{m}
whenever
p>m
and
6
6

6

It should be observed that assumption (1.3) in
[
124
]
, when rephrased for
(P_{\geq})
, gives necessarily
\varphi(t)=Ct^{p-1}
. However, the above restriction does not appear in Theorem 10 of
[
50
]
, which considers the case
f(t)\equiv 0
.

u(x)=o\left(r(x)^{\frac{p-m}{p-1}}\right)\qquad\text{as }\,r(x)\rightarrow\infty.

(5.26)

Condition (
5.26
) is sharp and related to the growth of the fundamental solution for the
p
-Laplacian (see
[
124
]
and Remark 10.3 in
[
40
]
). Further interesting results covering
p\geq m
can be found in
[
40
]
(Theorems 10.1 and 10.4 therein).

Next, let us show how Corollary
5.8
applies in the setting of the mean curvature operator to give the next

Theorem 5.11
.

Let
M
be a complete manifold satisfying

\liminf_{r\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\log\mathrm{vol}(B_{r})}{r^{1-\mu}}=0,

(5.27)

for some
\mu<1
. Let
b,f,l
satisfy (
1.5
) and

\begin{array}[]{l}\displaystyle b(x)\geq C\big(1+r(x)\big)^{-\mu}\qquad\text{on }\,M,\\[5.69046pt]
\displaystyle l(t)\geq C\frac{t}{\sqrt{1+t^{2}}}\qquad\text{on }\,\mathbb{R}^{+},\\[11.38092pt]
\text{$f$ is non-decreasing on $\mathbb{R}$ and $f\not\equiv 0$}\\[5.69046pt]
\end{array}

(5.28)

for some
C>0
. If
u\in\mathrm{Lip}_{\mathrm{loc}}(M)
is a non-constant solution of

\mathrm{div}\left(\frac{\nabla u}{\sqrt{1+|\nabla u|^{2}}}\right)=b(x)f(u)l(|\nabla u|)\qquad\text{on }\,M

(5.29)

with a polynomial growth, then
u
solves the minimal surface equation

\mathrm{div}\left(\frac{\nabla u}{\sqrt{1+|\nabla u|^{2}}}\right)=0\qquad\text{on }\,M

and it is bounded on one side. In particular, if (
5.27
) is strengthened to

\mathrm{Ric}\geq 0,\quad\qquad K\geq-\kappa^{2}\qquad\text{on M,}

(5.30)

for some constant
\kappa>0
(
K
the sectional curvature of
M
), then a solution of (
5.29
) with a polynomial growth, if any, is constant for each
\mu<1
.

Remark 5.12
.

The assumption
f\not\equiv 0
is necessary, as the example of affine functions solving the minimal graph equation on
\mathbb{R}^{m}
shows.

Remark 5.13
.

When
l\equiv 1
,
b\equiv 1
and
M=\mathbb{R}^{m}
, V. Tkachev
[
144
]
proved Theorem
5.11
for
C^{2}
solutions of (
5.29
) without any growth restriction, see also Thm. 10.4 of A. Farina’s survey
[
48
]
. The result has first been extended for mean curvature type operators by Y. Naito and Y. Usami
[
100
, Thm. 1]
, with a different argument using radialization in a way related to the one in Section
8
. An improvement of
[
144
,
100
]
for (
5.29
) with a nontrivial gradient dependence is shown in Theorem
8.31
below, while generalizations to a larger class of quasilinear operators can be found in
[
141
, Thm. 3]
, with the same method as in
[
144
]
, and in
[
123
, Thm. 8.1.3]
with an approach, by means of Khas’minskii potentials, close to the one in
[
100
]
.

Proof of Theorem
5.11
.

Since one of the sets
\{t\in\mathbb{R}\ :\ f(t)>0\}
and
\{t\in\mathbb{R}\ :\ f(t)<0\}
is non-empty, the monotonicity of
f
implies that either
f(t)\geq C_{1}
for
t>>1
, or
f(t)\leq-C_{1}
for
t<<-1
, for some constant
C_{1}>0
. Without loss of generality, we can suppose
f(t)\geq C_{1}
for
t>>1
. Using Corollary
5.8
, since
u
is non-constant we deduce
u^{*}<\infty
and
f(u^{*})\leq 0
. Again by the monotonicity of
f
, either
f=0
on
(-\infty,u^{*}]
or
f<0
somewhere. In the second case,
f(t)\leq-C
for
t<<-1
and thus, applying again Corollary
5.8
with
-u
replacing
u
and
-f(-t)
replacing
f(t)
we obtain
f(u_{*})\geq 0
. Therefore,
f=0
on
[u_{*},u^{*}]
. Summarizing, in both cases
u
solves the minimal surface equation

\mathrm{div}\left(\frac{\nabla u}{\sqrt{1+|\nabla u|^{2}}}\right)=0\qquad\text{on }\,M

(5.31)

(hence,
u
is smooth), and
u
is bounded on one side. If (
5.27
) is replaced by (
5.30
), the volume comparison implies
\mathrm{vol}(B_{r})\leq Cr^{m}
and therefore (
5.27
) holds for each
\mu<1
. The constancy of
u
is then a consequence of Theorem 1.3 in
[
137
]
, contradicting our assumption.
∎

Remark 5.14
.

It is an open (and, we believe, very interesting) problem to prove the constancy of positive solutions of

\mathrm{div}\left(\frac{\nabla u}{\sqrt{1+|\nabla u|^{2}}}\right)=0

under the only condition that
\mathrm{Ric}\geq 0
, that is, removing the requirement on the sectional curvature in (
5.30
). Indeed, the technique in
[
137
]
relies on uniform gradient estimates for
u
achieved via the Korevaar’s method, for which an assumption on the sectional curvature seems challenging to remove.

We now come to the proof of Theorem
5.4
. The result follows in a more or less direct way from the following refined maximum principle for slowly growing solutions of

\Delta_{\varphi}u\geq K(1+r)^{-\mu}\frac{\varphi(|\nabla u|)}{|\nabla u|^{\chi}}\qquad\text{on }\,\Omega_{\gamma},

(5.32)

where
\Omega_{\gamma}=\{x\in M\,:\,u(x)>\gamma\}
is the superlevel set of
u
at height
\gamma\in\mathbb{R}
.

Theorem 5.15
.

Let
M
be a complete Riemannian manifold and let the growth (
5.3
) be met for some
p,\bar{p}>1
. Let
\mu,\chi\in\mathbb{R}
verify (
5.4
)
and let
u\in\mathrm{Lip}_{\mathrm{loc}}(M)
be a function for which

\hat{u}=\limsup_{r(x)\rightarrow\infty}\frac{u_{+}(x)}{r(x)^{\sigma}}<\infty,

(5.33)

for some
\sigma\in\mathbb{R}^{+}_{0}
satisfying

0\leq\chi\sigma\leq\chi+1-\mu.

(5.34)

Suppose that either one of the following assumptions is met:

\begin{array}[]{lll}\chi\sigma<\chi+1-\mu&\quad\text{and}&\displaystyle\qquad\liminf_{r\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\log\mathrm{vol}(B_{r})}{r^{\chi+1-\mu-\chi\sigma}}=d_{0}<\infty;\\[5.69046pt]
\text{or}\\[5.69046pt]
\chi\sigma=\chi+1-\mu&\quad\text{and}&\displaystyle\qquad\liminf_{r\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\log\mathrm{vol}(B_{r})}{\log r}=d_{0}<\infty;\end{array}

(5.35)

If, for some
\gamma\in\mathbb{R}
, the open set
\Omega_{\gamma}
is non-empty and
u
is a non-constant, weak solution of (
5.32
) in
\Omega_{\gamma}
,
then

K\leq H\cdot\hat{u}^{\chi},

(5.36)

where, setting

\zeta=\chi+1-\mu,\qquad{\rm and}\quad d^{*}=\min\Big\{p-\sigma(p-1),\ \bar{p}-\sigma(\bar{p}-1)\Big\},

(5.37)

the constant
H=H(\sigma,\chi,p,\mu,d_{0})
is given by

\begin{array}[]{l}H=\left\{\begin{array}[]{rll}(i)&\ 0&\quad\text{if }\quad\chi>0,\quad\sigma=0;\\[5.69046pt]
(ii)&\ d_{0}\big[\zeta-\chi\sigma\big]^{\chi+1}&\quad\displaystyle\text{if }\quad\chi>0,\quad 0<\chi\sigma<\frac{\chi}{\chi+1}\zeta\\[8.5359pt]
(iii)&\ d_{0}\sigma^{\chi}(\zeta-\chi\sigma)&\quad\displaystyle\text{if }\quad\chi>0,\quad\frac{\chi}{\chi+1}\zeta\leq\chi\sigma<\zeta\\[11.38092pt]
(iv)&\ d_{0}(1-\mu)&\quad\text{if }\quad\chi=0,\quad\mu<1,\quad\sigma\geq 0\\[5.69046pt]
(v)&\ \sigma^{\chi}(d_{0}-d^{*})_{+}&\quad\displaystyle\text{if }\quad\chi\sigma=\zeta>0\ \ \text{ or }\ \ \chi\sigma=\zeta=0,\ \chi=0.\end{array}\right.\end{array}

(5.38)

Proof.

We can suppose
K>0
, otherwise the estimate is trivial. Note that (
5.32
) is invariant with respect to translations
u\mapsto u_{s}=u+s
. Fix
\beta>\hat{u}
. We claim that a suitable translated
u_{s}
satisfies

u_{s}\leq\beta(1+r)^{\sigma}\qquad\text{on }\,M,\qquad u_{s}>0\ \text{ somewhere.}

(5.39)

Indeed, if
\sigma>0
, (
5.33
) implies that
u<\beta(1+r)^{\sigma}
outside a large compact set
\Omega
, and translating
u
downwards we can achieve the same inequality also in
\Omega
, still keeping
u_{s}>0
somewhere. On the other hand, the claim is obvious if
\sigma=0
. In this last case, note that here we do
not
claim that
\hat{u}
is not attained: this would follow from a strong maximum principle, that to the best of our knowledge is unknown under the sole assumption (
5.3
). Using that the resulting
u_{s}
is positive somewhere, we can also assume
\gamma>0
. Hereafter, computations will be performed with
u=u_{s}
. Choose
\alpha>\beta
and define

v(x)=\alpha\big(1+r(x)\big)^{\sigma}-u(x),

so that

(\alpha-\beta)(1+r)^{\sigma}\leq v\leq\alpha(1+r)^{\sigma}\qquad\text{on }\,\Omega_{\gamma}.

(5.40)

Hereafter, with
C_{1},C_{2},C_{3},\ldots
we will denote positive absolute constants, that is, independent of
\sigma,\mu,\chi
. Fix a function
\lambda\in C^{1}(\mathbb{R})
such that

0\leq\lambda\leq 1,\quad\lambda\equiv 0\ \ \text{ on }\,(-\infty,\gamma],\qquad\lambda>0\ \ \text{on }\,(\gamma,\infty),\quad\lambda^{\prime}\geq 0,

and a cut-off function
\psi\in\mathrm{Lip}_{c}(M)
,
0\leq\psi\leq 1
, whose support has nontrivial intersection with
\Omega_{\gamma}
. Next, consider
F\in C^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
,
F=F(r,v)
, satisfying

F(r,v)>0,\qquad F_{v}=\frac{\partial F}{\partial v}(r,v)<0.

(5.41)

Suppose first that
\bar{p}\geq p
. We insert the test function

\psi^{\bar{p}}\lambda(u)F(v,r)\in\mathrm{Lip}_{c}(\overline{\Omega}_{\gamma})

(5.42)

in the weak definition of (
5.32
). Using
\lambda^{\prime}\geq 0
together with the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality we obtain

\begin{array}[]{l}\displaystyle K\int\psi^{\bar{p}}\lambda F(1+r)^{-\mu}\frac{\varphi(|\nabla u|)}{|\nabla u|^{\chi}}\leq\displaystyle\bar{p}\int\psi^{\bar{p}-1}\lambda F\varphi(|\nabla u|)|\nabla\psi|+\int\psi^{\bar{p}}\lambda F_{v}\varphi(|\nabla u|)|\nabla u|\\[14.22636pt]
\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\int\psi^{\bar{p}}\lambda\varphi(|\nabla u|)\left|\alpha\sigma(1+r)^{\sigma-1}F_{v}+F_{r}\right|.\end{array}

(5.43)

Rearranging,

\begin{array}[]{l}\displaystyle\int\psi^{\bar{p}}\lambda\left|F_{v}\right|\frac{\varphi(|\nabla u|)}{|\nabla u|^{\chi}}B(x,u)\leq\bar{p}\int\psi^{\bar{p}-1}\lambda F\varphi(|\nabla u|)|\nabla\psi|,\end{array}

(5.44)

with

\displaystyle B(x,u)=\displaystyle K(1+r)^{-\mu}\frac{F}{|F_{v}|}+|\nabla u|^{\chi+1}-|\nabla u|^{\chi}\left|-\alpha\sigma(1+r)^{\sigma-1}+\frac{F_{r}}{|F_{v}|}\right|.

(5.45)

Let us assume the validity of the following

\emph{claim:}\quad B(x,u)\geq\Lambda|\nabla u|^{\chi+1}\qquad\text{for some $\Lambda\in(0,1]$ independent of $r$.}

(5.46)

Plugging into (
5.44
) gives

\begin{array}[]{l}\displaystyle\frac{\Lambda}{\bar{p}}\int\psi^{\bar{p}}\lambda\left|F_{v}\right|\varphi(|\nabla u|)|\nabla u|\leq\int\psi^{\bar{p}-1}\lambda F\varphi(|\nabla u|)|\nabla\psi|,\end{array}

(5.47)

We now split the integrals on the subsets
\{|\nabla u|<1\}
and
\{|\nabla u|\geq 1\}
, where we apply different Young inequalities. Letting
p^{\prime},\bar{p}^{\prime}
be the conjugate exponents to
p
and
\bar{p}
, we can rewrite (
5.49
) as follows:

\begin{array}[]{l}\displaystyle\frac{\Lambda}{\bar{p}}\int_{\{|\nabla u|<1\}}\psi^{\bar{p}}\lambda\left|F_{v}\right|\left[\frac{\varphi(|\nabla u|)}{|\nabla u|^{p-1}}\right]|\nabla u|^{p}+\displaystyle\frac{\Lambda}{\bar{p}}\int_{\{|\nabla u|\geq 1\}}\psi^{\bar{p}}\lambda\left|F_{v}\right|\left[\frac{\varphi(|\nabla u|)}{|\nabla u|^{\bar{p}-1}}\right]|\nabla u|^{\bar{p}}\\[11.38092pt]
\displaystyle\qquad\leq\int_{\{|\nabla u|<1\}}\left[\psi^{\bar{p}}\lambda\left|F_{v}\right|\left(\frac{\varphi(|\nabla u|)}{|\nabla u|^{p-1}}\right)|\nabla u|^{p}\right]^{\frac{1}{p^{\prime}}}\left[\psi^{\bar{p}-1-\frac{\bar{p}}{p^{\prime}}}\lambda^{\frac{1}{p}}F|F_{v}|^{-\frac{1}{p^{\prime}}}\left(\frac{\varphi(|\nabla u|)}{|\nabla u|^{p-1}}\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}|\nabla\psi|\right]\\[11.38092pt]
\displaystyle\qquad\ \ +\int_{\{|\nabla u|\geq 1\}}\left[\psi^{\bar{p}}\lambda\left|F_{v}\right|\left(\frac{\varphi(|\nabla u|)}{|\nabla u|^{\bar{p}-1}}\right)|\nabla u|^{\bar{p}}\right]^{\frac{1}{\bar{p}^{\prime}}}\left[\lambda^{\frac{1}{\bar{p}}}F|F_{v}|^{-\frac{1}{\bar{p}^{\prime}}}\left(\frac{\varphi(|\nabla u|)}{|\nabla u|^{\bar{p}-1}}\right)^{\frac{1}{\bar{p}}}|\nabla\psi|\right]\end{array}

(5.48)

Observe that
\bar{p}\geq p
implies the non-negativity of the exponent
\bar{p}-1-\frac{\bar{p}}{p^{\prime}}\geq 0
for
\psi
above. We apply Young inequality
ab\leq(a\varepsilon)^{p^{\prime}}/p^{\prime}+(b/\varepsilon)^{p}/p
to the first term in the right-hand side of (
5.49
), and an analogous one with
\bar{\varepsilon},\bar{p},\bar{p}^{\prime}
to the second one. Rearranging, we obtain

\begin{array}[]{l}\displaystyle\left(\frac{\Lambda}{\bar{p}}-\frac{\varepsilon^{p^{\prime}}}{p^{\prime}}\right)\int_{\{|\nabla u|<1\}}\psi^{\bar{p}}\lambda\left|F_{v}\right|\left[\frac{\varphi(|\nabla u|)}{|\nabla u|^{p-1}}\right]|\nabla u|^{p}\\[11.38092pt]
+\displaystyle\left(\frac{\Lambda}{\bar{p}}-\frac{\bar{\varepsilon}^{\bar{p}^{\prime}}}{\bar{p}^{\prime}}\right)\int_{\{|\nabla u|\geq 1\}}\psi^{\bar{p}}\lambda\left|F_{v}\right|\left[\frac{\varphi(|\nabla u|)}{|\nabla u|^{\bar{p}-1}}\right]|\nabla u|^{\bar{p}}\\[11.38092pt]
\displaystyle\qquad\displaystyle\leq\frac{\varepsilon^{-p}}{p}\int_{\{|\nabla u|<1\}}\psi^{\bar{p}-p}\lambda F\left[\frac{F}{|F_{v}|}\right]^{p-1}\left(\frac{\varphi(|\nabla u|)}{|\nabla u|^{p-1}}\right)|\nabla\psi|^{p}\\[11.38092pt]
\displaystyle\qquad\ \ +\frac{\bar{\varepsilon}^{-\bar{p}}}{\bar{p}}\int_{\{|\nabla u|\geq 1\}}\lambda F\left[\frac{F}{|F_{v}|}\right]^{\bar{p}-1}\left(\frac{\varphi(|\nabla u|)}{|\nabla u|^{\bar{p}-1}}\right)|\nabla\psi|^{\bar{p}}\end{array}

(5.49)

Choose
\varepsilon,\bar{\varepsilon}
in such a way that both the coefficients in round brackets in the left-hand side are
\Lambda/(2\bar{p})
. From
\bar{p}\geq p
,
\psi\leq 1
and
\Lambda\leq 1
, using (
5.3
) we infer the inequality

\int\psi^{\bar{p}}\lambda\left|F_{v}\right|\varphi(|\nabla u|)|\nabla u|\leq C_{1}\left[\int\lambda F\left(\frac{F}{|F_{v}|}\right)^{p-1}|\nabla\psi|^{p}+\int\lambda F\left(\frac{F}{|F_{v}|}\right)^{\bar{p}-1}|\nabla\psi|^{\bar{p}}\right],

(5.50)

where
C_{1}
is some constant depending on
\Lambda
and on
p,\bar{p},C,\bar{C}
in (
5.3
). Fix
R_{0}\geq 1
large enough that
u
is not constant on
\Omega_{\gamma}\cap B_{R_{0}}\neq\emptyset
. Then, clearly
\nabla u
is not identically zero on
\Omega_{\gamma}\cap B_{R_{0}}
, because otherwise
u
would be constant on connected components of
\Omega_{\gamma}
, which would imply that
\Omega_{\gamma}\equiv M
and
u
be constant, contradiction. Fix
\delta\in(1/2,1)
. For
R>2R_{0}\geq 2
, we choose
\psi
in such a way that

0\leq\psi\leq 1,\quad\psi\equiv 1\ \text{ on }B_{\delta R},\quad\operatorname{supp}(\psi)\subset B_{R},\quad|\nabla\psi|\leq\frac{C_{2}}{(1-\delta)R},

(5.51)

for some absolute constant
C_{2}
.
Inserting into (
5.50
) and recalling that
\lambda\leq 1
we obtain

\begin{array}[]{l}\displaystyle\int_{\Omega_{\gamma}\cap B_{R_{0}}}\lambda\left|F_{v}\right|\varphi(|\nabla u|)|\nabla u|\\[14.22636pt]
\qquad\leq\quad\displaystyle C_{3}\left[\frac{1}{R^{p}}\int_{(B_{R}\backslash B_{\delta R})\cap\Omega_{\gamma}}F\left(\frac{F}{|F_{v}|}\right)^{p-1}+\frac{1}{R^{\bar{p}}}\int_{(B_{R}\backslash B_{\delta R})\cap\Omega_{\gamma}}F\left(\frac{F}{|F_{v}|}\right)^{\bar{p}-1}\right],\end{array}

(5.52)

for some
C_{3}=C_{3}(p,\bar{p},C,\bar{C},\Lambda,\delta)
. In the complementary case
\bar{p}\leq p
, we achieve (
5.52
) by simply exchanging the role of
p,\bar{p}
and the related inequalities on
\{|\nabla u|<1\}
,
\{|\nabla u|\geq 1\}
.

We now need to check the validity of the claim in (
5.46
), for a suitable choice of
F
. Observe that the expression of
B
in (
5.45
) is a function of the type

g(s)=P+s^{\chi+1}-Qs^{\chi},

for
s=|\nabla u|
and positive parameters

P=K(1+r)^{-\mu}\frac{F}{|F_{v}|},\qquad Q=\left|-\alpha\sigma(1+r)^{\sigma-1}+\frac{F_{r}}{|F_{v}|}\right|

depending on
r
. It is a calculus exercise to check that
g(s)\geq\Lambda s^{\chi+1}
on
\mathbb{R}^{+}_{0}
when either

\left\{\begin{array}[]{lll}\chi=0,&\quad Q\leq P&\quad\text{and}\qquad\Lambda\leq 1,\quad\text{or}\\[5.69046pt]
\chi>0,&\displaystyle\quad\frac{Q^{\chi+1}}{P}\leq\frac{(\chi+1)^{\chi+1}}{\chi^{\chi}}&\quad\text{and}\displaystyle\qquad\Lambda\leq 1-\frac{\chi}{(\chi+1)^{\frac{\chi+1}{\chi}}}\left(\frac{Q^{\chi+1}}{P}\right)^{1/\chi}.\end{array}\right.,

(5.53)

Having fixed a parameter
\theta\in(0,1)
, we will choose
F
in order to satisfy the next relations between
Q
and
P
:

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\text{if }\,\chi=0\,\text{ we want }\ \ Q=P,&\quad\text{and in this case set}\quad\Lambda=1;\\[5.69046pt]
\text{if }\,\chi>0\,\text{ we want }\ \ \frac{Q^{\chi+1}}{P}=\theta^{\chi}\frac{(\chi+1)^{\chi+1}}{\chi^{\chi}}&\quad\text{and in this case set}\displaystyle\quad\Lambda=1-\theta.\end{array}\right.

(5.54)

In this way, (
5.53
) and thus (
5.46
) is met. Observe that the first case in (
5.54
) can be obtained by letting
\chi\rightarrow 0
and then
\theta\rightarrow 0
in the second one. To meet the identities in (
5.54
), we necessarily need an upper bound for
Q/P
or
Q^{\chi+1}/P
that does not depend on
r
, and this suggests our choice of
F
, that will be different from case to case. Set for convenience

\eta=\mu+(\sigma-1)(\chi+1)=(\chi+1)\sigma-\zeta,

(5.55)

where
\zeta
is as in (
5.37
), and note that

\displaystyle\sigma-\eta=\zeta-\chi\sigma\geq 0.

(5.56)

Analysis of case
(ii)
, case
(i)
for
\zeta>0
, and case
(iv)
for
\sigma<1-\mu
:

\chi>0,\quad 0\leq\chi\sigma<\frac{\chi}{\chi+1}\zeta,\qquad\text{or}\qquad\chi=0,\quad\mu<1,\quad 0\leq\sigma<1-\mu.

Using the definition of
\eta
, these cases correspond to

\chi>0,\qquad\sigma\geq 0,\quad\eta<0.

Note that
\sigma>\eta
. We choose

F(v,r)=\exp\left\{-\tau v(1+r)^{-\eta}\right\},

(5.57)

for a real number
\tau>0
that will be specified later in order to satisfy the identity for
P,Q
in (
5.54
). Then, on
\Omega_{\gamma}

\frac{F}{|F_{v}|}=\frac{(1+r)^{\eta}}{\tau},\qquad\frac{F_{r}}{|F_{v}|}=\frac{v\eta}{(1+r)},

and hence, by (
5.40
) and using
\sigma>\eta
,
\eta<0

\displaystyle-\alpha(\sigma-\eta)(1+r)^{\sigma-1}\leq-\alpha\sigma(1+r)^{\sigma-1}+\frac{F_{r}}{|F_{v}|}\leq 0

Plugging into (
5.45
) we get

B(x,u)\geq\displaystyle\frac{K}{\tau}(1+r)^{\eta-\mu}+|\nabla u|^{\chi+1}-|\nabla u|^{\chi}\alpha(\sigma-\eta)(1+r)^{\sigma-1},

(5.58)

In view of (
5.55
), to satisfy (
5.54
) with

P=\frac{K}{\tau}(1+r)^{\eta-\mu},\qquad Q=\alpha(\sigma-\eta)(1+r)^{\sigma-1}

(5.59)

we need the identities

\begin{array}[]{ll}\displaystyle\frac{[\alpha(\sigma-\eta)]^{\chi+1}\tau}{K}=\theta^{\chi}\frac{(\chi+1)^{\chi+1}}{\chi^{\chi}}&\quad\text{if }\,\chi>0;\\[11.38092pt]
\displaystyle\frac{\alpha(\sigma-\eta)\tau}{K}=1&\quad\text{if }\,\chi=0.\end{array}

(5.60)

According to whether
\chi=0
or
>0
, we then define
\tau
as the value such that (
5.60
) holds. With this choice, (
5.46
) is satisfied with
\Lambda=1-\theta
(if
\chi>0
) or
\Lambda=1
(if
\chi=0
), and in view of our choice of
F
, (
5.52
) becomes

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\displaystyle\int_{B_{R_{0}}\cap\Omega_{\gamma}}\lambda F(1+r)^{-\eta}\varphi(|\nabla u|)|\nabla u|&\leq&\displaystyle C_{4}\left[\frac{1}{R^{p}}\int_{(B_{R}\backslash B_{\delta R})\cap\Omega_{\gamma}}F(1+r)^{\eta(p-1)}\right.\\[14.22636pt]
&&\displaystyle\left.+\frac{1}{R^{\bar{p}}}\int_{(B_{R}\backslash B_{\delta R})\cap\Omega_{\gamma}}F(1+r)^{\eta(\bar{p}-1)}\right]\end{array}

(5.61)

Where
C_{4}
also depends on
\tau
. Up to increasing
\bar{p}
, a change that does not alter the validity of (
5.3
), we can suppose that
\bar{p}\geq p
. On
(B_{R}\backslash B_{\theta R})\cap\Omega_{\gamma}
, (
5.40
) and
\sigma-\eta>0
,
\eta<0
give

F(v,r)\leq\exp\left(-\tau(\alpha-\beta)(\delta R)^{\sigma-\eta}\right),\qquad(1+r)^{\eta(\bar{p}-1)}\leq(1+r)^{\eta(p-1)}\leq 1,

Inserting into (
5.61
) and using
R^{\bar{p}}\geq R^{p}
, we eventually get

0<\displaystyle\int_{\Omega_{\gamma}\cap B_{R_{0}}}\lambda F(1+r)^{-\eta}\varphi(|\nabla u|)|\nabla u|\leq\frac{C_{5}}{R^{p}}\exp\left(-\tau(\alpha-\beta)(\delta R)^{\sigma-\eta}\right)\mathrm{vol}(B_{R}).

(5.62)

Because of (
5.35
) and (
5.56
), for each
d>d_{0}
there exists a sequence
\{R_{k}\}\uparrow\infty
such that

\mathrm{vol}(B_{R_{k}})\leq\exp\left\{dR_{k}^{\sigma-\eta}\right\}.

Substituting into (
5.62
) and letting
k\rightarrow\infty
,

\begin{array}[]{lcl}0&<&\displaystyle\int_{\Omega_{\gamma}\cap B_{R_{0}}}\lambda F(1+r)^{-\eta}\varphi(|\nabla u|)|\nabla u|\\[14.22636pt]
&\leq&\displaystyle C_{5}\limsup_{k\rightarrow\infty}\left(\frac{\exp\left\{-\tau(\alpha-\beta)\delta^{\sigma-\eta}R_{k}^{\sigma-\eta}+dR_{k}^{\sigma-\eta}\right\}}{R_{k}^{p}}\right).\end{array}

(5.63)

Being the left-hand side of the above inequality strictly bigger than zero, we deduce that necessarily
d\geq\tau(\alpha-\beta)\delta^{\sigma-\eta}
, and letting
\delta\rightarrow 1
and
d\downarrow d_{0}
. we get

d_{0}\geq\tau(\alpha-\beta)

(5.64)

Substituting the expression of
\tau
in (
5.60
), setting
\alpha=t\beta
with
t>1
and letting
\theta\rightarrow 1
we deduce

\begin{array}[]{ll}\displaystyle K\leq d_{0}\frac{\chi^{\chi}}{(\chi+1)^{\chi+1}}(\sigma-\eta)^{\chi+1}\frac{t^{\chi+1}}{t-1}\beta^{\chi}&\quad\text{if }\,\chi>0;\\[11.38092pt]
\displaystyle K\leq d_{0}(\sigma-\eta)\frac{t}{t-1}&\quad\text{if }\,\chi=0.\\[11.38092pt]
\end{array}

Minimizing with respect to
t\in(1,\infty)
and letting
\beta\downarrow\hat{u}
, we eventually get

\begin{array}[]{ll}K\leq d_{0}(\sigma-\eta)^{\chi+1}\hat{u}^{\chi}=d_{0}\big[\zeta-\chi\sigma\big]^{\chi+1}\hat{u}^{\chi}&\quad\text{if }\,\chi>0;\\[5.69046pt]
K\leq d_{0}(\sigma-\eta)=d_{0}(1-\mu)&\quad\text{if }\,\chi=0,\\[5.69046pt]
\end{array}

(5.65)

as claimed. We conclude by investigating part of case
(i)
, that is, when

\chi>0,\quad\sigma=0<\zeta.

Observe that a downward translation
u_{s}
of
u
still satisfies (
5.32
) with the same constant
K
(without loss of generality, we can suppose
\gamma=0
). Hence, by (
5.65
),

K\leq H\hat{u}_{s}^{\chi}\qquad\text{with }\quad H\leq d_{0}\big[\zeta-\chi\sigma\big]^{\chi+1}.

(5.66)

If
K>0
, since
u
is bounded above and
\chi>0
we can choose
u_{s}^{\chi}
positive and small enough to contradict (
5.66
). Hence, necessarily
K=0
, and a-posteriori we can choose
H=0
in (
5.38
) as required. At the end of the present proof, with the same trick we investigate the remaining case of
(i)
, that is, when
\sigma=\zeta=0
. Note that the trick is not possible if
\chi=0
, being
u_{s}^{\chi}\equiv 1
.

Analysis of case
(iii)
, and case
(iv)
for
\sigma\geq 1-\mu
:

\chi>0,\quad\frac{\chi}{\chi+1}\zeta\leq\chi\sigma<\zeta,\qquad\text{or}\qquad\chi=0,\quad\sigma\geq 1-\mu>0.

Again from the definition of
\eta
, these cases correspond to the range

0\leq\eta<\sigma,

for which we choose

F(v,r)=\exp\left\{-\tau v^{\frac{\sigma-\eta}{\sigma}}\right\}.

(5.67)

Also in these cases, we increase
\bar{p}
in order for
\bar{p}\geq p
to hold, since ultimately the size of
\bar{p}
will not affect the conclusion of the theorem. Performing computations analogous to the ones giving
(ii)
, we obtain the desired estimate

\begin{array}[]{ll}\displaystyle K\leq d_{0}\sigma^{\chi}(\sigma-\eta)\hat{u}^{\chi}=d_{0}\sigma^{\chi}\big[\zeta-\chi\sigma\big]\hat{u}^{\chi}&\quad\text{if }\,\chi>0;\\[8.5359pt]
\displaystyle K\leq d_{0}(\sigma-\eta)=d_{0}(1-\mu)&\quad\text{if }\,\chi=0.\\[8.5359pt]
\end{array}

Analysis of case
(v)
, and case
(i)
for
\zeta=0
:

\chi\sigma=\zeta>0,\quad\text{or}\quad\chi\sigma=\zeta=0.

In this case, by (
5.55
) it holds
\sigma-\mu=(\sigma-1)(\chi+1)
. We choose

F(v,r)=v^{-\tau},

(5.68)

\tau>0
to be determined. Then, using (
5.40
),

\begin{array}[]{lcl}B(x,u)&\geq&\displaystyle\frac{K}{\tau}(1+r)^{-\mu}v+|\nabla u|^{\chi+1}-|\nabla u|^{\chi}\alpha\sigma(1+r)^{\sigma-1}\\[14.22636pt]
&\geq&\displaystyle\frac{K(\alpha-\beta)}{\tau}(1+r)^{\sigma-\mu}+|\nabla u|^{\chi+1}-|\nabla u|^{\chi}\alpha\sigma(1+r)^{\sigma-1},\end{array}

(5.69)

and (
5.54
) applied with

P=\frac{K(\alpha-\beta)}{\tau}(1+r)^{\sigma-\mu},\qquad Q=\alpha\sigma(1+r)^{\sigma-1}

implies the identities

\begin{array}[]{ll}\displaystyle\frac{(\alpha\sigma)^{\chi+1}\tau}{K(\alpha-\beta)}=\theta^{\chi}\frac{(\chi+1)^{\chi+1}}{\chi^{\chi}}&\quad\text{if }\,\chi>0;\\[11.38092pt]
\displaystyle\frac{\alpha\sigma\tau}{K(\alpha-\beta)}=1&\quad\text{if }\,\chi=0.\end{array}

(5.70)

Having specified
\tau
to satisfy (
5.70
), (
5.46
) is met and (
5.52
) with
\delta=3/4
now reads

0<\displaystyle\int_{\Omega_{\gamma}\cap B_{R_{0}}}\lambda\left|F_{v}\right|\varphi(|\nabla u|)|\nabla u|\leq\displaystyle C_{4}\int_{(B_{R}\backslash B_{3R/4})\cap\Omega_{\gamma}}\frac{v^{p-1-\tau}}{R^{p}}+\frac{v^{\bar{p}-1-\tau}}{R^{\bar{p}}}.

(5.71)

Estimating
v
with the aid of (
5.40
), choosing the upper or lower bound according to the sign of
p-1-\tau
and
\bar{p}-1-\tau
, the right-hand side is bounded from above by

C_{5}\mathrm{vol}(B_{R})\Big(R^{-p+\sigma(p-1-\tau)}+R^{-\bar{p}+\sigma(\bar{p}-1-\tau)}\Big),

for a suitable constant
C_{5}>0
. Because of
(
5.35
)
, for
d>d_{0}
we consider a sequence
\{R_{k}\}
for which
\mathrm{vol}(B_{R_{k}})\leq R_{k}^{d}
. Evaluating (
5.52
) on
R_{k}
, and letting
k\rightarrow\infty
in (
5.71
) and then
d\downarrow d_{0}
, we deduce that necessarily

d_{0}\geq\min\big\{p-\sigma(p-1-\tau),\bar{p}-\sigma(\bar{p}-1-\tau)\big\},

that is, by (
5.37
),

\tau\sigma\leq d_{0}-\min\Big\{p-\sigma(p-1),\bar{p}-\sigma(\bar{p}-1)\Big\}=d_{0}-d^{*}.

(5.72)

(i)

If
d_{0}<d^{*}
or
d_{0}=d^{*}
and
\sigma>0
, then there exists no
\tau>0
satisfying (
5.72
). Thus,
K>0
leads to a contradiction, and we can therefore choose
H=0
. This proves
(v)
for
d_{0}<d_{*}
and for
d_{0}=d^{*}
,
\sigma>0
, as well as
(i)
for
\chi>0
,
\sigma=\zeta=0
,
d_{0}<d^{*}
.

(ii)

If
d_{0}\geq d^{*}
then, inserting the expression of
\tau
obtained from (
5.70
) in (
5.72
), setting
\alpha=t\beta
for
t>1
, solving (
5.72
) with respect to
K
, letting
\theta\uparrow 1
and
\beta\downarrow\hat{u}
we deduce

\begin{array}[]{ll}\displaystyle K\leq\left[d_{0}-d^{*}\right]\frac{\chi^{\chi}}{(\chi+1)^{\chi+1}}\hat{u}^{\chi}\sigma^{\chi}\frac{t^{\chi+1}}{t-1}&\quad\text{if }\,\chi>0;\\[11.38092pt]
\displaystyle K\leq\left[d_{0}-d^{*}\right]\frac{t}{t-1}&\quad\text{if }\,\chi=0,\end{array}

and minimizing over
t\in(1,\infty)
we get for both
\chi>0
and
\chi=0

K\leq\left[d_{0}-d^{*}\right]\sigma^{\chi}\hat{u}^{\chi}.

(5.73)

This concludes the cases
d_{0}>d^{*}
and
\sigma>0
, and
d_{0}\geq d^{*}
and
\chi=0
. To deal with the remaining part of
(i)
, that is,
\chi>0
,
\sigma=\zeta=0
and
d_{0}\geq d^{*}
, we can consider a downward translation
u_{s}
of
u
in place of
u
, and
\gamma=0
. Then,
u_{s}
satisfies (
5.32
) with the same constant
K
, hence (
5.73
) holds for each
\hat{u}_{s}
. However, from
\chi>0
,
\hat{u}_{s}^{\chi}
can be made as small as we wish, and since we have assumed
K>0
this would contradict (
5.73
). Concluding, necessarily
K\leq 0
, and
H
can be chosen to be zero, as required.

∎

We now prove Theorem
5.4

Proof of Theorem
5.4
.

Suppose, by contradiction, that either
u^{*}=\infty
or
f(u^{*})>0
. Because of the second in (
5.21
) and the continuity of
f
, in both of the cases there exists
\gamma<u^{*}
sufficiently close to
u^{*}
such that
f(t)\geq C>0
on
(\gamma,\infty)
, for some constant
C>0
. By (
5.21
),
u
would solve

\Delta_{\varphi}u\geq K(1+r)^{-\mu}\frac{\varphi(|\nabla u|)}{|\nabla u|^{\chi}}\qquad\text{on }\,\Omega_{\gamma},

For some
K>0
. To apply Theorem
5.15
, we shall consider

d^{*}=\min\Big\{p-\sigma(p-1),\bar{p}-\sigma(\bar{p}-1)\Big\}.

As said before,
p
(respectively,
\bar{p}
) in (
5.3
) can be reduced (resp. increased) as much as we wish, still keeping the validity of (
5.3
). Therefore,

-

if
\sigma\leq 1
, we can increase
\bar{p}
up to satisfy
\bar{p}\geq p
, that gives
d^{*}=p-\sigma(p-1)
. In particular,
d^{*}=p
when
\sigma=0
;

-

if
\sigma>1
, we can reduce
p
to satisfy
\bar{p}\geq p
, that now implies
d^{*}=\bar{p}-\sigma(\bar{p}-1)
.

The volume growth conditions in the second lines of (
5.6
) and (
5.9
) can therefore be rewritten as

\liminf_{r\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\log\mathrm{vol}(B_{r})}{\log r}\leq d^{*}.

We are in the position to apply Theorem
5.15
and deduce
0<K\leq H\hat{u}^{\chi}
. We reach a contradiction by proving that either
H=0
or
\hat{u}^{\chi}=0
. We split into cases:

-

If
\sigma=0
and
\chi>0
, then we are in case
(i)
of Theorem
5.15
and (
5.35
) is satisfied because of (
5.6
), thus
H=0
, contradiction.

-

If
\sigma\geq 0
and
\chi=0
, then we are either in case
(iv)
or in case
(v)
of Theorem
5.15
, according to whether
\mu<1
or
\mu=1
. In case
(iv)
, our growth requirements (
5.6
) and (
5.9
) for
\chi=0
,
\mu<1
imply that
d_{0}=0
in (
5.35
). Applying Theorem
5.15
we get
H\leq d_{0}(1-\mu)=0
, contradiction. In case
(v)
, as said conditions (
5.6
) and (
5.9
) for
\chi=0
,
\mu=1
are equivalent to
d_{0}\leq d^{*}
. By Theorem
5.15
,
H\leq\sigma^{\chi}(d_{0}-d^{*})_{+}=0
, contradiction.

-

If
\sigma>0
,
\chi>0
, then by (
5.7
) we get
\hat{u}^{\chi}=0
, contradiction.

We have thus proved
u^{*}<\infty
and
f(u^{*})\leq 0
. If now
u
satisfies
(P_{=})
and (
5.10
) is in force, we can apply the result both to
u
and to
\bar{u}=-u
, noting that

\Delta_{\varphi}\bar{u}=b(x)\bar{f}(\bar{u})l(|\nabla\bar{u}|),\qquad\bar{f}(t)=-f(-t),

and that
\bar{f}(t)\geq C
for
t
large enough. From
\bar{f}(\bar{u}^{*})\leq 0
we get
f(u_{*})\geq 0
, and (
5.11
) follows.
∎

Remark 5.16
.

We now check Remark
5.5
. Since the third in (
5.9
) corresponds to case
(v)
of Theorem
5.15
, if
d_{0}\leq d^{*}
we achieve the contradiction
0<K\leq H\hat{u}^{\chi}
irrespectively to the vanishing of
\hat{u}
, as claimed.

5.2.1
Bernstein theorems for minimal and MCF soliton graphs

We now apply Theorem
5.4
to deduce some Bernstein type results for prescribed mean curvature graphs. We consider an ambient space
(\bar{M}^{m+1},(\,,\,))
with the warped product structure

\bar{M}=\mathbb{R}\times_{h}M,\qquad(\,,\,)=\mathrm{d}s^{2}+h(s)^{2}\langle\,,\,\rangle

(5.74)

for some complete manifold
(M^{m},\langle\,,\,\rangle)
and some
0<h\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
. Given
v:M\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
, we consider the graph

\Sigma=\Big\{(v(x),x)\in\bar{M}\ \ :\ \ x\in M\Big\}.

In the next theorems we always consider
entire
graphs, that is, graphs defined on all of
M
. As in Subsection
1.1
in the Introduction, we let
\Phi_{t}
be the flow of the conformal field
X=h(s)\partial_{s}
, and we note that its flow parameter
t
starting from the slice
\{s=0\}
satisfies (
1.9
):

t=\int_{0}^{s}\frac{\mathrm{d}\sigma}{h(\sigma)},\qquad t:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow t(\mathbb{R})=I.

(5.75)

We define
\lambda(t)=h(s(t))
,
u(x)=t(v(x))
, and note that
u:M\rightarrow I
.

Theorem 5.17
.

Let
(M^{m},\langle\,,\,\rangle)
be a complete manifold, and consider the warped product
\bar{M}=\mathbb{R}\times_{h}M
. Suppose that
h
satisfies:

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}h^{\prime}(s)s\geq Cs\qquad\text{for }\,|s|\geq r_{0};\\[8.5359pt]
h^{-1}\in L^{1}(+\infty)\cap L^{1}(-\infty),\end{array}\right.

(5.76)

for some constants
C,s_{0}>0
. If the volume growth of geodesic balls in
M
satisfies

\liminf_{r\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\log\mathrm{vol}(B_{r})}{r^{2}}<\infty,

(5.77)

Then every entire, geodesic minimal graph
\Sigma
over
M
is bounded and, letting
v:M\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
be the graph function,

h^{\prime}(v^{*})\leq 0\leq h^{\prime}(v_{*}).

(5.78)

In particular, if
h
is strictly convex,
\Sigma
is the totally geodesic slice
\{s=s_{1}\}
, where
s_{1}
is the unique minimum of
h
.

Proof.

By (
1.11
) and the minimality of
\Sigma
,
u
solves

\mathrm{div}\left(\frac{\nabla u}{\sqrt{1+|\nabla u|^{2}}}\right)=m\frac{\lambda_{t}(u)}{\lambda(u)}\frac{1}{\sqrt{1+|\nabla u|^{2}}}

The second in (
5.76
) implies that
I
is a bounded interval, and thus
u:M\rightarrow I
is bounded. Taking into account that

f(t)=\frac{\lambda_{t}(t)}{\lambda(t)}=h^{\prime}(s(t))\geq C\qquad\text{for }\,t\geq t(s_{0}),

(5.79)

we get

\mathrm{div}\left(\frac{\nabla u}{\sqrt{1+|\nabla u|^{2}}}\right)\geq\frac{mC}{\sqrt{1+|\nabla u|^{2}}}\qquad\text{on }\,\{u>t(s_{0})\}.

If the set
\{u>t(s_{0})\}
were non-empty, we can apply Theorem
5.15
with
\mu=0
,
\chi=1
,
p=\bar{p}=2
and
K=mC
to obtain a contradiction. Therefore,
u
is bounded from above and from Theorem
5.4
we deduce
f(u^{*})\leq 0
. Analogously, considering
-u
we infer
f(u_{*})\geq 0
, and thus (
5.78
) follows by changing variables. Note that, because of the first in (
5.76
), (
5.78
) implies the boundedness of
v
. If
h
is strictly convex, then by (
5.76
) it has a unique stationary point (a minimum)
s_{1}
, and (
5.78
) gives
v^{*}\leq s_{1}\leq v_{*}
, that is,
v\equiv s_{1}
. We remark that each slice
\{s=s_{2}\}
is totally umbilical in
\bar{M}
, with second fundamental form
h^{\prime}(s_{2})/h(s_{2})\langle\,,\,\rangle
in the direction
-\partial_{s}
.
∎

Theorem 5.18
.

Let
\bar{M}=\mathbb{R}\times_{h}M
be as above, and suppose that
h
satisfies:

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}h^{\prime}>0\quad\text{on }\,\mathbb{R},\\[8.5359pt]
h^{\prime}(s)\geq C\qquad\text{for }\,s\geq s_{0};\\[8.5359pt]
h^{-1}\in L^{1}(+\infty),\end{array}\right.

(5.80)

for some constants
C,s_{0}>0
. If
M
is complete and the volume growth of geodesic balls in
M
satisfies

\liminf_{r\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\log\mathrm{vol}(B_{r})}{r^{2}}<\infty,

(5.81)

Then there are no entire, geodesic minimal graphs over
M
.

Proof.

The proof is analogous. By the third in (
5.80
),
u:M\rightarrow I
is bounded from above, hence applying Theorems
5.15
and
5.4
we get
h^{\prime}(v^{*})\leq 0
, contradicting the first in (
5.80
).
∎

Proof of Theorem
1.22
.

It is immediate from Theorems
5.17
and
5.18
, respectively for cases
(i)
and
(ii)
. Note that, in
(i)
, the strict convexity of
h
and
h^{-1}\in L^{1}(-\infty)\cap L^{1}(+\infty)
imply that
sh^{\prime}(s)\geq Cs
for
|s|
large enough.
∎

Remark 5.19
.

As a direct corollary we deduce Do Carmo-Lawson’s result
[
43
]
in the case
H=0
, Theorem
1.3
in the Introduction: there are no geodesic, entire minimal graphs over horospheres, and the only geodesic, entire minimal graph over a totally geodesic hypersphere
M
is
M
itself. To see the first claim, apply Theorem
5.18
to the warped product
\mathbb{H}^{m+1}=\mathbb{R}\times_{e^{s}}\mathbb{R}^{m}
, and note that
h(s)=e^{s}
satisfies all the requirements in (
5.80
). For the second claim, apply Theorem
5.17
to
\mathbb{H}^{m+1}=\mathbb{R}\times_{\cosh s}\mathbb{H}^{m}
.

The case of non-constant mean curvature will be investigated in Section
6
. Now, we focus on MCF solitons, starting with product ambient manifolds. Our first result is for self-translators, Theorem
1.23
in the Introduction:

Proof of Theorem
1.23
.

In the product case,
h(s)=1
and thus
t=s
,
\lambda(t)=1
,
u(x)=t(v(x))=v(x)
. By (
1.15
), a soliton for the field
\partial_{s}
satisfies the equation

\mathrm{div}\left(\frac{\nabla u}{\sqrt{1+|\nabla u|^{2}}}\right)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{1+|\nabla u|^{2}}}\qquad\text{on }\,M.

We apply the first part of Theorem
5.4
with the choices
f(t)\equiv 1
,
b\equiv 1
,
\chi=1
,
\mu=0
,
p=\bar{p}=2
: since the first two in (
5.9
) correspond to (
1.48
), we infer from Theorem
5.4
that
u
is bounded from above and
f(u^{*})\leq 0
, contradiction.
∎

We next examine more closely the case of self-translators in Euclidean space, in particular the case when the translation vector field
Y
differs from the vertical field
\partial_{s}
.

Theorem 5.20
.

Let
(\mathbb{R}^{m+1},(\,,\,))=\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{m}
with coordinates
(s,x)
, and let
\Sigma=\{(v(x),x):x\in\mathbb{R}^{m}\}
be an entire graph. Assume that

\limsup_{r(x)\rightarrow\infty}\frac{|v(x)|}{r(x)}=\hat{v}<\infty

(5.82)

Then,
\Sigma
cannot be a self-translator with respect to any vector
Y
whose angle
\vartheta\in(0,\pi/2)
with the horizontal hyperplane
\mathbb{R}^{m}
satisfies

\tan\vartheta>\hat{v}.

(5.83)

In particular, if
\hat{v}=0
,
\Sigma
cannot be a self-translator with respect to a vector
Y
which is not tangent to the horizontal
\mathbb{R}^{m}
.

Proof.

If we reflect
\Sigma
with respect to the horizontal hyperplane, the reflected graph is a self-translator with respect to the reflection of
Y
. Therefore, without loss of generality we can assume that
(Y,\partial_{t})>0
, and that
Y
has unit norm, by time rescaling. Moreover,
Y\neq\partial_{t}
since
\vartheta<\pi/2
. Therefore, up to a rotation of coordinates on
\mathbb{R}^{m}
,
Y=\cos\vartheta e_{1}+\sin\vartheta\partial_{t}
, where
e_{1}
is the gradient of the first coordinate function
x_{1}
and
\sin\vartheta>0
. In view of (
1.10
) and (
1.11
), and since
h=1
,
t=s
, the soliton equation
mH=(Y,\nu)
satisfied by
u(x)=t(v(x))=v(x)
reads

\mathrm{div}\left(\frac{\nabla u}{\sqrt{1+|\nabla u|^{2}}}\right)=mH=\frac{1}{\sqrt{1+|\nabla u|^{2}}}\left[\sin\vartheta-\cos\vartheta\langle\nabla u,e_{1}\rangle\right],

Rearranging,

\mathrm{div}\left(\frac{\nabla u}{\sqrt{1+|\nabla u|^{2}}}\right)+\left\langle\frac{\nabla u}{\sqrt{1+|\nabla u|^{2}}},\nabla(x_{1}\cos\vartheta)\right\rangle=\frac{\sin\vartheta}{\sqrt{1+|\nabla u|^{2}}},

that we rewrite as

e^{-x_{1}\cos\vartheta}\mathrm{div}\left(e^{x_{1}\cos\vartheta}\frac{\nabla u}{\sqrt{1+|\nabla u|^{2}}}\right)=\frac{\sin\vartheta}{\sqrt{1+|\nabla u|^{2}}}.

(5.84)

The operator in the left-hand side is in divergence form if we consider the weighted volume measure
e^{x_{1}\cos\vartheta}\mathrm{d}x
, with
\mathrm{d}x
the Euclidean volume. For these weighted operators, the proof of Theorems
5.15
and
5.4
follow verbatim by replacing the Euclidean volume with the weighted volume

\mathrm{vol}_{x_{1}\cos\vartheta}(B_{r})=\int_{B_{r}}e^{x_{1}\cos\vartheta}\mathrm{d}x.

Explicit computation gives

\liminf_{r\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\log\mathrm{vol}_{x_{1}\cos\theta}(B_{r})}{r}=\cos\vartheta<\infty.

(5.85)

Suppose by contradiction that (
5.83
) holds. In particular,
u
is non-constant. We apply Theorem
5.15
to (
5.86
) with the choices

K=\sin\vartheta,\ \ \sigma=1,\ \ \chi=1,\ \ \mu=0,\ \ d_{0}=\cos\vartheta,\ \ p=\bar{p}=2,

to conclude from case
(iii)
in (
5.38
) that
\sin\vartheta\leq(\cos\vartheta)\hat{u}
. Since
u=v
, we eventually contradict (
5.83
).
∎

Remark 5.21
.

The requirement
\vartheta>0
, that is,
(Y,\partial_{t})\neq 0
, is essential in the above theorem because otherwise the slices
\{s=\mathrm{const}\}
are trivial self-translators. Furthermore, condition (
5.83
) is sharp. Indeed, a totally geodesic hyperplane
\Sigma
corresponding to an affine, non-constant solution
v
is a self-translator with respect to each vector
Y\in T\Sigma
, and in this case
\tan\vartheta=\hat{v}
.

Remark 5.22
.

A result related to Theorem
5.20
appears in
[
10
]
, where the authors proved that there exist no nontrivial complete self-translator (i.e. not a hyperplane) whose Gauss image lies in a geodesic ball of
\mathbb{S}^{m}
of radius
<\pi/2
. Their assumption implies that
\Sigma
is a graph with respect to the plane orthogonal to the center of the ball (not necessary the direction of translation), and the graph function
v
has bounded gradient
7
7

7

In fact, they also require that
H
be bounded, but this automatically follows from the self-translator equation
\overrightarrow{H}=Y^{\perp}
, being
Y
constant.
. Our requirements (
5.82
) and (
5.83
) seem to be skew with their one. However, it might be possible that a suitable gradient estimate guarantees that a self-translator satisfying (
5.82
) has automatically bounded gradient. If this is the case, the main result in
[
10
]
would imply that the graph
\Sigma
of
\hat{v}
be a hyperplane, which would prove Theorem
5.20
in view of Remark
5.21
.

Our last application is for entire graphs
\Sigma^{m}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{m+1}
which are self-expanders for the mean curvature flow, that is, they move under MCF along the integral curves of the position vector field

Y(\bar{x})=(\bar{x}^{j}-q^{j})\partial_{j}=\frac{1}{2}\bar{\nabla}|\bar{x}-q|^{2}\qquad\forall\,\bar{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{m+1},

for some fixed origin
q\in\mathbb{R}^{m+1}
.

Theorem 5.23
.

Let
\Sigma=\{(v(x),x):x\in\mathbb{R}^{m}\}
be an entire graph in
(\mathbb{R}^{m+1},(\,,\,))
. If
\Sigma
is a self-expander for the MCF and
v
is bounded, then
\Sigma
is a hyperplane.

Proof.

Without loss of generality, we can assume that the center
q
of the homothetic field
Y
is placed at the origin. We let
(s,x)\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{m}=\mathbb{R}^{m+1}
be coordinates on
\mathbb{R}^{m+1}
. If
\rho(x):\mathbb{R}^{m}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
denotes the distance to the origin in
\mathbb{R}^{m}
, then

Y\big(v(x),x\big)=x^{j}\partial_{j}+v\partial_{s}=\frac{1}{2}\nabla(\rho^{2})+v\partial_{s}.

In view of (
1.10
) and (
1.11
), and since
h=1
,
t=s
, the soliton equation
mH=(Y,\nu)
satisfied by
u(x)=t(v(x))=v(x)
reads

\mathrm{div}\left(\frac{\nabla u}{\sqrt{1+|\nabla u|^{2}}}\right)=mH=\frac{1}{\sqrt{1+|\nabla u|^{2}}}\left[u-\langle\nabla u,\nabla\frac{\rho^{2}}{2}\rangle\right].

Rearranging,

\mathrm{div}\left(\frac{\nabla u}{\sqrt{1+|\nabla u|^{2}}}\right)+\left\langle\frac{\nabla u}{\sqrt{1+|\nabla u|^{2}}},\nabla\frac{\rho^{2}}{2}\right\rangle=\frac{u}{\sqrt{1+|\nabla u|^{2}}},

that we rewrite as

e^{-\rho^{2}/2}\mathrm{div}\left(e^{\rho^{2}/2}\frac{\nabla u}{\sqrt{1+|\nabla u|^{2}}}\right)=\frac{u}{\sqrt{1+|\nabla u|^{2}}}.

(5.86)

Suppose that
v
is non-constant. An explicit computation shows that

\liminf_{r\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\log\mathrm{vol}_{e^{\rho^{2}/2}}(B_{r})}{r^{2}}<\infty.

Since, by assumption,
v
is bounded, we can apply Theorem
5.4
(adapted to weighted volumes) with the choices
\chi=1
,
\mu=0
,
p=\bar{p}=2
,
f(t)=t
to deduce
f(u^{*})\leq 0
, that is,
u\leq 0
. Applying the same theorem to
-u
we infer
u\equiv 0
, that is,
\Sigma
is a hyperplane containing the origin of
Y
.
∎

Remark 5.24
.

It is worth to stress that, differently from
[
46
,
152
,
10
]
, Theorems
1.23
,
5.20
and
5.23

do not use the conformality of the soliton vector field
Y
.
In fact, what is needed to apply our results, possibly with a weighted volume, is just that the field
Y
can be decomposed as

Y(s,x)=h^{2}(s)\nabla^{\Sigma_{s}}g+\beta(s,x)\partial_{s}

where
\Sigma_{s_{0}}=\{s=s_{0}\}
,
g\in C^{1}(\mathbb{R}\times M)
just depends on the second variable, and
\beta\in C(M\times\mathbb{R})
.

5.3
Counterexamples

To show the sharpness of Theorem
5.4
, we consider a model manifold
M_{g}^{m}
with radial sectional curvature

K_{\mathrm{rad}}=-\kappa^{2}\big(1+r^{2}\big)^{\alpha/2}\qquad\text{for }\,r(x)\geq 1,

for some
\kappa>0
and
\alpha\geq-2
. By Propositions 2.1 and 2.11 in
[
113
]
, as
r(x)\rightarrow\infty

\Delta r\geq\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\displaystyle(m-1)\kappa r^{\alpha/2}(1+o(1))&\quad\text{if }\,\alpha>-2;\\[8.5359pt]
\displaystyle\frac{(m-1)\bar{\kappa}}{r}(1+o(1))&\quad\text{if }\,\alpha=-2,\end{array}\right.

(5.87)

where
\bar{\kappa}=(1+\sqrt{1+4\kappa^{2}})/2
, and thus

\log\mathrm{vol}B_{r}\sim\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}r^{1+\frac{\alpha}{2}}&\quad\text{if }\,\alpha>-2;\\[5.69046pt]
\big[(m-1)\bar{\kappa}+1\big]\log r&\quad\text{if }\,\alpha=-2,\end{array}\right.

(5.88)

as
r\rightarrow\infty
. If
\alpha=-2
, letting
\kappa\rightarrow 0
we deduce the classical expressions for
\Delta r
and
\mathrm{vol}(B_{r})
for the Euclidean space. We shortly write the inequalities in (
5.87
) as
\Delta r\geq\zeta r^{\alpha/2}
, where
\zeta
tends to
(m-1)\kappa
(if
\alpha>-2
) or to
(m-1)\bar{\kappa}
(if
\alpha=-2
) as
r(x)\rightarrow\infty
. We are going to find an operator
\Delta_{\varphi}
meeting (
1.3
) and (
5.3
) with the following property: given
\mu\in\mathbb{R}
,
0\leq\chi\leq p-1
and
\sigma>0
, we will construct an unbounded, radial solution
u\in C^{\infty}(M_{g}\backslash\{o\})
, increasing as a function of
r
, solving

\Delta_{\varphi}u\geq K(1+r)^{-\mu}\frac{\varphi(|\nabla u|)}{|\nabla u|^{\chi}}

(5.89)

if
r
is large enough (equivalently, for a high enough upper level set). The solution is
u(x)=r(x)^{\sigma}
whenever one of the following conditions hold:

\begin{array}[]{ll}1)&\quad\chi\sigma>\chi+1-\mu;\\[5.69046pt]
2)&\quad\chi\sigma=\chi+1-\mu,\qquad\text{and}\quad\alpha>-2;\\[5.69046pt]
3)&\quad\chi\sigma=\chi+1-\mu,\qquad\alpha=-2,\qquad\sigma\in(0,1]\qquad\text{and}\\[5.69046pt]
&\displaystyle\lim_{r\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\log\mathrm{vol}(B_{r})}{\log r}>p-\sigma(p-1);\\[11.38092pt]
4)&\quad\chi\sigma<\chi+1-\mu,\qquad\alpha>-2,\qquad\text{and}\\[5.69046pt]
&\displaystyle\lim_{r\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\log\mathrm{vol}(B_{r})}{r^{\chi+1-\mu-\chi\sigma}}>0.\end{array}

(5.90)

Moreover, the solution is
u(x)=r(x)^{\sigma}/\log r(x)
when either

\begin{array}[]{ll}5)&\quad\chi\sigma<\chi+1-\mu,\qquad\chi>0,\qquad\text{and}\\[5.69046pt]
&\displaystyle\lim_{r\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\log\mathrm{vol}(B_{r})}{r^{\chi+1-\mu-\chi\sigma}}=\infty,\qquad\text{or}\\[11.38092pt]
6)&\quad\chi\sigma<\chi+1-\mu,\qquad\chi=0,\qquad\text{and}\\[5.69046pt]
&\displaystyle\lim_{r\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\log\mathrm{vol}(B_{r})}{r^{\chi+1-\mu-\chi\sigma}}\in(0,\infty).\end{array}

(5.91)

In any of
1),\ldots,6)
, observe that the bound
\mu\leq\chi+1
s not needed. Once we establish (
5.89
) on
\Omega_{\gamma}
for large enough
\gamma>0
, we can choose
f\in C(\mathbb{R})
increasing and satisfying
f\equiv 0
on
(0,\gamma)
,
f=K
on
(2\gamma,\infty)
,
0\leq f\leq K
on
\mathbb{R}
. By the pasting Lemma,
\bar{u}=\max\{u,\gamma\}
is a
\mathrm{Lip}_{\mathrm{loc}}
solution of

\Delta_{\varphi}\bar{u}\geq(1+r)^{-\mu}f(\bar{u})\frac{\varphi(|\nabla\bar{u}|)}{|\nabla\bar{u}|^{\chi}}\qquad\text{on }\,M.

(5.92)

(here we used
\chi\leq p-1
, to guarantee that
\varphi(t)/t^{\chi}
does not diverge as
t\rightarrow 0
and hence that the constant
\gamma
solves (
5.92
)). The existence of
\bar{u}
under any of
1),\ldots,6)
above shows the sharpness of the parameter ranges (
5.4
) and (
5.8
), and of the growth conditions (
5.7
) for
u
and (
5.9
) for
\mathrm{vol}(B_{r})
. In particular,

-

in
(2)
, all the assumptions are satisfied but the third in (
5.9
), where the liminf is
\infty
, while in
(3)
, the liminf is finite but bigger than the threshold
p-\sigma(p-1)
for
\sigma\leq 1
;

-

in
(4)
the requirements in the first of (
5.9
) are all met, but
u_{+}=O(r^{\sigma})
instead of
u_{+}=o(r^{\sigma})
.

-

in
(5)
and
(6)
,
u_{+}=o(r^{\sigma})
but the requirements in the first of (
5.9
) barely fail.

To show (
5.89
) first note that, because of (
5.88
), the volume growth conditions in
3)
and
4)
are equivalent, respectively, to

(m-1)\bar{\kappa}+1>p-\sigma(p-1),\qquad\text{and}\qquad\frac{\alpha}{2}+1\geq\chi+1-\mu-\chi\sigma.

(5.93)

Consider

\varphi(t)=\frac{t^{p-1}}{(1+t)^{q-1}},\quad p>1,\ 1\leq q\leq p,

(5.94)

and we search for solutions of the form
u(x)=h\big(r(x)\big)
, for some increasing
0<h\in C^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})
. Then,

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\Delta_{\varphi}u&=&\displaystyle\varphi^{\prime}(h^{\prime})h^{\prime\prime}+\varphi(h^{\prime})\Delta r\geq\varphi^{\prime}(h^{\prime})h^{\prime\prime}+\zeta\varphi(h^{\prime})r^{\alpha/2}\\[11.38092pt]
&=&\displaystyle\frac{(h^{\prime})^{p-2}}{(1+h^{\prime})^{q}}\Big\{\left[(p-1)+(p-q)h^{\prime}\right]h^{\prime\prime}+\zeta r^{\alpha/2}h^{\prime}(1+h^{\prime})\Big\}.\end{array}

(5.95)

Set
h(t)=t^{\sigma}
,
\sigma>0
. Then,

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\Delta_{\varphi}u&\geq&\displaystyle C_{1}\frac{r^{(\sigma-1)(p-1)-1}}{(1+\sigma r^{\sigma-1})^{q}}\cdot\\[11.38092pt]
&&\displaystyle\cdot\Big\{\left[(p-1)+\sigma(p-q)r^{\sigma-1}\right](\sigma-1)+\zeta r^{\alpha/2+1}(1+\sigma r^{\sigma-1})\Big\}\end{array}

(5.96)

for some
C_{1}(p,\sigma)>0
. If
\sigma\geq 1
, getting rid of the first term in brackets and using the definition of
\zeta
we obtain

\Delta_{\varphi}u\geq C_{2}\frac{r^{(\sigma-1)(p-1)-1+\left(\alpha/2+1\right)}}{(1+\sigma r^{\sigma-1})^{q-1}}

(5.97)

On the other hand, if
\sigma\in(0,1]
and
\alpha>-2
, the term in between brackets in (
5.96
) is

(p-1)(\sigma-1)(1+o(1))+\zeta r^{\alpha/2+1}(1+o(1))\geq C_{3}\zeta r^{\alpha/2+1},

(5.98)

for large enough
r
, and the last inequality of (
5.97
) still holds. If
\sigma\in(0,1)
and
\alpha=-2
, the term is

\big[(p-1)(\sigma-1)+\zeta\big](1+o(1)),

while if
\sigma=1
,
\alpha=-2
, the term is simply
2\zeta
. If
r
is large enough, the last expression is bounded from below by a positive constant whenever
\lim_{r\rightarrow\infty}\zeta>-(\sigma-1)(p-1)
, that is, when the first in (
5.93
) is met (i.e. the volume growth in
3)
of (
5.90
)). Summarizing, for each
\sigma>0
(if
\sigma\leq 1
and
\alpha=-2
, under the growth condition in
3)
of (
5.90
)), the function
u
turns out to solve (
5.97
) for suitable constant
C_{2}>0
. Since

(1+r)^{-\mu}\frac{\varphi(|\nabla u|)}{|\nabla u|^{\chi}}\leq C_{4}\frac{r^{-\mu+(\sigma-1)(p-1-\chi)}}{(1+\sigma r^{\sigma-1})^{q-1}},

(
5.89
) holds for
r
large enough provided that

-\mu+(\sigma-1)(p-1-\chi)\leq(\sigma-1)(p-1)-1+\left(\alpha/2+1\right),

that is, simplifying, if
\frac{\alpha}{2}+1\geq\chi+1-\mu-\chi\sigma
. The relation is automatically satisfied both in cases
1)
,
2)
and
3)
of (
5.90
), and in case
4)
it is equivalent to the growth condition. We have thus shown (
5.89
), as required.

To prove
(5)
and
(6)
, we use
h(t)=t^{\sigma}/\log t
in (
5.95
) and we consider for convenience the
p
-Laplacian (
q=0
) to obtain

\displaystyle\displaystyle\Delta_{\varphi}u\geq C_{1}\frac{r^{(\sigma-1)(p-1)-1}}{\log^{p-1}t}\Big\{(p-1)(\sigma-1)\big(1+o(1)\big)+\zeta r^{1+\frac{\alpha}{2}}\big(1+o(1)\big)\Big\},

as
r\rightarrow\infty
, for some constant
C_{1}(\sigma,p)>0
.

In order to meet the volume conditions in
(5),(6)
and
\chi\sigma<\chi+1-\mu
, necessarily
\alpha>-2
and thus

\displaystyle\displaystyle\Delta_{\varphi}u\geq C_{2}\frac{r^{(\sigma-1)(p-1)+\frac{\alpha}{2}}}{\log^{p-1}t}\big(1+o(1)\big)

as
r\rightarrow\infty
. On the other hand, by the definition of
\Delta_{\varphi}
on
\Omega_{\gamma}
we have

(1+r)^{-\mu}\frac{\varphi(|\nabla u|)}{|\nabla u|^{\chi}}\leq C_{3}\frac{r^{-\mu+(\sigma-1)(p-1)-\chi(\sigma-1)}}{\log^{p-1}r}\log^{\chi}r

Combining the last two inequalities, we examine two cases.

-

In case
(5)
,
\chi>0
and
u
solves (
5.89
) whenever
\alpha
is big enough to satisfy

(\sigma-1)(p-1)+\frac{\alpha}{2}>-\mu+(\sigma-1)(p-1)-\chi(\sigma-1)

that is,

1+\frac{\alpha}{2}>\chi+1-\mu-\chi\sigma>0,

(5.99)

that in view of (
5.88
) implies

\lim_{r\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\log\mathrm{vol}(B_{r})}{r^{\chi+1-\mu-\chi\sigma}}=\infty,

as required.

-

In case
(6)
,
\chi=0
and for
u
to solve (
5.89
) it is sufficient the weak inequality

1+\frac{\alpha}{2}\geq\chi+1-\mu-\chi\sigma>0,

and choosing
\alpha
in order to meet the equality sign, we obtain

\lim_{r\to\infty}\frac{\log\mathrm{vol}(B_{r})}{r^{\chi+1-\mu-\chi\sigma}}\in(0,\infty),

as required.

Remark 5.25
.

Example
5.94
satisfies (
5.3
) with
\bar{p}=p-q
. The case

\begin{array}[]{ll}3^{\prime})&\quad\chi\sigma=\chi+1-\mu,\qquad\alpha=-2,\qquad\sigma>1\qquad\text{and}\\[5.69046pt]
&\displaystyle\lim_{r\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\log\mathrm{vol}(B_{r})}{\log r}>\bar{p}-\sigma(\bar{p}-1)\end{array}

is not covered by our counterexamples.

6
Strong maximum principle and Khas’minskii potentials

The aim of this section is to prove Theorem
1.14
in the Introduction. We observe that the argument is based on the existence of what we call a “Khas’minskii potential”, according to the following

Definition 6.1
.

A Khas’minskii potential at
o\in M
is a function
\bar{w}
depending on the parameters
r_{0}
,
r_{1}
,
\eta
,
K
,
\varepsilon>0
satisfying the next requirements:
