\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\Delta_{\varphi}\bar{w}\leq Kb(x)l(|\nabla\bar{w}|)\qquad\text{on }\,M\backslash B_{r_{0}}\\[5.69046pt]
\bar{w}>0\qquad\text{on }\,M\backslash B_{r_{0}},\\[5.69046pt]
\bar{w}\leq\eta\qquad\text{ on }\,B_{r_{1}}\backslash B_{r_{0}},\\[5.69046pt]
\bar{w}(x)\rightarrow+\infty\qquad\text{as }\,r(x)\rightarrow\infty,\\[5.69046pt]
|\nabla\bar{w}|\leq\varepsilon\quad\text{on }\,M\backslash B_{r_{0}}\end{array}\right.

(6.1)

The strategy to prove Theorem
1.14
is as follows: assuming, by contradiction, that
(\mathrm{SMP}_{\infty})
does not hold, the Khas’minskii potential
w
will be compared to a non-constant, bounded solution
u\in C^{1}(M)
of

\Delta_{\varphi}u\geq Kb(x)l(|\nabla u|)

on an appropriate subset of
\Omega_{\eta,\varepsilon}
to reach the conclusion. This approach is very old and we can trace it back, for instance, to Phrágmen-Lindelöff in the realm of classical complex analysis. In more recent times, it has been used by Redheffer
[
130
,
129
]
, and later refined in
[
110
, Sect. 6]
,
[
111
, Thm. 18]
and
[
2
, Ch. 3]
. A similar approach, although with a somehow different point of view, was systematically used by Serrin
[
143
]
and recently by Pucci-Serrin
[
123
, Thm. 8.1.1]
. In all of the quoted results, the Khas’minskii potential (or variants thereof) is either a data of the problem or it is constructed “ad-hoc”. These methods seem difficult to be applied when a nontrivial gradient term
l
is present. Thus, we provide the existence of an appropriate potential via the solution of an associated ODE problem, see Lemma
6.2
below, that will be coupled with a condition on the Ricci tensor to transplant the function on the original manifold.

6.1
Ricci curvature and
(\mathrm{SMP}_{\infty})

To investigate the ODE problem mentioned above, we assume the following:

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\displaystyle\varphi\in C(\mathbb{R}^{+}_{0})\cap C^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{+}),\qquad\varphi(0)=0,\qquad\varphi^{\prime}>0\ \text{ on }\,\mathbb{R}^{+},\\[5.69046pt]
\displaystyle f\in C(\mathbb{R}),\qquad f(0)=0,\qquad f>0\ \text{ on }\,\mathbb{R}^{+},\\[5.69046pt]
\displaystyle l\in C(\mathbb{R}^{+}_{0}),\qquad l>0\ \text{ on }\,\mathbb{R}^{+},\\[5.69046pt]
\beta\in C(\mathbb{R}^{+}_{0}),\qquad\beta>0\ \text{ on }\,\mathbb{R}^{+}_{0},\\[2.84544pt]
\end{array}\right.

(6.2)

and the next growth conditions:

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\displaystyle\frac{t\varphi^{\prime}(t)}{l(t)}\in L^{1}(0^{+}),&\\[11.38092pt]
\displaystyle l(t)\geq C_{1}\frac{\varphi(t)}{t^{\chi}}&\quad\text{on }\,(0,1]\ \text{ for some }\,C_{1}>0,\ \chi\geq 0\\[11.38092pt]
\displaystyle\varphi(t)\leq Ct^{p-1}&\quad\text{on }\,[0,1],\ \text{ for some }\,C>0,\ p>1.\end{array}\right.

(6.3)

Fix a “volume” function
v\in C^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})
such that

v>0\ \text{ on }\mathbb{R}^{+},\qquad v^{\prime}\geq 0\qquad\text{on }\,\mathbb{R}^{+}.

(6.4)

We are now ready to prove

Lemma 6.2
.

Assume the validity of (
6.2
), (
6.3
), (
6.4
) and furthermore suppose that

\begin{array}[]{l}\displaystyle\beta(r)\geq C(1+r)^{-\mu}\qquad\text{on $\,\mathbb{R}^{+}_{0}$, for some $\,\mu\leq\chi+1$,}\\[8.5359pt]
\displaystyle\limsup_{r\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{v(r)}\int_{r_{0}}^{r}\frac{v(s)}{(1+s)^{\mu}}\mathrm{d}s<\infty,\end{array}

(6.5)

for some (hence any)
r_{0}>0
, and that either

\begin{array}[]{l}\displaystyle\mu<\chi+1\quad\text{ and }\quad\displaystyle\liminf_{t\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\log\int_{r_{0}}^{t}v}{t^{\chi+1-\mu}}<\infty\quad(=0\ \text{ if }\,\chi=0),\ \ \text{ or}\\[11.38092pt]
\displaystyle\mu=\chi+1\quad\text{ and }\quad\displaystyle\liminf_{t\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\log\int_{t_{0}}^{t}v}{\log t}<\infty\quad(\leq p\ \text{ if }\,\chi=0).\\[11.38092pt]
\end{array}

(6.6)

Then, for each
0<r_{0}<r_{1}
,
\eta>0
and
\varepsilon>0
there exists a function
w\in C^{1}([r_{0},\infty))
satisfying:

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\big[v\varphi(w^{\prime})\big]^{\prime}\leq v\beta f(w)l(|w^{\prime}|)\qquad\text{on }\,[r_{0},\infty)\\[5.69046pt]
w>0,\ w^{\prime}>0\qquad\text{on }\,[r_{0},\infty),\\[5.69046pt]
w\leq\eta\qquad\text{ on }\,[r_{0},r_{1}],\\[5.69046pt]
w(t)\rightarrow\infty\qquad\text{as }\,t\rightarrow\infty,\\[5.69046pt]
|w^{\prime}|\leq\varepsilon\quad\text{on }\,[r_{0},\infty).\end{array}\right.

(6.7)

Proof.

Clearly, it is enough to prove the result for
\beta(r)=C(1+r)^{-\mu}
and for
\eta
and
\varepsilon
small enough. First of all, we modify
f,l,\varphi
and choose a suitable
\eta
. These adjustments will be essential to prove the
L^{\infty}
-gradient bound for
w
. Fix
\xi\in(0,1]
and choose
\bar{l}(t)
satisfying

\begin{array}[]{l}\displaystyle\bar{l}\in C(\mathbb{R}^{+}_{0}),\qquad\bar{l}>0\ \text{ on }\,\mathbb{R}^{+},\qquad\bar{l}\leq 2\|l\|_{L^{\infty}([0,\xi])}\ \text{ on }\,\mathbb{R}^{+},\\[8.5359pt]
\bar{l}(t)=l(t)\ \text{ if }\,t\in[0,\xi),\qquad\bar{l}(t)t^{\chi}\geq\bar{C}_{2}\ \text{ on }\,[\xi,\infty),\end{array}

(6.8)

for some constant
\bar{C}_{2}
(here, we use
\chi\geq 0
). Regarding
\varphi
, we choose
\bar{\varphi}\in C^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{+}_{0})
such that

\bar{\varphi}^{\prime}>0\ \text{ on }\mathbb{R}^{+},\quad\bar{\varphi}=\varphi\ \text{ on }\,(0,\xi),\quad\bar{\varphi}\leq\bar{C}_{1}t^{\chi}\bar{l}(t)\ \text{ on }\,[\xi,\infty)

note that this is possible if
\bar{C}_{1}
is sufficiently large, by the last of (
6.8
). By construction, since
\varphi(0)=0
,

\bar{\varphi}(t)\leq Ct^{p-1}\ \text{ on }\,[0,1],\quad\bar{\varphi}(t)\leq C_{3}t^{\chi}\ \text{ on }\,[\xi,\infty),\quad\bar{l}(t)\geq C_{4}\frac{\bar{\varphi}(t)}{t^{\chi}}\qquad\text{on }\,\mathbb{R}^{+},

(6.9)

for some constants
C_{3},C_{4}>0
. For
\eta>0
and
\xi>0
, we introduce the notation

\begin{array}[]{ll}\beta_{0}=\min_{[r_{0},r_{1}]}\beta,&\quad\beta_{1}=\max_{[r_{0},r_{1}]}\beta;\\[5.69046pt]
v_{0}=\min_{[r_{0},r_{1}]}v,&\quad v_{1}=\max_{[r_{0},r_{1}]}v;\\[5.69046pt]
f_{2\eta}=\max_{[0,2\eta]}f,&\quad l_{\xi}=\max_{[0,\xi]}\bar{l}.\end{array}

(6.10)

Given
\sigma\in(0,\xi)
to be specified later, we choose
\eta_{\sigma}\in[0,\sigma)
small enough in order to satisfy

\frac{v_{1}}{v_{0}}\left[(r_{1}-r_{0})\beta_{1}f_{2\eta_{\sigma}}l_{\xi}+\bar{\varphi}\left(\frac{\eta_{\sigma}}{r_{1}-r_{0}}\right)\right]<\bar{\varphi}(\sigma).

(6.11)

This is possible because
\bar{\varphi}(0)=0
and
f_{2\eta_{\sigma}}\rightarrow 0
as
\eta_{\sigma}\rightarrow 0
(since
f(0)=0
). We next choose
f_{\sigma}\in C(\mathbb{R}_{0}^{+})
satisfying

\begin{array}[]{ll}0\leq f_{\sigma}(t)\leq\min\big\{f(t),1\big\},&\qquad f_{\sigma}(t)=0\ \ \text{ if }\,t\leq\eta_{\sigma},\\[5.69046pt]
f_{\sigma}>0\quad\text{if }\,t>\eta_{\sigma},&\qquad f_{\sigma}(\eta_{\sigma}+t)\leq K^{\prime}(t)\ \text{ for }\,t\in[0,\xi],\end{array}

(6.12)

where
K(t)
is the function defined in (
1.19
). The last condition can be satisfied because of the positivity of
\varphi^{\prime}
and
l
on
\mathbb{R}^{+}
, hence of
K^{\prime}
. We consider the Dirichlet problem

\begin{cases}\big([v\bar{\varphi}(w_{\sigma}^{\prime})\big)]^{\prime}=\sigma v\beta f_{\sigma}(w_{\sigma})\bar{l}(|w_{\sigma}^{\prime}|)\quad\text{on }\,[r_{0},r_{1}],\\[2.84544pt]
w_{\sigma}(r_{0})=\eta_{\sigma},\qquad w_{\sigma}(r_{1})=2\eta_{\sigma},\\[2.84544pt]
\eta_{\sigma}\leq w_{\sigma}\leq 2\eta_{\sigma},\qquad w_{\sigma}^{\prime}>0\ \text{ on }\,[r_{0},r_{1}],\end{cases}

(6.13)

We claim that a solution
w_{\sigma}\in C^{1}([r_{0},r_{1}])
exists if
\sigma>0
is small enough. Indeed, one can apply Theorem
3.1
with the following choices:

\begin{array}[]{l}t=r-r_{0},\qquad T=r_{1}-r_{0},\qquad\quad\wp(t)=v(t+r_{0}),\\[5.69046pt]
w(t)=w_{\sigma}(r_{0}+t)-\eta_{\sigma},\qquad a(t)=\beta(r_{0}+t)\end{array}

and
f(t),\varphi(t),l(t)
in Theorem
3.1
replaced, respectively, by
\sigma f_{\sigma}(\eta_{\sigma}+t),\bar{\varphi}(t)
and
\bar{l}(t)
(note that
f(0)=0
). It is easy to see that (
6.11
) implies (
3.8
) for each
\sigma\in(0,\xi]
, hence Theorem
3.1
can be applied to guarantee the existence of a solution
w_{\sigma}
of (
6.13
) together with the bound
|w_{\sigma}^{\prime}|\leq\sigma
. We are left to prove that
w_{\sigma}^{\prime}>0
on
[r_{0},r_{1}]
, provided that
\sigma
is small enough. Because of Lemma
3.2
, it is enough to show
w_{\sigma}^{\prime}(r_{0})>0
and to this aim we follow the argument in Proposition
3.7
, see also Remark
3.8
. Indeed, suppose by contradiction that
w_{\sigma}^{\prime}(r_{0})=0
. By Lemma
3.2
, there exists
\bar{r}_{\sigma}\in[r_{0},r_{1})
such that
w_{\sigma}=\eta_{\sigma}
for
r\leq\bar{r}_{\sigma}
,
w_{\sigma}^{\prime}(\bar{r}_{\sigma})=0
and
w_{\sigma}^{\prime}>0
on
(\bar{r}_{\sigma},r_{1}]
. Expanding (
6.13
) and using
v^{\prime}\geq 0
we deduce

\frac{w_{\sigma}^{\prime}\bar{\varphi}^{\prime}(w_{\sigma}^{\prime})}{\bar{l}(w_{\sigma}^{\prime})}w_{\sigma}^{\prime\prime}\leq\sigma\beta_{1}f_{\sigma}(w_{\sigma})w_{\sigma}^{\prime},

and integrating on
(\bar{r}_{\sigma},r)
we get

\bar{K}(w_{\sigma}^{\prime})\leq\sigma\beta_{1}F_{\sigma}(w_{\sigma}),

(6.14)

where

\bar{K}(t)=\int_{0}^{t}\frac{s\bar{\varphi}^{\prime}(s)}{\bar{l}(s)}\mathrm{d}s,\qquad F_{\sigma}(t)=\int_{\eta_{\sigma}}^{t}f_{\sigma}(s)\mathrm{d}s.

Because of (
6.12
), for
t\in[0,\xi+\eta_{\sigma}]

F_{\sigma}(t)\leq\int_{\eta_{\sigma}}^{t}K^{\prime}(s-\eta_{\sigma})\mathrm{d}s=K(t-\eta_{\sigma})\equiv\bar{K}(t-\eta_{\sigma}),

where in the last inequality we have used
\bar{\varphi}=\varphi
and
\bar{l}=l
on
[0,\xi]
. Choosing
\sigma\leq\beta_{1}^{-1}
from (
6.14
) we deduce the inequality
\bar{K}(w_{\sigma}^{\prime})\leq\bar{K}(w_{\sigma}-\eta_{\sigma})
, and therefore
w_{\sigma}^{\prime}\leq w_{\sigma}-\eta_{\sigma}
. By Gronwall’s inequality and
w_{\sigma}^{\prime}(\bar{r}_{\sigma})=0
we obtain
w_{\sigma}\equiv\eta_{\sigma}
on
[\bar{r}_{\sigma},r_{1}]
, contradicting
w_{\sigma}(r_{1})=2\eta_{\sigma}
.

We now let
[r_{0},R)
,
R>r_{1}
be the maximal interval where
w_{\sigma}
is defined. Integrating (
6.13
), and using
|w_{\sigma}^{\prime}|\leq\sigma
on
[r_{0},r_{1}]
and
\|f_{\sigma}\|_{\infty}\leq 1
, for
r\in[r_{0},R)
we get

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\displaystyle\bar{\varphi}\big(w_{\sigma}^{\prime}(r)\big)&=&\displaystyle\frac{1}{v(r)}\left[\bar{\varphi}\big(w_{\sigma}^{\prime}(r_{0})\big)v(r_{0})+\sigma\int_{r_{0}}^{r}v(s)\beta(s)f_{\sigma}(w_{\sigma})\bar{l}(w_{\sigma}^{\prime})\mathrm{d}s\right]\\[11.38092pt]
&\leq&\displaystyle\frac{1}{v(r)}\left[\bar{\varphi}(\sigma)v(r_{0})+\sigma\|f_{\sigma}\|_{\infty}\|\bar{l}\|_{\infty}\int_{r_{0}}^{r}v(s)\beta(s)\mathrm{d}s\right]\\[11.38092pt]
&\leq&\displaystyle C(\bar{\varphi}(\sigma)+\sigma),\end{array}

(6.15)

where
C>0
is a constant depending on
r_{0}
but independent of
R,\sigma
, and where the last inequality follows from
v^{\prime}\geq 0
, the second in (
6.5
) and
\beta(r)=(1+r)^{-\mu}
. In particular, if
\sigma
is small enough then
w_{\sigma}
has bounded gradient. Since
w_{\sigma}
is also increasing, necessarily
R=\infty
, otherwise one could extend the solution beyond
R
. Up to a further reduction of
\sigma
, by (
6.15
) we can guarantee
|w_{\sigma}^{\prime}|\leq\varepsilon
on
[r_{0},\infty)
,
\varepsilon
being the parameter in the statement of the Lemma that we can assume to belong on
(0,\xi)
. On the other hand, from the first line in (
6.15
) and the positivity of
w_{\sigma}^{\prime}
on
[r_{0},r_{1}]
we deduce
w_{\sigma}^{\prime}>0
on all of
[r_{0},\infty)
. It is clear that, for each
\sigma<\xi
, by construction
w_{\sigma}
solves

\big[v\varphi(w_{\sigma}^{\prime})\big]^{\prime}=\big[v\bar{\varphi}(w_{\sigma}^{\prime})\big]^{\prime}=\sigma v\beta f_{\sigma}(w_{\sigma})\bar{l}(w_{\sigma}^{\prime})\leq v\beta f(w_{\sigma})l(w_{\sigma}^{\prime}),

as required. We are left to prove that
w_{\sigma}(r)\rightarrow\infty
as
r\rightarrow\infty
. Suppose, by contradiction, that
w_{\sigma}^{*}=\sup_{[r_{0},\infty)}w_{\sigma}<\infty
, and consider the
C^{2}
-model manifold
M_{g}
with metric given, in polar coordinates, by

\mathrm{d}s_{g}^{2}=\mathrm{d}r^{2}+g(r)^{2}(\,,\,)_{1},\qquad g(r)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}r&\quad\text{for }\,r\in(0,r_{0}/2)\\[5.69046pt]
v(r)^{\frac{1}{m-1}}&\quad\text{for }\,r\geq r_{0}.\end{array}\right.

By construction, the radial function
w_{\sigma}(r)
on
M_{g}
is a solution of

\Delta_{\bar{\varphi}}w_{\sigma}=\sigma\beta(r)f_{\sigma}(w_{\sigma})\bar{l}(|\nabla w_{\sigma}|)\qquad\text{on }\,M_{g}\backslash\overline{B}_{r_{0}},

and from
w_{\sigma}^{\prime}>0
we get
w_{\sigma}^{*}>\eta_{\sigma}
. Consider the Lipschitz extension of
w_{\sigma}
obtained by setting
w_{\sigma}=\eta_{\sigma}
on
B_{r_{0}}
. An analogous reasoning as in Lemma
4.6
shows that,
f_{\sigma}(\eta_{\sigma})=0
and
w_{\sigma}^{\prime}>0
guarantee that the extended function solves
\Delta_{\bar{\varphi}}w_{\sigma}\geq\sigma\beta(r)f_{\sigma}(w_{\sigma})\bar{l}(|\nabla w_{\sigma}|)
on
M_{g}
. Combining properties (
6.5
) (for
b
) and (
6.9
) (for
\bar{\varphi},\bar{l}
) with the volume growth conditions (
6.6
), and fixing any
\bar{p}>\chi+1
, we are in the position to apply case
(i)
of Theorem
5.4
to deduce that necessarily
f_{\sigma}(w_{\sigma}^{*})\leq 0
, hence
w_{\sigma}\leq\eta_{\sigma}
because of (
6.12
). This contradicts the previously established inequality
w_{\sigma}^{*}>\eta_{\sigma}
, and concludes the proof.
∎

Remark 6.3
.

We stress that no growth condition on
\varphi
at infinity is required. Indeed, we applied the weak maximum principle (Theorem
5.4
) to the modification
\bar{\varphi}
, but a-posteriori just the value of
\varphi
for sufficiently small
t
is needed to produce the solution.

Remark 6.4
.

The simultaneous validity of the second in (
6.5
) and of (
6.6
) requires a delicate balancing between
\mu
and
v(r)
, since (
6.5
) is easier to satisfy for
\mu
large while (
6.6
) forces an upper bound on
\mu
. For various examples of
v(r)
with geometric interest, in particular for those appearing in the next theorem, there is a non-empty interval of
\mu
for which both the conditions are met.

We are now ready to prove Theorem
1.14
. We report here the statement to facilitate the reading.

Theorem 6.5
.

Let
M^{m}
be a complete Riemannian manifold of dimension
m\geq 2
such that, for some origin
o\in M
, the distance function
r(x)
from
o
satisfies

\mathrm{Ric}(\nabla r,\nabla r)\geq-(m-1)\kappa^{2}\big(1+r^{2}\big)^{\alpha/2},

(6.16)

for some
\kappa\geq 0
and
\alpha\geq-2
. Let
l,\varphi
satisfy (
6.2
) and (
6.3
), for some
\chi\geq 0
and
p>1
. Consider
0<b\in C(M)
such that

b(x)\geq C\big(1+r(x)\big)^{-\mu}\qquad\text{on }\,M,

for some constants
C>0
,
\mu\in\mathbb{R}
. Assume

\mu\leq\chi-\frac{\alpha}{2}\quad\text{and either}\quad\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\alpha\geq-2\quad\text{and}\quad\chi>0,\quad\text{or}\\[5.69046pt]
\alpha=-2,\quad\chi=0\quad\text{and}\quad\bar{\kappa}\leq\frac{p-1}{m-1},\end{array}\right.

(6.17)

with
\bar{\kappa}=\frac{1}{2}\big(1+\sqrt{1+4\kappa^{2}}\big)
.
Then, the operator
(bl)^{-1}\Delta_{\varphi}
satisfies
(\mathrm{SMP}_{\infty})
.

Proof.

Suppose, by contradiction, that there exists a non-constant
u\in C^{1}(M)
with
u^{*}=\sup_{M}u<\infty
, and
\eta,\varepsilon>0
such that

\Delta_{\varphi}u\geq 2Kb(x)l(|\nabla u|)\qquad\text{on }\,\Omega_{\eta,\varepsilon}=\big\{x:u(x)>\eta,\ |\nabla u(x)|<2\varepsilon\big\},

for some constant
K>0
. In particular,
\Delta_{\varphi}u\geq 0
in
\Omega_{\eta,\varepsilon}
, and thus, since maximal points of
u
belong to
\Omega_{\eta,\varepsilon}
, applying the finite maximum principle (Theorem
4.8
) to
u^{*}-u
with
f\equiv 0
we deduce that
u^{*}
is never attained. Consequently, since
u\in C^{1}(M)
we infer that
\Omega_{\eta,\varepsilon}
is unbounded. In what follows, balls are always considered to be centered at
o
. Fix
r_{0}>0
and choose
\gamma\in(0,\eta/2)
in such a way that

u(x)<u^{*}-4\gamma\qquad\text{for each }\,x\in B_{r_{0}}.

Next, we choose
\bar{x}\in\Omega_{\eta,\varepsilon}
such that
u(\bar{x})>u^{*}-\gamma
, and a large ball
B_{r_{1}}\Subset M
containing
\overline{B}_{r_{0}}\cup\{\bar{x}\}
. By (
6.16
) and
[
113
, Prop. 2.1]
,

\Delta r\leq(m-1)\frac{g^{\prime}(r)}{g(r)}\qquad\text{weakly on $M$,}

(6.18)

for some
g(r)\in C^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+}_{0})
increasing and satisfying (
9.17
), that is,

g(r)\asymp\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\exp\left\{\frac{2\kappa}{2+\alpha}(1+r)^{1+\frac{\alpha}{2}}\right\}&\qquad\text{if }\,\alpha\geq 0\\[11.38092pt]
r^{-\frac{\alpha}{4}}\exp\left\{\frac{2\kappa}{2+\alpha}r^{1+\frac{\alpha}{2}}\right\}&\qquad\text{if }\,\alpha\in(-2,0)\\[11.38092pt]
r^{\bar{\kappa}},\quad\bar{\kappa}=\frac{1+\sqrt{1+4\kappa^{2}}}{2}&\qquad\text{if }\,\alpha=-2\end{array}\right.

(6.19)

as
r\rightarrow\infty
. Define
v_{g}(r)=\mathrm{vol}(\mathbb{S}^{m-1})g(r)^{m-1}
, and note that

\log\int_{r_{0}}^{r}v_{g}\sim\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\frac{2\kappa(m-1)}{2+\alpha}r^{1+\frac{\alpha}{2}}&\quad\text{if }\,\alpha>-2;\\[5.69046pt]
\big[(m-1)\bar{\kappa}+1\big]\log r&\quad\text{if }\,\alpha=-2\end{array}\right.

(6.20)

as
r\rightarrow\infty
. For each
\alpha\geq-2
, set

\beta(r)=C(1+r)^{-\bar{\mu}},\qquad\text{with}\qquad\bar{\mu}=\chi-\frac{\alpha}{2}.

Because of (
6.17
),
\mu\leq\bar{\mu}
and therefore

b(x)\geq\beta\big(r(x)\big)

(6.21)

Furthermore, by (
6.19
) and the fact that
\bar{\mu}\geq-\alpha/2
we get, for each
r_{0}>0
,

\limsup_{r\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{v_{g}(r)}\int_{r_{0}}^{r}\frac{v_{g}(s)}{(1+s)^{\bar{\mu}}}\mathrm{d}s<\infty

(6.22)

while, in view of (
6.20
) we obtain

\begin{array}[]{ll}\text{if $\alpha>-2$, $\quad$ then $\bar{\mu}<\chi+1$ and }&\quad\displaystyle\lim_{r\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\log\int_{r_{0}}^{r}v}{r^{\chi+1-\bar{\mu}}}<\infty;\\[14.22636pt]
\text{if $\alpha=-2$, $\quad$ then $\bar{\mu}=\chi+1$ and }&\quad\displaystyle\lim_{r\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\log\int_{r_{0}}^{r}v}{\log r}<\infty\quad\text{($\leq p$ if $\chi=0$).}\end{array}

We are in the position to apply Lemma
6.2
: for
\eta=\gamma_{\varepsilon}\leq\gamma
small enough, there exists
w\in C^{1}([r_{0},\infty))
satisfying:

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\big[v_{g}\varphi(w^{\prime})\big]^{\prime}\leq v_{g}K\beta l(|w^{\prime}|)\qquad\text{on }\,[r_{0},\infty)\\[5.69046pt]
w>0,\ w^{\prime}>0\qquad\text{on }\,[r_{0},\infty),\\[5.69046pt]
w\leq\gamma_{\varepsilon}\qquad\text{ on }\,[r_{0},r_{1}],\\[5.69046pt]
w(r)\rightarrow\infty\qquad\text{as }\,r\rightarrow\infty,\\[5.69046pt]
|w^{\prime}|\leq\varepsilon\quad\text{on }\,[r_{0},\infty)\end{array}\right.

(6.23)

We define the radial function
\bar{w}(x)=w(r(x))
, and we note that, because of (
6.18
),
w^{\prime}>0
and (
6.21
),
\bar{w}
solves

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\Delta_{\varphi}\bar{w}\leq K\beta(r)l(w^{\prime}(r))\leq Kb(x)l(w^{\prime}(r))\qquad\text{on }\,M\backslash B_{r_{0}}\\[5.69046pt]
\bar{w}>0\qquad\text{on }\,M\backslash B_{r_{0}},\\[5.69046pt]
\bar{w}\leq\gamma_{\varepsilon}\qquad\text{ on }\,B_{r_{1}}\backslash B_{r_{0}},\\[5.69046pt]
\bar{w}(x)\rightarrow+\infty\qquad\text{as }\,r(x)\rightarrow\infty,\\[5.69046pt]
|\nabla\bar{w}|\leq\varepsilon\quad\text{on }\,M\backslash B_{r_{0}}\end{array}\right.

(6.24)

Let
\Gamma
be the set of maxima of
u-\bar{w}
, which is non-empty and compact since
\bar{w}
has compact sublevel sets. For each
x\in\Gamma

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\displaystyle u(x)-\bar{w}(x)&\geq&\displaystyle u(\bar{x})-\bar{w}(\bar{x})>u^{*}-\gamma-\gamma_{\varepsilon}\geq u^{*}-2\gamma\\[5.69046pt]
&>&\displaystyle\max_{B_{r_{0}}}u\geq\max_{B_{r_{0}}}(u-\bar{w}),\end{array}

(6.25)

hence
\Gamma\subset M\backslash\overline{B}_{r_{0}}
. From the first line in (
6.25
), we get

u(x)\geq u^{*}-2\gamma+\bar{w}(x)\geq u^{*}-2\gamma>u^{*}-\eta,

and thus
\Gamma\subset\big(M\backslash\overline{B}_{r_{0}}\big)\cap\{u>u^{*}-\eta\}
. Furthermore, for each
x\in\Gamma\backslash\mathrm{cut}(o)
it holds

|\nabla u(x)|=|\nabla\bar{w}(x)|=w^{\prime}\big(r(x)\big)\leq\varepsilon.

We claim that the same relation holds even for
x\in\Gamma\cap\mathrm{cut}(o)
. Let
\sigma:[0,r(x)]\rightarrow M
be a unit speed minimizing geodesic from
o
to
x
, and for
0<\tau<<1
define
r_{\tau}(\cdot)=\tau+\mathrm{dist}(.,\sigma(\tau))
. Then,
r_{\tau}\geq r
, with equality at
x
, and furthermore
r_{\tau}
is smooth around
x
. Setting
\bar{w}_{\tau}(x)=w(r_{\tau}(x))
,
w^{\prime}>0
implies that
\bar{w}_{\tau}\geq\bar{w}
, with equality at
x
. Hence
x
is a maximum for
u-\bar{w}_{\tau}
, which gives

|\nabla u(x)|=|\nabla w_{\tau}(x)|=w^{\prime}\big(r_{\tau}(x)\big)=w^{\prime}\big(r(x)\big)\leq\varepsilon.

We have therefore shown that
\Gamma\Subset\Omega_{\eta,\varepsilon}
. Equality
|\nabla u|=w^{\prime}(r)
, combined with
w\in C^{1}
and
w^{\prime}>0
on
[r_{0},\infty)
, guarantee the existence of
\delta>0
such that
\delta\leq|\nabla u|\leq\varepsilon
on
\Gamma
. Using the continuity and positivity of
l
, we can fix a small open neighbourhood
V\Subset\Omega_{\eta,\varepsilon}
of
\Gamma
of the form
\{u-\bar{w}>c\}
,
c
close enough to
\max\{u-\bar{w}\}
, such that
l(|\nabla u|)\geq 2^{-1}l(w^{\prime}(r))
on
V
. Consequently,

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\displaystyle\Delta_{\varphi}u\geq 2Kb(x)l(|\nabla u|)\geq Kb(x)l\big(w^{\prime}(r(x)\big)\geq\Delta_{\varphi}\bar{w}=\Delta_{\varphi}(\bar{w}+c)\qquad\text{on }\,V\\[8.5359pt]
u=\bar{w}+c\qquad\text{on }\,\partial V.\end{array}\right.

By comparison,
u\leq\bar{w}+c
on
V
, contradicting the very definition of
V
.
∎

Remark 6.6
.

Unfortunately, Lemma
6.2
cannot be applied as above to prove Theorem
6.5
also in the range
\alpha>-2
and
\chi=0
. We recall that, for the
p
-Laplace operator, this corresponds to gradient terms with borderline growth
l(t)\asymp t^{p-1}
. In fact, for (
6.22
) to hold it is necessary that
\bar{\mu}\geq-\alpha/2
, but on the other hand, because of (
6.20
),

\begin{array}[]{l}\displaystyle\text{if $\bar{\mu}<\chi+1=1$}\quad\text{then}\quad\liminf_{r\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\log\int_{r_{0}}^{r}v}{r^{\chi+1-\bar{\mu}}}=0\quad\text{iff}\quad\bar{\mu}<\chi-\frac{\alpha}{2}=-\frac{\alpha}{2}\\[14.22636pt]
\displaystyle\text{if $\bar{\mu}=\chi+1=1$}\quad\text{then}\quad\liminf_{r\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\log\int_{r_{0}}^{r}v}{\log r}\leq p\quad\text{does not hold for any $\alpha>-2$}.\end{array}

Therefore, no choice of
\bar{\mu}
is admissible for Lemma
6.2
.

To better appreciate Theorem
6.5
, we express it for the mean curvature operator, both in the Euclidean space
\mathbb{R}^{m}
and in the hyperbolic space
\mathbb{H}^{m}
.

Corollary 6.7
.

Let
l\in C(\mathbb{R}_{0}^{+})
satisfy

l(t)\geq C_{1}\frac{t^{1-\chi}}{\sqrt{1+t^{2}}}\qquad\text{on }\,[0,1],

(6.26)

for some
\chi\in[0,1]
. Then,
(\mathrm{SMP}_{\infty})
holds for the operator

\big(1+r(x)\big)^{\mu}l\big(|\nabla u|\big)^{-1}\mathrm{div}\left(\frac{\nabla u}{\sqrt{1+|\nabla u|^{2}}}\right),

(i)

in
\mathbb{R}^{m}
, provided that
\mu\leq\chi+1
and either
\chi>0
or
\chi=0
and
m=2
;

(ii)

in
\mathbb{H}^{m}
, provided that
\mu\leq\chi
and
\chi>0
.

Proof.

Let
\varphi(t)=t/\sqrt{1+t^{2}}
, and choose
p=2
in (
6.3
). To recover the Euclidean space set
\kappa=0
,
\alpha=-2
, while for the hyperbolic space set
\kappa=1
,
\alpha=0
. The rest of the proof is a direct application of Theorem
6.5
.
∎

As a further application of Theorem
6.5
in the next corollary we obtain a Liouville theorem for bounded solutions of

\Delta_{p}u\geq b(x)f(u)|\nabla u|^{q}-\bar{b}(x)\bar{f}(u)|\nabla u|^{\bar{q}}.

(6.27)

Corollary 6.8
.

Let
(M,\langle\,,\,\rangle)
be a
m-
dimensional complete manifold satisfying

\mathrm{Ric}(\nabla r,\nabla r)\geq-(m-1)\kappa^{2}\big(1+r^{2}\big)^{\alpha/2}\qquad\text{on }\,\mathcal{D}_{o},

(6.28)

for some
\kappa\geq 0
,
\alpha\geq-2
. Consider
0<b\in C(M)
such that

b(x)\geq C_{1}\big(1+r(x)\big)^{-\mu}\qquad\text{on }\,M,

for some
C_{1}>0
. Let
f,\bar{f}\in C(\mathbb{R})
,
\bar{b}\in C(M)
and
C>0
such that

\bar{b}\leq Cb\quad\text{on }\,M,\qquad\bar{f}\leq Cf\quad\text{on }\,\mathbb{R}.

(6.29)

Fix

p\in(1,\infty),\qquad q\in[0,p-1),\qquad\bar{q}>q

and consider a bounded above solution
u\in C^{1}(M)
of

\Delta_{p}u\geq b(x)f(u)|\nabla u|^{q}-\bar{b}(x)\bar{f}(u)|\nabla u|^{\bar{q}}.

(6.30)

If

\mu\leq p-1-q-\frac{\alpha}{2}

(6.31)

and
u
is non-constant, then
f(u^{*})\leq 0
. In particular, if
u\in C^{1}(M)\cap L^{\infty}(M)
solves (
6.27
) with the equality sign, and

C^{-1}f\leq\bar{f}\leq Cf\qquad\text{on }\,\mathbb{R},

(6.32)

then,
u
must be constant in each of the following cases:

(i)

f<0
on
(-\infty,t_{0})
and
f>0
on
(t_{0},\infty)
;

(ii)

f
has no zeroes.

Remark 6.9
.

If
q=0
, under
(i)
above the only constant solution of (
6.27
) with the equality sign is
u\equiv t_{0}
, while, under
(ii)
, (
6.27
) with the equality sign does not admit any constant solution.

Proof.

Suppose by contradiction that
f(u^{*})=4K>0
, and pick
\eta<u^{*}
such that
f(t)>2K
if
t>\eta
. Because of (
6.30
) and (
6.29
), on

\Omega_{\eta,\varepsilon}=\big\{x\in M\ \ :\ \ u(x)>\eta,\ |\nabla u(x)|<\varepsilon\big\}

we have

\Delta_{p}u\geq b(x)f(u)|\nabla u|^{q}\Big(1-C^{2}|\nabla u|^{\bar{q}-q}\Big)\geq 2Kb(x)|\nabla u|^{q}\big(1-C^{2}\varepsilon^{\bar{q}-q}\big),

and since
\bar{q}>q
we can choose
\varepsilon>0
small enough that

\Delta_{p}u\geq Kb(x)|\nabla u|^{q}\qquad\text{on }\,\Omega_{\eta,\varepsilon}.

(6.33)

Set
\chi=p-1-q\in(0,p-1]
. Then, in our assumptions, we can apply Theorem
6.5
to deduce that
(bl)^{-1}\Delta_{p}
satisfies
(\mathrm{SMP}_{\infty})
. Consequently, from (
6.33
) we get
K\leq 0
, contradiction.

Suppose now, by contradiction, that
u\in C^{1}(M)\cap L^{\infty}(M)
is a non-constant solution of (
6.27
) with the equality sign. By the first part of the proof we get
f(u^{*})\leq 0
. Next, observe that
\bar{u}=-u
solves

\Delta_{p}\bar{u}=b(x)f_{1}(\bar{u})|\nabla\bar{u}|^{q}-\bar{b}(x)\bar{f}_{1}(\bar{u})|\nabla\bar{u}|^{\bar{q}},

(6.34)

with
f_{1}(t)=-f(-t)
and
\bar{f}_{1}(t)=-\bar{f}(-t)
. In view of (
6.32
), applying again the first part we obtain
f_{1}(\bar{u}^{*})\leq 0
, that is,
f(u_{*})\geq 0
with
u_{*}=\inf_{M}u
. From
f(u^{*})\leq 0\leq f(u_{*})
and using
(i)
or
(ii)
, we deduce that
u
is necessarily constant, contradiction.
∎

Remark 6.10
.

Theorem
6.5
could be improved to include slowly growing solutions of
(P_{\geq})
as in
(ii)
of Theorem
5.4
, provided that one is able to estimate from below the order of growth of a family of Khas’minskii potentials (
6.1
) in a way independent of the origin
o
and of
\eta,r_{0},r_{1},\varepsilon
. If this holds, repeating the proof verbatim one shows that any solution
u
of
(P_{\geq})
on
M
, or on some upper level set, is bounded from above and satifies
f(u^{*})\leq 0
whenever

u_{+}(x)=o\big(\bar{w}(x)\big)\qquad\text{as }\,r(x)\rightarrow\infty,

with
\bar{w}
being any of such Khas’minskii potentials. Growth estimates are achieved provided that one can explicitly exhibit
\bar{w}
, and this is the case when
l(0)>0
. Indeed, when
l(0)>0
the first two of (
6.3
) are automatically satisfied, and to produce solutions of (
6.1
) we can consider radial solutions of

\big(v_{g}\varphi(w^{\prime})\big)^{\prime}=\sigma v_{g}\beta\qquad\text{on }\,[r_{0},\infty),

for small enough
\sigma>0
. Explicit integration with
w(r_{0})=w^{\prime}(r_{0})=0
gives

w(r)=\int_{r_{0}}^{r}\varphi^{-1}\left(\frac{\sigma}{v_{g}(t)}\int_{r_{0}}^{t}v_{g}(s)\beta(s)\mathrm{d}s\right)\mathrm{d}t.

This approach has been developed in Section 6 of
[
110
]
and in
[
111
, Thm. 18]
, the latter dealing with inequality

\Delta u\geq(1+r)^{-\mu}f(u)l(|\nabla u|)

on complete manifolds satisfying
\mathrm{Ric}\geq-(m-1)\kappa^{2}\langle\,,\,\rangle
, for some
\kappa>0
, for increasing
f
and for
\mu\in[0,1]
. The conclusion
f(u(x))\leq 0
on
M
is shown to hold provided that, as
r(x)\rightarrow\infty
,

u(x)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}o\big(r(x)^{1-\mu}\big)&\quad\text{if }\,\mu\in[0,1),\\[5.69046pt]
o\big(\log r(x)\big)&\quad\text{if }\,\mu=1.\end{array}\right.

Inspection shows that the case
\mu<1
well fits with
(ii)
of Theorem
5.4
(apply with
\varphi(t)=t
,
\sigma=1-\mu
,
\chi=1
and use Bishop-Gromov comparison to check the first of (
5.9
)). Case
\mu=1
, on the other hand, has no analogue in Theorem
5.4
.

6.1.1
Bernstein theorems for prescribed mean curvature

We now apply
(\mathrm{SMP}_{\infty})
to entire graphs with prescribed mean curvature in a warped product
\bar{M}=\mathbb{R}\times_{h}M
. We recall that the mean curvature of the totally umbilic slice
\{s=s_{0}\}
of
\bar{M}
in the upward direction
\partial_{s}
is

H_{\partial_{s}}\big(\{s=s_{0}\}\big)=-\frac{h^{\prime}(s_{0})}{h(s_{0})}.

The next theorem gives an a-priori estimate for entire graphs with prescribed mean curvature, and in particular it characterizes all constant mean curvature entire graphs. For simplicity, we state the result for warped products with
h(s)=\cosh s
, a class including the fibration
\mathbb{H}^{m+1}=\mathbb{R}\times_{\cosh s}\mathbb{H}^{m}
by hyperspheres
\{s=s_{0}\}
of constant mean curvature
H=-\tanh s_{0}\in(-1,1)
.

Theorem 6.11
.

Let
\bar{M}=\mathbb{R}\times_{\cosh s}M
, for some complete manifold
(M^{m},\langle\,,\,\rangle)
whose Ricci tensor satisfies

\mathrm{Ric}(\nabla r,\nabla r)\geq-(m-1)\kappa^{2}(1+r)^{2}\qquad\text{on }\,\mathcal{D}_{o},

for some constant
\kappa>0
. Fix a constant
H_{0}\in(-1,1)
, and consider an entire geodesic graph of
v:M\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
with prescribed curvature
H(x)\geq-H_{0}
in the upward direction. Then,
v
is bounded from above and satisfies

v^{*}\leq\mathrm{arctanh}(H_{0}).

(6.35)

In particular,

(i)

there is no entire graph with prescribed mean curvature satisfying
|H(x)|\geq 1
on
M
;

(ii)

the only entire graph with constant mean curvature
H_{0}\in(-1,1)
in the upward direction is the totally umbilic slice
\{s=\mathrm{arctanh}(H_{0})\}
.

Proof.

Define
t,\lambda(t)
and
u(x)
as in Subsection
1.1
in the Introduction with
h(s)=\cosh s
:

t(s)=\int_{0}^{s}\frac{\mathrm{d}\sigma}{\cosh\sigma}=2\arctan(e^{s})-\frac{\pi}{2},\quad\lambda(t)=h(s(t)),\quad u(x)=t(v(x)).

Note that
u:M\rightarrow\left(-\frac{\pi}{2},\frac{\pi}{2}\right)
. Since
\lambda(u)=\cosh v
and
\lambda_{t}(u)/\lambda(u)=\sinh v
, by (
1.11
)
u
satisfies

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\displaystyle\mathrm{div}\left(\frac{\nabla u}{\sqrt{1+|\nabla u|^{2}}}\right)&=&\displaystyle m\cosh v\left[H(x)+\tanh v\frac{1}{\sqrt{1+|\nabla u|^{2}}}\right]\\[14.22636pt]
&\geq&\displaystyle m\cosh v\left[-H_{0}+\tanh v\frac{1}{\sqrt{1+|\nabla u|^{2}}}\right].\end{array}

(6.36)

Suppose, by contradiction, that the following upper level set of
v
(hence, of
u
) is non-empty for some
\eta>0
:

\Omega_{\eta}=\{\tanh v>H_{0}+\eta\}.

Then,

\displaystyle\mathrm{div}\left(\frac{\nabla u}{\sqrt{1+|\nabla u|^{2}}}\right)\geq\frac{m\cosh v}{\sqrt{1+|\nabla u|^{2}}}\left[\eta-H_{0}(\sqrt{1+|\nabla u|^{2}}-1)\right]\qquad\text{on }\,\Omega_{\eta}.

(6.37)

If
H_{0}<0
, from
\cosh v\geq 1
we deduce

\displaystyle\mathrm{div}\left(\frac{\nabla u}{\sqrt{1+|\nabla u|^{2}}}\right)\geq\frac{m\eta}{\sqrt{1+|\nabla u|^{2}}}\qquad\text{on }\,\Omega_{\eta}.

(6.38)

On the other hand, if
H_{0}>0
, for
\varepsilon>0
we consider the set
\Omega_{\eta,\varepsilon}=\Omega_{\eta}\cap\{|\nabla u|<\varepsilon\}
. Note that
\Omega_{\eta,\varepsilon}
is non-empty by Ekeland’s principle, since
M
is complete. If
\varepsilon
is sufficiently small, the term in square brackets is less than
\eta/2
, and since
\cosh v\geq 1
we deduce

\displaystyle\mathrm{div}\left(\frac{\nabla u}{\sqrt{1+|\nabla u|^{2}}}\right)\geq\frac{m\eta}{2\sqrt{1+|\nabla u|^{2}}}\qquad\text{on }\Omega_{\eta,\varepsilon}.

(6.39)

We now apply Theorem
6.5
with the choices
\alpha=1
,
\mu=0
,
\chi=1
to deduce the validity of
(\mathrm{SMP}_{\infty})
for the operator
l^{-1}\Delta_{\varphi}
, with

\varphi=\frac{t}{\sqrt{1+t^{2}}},\qquad l(t)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{1+t^{2}}}.

Since
u
is bounded from above, applying
(\mathrm{SMP}_{\infty})
to (
6.39
) (for
H_{0}>0
) or to (
6.38
) (for
H_{0}<0
) we reach the desired contradiction.

To prove
(i)
, suppose that
|H(x)|\geq 1
on
M
. Since
\cosh s
is even, the graph of
-v
has curvature
-H(x)
in the upward direction. Thus, up to replacing
v
with
-v
we can suppose that
H(x)\geq 1
. Applying the first part of the theorem to any
H_{0}>-1
we obtain
v^{*}\leq\mathrm{arctanh}(H_{0})
, and the non-existence of
v
follows by letting
H_{0}\rightarrow-1
.

To prove
(ii)
, let
H(x)=-H_{0}\in(-1,1)
be the mean curvature of the graph of
v
in the upward direction. Then, Theorem
6.11
gives
\tanh v^{*}\leq H_{0}
. On the other hand, the graph of
-v
has mean curvature
H(x)=H_{0}
in the upward direction, and applying again Theorem
6.11
we deduce
\tanh[(-v)^{*}]\leq-H_{0}
, that is,
\tanh v_{*}\geq H_{0}
. Combining the two estimates gives
v\equiv\mathrm{arctanh}(H_{0})
, as required.
∎

Remark 6.12
.

If
H_{0}<0
, to conclude from (
6.38
) it is sufficient to require the validity of
(\mathrm{WMP}_{\infty})
.

Remark 6.13
.

Observe that
(ii)
generalizes item
(ii)
in Do Carmo-Lawson Theorem
1.3
: it is sufficient to apply Theorem
6.11
to
\mathbb{H}^{m+1}
with the warped product structure
\mathbb{R}\times_{\cosh r}\mathbb{H}^{m}
.

Remark 6.14
.

The above result can be generalized, with the same proof, to warped products
\mathbb{R}\times_{h}M
for
h
satisfying

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}h\ \ \text{ even},\\[5.69046pt]
h^{-1}\in L^{1}(-\infty)\cap L^{1}(+\infty),\\[5.69046pt]
(h^{\prime}/h)^{\prime}>0\qquad\text{on }\,\mathbb{R}.\end{array}\right.

We leave the statement to the interested reader.

7
The compact support principle

Consider the problem

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\Delta_{\varphi}u\geq b(x)f(u)l(|\nabla u|)\qquad\text{on }\,\Omega\,\text{ end of $M$.}\\[5.69046pt]
\displaystyle u\geq 0,\qquad\lim_{x\in\Omega,\,x\rightarrow\infty}u(x)=0.\end{array}\right.

(7.1)

We recall that an end
\Omega\subset M
is a connected component with non-compact closure of
M\backslash K
, for some compact set
K
. In this section, we investigate the necessity and sufficiency of condition

\frac{1}{K^{-1}\circ F}\in L^{1}(0^{+})

(KO
0
)

for the validity of the compact support principle
(\mathrm{CSP})
, that is, the statement that each
u
solving (
7.1
) has compact support. We assume the following:

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\varphi\in C(\mathbb{R}^{+}_{0})\cap C^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{+}),\qquad\varphi(0)=0,\qquad\varphi^{\prime}>0\ \text{ on }\,\mathbb{R}^{+},\\[8.5359pt]
f\in C(\mathbb{R}),\qquad f\geq 0\ \text{ in }\,(0,\eta_{0}),\ \text{ for some }\,\eta_{0}\in(0,\infty),\\[8.5359pt]
l\in C(\mathbb{R}^{+}_{0}),\qquad l>0\ \text{on }\,\mathbb{R}^{+},\end{array}\right.

(7.2)

and moreover

\frac{t\varphi^{\prime}(t)}{l(t)}\in L^{1}(0^{+}).

(7.3)

Having defined
F,K
as in (
1.20
) and (
1.19
), that is,

K(t)=\int_{0}^{t}\frac{s\varphi^{\prime}(s)}{l(s)}\mathrm{d}s,\qquad F(t)=\int_{0}^{t}f(s)\mathrm{d}s,

(7.4)

set
K_{\infty}=\lim_{t\rightarrow\infty}K(t)\in(0,\infty]
; since
\varphi^{\prime}>0
, the inverse
K^{-1}:[0,K_{\infty})\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{+}
exists, and (
KO0
) is meaningful. In most of the results, we also require

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\text{$f$ is $C$-increasing on $[0,\eta_{0})$,}\\[5.69046pt]
\text{$l$ is $C$-increasing on $[0,\xi)$, for some $\xi>0$.}\end{array}\right.

(7.5)

We underline that condition
f(0)l(0)=0
does not appear in (
7.2
). In fact, some of the next results do not need it. As usual, having fixed a relatively compact, smooth open set
\mathcal{O}\subset M
we denote with
r(x)=\mathrm{dist}(x,\mathcal{O})
.

7.1
Necessity of (
KO0
) for the compact support principle

Suppose the failure (

¬

KO0
) of the Keller-Osserman condition. Because of Theorem
4.8
, under assumptions (
7.2
), (
7.3
) and
f(0)l(0)=0
each
C^{1}
solution of (
7.1
)
with the equality sign
must satisfy
(\mathrm{FMP})
, and consequently it cannot be compactly supported. However, finding solutions with the equality sign for (
7.1
), and especially proving their
C^{1}
-regularity, seems to be tricky in the generality of (
7.2
) and (
7.3
). For this reason, we follow a different path producing, on
each
complete manifold, radial solutions of inequality (
7.1
) which are positive on
\Omega=M\backslash B_{r_{0}}(\mathcal{O})
. The
C^{1}
-regularity will be therefore a consequence of the assumption that the radial function be smooth, that is, that the origin
\mathcal{O}
be a pole of
M
.

The key step is provided by the following theorem that considers the exterior Dirichlet problem. Fix
r_{0}>0
and functions
v,\beta
satisfying

\begin{array}[]{l}v\in C^{1}\big([r_{0},\infty)\big),\qquad v>0,\ v^{\prime}\geq 0\quad\text{on }\,[r_{0},\infty);\\[5.69046pt]
\beta\in C\big([r_{0},\infty)\big),\qquad\beta>0\quad\text{on }\,[r_{0},\infty).\end{array}

(7.6)

For
\eta,\xi>0
, define
f_{\eta},l_{\xi}
as in (
3.6
), that is,

f_{\eta}=\max_{[0,\eta]}f,\qquad l_{\xi}=\max_{[0,\xi]}l.

We are ready to state

Theorem 7.1
.

Let
\varphi,f,l
satisfy (
7.2
) and

\left\{\begin{array}[]{lcl}\text{$f$ is non-decreasing on $(0,\eta_{0})$,}\\[5.69046pt]
l\in\mathrm{Lip}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{+}),\\[5.69046pt]
f(0)l(0)=0.\end{array}\right.

(7.7)

Fix
r_{0}>0
and let
v,\beta
as in (
7.6
).
Then, for each
R>0
,
\xi\in(0,1)
and
\eta\in(0,\eta_{0})
(with
\eta_{0}
as in (
3.2
)) satisfying

\frac{v(r_{0}+R)}{v(r_{0})}\varphi\left(\frac{\eta}{R}\right)+f_{\eta}l_{\xi}\left[\sup_{[r_{0},r_{0}+R)}\frac{1}{v(r)}\int_{r_{0}}^{r}v(s)\beta(s)\mathrm{d}s\right]<\varphi(\xi),

(7.8)

there exists a solution
z\in C^{1}([r_{0},\infty))
of

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\big[v\varphi(z^{\prime})\big]^{\prime}=\beta vf(z)l(|z^{\prime}|)&\quad\text{on }\,[r_{0},\infty)\\[5.69046pt]
z(r_{0})=\eta,\qquad-\xi<z^{\prime}\leq 0&\quad\text{on }\,[r_{0},\infty).\end{array}\right.

(7.9)

Furthermore, if

\varphi^{-1}\left(\frac{c}{v(r)}\right)\in L^{1}(\infty),\qquad\text{for some constant }\,c>0,

(7.10)

there exists
\eta_{1}=\eta_{1}(v,c,\varphi)
such that, for each
\eta\in(0,\min\{\eta_{0},\eta_{1}\})
satisfying (
7.8
),
z(r)\rightarrow 0
as
r\rightarrow\infty
.

Remark 7.2
.

Condition (
7.10
), to be meaningful, needs to be considered on the interval of integration of the type
[r_{c},\infty)
where the integrand is well defined, that is, because of the monotonicity of
v
, for
c<v(r_{c})\varphi(\infty)
. The existence of such
r_{c}
is implicit since the validity of (
7.10
) and the monotonicity of
v
force
\lim_{r\rightarrow\infty}v(r)=\infty
.

Proof.

Set

h=\max\left\{1,\,2\frac{\eta}{R}\right\}.

We define
\bar{\varphi},\bar{l}
on
\mathbb{R}^{+}_{0}
as follows:

\begin{array}[]{l}\displaystyle\bar{\varphi}(t)=\varphi(t)\ \text{ on }\,[0,h],\qquad\bar{\varphi}(t)=\varphi(h)+(t-h)\ \text{ on }\,(h,\infty);\\[5.69046pt]
\bar{l}\in C(\mathbb{R}^{+}_{0}),\qquad\bar{l}=l\quad\text{on }\,[0,\xi],\qquad 0<\bar{l}\leq l_{\xi}\quad\text{on }\,\mathbb{R}^{+},\end{array}

and we extend
\bar{\varphi}
to an odd function on the entire
\mathbb{R}
. For each
j\in\mathbb{N}
,
j\geq 1
set also

\qquad\wp_{j}(t)=v(r_{0}+jR-t),\qquad a_{j}(t)=\beta(r_{0}+jR-t),

and let
w_{j}
be a solution of the Dirichlet problem

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\big[\wp_{j}\bar{\varphi}(w_{t})\big]_{t}=a_{j}\wp_{j}f(w)\bar{l}(|w_{t}|)\quad\text{on }\,[0,jR],\\[5.69046pt]
w(0)=0,\qquad w(jR)=\eta,\\[5.69046pt]
0\leq w\leq\eta,\qquad w_{t}\geq 0\quad\text{on }\,[0,jR],\\[5.69046pt]
\end{array}\right.

(7.11)

where the subscript
t
denotes differentiation in the
t
variable. We stress that
w_{j}
exists for each
j
. Indeed, we shall apply Theorem
3.1
with the parameter
\xi
replaced by some suitably chosen
\bar{\xi}
. Note that (
3.8
) is satisfied up to choosing
\bar{\xi}
sufficiently large, because
\bar{\varphi}(\infty)=\infty
and
\bar{l}_{\bar{\xi}}\leq l_{\xi}
. Observe that
\bar{\xi}
might depend on
j
, but this does not affect the rest of the proof. From
\wp^{\prime}\leq 0
, again by Theorem
3.1
we deduce

0\leq(w_{j})_{t}\leq\bar{\varphi}^{-1}\left(\frac{\wp(0)}{\wp(jR)}\bar{\varphi}\left(\frac{\eta}{jR}\right)+f_{\eta}l_{\xi}\left[\sup_{[0,jR]}\frac{1}{\wp_{j}(t)}\int_{0}^{t}\wp_{j}(s)a_{j}(s)\mathrm{d}s\right]\right).

(7.12)

Set
z_{j}(r)=w_{j}(r_{0}+jR-r)
, and note that
z_{j}
solves

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\big[v\bar{\varphi}(z_{j}^{\prime})\big]^{\prime}=\beta vf(z_{j})\bar{l}(|z_{j}^{\prime}|)\quad\text{on }\,(r_{0},r_{0}+jR),\\[5.69046pt]
z_{j}(r_{0})=\eta,\qquad z_{j}(r_{0}+jR)=0\\[5.69046pt]
0\leq z_{j}\leq\eta,\qquad z_{j}^{\prime}\leq 0\quad\text{on }\,[r_{0},r_{0}+jR].\\[5.69046pt]
\end{array}\right.

(7.13)

Next, we estimate the derivative
z_{j}^{\prime}
uniformly in
j
. First, observe that integrating on
[t_{1},t_{2}]
the inequality
[v\bar{\varphi}(z^{\prime})]^{\prime}\geq 0
that follows from (
7.13
) and (
3.2
), we deduce

v(t_{2})\big[\bar{\varphi}\big(z^{\prime}(t_{2})\big)-\bar{\varphi}\big(z^{\prime}(t_{1})\big)\big]\geq\big[v(t_{1})-v(t_{2})\big]\bar{\varphi}\big(z^{\prime}(t_{1})\big)\geq 0.

Using
\bar{\varphi}(z^{\prime})\leq 0
and
v^{\prime}\geq 0
we conclude that
\bar{\varphi}(z^{\prime})
, hence
z^{\prime}
, is increasing. In particular, since
z_{j}^{\prime}\leq 0
, we have
|z_{j}^{\prime}|\leq|z_{j}^{\prime}(r_{0})|
.

Claim:

\{z_{j}\}
in an increasing sequence,

We show that
z_{j}\leq z_{j+1}
on
[r_{0},r_{0}+jR]
. Applying Lemma
3.2
to
w_{j}
and rephrasing for
z_{j}
, there exists
r_{j}\in(r_{0},r_{0}+jR]
such that
z_{j}>0
,
z_{j}^{\prime}<0
on
[r_{0},r_{j})
while
z_{j}=0
on
[r_{j},r_{0}+jR)
. On
(r_{0},r_{j})
it holds

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\big[v\bar{\varphi}(z_{j}^{\prime})\big]^{\prime}=\beta vf(z_{j})\bar{l}(|z_{j}^{\prime}|)\quad\text{on }\,(r_{0},r_{j}),\\[5.69046pt]
\big[v\bar{\varphi}(z_{j+1}^{\prime})\big]^{\prime}=\beta vf(z_{j+1})\bar{l}(|z_{j+1}^{\prime}|)\quad\text{on }\,(r_{0},r_{j}),\\[5.69046pt]
z_{j}(r_{0})=z_{j+1}(r_{0})=\eta,\qquad z_{j}(r_{j})=0\leq z_{j+1}(r_{j}).\end{array}\right.

(7.14)

The inequality
z_{j}\leq z_{j+1}
on
[r_{0},r_{j}]
, hence on
[r_{0},r_{0}+jR]
, is then a consequence of the comparison result in Proposition
4.2
applied to the model manifold
M_{g}=[r_{0},\infty)\times\mathbb{S}^{m-1}
with the radially symmetric
C^{1}
-metric

\mathrm{d}r^{2}+g(r)^{2}(\,,\,)_{1},\qquad\text{with}\qquad g(r)=v(r)^{\frac{1}{m-1}},

recall also Remark
4.4
.

The convexity and monotonicity of
z_{j}
, together with the above claim, imply the uniform estimate
|z_{j}^{\prime}|\leq|z_{1}^{\prime}(r_{0})|
. Changing variables in (
7.12
) and exploiting (
7.8
),

\begin{array}[]{lcl}|z_{j}^{\prime}|&\leq&\displaystyle|z_{1}^{\prime}(r_{0})|\\[14.22636pt]
&\leq&\displaystyle\bar{\varphi}^{-1}\left(\frac{v(r_{0}+R)}{v(r_{0})}\bar{\varphi}\left(\frac{\eta}{R}\right)+f_{\eta}l_{\xi}\left[\sup_{[r_{0},r_{0}+R]}\frac{1}{v(r)}\int_{r_{0}}^{r}v(s)\beta(s)\mathrm{d}s\right]\right)<\xi,\end{array}

where we used again the identity
\bar{\varphi}=\varphi
on
[0,h]
, the definition of
h
and
\xi<1
. Thererefore, by Ascoli-Arzelá the sequence
\{z_{j}\}
converges locally uniformly to a solution
z\in C^{1}([r_{0},\infty))
of

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\big[v\bar{\varphi}(z^{\prime})\big]^{\prime}=\beta vf(z)\bar{l}(|z^{\prime}|)\quad\text{on }\,(r_{0},\infty),\\[5.69046pt]
z(r_{0})=\eta,\\[5.69046pt]
0\leq z\leq\eta,\qquad-\xi<z^{\prime}\leq 0\quad\text{on }\,[r_{0},\infty),\end{array}\right.

(7.15)

Since
\bar{\varphi}=\varphi
and
\bar{l}=l
on
(0,\xi)\subset(0,1)
,
z
is the desired solution of (
7.15
). Eventually, we assume (
7.10
), which in particular implies that
\lim_{r\rightarrow\infty}v(r)=\infty
, and we choose
r_{c}
such that

r_{c}\geq r_{0},\quad\frac{c}{v(r_{c})}\leq\varphi(1).

Define

\bar{z}(r)=\int_{r}^{\infty}\varphi^{-1}\left(\frac{c}{v(s)}\right)\mathrm{d}s\qquad\text{on }\,[r_{c},\infty).

From
\bar{\varphi}=\varphi
on
[0,h]\supset[0,1]
,
\bar{z}
solves
0=\big[v\varphi(\bar{z}^{\prime})\big]^{\prime}=\big[v\bar{\varphi}(\bar{z}^{\prime})\big]^{\prime}=0
on
[r_{c},\infty)
. Choose now

\eta_{1}=\int_{r_{c}}^{\infty}\varphi^{-1}\left(\frac{c}{v(s)}\right)\mathrm{d}s

and consider
\eta
satisfying the further restriction
\eta\in(0,\min\{\eta_{0},\eta_{1}\})
. For
j
large enough, since
z_{j}(r_{c})\leq z_{j}(r_{0})=\eta<\eta_{1}=\bar{z}(r_{c})
, by the comparison Proposition
4.2
and the non-negativity of
\beta,f(z_{j})
and
l(|z_{j}^{\prime}|)
we get
\bar{z}\geq z_{j}
on
[r_{c},r_{0}+jR]
, thus
\bar{z}\geq z
on
[r_{c},\infty)
. The thesis follows since
\bar{z}\rightarrow 0
as
r\rightarrow\infty
.
∎

We are ready to prove our main result, Theorem
1.37
in the Introduction, in the following more general form: it says, loosely speaking, that there is no geometric obstruction for (
KO0
) to be necessary for the compact support principle.

Theorem 7.3
.

Let
(M^{m},\langle\,,\,\rangle)
be a complete manifold, and let
\varphi,f,l
satisfy (
7.2
), (
7.3
), (
7.5
) and

\begin{array}[]{l}\displaystyle l\in\mathrm{Lip}_{\mathrm{loc}}\big((0,\xi_{0})\big)\\[5.69046pt]
f(0)l(0)=0.\end{array}

Then, for each

origin
\mathcal{O}\subset M
with associated distance
r(x)=\mathrm{dist}(x,\mathcal{O})
,

r_{0}>0
,
\xi\in(0,\xi_{0})
(with
\xi_{0}
as in (
7.5
)),

0<b\in C\big(M\backslash B_{r_{0}}(\mathcal{O})\big)
,

there exists
\eta\in(0,\eta_{0})
sufficiently small and a radial solution
u\in\mathrm{Lip}(M\backslash B_{r_{0}}(\mathcal{O}))
of

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\Delta_{\varphi}u\geq b(x)f(u)l(|\nabla u|)\qquad\text{weakly on }\,M\backslash B_{r_{0}}(\mathcal{O}),\\[5.69046pt]
0\leq u\leq\eta\quad\text{on }\,M\backslash B_{r_{0}}(\mathcal{O}),\\[5.69046pt]
u=\eta\quad\text{on }\,\partial B_{r_{0}}(\mathcal{O}),\qquad u(x)\rightarrow 0\quad\text{as }\,r(x)\rightarrow\infty,\\[5.69046pt]
|\nabla u|<\xi\qquad\text{on }\,M\backslash B_{r_{0}}(\mathcal{O}).\end{array}\right.

Moreover, if (

¬

KO0
) holds then
u>0
on
M\backslash\mathcal{O}
. In particular, if
\mathcal{O}
is a pole for
M
,
u\in C^{1}\big(M\backslash B_{r_{0}}(\mathcal{O})\big)
and (
KO0
) is necessary for the validity of the compact support principle
(\mathrm{CSP})
.

Proof.

We choose
0<\bar{g}\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{+}_{0})
enjoying the following properties:

(i)

if
H_{-\nabla r}
is the mean curvature of
\partial\mathcal{O}
with respect to the inward pointing unit normal
-\nabla r
,

(m-1)\frac{\bar{g}^{\prime}(0)}{\bar{g}(0)}>\max\left\{0,\sup_{\partial\cal O}H_{-\nabla r}\right\};

(7.16)

(ii)

setting
\bar{v}(r)=\mathrm{vol}(\mathbb{S}^{m-1})\bar{g}(r)^{m-1}
,

\begin{array}[]{l}\bar{v}^{\prime}\geq 0\qquad\text{on }\,\mathbb{R}^{+},\\[5.69046pt]
\displaystyle\bar{v}(r)\geq\max\left\{1,\left[\varphi\left(\frac{1}{r^{2}}\right)\right]^{-1}\right\}\quad\text{for }\,r\geq 1.\end{array}

(7.17)

Note that, for
r\geq 1
,

\varphi^{-1}\left(\frac{1}{\bar{v}(r)}\right)\leq\varphi^{-1}\left(\varphi\left(\frac{1}{r^{2}}\right)\right)=\frac{1}{r^{2}}\in L^{1}(\infty).

(7.18)

Next, choose
0\leq\bar{G}\in C(\mathbb{R}^{+}_{0})
in such a way that

\mathrm{Ric}\geq-(m-1)\bar{G}(r)\langle\,,\,\rangle\qquad\text{on }\,M,

and define
G(r)=\max\{\bar{G},\bar{g}^{\prime\prime}/\bar{g}\}
. Let
g\in C^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+}_{0})
solve

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}g^{\prime\prime}-Gg=0\quad\text{on }\,\mathbb{R}^{+}\\[5.69046pt]
\displaystyle\frac{g^{\prime}(0)}{g(0)}=\frac{\bar{g}^{\prime}(0)}{\bar{g}(0)},\end{array}\right.

(7.19)

and let
M_{g}
be the model associated to
g
. By construction and by (
7.18
), setting
v_{g}(r)=\mathrm{vol}(\mathbb{S}^{m-1})g(r)^{m-1}
it holds

\begin{array}[]{l}\mathrm{Ric}\geq-(m-1)G(r)\langle\,,\,\rangle\qquad\text{on }\,M,\\[5.69046pt]
v_{g}(r)\geq\bar{v}(r)\qquad\text{on }\,\mathbb{R}^{+}\,\text{ by Sturm comparison,}\\[5.69046pt]
\displaystyle\varphi^{-1}\left(\frac{1}{v_{g}(t)}\right)\in L^{1}(\infty).\end{array}

(7.20)

We define

\bar{f}(t)=\sup_{[0,t]}f(s),\qquad\beta(r)=\sup_{\partial B_{r}}b,

and note that, since
f
is
C
-increasing,

f(t)\leq\bar{f}(t)\leq Cf(t)\qquad\forall\,t\in[0,\eta_{0}).

(7.21)

Choose
\bar{l}\in\mathrm{Lip}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{+})
such that

\bar{l}\geq l\quad\text{on }\,\mathbb{R}^{+}_{0},\qquad\bar{l}\equiv l\quad\text{on }\,[0,\xi].

Eventually, fix
r_{0}\geq 1
and choose
R
small enough that

\bar{f}_{\eta_{0}}\bar{l}_{1}\left[\sup_{[r_{0},r_{0}+R)}\frac{1}{v_{g}(r)}\int_{r_{0}}^{r}v_{g}(s)\beta(s)\mathrm{d}s\right]<\frac{\varphi(\xi)}{2}.

Then, pick
\eta\in(0,\eta_{0})
small enough to enjoy

\frac{v_{g}(r_{0}+R)}{v_{g}(r_{0})}\varphi\left(\frac{\eta}{R}\right)<\frac{\varphi(\xi)}{2}.

Since
\bar{f}
is increasing,
\eta\in(0,\eta_{0})
and
\xi\in(0,1)
, the last two inequalities imply

\frac{v_{g}(r_{0}+R)}{v_{g}(r_{0})}\varphi\left(\frac{\eta}{R}\right)+\bar{f}_{\eta}\bar{l}_{\xi}\left[\sup_{[r_{0},r_{0}+R)}\frac{1}{v_{g}(r)}\int_{r_{0}}^{r}v_{g}(s)\beta(s)\mathrm{d}s\right]<\varphi(\xi).

(7.22)

We are therefore in the position to apply Theorem
7.1
and infer the existence of
\eta_{1}
such that, for each
\eta\in(0,\min\{\eta_{0},\eta_{1}\})
satisfying (
7.22
), there exists
z
solving

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\big[v_{g}\varphi(z^{\prime})\big]^{\prime}=\beta v_{g}\bar{f}(z)\bar{l}(|z^{\prime}|)&\quad\text{on }\,[r_{0},\infty)\\[5.69046pt]
z(r_{0})=\eta,\qquad-\xi<z^{\prime}\leq 0&\quad\text{on }\,[r_{0},\infty),\\[5.69046pt]
z(r)\rightarrow 0\qquad\text{as }\,r\rightarrow\infty.\end{array}\right.

(7.23)

We consider
u(x)=z(r(x))
on
M\backslash B_{r_{0}}(\mathcal{O})
, with
r(x)=\mathrm{dist}(x,\mathcal{O})
. The first of (
7.20
) together with (
7.19
) and (
7.16
) imply, via the Laplacian comparison theorem (Thm.
9.3
in Appendix A), the inequality

\Delta r\leq\frac{v_{g}^{\prime}(r)}{v_{g}(r)}\qquad\text{weakly on }\,M\backslash\mathcal{O},

hence

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\Delta_{\varphi}u&\geq&\displaystyle\varphi^{\prime}(z^{\prime})z^{\prime\prime}+\varphi(z^{\prime})\Delta r\geq\varphi^{\prime}(z^{\prime})z^{\prime\prime}+\varphi(z^{\prime})\frac{v_{g}^{\prime}}{v_{g}}\\[8.5359pt]
&=&v_{g}^{-1}\big[v_{g}\varphi(z^{\prime})\big]^{\prime}=\beta\bar{f}(z)\bar{l}(|z^{\prime}|)\geq b(x)f(z)l(|z^{\prime}|)\\[8.5359pt]
&=&b(x)f(u)l(|\nabla u|)\end{array}

weakly on
M\backslash B_{r_{0}}(\mathcal{O})
. This concludes the first part of the theorem.

Next, we prove that if (

¬

KO0
) holds then
z>0
on
[r_{0},\infty)
. To see this, we consider the radial function
v(x)=z(r(x))
on the model
M_{g}
, that satisfies

\Delta_{\varphi}v=v_{g}^{-1}\big[v_{g}\varphi(z^{\prime})\big]^{\prime}=\beta(r)\bar{f}(z)\bar{l}(|z^{\prime}|)=\beta(r)\bar{f}(v)l(|\nabla v|)

where we used
|z^{\prime}|<\xi
and
l=\bar{l}
on
[0,\xi]
. Moreover,
v=\eta
on
\{r=r_{0}\}
. Set

\bar{F}(t)=\int_{0}^{t}\bar{f}(s)\mathrm{d}s.

Because of (
7.21
), Lemma
3.6
guarantees that (

¬

KO0
) is equivalent to

\frac{1}{K^{-1}\circ\bar{F}}\not\in L^{1}(0^{+}).

Therefore, we can apply Theorem
4.8
on the set
\Omega=\{r>r_{0}\}\subset M_{g}
to deduce that
v>0
on
\Omega
, as claimed.
∎

7.2
Sufficiency of (
KO0
) for the compact support principle

7.2.1
General operators, and no cut-locus

As remarked in the Introduction, (
KO0
) alone is not sufficient to prove
(\mathrm{CSP})
on complete manifolds. Indeed, the influence of geometry is for this property particularly subtle, as confirmed by the next refinement of Example
1.35
.

Example 7.4
.

For
\delta\geq 0
, consider a model
M_{\delta}=(\mathbb{R}^{m},\mathrm{d}s_{\delta}^{2})
(cf. also Example
9.4
below) with

\mathrm{d}s_{\delta}^{2}=\mathrm{d}t^{2}+g_{\delta}(t)^{2}(\,,\,)_{1},\qquad\text{where}\quad\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}g_{\delta}\in C^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+}_{0})&g_{\delta}>0\quad\text{on }\,\mathbb{R}^{+}\\[5.69046pt]
g_{\delta}(t)=t&\text{if }\,t\leq 1/4\\[5.69046pt]
g_{\delta}(t)=\exp\{-t^{\delta}\}&\text{if }\,t\geq 1.\end{array}\right.

Define
\mathcal{O}=B_{1}(o)
, and let
r=t-1
be the distance from
\cal O
. We have

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\displaystyle\operatorname{II}_{-\nabla r}&=&\displaystyle-\delta\mathrm{d}s_{\delta}^{2},\\[5.69046pt]
K_{\mathrm{rad}}(r)&=&\displaystyle-\delta\big[-(\delta-1)(1+r)^{\delta-2}+\delta(1+r)^{2\delta-2}\big]\leq-\delta^{2}(1+r)^{\delta-2}\left[(1+r)^{\delta}-1\right],\\[5.69046pt]
\Delta r&=&\displaystyle-(m-1)\delta(1+r)^{\delta-1}.\end{array}

Define
\alpha=2\delta-2\geq-2
, and let
\mu,\chi,\omega\in\mathbb{R}
satisfy

\mu>\chi-\frac{\alpha}{2},\qquad\omega<\chi.

(7.24)

It is easy to show that, for

\sigma\in\left(0,\frac{\mu-\chi+\alpha/2}{\chi-\omega}\right],

and for each
p>1
, the function
v(x)=(1+r(x))^{-\sigma}
is a bounded, positive solution of

\Delta_{p}v\geq C(1+r)^{-\mu}v^{\omega}|\nabla v|^{p-1-\chi}\qquad\text{on }\,M_{\delta}\backslash\cal O,

for some constant
C>0
, and furthermore
v(x)\rightarrow 0^{+}
as
x
diverges. However, defining
f(t)=t^{\omega}
and
l(t)=t^{p-1-\chi}
, inequality
\omega<\chi
implies

\frac{1}{K^{-1}\circ F}=C_{1}s^{-\frac{\omega+1}{\chi+1}}\in L^{1}(0^{+}).

Thus, condition (
KO0
) is met but
(\mathrm{CSP})
fails on
M_{\delta}
.

As in
[
123
,
120
]
, the proof of
(\mathrm{CSP})
will be achieved via the construction of a compactly supported,
C^{1}
-supersolution
\bar{w}
for (
7.1
). Under the above assumptions on
\varphi,b,f,l
, to produce
\bar{w}
one could try to use the solution
w
of the related ODE (
3.5
) in a way analogous to the one in the proof of the finite maximum principle (Theorem
4.8
). However, a direct use of
w
seems difficult, also because of the delicate interplay between the threshold
\eta
in (
3.5
) and the global behaviour of the constants
a_{1},\wp_{1},\wp_{0},l_{\xi}
in Theorem
3.1
, depending on the interval
[0,T]
under consideration. As we shall see, a certain independence between
\eta
and
a_{1},\wp_{1},\wp_{0},l_{\xi}
is key to conclude
(\mathrm{CSP})
from the existence of
w
. To overcome the problem, we will use a different technique: instead of solving a Dirichlet problem we will exhibit an explicit, compactly supported supersolution by a direct use of (
KO0
), an idea that is closer to the one in
[
123
,
120
,
132
]
, which in turn are improvements of
[
125
]
. However, extending the method therein to non-constant
b,l
presents nontrivial hurdles and calls for new ideas. To this aim, we shall assume some further conditions, that although seemingly somewhat articifial are still quite general, and enable us to capture the right growths to get a sharp result.

To exhibit
\bar{w}
we follow two slightly different constructions that need a (mildly) different set of assumptions. Besides (
7.2
), (
7.3
) and (
7.5
), for the first construction we require

(C_{1})

there exists a constant
k_{1}\geq 1
such that

tK^{\prime}(t)\leq k_{1}K(t)\qquad\text{for each }\,t\in(0,1];

(C_{2})

there exists a constant
k_{2}\geq 1
such that

K^{\prime}(st)\leq k_{2}K^{\prime}(s)K^{\prime}(t)\qquad\text{for each }\,s,t\in(0,1];

(C_{3})

there exists a constant
\bar{C}\geq 1
such that

\frac{t}{K^{-1}(t)}\quad\text{is $\bar{C}$-increasing,}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\frac{t}{K^{-1}(t)}\rightarrow 0\quad\text{as }\,t\rightarrow 0.

(C_{4})

there exists a constant
c_{F}\geq 1
such that

\frac{F(t)}{K^{-1}(F(t))}\leq c_{F}f(t)\qquad\text{for each }\,t\in(0,\min\{1,\eta_{0}\}).

Note that these requirements are all related to the behaviour of the various functions considered in a right neighbourhood of zero.

Example 7.5
.

Having fixed

p>1,\qquad\chi\in[0,p-1],\qquad\omega>0,

the prototype example of
f,l,\varphi
is given by

\varphi^{\prime}(t)\asymp t^{p-2},\qquad f(t)\asymp t^{\omega},\qquad l(t)\asymp t^{p-1-\chi}

as
t\rightarrow 0^{+}
. Then,
K(t)\asymp t^{\chi+1}
satisfies
(C_{1}),(C_{2})
, while
(C_{3})
and
(C_{4})
are met if and only if, respectively,
\chi>0
and
\omega\leq\chi
.

Suppose that

b(x)\geq\beta\big(r(x)\big)\qquad\text{for }\,r(x)\geq r_{0}.

We express the relation between
K
and
\beta
in terms of an auxiliary weight
\bar{\beta}
, that is tied with
K
in the way expressed by the next two conditions. Later, (
7.25
) in Proposition
7.8
will relate
\beta
to
\bar{\beta}
.

(\beta_{1})

0<\beta\in C([r_{0},\infty))
,
\quad\bar{\beta}\in C^{1}([r_{0},\infty))
,
\quad 0<\bar{\beta}<K_{\infty},\ \ \bar{\beta}^{\prime}\leq 0
on
[r_{0},\infty)
;

(\beta_{2})

there exists a constant
c_{\beta}\geq 1
such that

\frac{-\bar{\beta}^{\prime}(t)}{K^{-1}(\bar{\beta}(t))}\leq c_{\beta}\bar{\beta}(t)\qquad\text{for each }\,t\in[r_{0},\infty).

Remark 7.6
.

Note that
(\beta_{2})
is meaningful since
\bar{\beta}<K_{\infty}
because of
(\beta_{1})
.

Example 7.7
.

Referring to Example
7.5
, a borderline behaviour of
\bar{\beta}
for the validity of
(\beta_{2})
is

\bar{\beta}(t)=(1+t)^{-\chi-1}.

We first describe our main ODE result, that should be compared to Lemma 4.1 in
[
136
]
. Here, we consider a different and (in some cases) weaker set of assumptions, and the proof that we present is considerably simpler. Below, we will describe in more detail the interplay between the two results.

Proposition 7.8
.

Let
\varphi,l,f
satisfy (
7.2
), (
7.3
) and (
7.5
), and assume the validity of
(C_{1}),\ldots,(C_{4})
and
(\beta_{1}),(\beta_{2})
. Having fixed a non-negative
\theta\in C([r_{0},\infty))
, suppose that

\max\left\{\frac{\bar{\beta}(s)}{\beta(s)},\ \frac{\theta(s)\bar{\beta}(s)}{\beta(s)K^{-1}(\bar{\beta}(s))}\right\}\in L^{\infty}\big([r_{0},\infty)\big).

(7.25)

If

\frac{1}{K^{-1}\circ F}\in L^{1}(0^{+}),

(
\mathrm{KO}_{0}
)

then for each
\varepsilon>0
there exists
\lambda\in(0,1)
such that the following holds: for each
R\geq r_{0}
, there exists
R_{1}>R
and a function
w
with the following properties:

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}w\in C^{1}([R,\infty))\quad\text{and $C^{2}$ except possibly at $R_{1}$;}\\[5.69046pt]
0\leq w\leq\lambda,\qquad w(R)=\lambda,\qquad w\equiv 0\quad\text{on }\,[R_{1},\infty),\\[5.69046pt]
w^{\prime}<0\quad\text{on }\,[R,R_{1}),\qquad|w^{\prime}|\leq\varepsilon\quad\text{on }\,[R,\infty),\\[5.69046pt]
\big(\varphi(w^{\prime})\big)^{\prime}-\theta(r)\varphi(w^{\prime})\leq\varepsilon\beta(r)f(w)l(|w^{\prime}|)\qquad\text{on }\,[R,\infty).\end{array}\right.

(7.26)

Remark 7.9
.

Inspecting the proof of Proposition
7.8
, we can weaken the third in (
1.5
) to
l\in L^{\infty}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{+}_{0})\cap C(\mathbb{R}^{+})
and
l>0
on
\mathbb{R}^{+}
, that is, the continuity of
l
at
t=0
is not needed. This will be used in the proof of Theorem
1.38
.

Remark 7.10
.

Condition (
7.25
) relates
\beta
to
\bar{\beta}
. For instance, in Example
7.5
, we can set
\bar{\beta}(t)=c(1+t)^{-\chi-1}
for a constant
c
small enough to satisfy
(\beta_{1})
, and choose
\beta(t)=(1+t)^{-\mu}
, for some
\mu\in\mathbb{R}
. Then, (
7.25
) is met if and only if

\mu\leq\chi+1\qquad\text{and}\qquad\theta(s)s^{\mu-\chi}\in L^{\infty}([r_{0},\infty)).

To begin the proof, we need a simple technical lemma.

Lemma 7.11
.

Assume (
7.5
) and (
7.3
), and also
(C_{1}),(C_{2}),(\beta_{1}),(\beta_{2})
. Then, the following properties hold:

(K_{1})

K(st)\leq k_{1}k_{2}K(s)K(t)\quad
for each
\ \ s,t\in[0,1]
;

(K_{2})

K^{-1}(\tau)K^{-1}(\rho)\leq K^{-1}(k_{1}k_{2}\tau\rho)\quad
for each
\ \ \tau,\rho\in\left(0,\min\left\{1,\frac{K_{\infty}}{k_{1}k_{2}}\right\}\right)
;

(K\beta)

For each
\sigma\in(0,1)
,

K^{-1}\big(\sigma\bar{\beta}\big)\not\in L^{1}(\infty);

Proof.

Property
(K_{1})
follows immediately from
(C_{2})
and
(C_{1})
by integration:

K(st)=\int_{0}^{st}K^{\prime}(\tau)\mathrm{d}\tau=s\int_{0}^{t}K^{\prime}(s\zeta)\mathrm{d}\zeta\leq k_{2}sK^{\prime}(s)K(t)\leq k_{2}k_{1}K(s)K(t).

To show
(K_{2})
, use
(K_{1})
with the choices
s=K^{-1}(\tau)
,
t=K^{-1}(\rho)
and then apply
K^{-1}
. To prove
(K\beta)
first observe that, because of
(K_{2})
,
K^{-1}(\sigma\bar{\beta})\geq C_{\sigma}K^{-1}(\bar{\beta})
for some
C_{\sigma}>0
, and thus it is sufficient to restrict to
\sigma=1
. Using
(\beta_{2})
,

c_{\beta}K^{-1}(\bar{\beta})\geq-\frac{\bar{\beta}^{\prime}}{\bar{\beta}}.

(7.27)

If
\bar{\beta}
is bounded from below by a positive constant, then
K^{-1}(\bar{\beta})
is not infinitesimal and clearly
(K\beta)
is met. Otherwise, from
\bar{\beta}^{\prime}\leq 0
we get
\bar{\beta}(r)\rightarrow 0
as
r\rightarrow\infty
, and integrating (
7.27
) on
[r_{1},r)
we deduce

c_{\beta}\int_{r_{1}}^{r}K^{-1}(\bar{\beta})\geq\log\bar{\beta}(r_{1})-\log\bar{\beta}(r)\rightarrow+\infty\quad\text{as }\,r\rightarrow\infty,

(7.28)

as claimed.
∎

Proof of Proposition
7.8
.

Let
\sigma,\lambda\in(0,\eta_{0})
to be specified later. Because of
(K\beta)
in Lemma
7.11
and (

KO
0

), there exists
R_{\sigma}=R_{\sigma}(\sigma,\lambda,R)>R
such that

\int_{0}^{\lambda}\frac{\mathrm{d}s}{K^{-1}(F(s))}=\int_{R}^{R_{\sigma}}K^{-1}\big(\sigma\bar{\beta}(s)\big)\mathrm{d}s.

(7.29)

We define implicitly a function
\alpha
via the identity

\int_{0}^{\alpha(t)}\frac{\mathrm{d}s}{K^{-1}(F(s))}=\int_{R_{\sigma}-t}^{R_{\sigma}}K^{-1}\big(\sigma\bar{\beta}(s)\big)\mathrm{d}s.

(7.30)

Clearly,
\alpha(0)=0
,
\alpha(R_{\sigma}-R)=\lambda
,
\alpha>0
on
(0,R_{\sigma}-R)
and, differentiating,

\begin{array}[]{l}\displaystyle\alpha^{\prime}(t)=K^{-1}\big(F(\alpha(t)\big)K^{-1}\big(\sigma\tilde{\beta}(t)\big)>0\quad\text{on }\,(0,R_{\sigma}-R),\\[8.5359pt]
\text{where}\quad\tilde{\beta}(t)=\bar{\beta}(R_{\sigma}-t),\qquad\text{and}\qquad\alpha^{\prime}(0)=0.\end{array}

(7.31)

Note that
\tilde{\beta}
is non-decreasing by
(\beta_{1})
. By construction,
\alpha\in[0,\lambda]\subset[0,\eta_{0})
, and

\begin{array}[]{lcl}0<\displaystyle\alpha^{\prime}(s)&\leq&\displaystyle K^{-1}\big(F(\alpha)\big)K^{-1}\big(\sigma\|\bar{\beta}\|_{\infty}\big)\\[5.69046pt]
&\leq&\displaystyle K^{-1}\big(F(\lambda)\big)K^{-1}\big(\|\bar{\beta}\|_{\infty}\big)\qquad\text{on }\,(0,R_{\sigma}-R).\end{array}

(7.32)

We can therefore reduce
\sigma
and
\lambda
, independently, in such a way that

\quad K^{-1}(F(\alpha))\leq\min\{1,\xi_{0}\},\qquad K^{-1}\big(\sigma\tilde{\beta}\big)\leq 1

(7.33)

on
[0,R_{\sigma})
. For convenience, we define

\rho=K^{-1}\big(F(\alpha(t)\big),\qquad\tau=K^{-1}\big(\sigma\tilde{\beta}(t)\big)

applying
K
to (
7.31
) and differentiating, we obtain

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\displaystyle\big(K(\alpha^{\prime})\big)^{\prime}&=&\displaystyle K^{\prime}(\rho\tau)[\rho^{\prime}\tau+\rho\tau^{\prime}]\mathrel{{\mathop{\leq}\limits}}k_{2}K^{\prime}(\rho)K^{\prime}(\tau)\left[\frac{f(\alpha)\alpha^{\prime}\tau}{K^{\prime}(\rho)}+\frac{\rho\sigma\tilde{\beta}^{\prime}}{K^{\prime}(\tau)}\right]\\[11.38092pt]
&\mathrel{{\mathop{\leq}\limits}}&k_{2}k_{1}\Big[f(\alpha)\alpha^{\prime}K(\tau)+\sigma\tilde{\beta}^{\prime}K(\rho)\Big]\\[11.38092pt]
&=&\displaystyle k_{2}k_{1}\Big[f(\alpha)\alpha^{\prime}\sigma\tilde{\beta}+\sigma\tilde{\beta}^{\prime}F(\alpha)\Big]\end{array}

(7.34)

However, by
(C_{4})
and
(\beta_{2})
, together with
(K_{2})
in Lemma
7.11
applied twice,

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\displaystyle F(\alpha)&\leq&\displaystyle c_{F}K^{-1}\big(F(\alpha)\big)f(\alpha),\\[8.5359pt]
\displaystyle\tilde{\beta}^{\prime}&\leq&\displaystyle c_{\beta}K^{-1}\big(\tilde{\beta}\big)\tilde{\beta}\mathrel{{\mathop{\leq}\limits}}\displaystyle\frac{\bar{c}_{\beta}}{K^{-1}(\sigma)}K^{-1}\big(\sigma\tilde{\beta}\big)\tilde{\beta}\end{array}

(7.35)

for some
\bar{c}_{\beta}
depending on

. Inserting into (
7.34
) and recalling (
7.31
) we get

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\displaystyle\big(K(\alpha^{\prime})\big)^{\prime}&\leq&\displaystyle k_{2}k_{1}\sigma\Big[f(\alpha)\alpha^{\prime}\tilde{\beta}+\frac{c_{F}\bar{c}_{\beta}}{K^{-1}(\sigma)}\tilde{\beta}K^{-1}(F(\alpha))K^{-1}(\sigma\tilde{\beta})f(\alpha)\Big]\\[11.38092pt]
&=&\displaystyle\displaystyle k_{2}k_{1}\sigma\tilde{\beta}f(\alpha)\alpha^{\prime}\Big[1+\frac{c_{F}\bar{c}_{\beta}}{K^{-1}(\sigma)}\Big]\leq\frac{C_{1}\sigma}{K^{-1}(\sigma)}\tilde{\beta}f(\alpha)\alpha^{\prime},\\[11.38092pt]
\end{array}

(7.36)

for some constant
C_{1}
depending on
k_{1},k_{2},\bar{c}_{\beta},c_{F}
. Using the definition of
K^{\prime}
and
\alpha^{\prime}>0
we can simplify (
7.36
) to deduce

\varphi^{\prime}(\alpha^{\prime})\alpha^{\prime\prime}\leq\frac{C_{1}\sigma}{K^{-1}(\sigma)}\tilde{\beta}f(\alpha)l(\alpha^{\prime}).

(7.37)

Moreover, observe that the first in (
7.34
) and the definition of
\rho,\tau
imply
(K(\alpha^{\prime}))^{\prime}\geq 0
and hence, expanding,
\alpha^{\prime\prime}\geq 0
.

We now integrate (
7.37
) on
[0,t)
and use
\alpha^{\prime}(0)=0
,
\varphi(0)=0
to obtain

\varphi\big(\alpha^{\prime}(t)\big)\leq\frac{C_{1}\sigma}{K^{-1}(\sigma)}\int_{0}^{t}\tilde{\beta}f(\alpha)l(\alpha^{\prime}).

(7.38)

Apply the third in (
7.5
) (note that
\alpha^{\prime\prime}\geq 0
) we get

\varphi\big(\alpha^{\prime}(t)\big)\leq\frac{C_{2}\sigma}{K^{-1}(\sigma)}f\big(\alpha(t)\big)l\big(\alpha^{\prime}(t)\big)\int_{0}^{t}\tilde{\beta}

(7.39)

for some constant
C_{2}>0
. Next, we exploit (
7.29
) to estimate from above the integral in (

). To this end, we use
(C_{3})
,
\tilde{\beta}^{\prime}\geq 0
together with the second in (

) to get

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\tilde{\beta}&=&\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\frac{\tilde{\beta}}{K^{-1}(\tilde{\beta})}K^{-1}(\tilde{\beta})\mathrel{{\mathop{\leq}\limits}}\bar{C}\frac{\tilde{\beta}(t)}{K^{-1}(\tilde{\beta}(t))}\int_{0}^{t}K^{-1}(\tilde{\beta})\\[11.38092pt]
&\mathrel{{\mathop{\leq}\limits}}&\displaystyle\frac{\bar{C}\bar{c}_{\beta}}{K^{-1}(\sigma)}\frac{\tilde{\beta}(t)}{K^{-1}(\tilde{\beta}(t))}\int_{R}^{R_{\sigma}}K^{-1}(\sigma\tilde{\beta})\\[11.38092pt]
&=&\displaystyle\frac{C_{3}}{K^{-1}(\sigma)}\frac{\tilde{\beta}(t)}{K^{-1}(\tilde{\beta}(t))}\int_{0}^{\lambda}\frac{\mathrm{d}s}{K^{-1}(F(s))}.\end{array}

(7.40)

Define
\tilde{\theta}(t)=\theta(R_{\sigma}-t)
. Plugging together (

), (

) and (

), because of (

) we obtain

\begin{array}[]{l}\displaystyle\big(\varphi\big(\alpha^{\prime}\big)\big)^{\prime}+\tilde{\theta}(t)\varphi\big(\alpha^{\prime}\big)\\[8.5359pt]
\displaystyle\quad\leq\ \ \beta(R_{\sigma}-t)f\big(\alpha\big)l\big(\alpha^{\prime}\big)\left[\frac{\tilde{\beta}(t)}{\beta(R_{\sigma}-t)}\frac{C_{1}\sigma}{K^{-1}(\sigma)}\right.\\[14.22636pt]
\displaystyle\qquad+\left.\frac{C_{4}\sigma}{[K^{-1}(\sigma)]^{2}}\frac{\tilde{\theta}(t)\tilde{\beta}(t)}{\beta(R_{\sigma}-t)K^{-1}(\tilde{\beta}(t))}\int_{0}^{\lambda}\frac{\mathrm{d}s}{K^{-1}(F(s))}\right]\\[14.22636pt]
\displaystyle\quad\leq\ \ \beta(t)f\big(\alpha\big)l\big(\alpha^{\prime}\big)\left[\left\|\frac{\bar{\beta}}{\beta}\right\|_{\infty}\frac{C_{1}\sigma}{K^{-1}(\sigma)}+\frac{C_{4}\sigma}{[K^{-1}(\sigma)]^{2}}\left\|\frac{\theta\bar{\beta}}{\beta K^{-1}(\bar{\beta})}\right\|_{\infty}\int_{0}^{\lambda}\frac{\mathrm{d}s}{K^{-1}(F(s))}\right]\\[14.22636pt]
\end{array}

(7.41)

Next, using the second in
(C_{3})
we can choose
\sigma
sufficiently small to make the first term in square brackets smaller than
\varepsilon/2
. We can then choose
\lambda>0
small enough to make the second term smaller than
\varepsilon/2
. Eventually, define

w(r)=\alpha(R_{\sigma}-r)\qquad\text{and}\qquad R_{1}=R_{\sigma}.

(7.42)

Because of (
7.32
)

|w^{\prime}|\leq K^{-1}\big(F(\lambda)\big)K^{-1}\big(\|\bar{\beta}\|_{\infty}\big)

Up to reducing
\lambda
further,
|w^{\prime}|\leq\varepsilon
on
[R,R_{1})
(with

as in the statement of the Proposition). Using the definition of

and the fact that

, it is immediate to check that

satisfies all the properties listed in (
