).
∎

Remark 7.12
.

A crucial feature of the above construction is that
\lambda
is independent of
R\geq r_{0}
. This will allow to construct compactly supported supersolutions attaining value
\lambda
on the boundary of any fixed geodesic sphere
\partial B_{R}(\cal O)
with
R\geq r_{0}
.

With this preparation, we can now prove our first main result for the compact support principle. We recall that a pole
\mathcal{O}\subset M
is a smooth, relatively compact open set such that the normal exponential map realizes a diffeomorphism between
M\backslash\cal O
and
\partial\cal O\times\mathbb{R}^{+}
. Let
\operatorname{II}_{-\nabla r}
be the second fundamental form of
\partial\cal O
in the direction pointing towards
\mathcal{O}
.

Theorem 7.13
.

Let
(M,\langle\,,\,\rangle)
be a manifold possessing a pole
\mathcal{O}
, and let
r(x)=\mathrm{dist}(x,\mathcal{O})
. Suppose that

\begin{array}[]{l}\displaystyle K_{\mathrm{rad}}\leq-\kappa^{2}(1+r)^{\alpha}\qquad\text{on }\,M\backslash\mathcal{O},\quad\text{for some }\,\kappa\geq 0,\ \alpha\geq-2;\\[8.5359pt]
\operatorname{II}_{-\nabla r}\geq\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}-\kappa\langle\,,\,\rangle&\quad\text{if }\,\alpha\geq 0\ \text{or }\ \kappa=0,\\[5.69046pt]
-\left[\frac{\alpha+\sqrt{\alpha^{2}+16\kappa^{2}}}{4}\right]\langle\,,\,\rangle&\quad\text{otherwise},\end{array}\right.\end{array}

(7.43)

where
\operatorname{II}_{-\nabla r}
denotes the second fundamental form of
\partial\mathcal{O}
in the inward-pointing direction. Fix
0<b\in C(M)
, and let
0<\beta\in C([r_{0},\infty))
such that

b(x)\geq\beta\big(r(x)\big)\qquad\text{ for }\,r(x)\geq r_{0}.

Let
\varphi,f,l
satisfy (
7.2
), (
7.3
), (
7.5
) and
(C_{1}),\ldots,(C_{4})
. Assume that, for some
\bar{\beta}
matching
(\beta_{1})
and
(\beta_{2})
, it holds

\max\left\{\frac{\bar{\beta}(s)}{\beta(s)},\ \frac{s^{\alpha/2}\bar{\beta}(s)}{\beta(s)K^{-1}(\bar{\beta}(s))}\right\}\in L^{\infty}\big([r_{0},\infty)\big).

(7.44)

Then,

\eqref{KO_zero}\qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad\text{$(\mathrm{CSP})$ holds for \eqref{csp_problem}.}

If moreover

l\in\mathrm{Lip}_{\mathrm{loc}}\big((0,\xi_{0})\big),\qquad f(0)l(0)=0,

then

\eqref{KO_zero}\qquad\Longleftrightarrow\qquad\text{$(\mathrm{CSP})$ holds for \eqref{csp_problem}.}

Proof.

We first prove implication
\eqref{KO_zero}\Rightarrow(\mathrm{CSP})
. Note that it is enough to consider solutions of (
7.1
) when
\Omega=\Omega_{r_{0}}
is a connected component of
M\backslash B_{r_{0}}(\cal O)
. Let now
u
be a
C^{1}
solution of (
7.1
) on
\Omega_{r_{0}}
. By Proposition
9.8
applied with, respectively,

\begin{array}[]{l}\displaystyle G(r)=\kappa^{2}(1+r)^{\alpha},\qquad\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{lll}\theta_{*}=0,&\quad D=D_{-}(\theta_{*})=-1&\quad\text{if }\,\alpha\geq 0;\\[5.69046pt]
\theta_{*}=\frac{\alpha}{2\kappa},&\quad D=D_{-}(\theta_{*})=-\frac{\alpha+\sqrt{\alpha^{2}+16\kappa^{2}}}{4\kappa}&\quad\text{if }\,\alpha\in[-2,0)\end{array}\right.\\[11.38092pt]
C=1,\qquad\lambda=D\kappa\end{array}

we deduce that

g(t)=\exp\left\{D\int_{0}^{t}\kappa(1+s)^{\alpha/2}\mathrm{d}s\right\}

is a positive solution on
\mathbb{R}^{+}
of
g^{\prime\prime}-Gg\leq 0
,
g(0)=1
,
g^{\prime}(0)=D\kappa
, and thus by the Laplacian comparison theorem from below (cf. Appendix A)

\Delta r\geq(m-1)\frac{g^{\prime}(r)}{g(r)}=(m-1)D\kappa(1+r)^{\alpha/2}\qquad\text{for }\,r>0.

(7.45)

Because of (
7.44
), we can apply Proposition
7.8
with

\theta(t)=(m-1)|D|\kappa(1+r)^{\alpha/2},\qquad\varepsilon=1/2C

(
C
the increasing constant in the third of (
7.5
)) to infer the existence of
\lambda
sufficiently small such that, for each chosen
R\geq r_{0}
, there exists a solution
w
of

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}w\in C^{1}([R,\infty))\quad\text{and $C^{2}$ except possibly at some $R_{1}>R$;}\\[5.69046pt]
w\geq 0,\quad\text{on }\,[R,\infty),\qquad w\equiv 0\quad\text{on }\,[R_{1},\infty),\\[8.5359pt]
w(R)=\lambda,\qquad w^{\prime}<0\quad\text{on }\,[R,R_{1}),\\[5.69046pt]
\displaystyle\big(\varphi(w^{\prime})\big)^{\prime}-\theta(r)\varphi(w^{\prime})\leq\frac{1}{2C}\beta(r)f(w)l(|w^{\prime}|)\qquad\text{on }\,[R,\infty).\end{array}\right.

(7.46)

We then specify
R\geq r_{0}
large enough to satisfy

u(x)<\lambda\qquad\text{for }\,r(x)\geq R.

(7.47)

Defining
\bar{w}(x)=w(r(x))
and using that
\cal O
is a pole, we obtain

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\bar{w}\in C^{1}(M\backslash B_{R}(\cal O));\\[5.69046pt]
\bar{w}\geq 0,\qquad\bar{w}\equiv 0\quad\text{on }\,M\backslash B_{R_{1}}(\cal O),\\[8.5359pt]
\bar{w}=\lambda\quad\text{on }\,\partial B_{R}(\mathcal{O}),\qquad|\nabla\bar{w}|>0\quad\text{on }\,B_{R_{1}}(\mathcal{O})\backslash B_{R}(\mathcal{O})\end{array}\right.

(7.48)

and also, since
w^{\prime}\leq 0
and
\varphi
is odd, by (
7.45
)

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\displaystyle\Delta_{\varphi}\bar{w}&=&\displaystyle\big(\varphi(w^{\prime})\big)^{\prime}+\varphi(w^{\prime})\Delta r\leq\big(\varphi(w^{\prime})\big)^{\prime}-\theta(r)\varphi(w^{\prime})\leq\displaystyle\frac{1}{2C}\beta(r)f(w)l(|w^{\prime}|)\\[8.5359pt]
&=&\displaystyle\displaystyle\frac{1}{2C}\beta(r)f(\bar{w})l(|\nabla\bar{w}|)\qquad\text{weakly on }\,M\backslash B_{R}(\cal O).\end{array}

(7.49)

Define
\Omega_{R}=\Omega\cap(M\backslash B_{R}(\cal O))
. By assumption,
u<\lambda=\bar{w}
on
\partial\Omega_{R}
, and we are going to show that
u\leq\bar{w}
on
\Omega_{R}
. Once this is shown, then clearly
u
has compact support since
\bar{w}
does, concluding our proof. We reason by contradiction and we suppose that
c=\sup_{\Omega_{R}}(u-\bar{w})>0
. For
\delta\in(0,c)
, set
U_{\delta}=\{u-\bar{w}>\delta\}\neq\emptyset
. Note that
U_{\delta}\cap\partial\Omega_{R}=\emptyset
, and moreover
U_{\delta}
is relatively compact since
v
vanishes at infinity and
\bar{w}\geq 0
. On the compact set
\Gamma=\{u-\bar{w}=c\}
identity
|\nabla\bar{w}|=|\nabla u|
holds. We claim that
\inf_{\Gamma}|\nabla\bar{w}|>0
. Suppose, by contradiction, that
|\nabla\bar{w}(x)|=0
for some
x\in\Gamma
. By the construction of
\bar{w}
,
x\in M\backslash B_{R_{1}}(\cal O)
and we examine two cases.

(i)

x\in M\backslash\overline{B_{R_{1}}(\cal O)}
. In this case,
\bar{w}\equiv 0
in a small neighbourhood
V
of
x
, and thus, by the definition of
\Gamma
,
x
is a local maximum of
u
on
V
. Since

\Delta_{\varphi}u\geq b(x)f(u)l(|\nabla u|)\geq 0,

applying the finite maximum principle in Theorem
4.8
to
c-u
we deduce that
u\equiv c
on
V
. Therefore, the set where
u=c
is open, closed and non-empty in
\Omega_{R}\backslash B_{R_{1}}(\cal O)
, and we conclude
u\equiv c
on the connected component of
\Omega_{R}\backslash\overline{B_{R_{1}}(\cal O)}
containing
x
. Note then that
\Omega_{R}\backslash\overline{B_{R_{1}}(\cal O)}
is connected and unbounded: in fact, since
\cal O
is a pole of
M
the normal exponential map realizes a diffeomorphism between
M\backslash\cal O
and
\partial\cal O\times\mathbb{R}^{+}
. In particular,
\Omega
is diffeomorphic to
K\times(r_{0},\infty)
for some connected component
K\subset\partial\cal O
, and
\Omega_{R}\backslash\overline{B_{R_{1}}(\cal O)}
is diffeomorphic to the connected set
K\times(R_{1},\infty)
. Concluding,
u\equiv c
on
\Omega_{R}\backslash\overline{B_{R_{1}}(\cal O)}
, a contradiction since
u
is assumed to vanish at infinity.

(ii)

x\in\partial B_{R_{1}}(\mathcal{O})
. In this case,
\nabla u(x)=\nabla\bar{w}(x)=0
and
u
on
\Omega_{R}\backslash B_{R_{1}}(\cal O)
has a boundary, global maximum at
x
. Moreover, by
(i)
the set
\Gamma
does not intersect
\Omega_{R}\backslash\overline{B_{R_{1}}(\cal O)}
, hence
u<c
on
\Omega_{R}\backslash\overline{B_{R_{1}}(\cal O)}
. Let
\gamma(t)
be a ray from
\cal O
with
\gamma(R_{1})=x
. As in the proof of the finite maximum principle and the Hopf Lemma, (Thm.
4.8
), on a small enough annulus
E_{\rho}=B_{2\rho}\backslash B_{\rho}
centered at
x_{0}=\gamma(R_{1}+2\rho)
we can construct a solution of

\begin{array}[]{l}\Delta_{\varphi}v\geq 0,\qquad v=0\ \text{ on }\,\partial B_{2\rho},\quad v=\eta<c-\max_{\partial B_{\rho}}u\quad\text{on }\,\partial B_{\rho}\\[8.5359pt]
\langle\nabla v,\nabla r_{x_{0}}\rangle<0\quad\text{on }\,\partial B_{2\rho},\qquad|\nabla v|>0\quad\text{on }\,\overline{E}_{\rho}.\end{array}

(7.50)

where
r_{x_{0}}
is the distance from
x_{0}
. With the aid of Proposition
4.1
, we can compare
u
with
c-v
on
E_{\rho}\subset\Omega\backslash B_{R_{1}}(\cal O)
since

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\Delta_{\varphi}u\geq b(x)f(u)l(|\nabla u|)\geq 0\geq\Delta_{\varphi}(c-v),\\[8.5359pt]
u-(c-v)=u-c\leq 0\quad\text{on }\,\partial B_{2\rho},\\[5.69046pt]
u-(c-v)\leq\max_{\partial B_{\rho}}u-c+\eta<0\quad\text{on }\,\partial B_{\rho}\end{array}\right.

to deduce
u\leq c-v
on
E_{\rho}
. Since equality holds at
x\in\partial E_{\rho}
, we get

0\leq\langle\nabla(u-c+v),\nabla r_{x_{0}}\rangle(x)=\langle\nabla v,\nabla r_{x_{0}}\rangle(x)<0,

contradiction.

We have therefore shown that
|\nabla\bar{w}|=|\nabla u|>0
on
\Gamma
, and we are in the position to conclude as usual: from
l>0
on
\mathbb{R}^{+}
the quotient
l(|\nabla\bar{w}|)/l(|\nabla u|)
is continuous and
\leq 2
on
U_{\delta}
, for
\delta
sufficiently close to
c
. By the
C
-increasing property of
f
,

f\big(\bar{w}(x)\big)l\big(|\nabla\bar{w}(x)|\big)\leq 2Cf\big(u(x)\big)l\big(|\nabla u(x)|\big)\qquad\forall\,x\in U_{\delta},

and thus

\Delta_{\varphi}\bar{w}\leq\frac{1}{2C}b(x)f(\bar{w})l(|\nabla\bar{w}|)\leq b(x)f(u)l(|\nabla u|)\leq\Delta_{\varphi}u

on
U_{\delta}
, with
u=\bar{w}+\delta
on
\partial U_{\delta}
. By the comparison Proposition
4.1
,
u\leq\bar{w}+\delta
on
U_{\delta}
, contradicting the very definition of
U_{\delta}
and concluding the proof.

The reverse implication
(\mathrm{CSP})\Rightarrow\eqref{KO_zero}
, under the further assumptions
l\in\mathrm{Lip}_{\mathrm{loc}}\big((0,\xi_{0})\big)
and
f(0)l(0)=0
, is a direct consequence of Theorem
7.3
.
∎

We next specialize Theorem
7.13
, and we prove Theorem
1.38
, that we rewrite for the reader’s convenience.

Theorem 7.14
.

Let
(M,\langle\,,\,\rangle)
be a manifold with a pole
\mathcal{O}
such that, setting
r(x)=\mathrm{dist}(x,\mathcal{O})
, (
7.43
) holds for some
\alpha\geq-2
,
\kappa\geq 0
. Consider
\varphi,b,f,l
satisfying (
1.3
), (
1.5
), (
1.29
) and (
1.67
). Fix
\chi,\mu\in\mathbb{R}
with

\chi>0,\qquad\mu\leq\chi-\frac{\alpha}{2}

(7.51)

and assume that

\begin{array}[]{ll}l(t)\asymp t^{1-\chi}\varphi^{\prime}(t)&\quad\text{for }\,t\in(0,1),\\[5.69046pt]
b(x)\geq C_{1}\big(1+r(x)\big)^{-\mu}&\quad\text{for }\,r(x)\geq r_{0},\end{array}

(7.52)

for some constant
C_{1}>0
. If there exists a constant
c_{F}\geq 1
such that

F(t)^{\frac{\chi}{\chi+1}}\leq c_{F}f(t)\qquad\text{for each }\,t\in(0,\eta_{0}),

(7.53)

then,

\text{$(\mathrm{CSP})$ holds for $(P_{\geq})$}\qquad\Longleftrightarrow\qquad\eqref{KO_zero}.

(7.54)

Proof.

First, we note that requirements (
1.3
), (
1.5
), (
1.29
) and (
1.67
) on
\varphi,f,l
correspond to (
7.2
), (
7.3
), (
7.5
) and

f>0\ \text{ on }\,(0,\eta_{0}),\qquad l\in\mathrm{Lip}_{\mathrm{loc}}((0,\xi_{0})),\qquad f(0)l(0)=0.

We can therefore apply Theorem
7.3
to deduce the validity of implication
(\mathrm{CSP})\Rightarrow\eqref{KO_zero}
.

Viceversa, assume (
KO0
), that in view of (
7.52
) is equivalent to

F^{-\frac{1}{\chi+1}}\in L^{1}(0^{+}).

(7.55)

We first observe that it is enough to prove
(\mathrm{CSP})
when

\begin{array}[]{ll}\displaystyle l(t)=C_{2}t^{1-\chi}\varphi^{\prime}(t)&\quad\text{if }\,t\in(0,1),\\[5.69046pt]
b(x)=C_{1}\big(1+r(x)\big)^{-\mu}&\quad\text{if }\,r(x)\geq r_{0},\end{array}

for constants
C_{1},C_{2}>0
. Indeed, the Keller-Osserman condition for
l(t)=C_{2}t^{1-\chi}\varphi^{\prime}(t)
is still (
7.55
). As underlined in Remark
7.9
, although
t^{1-\chi}\varphi^{\prime}(t)\in L^{\infty}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{+}_{0})
might fail to be continuous at
t=0
we can still apply Proposition
7.8
, and thus Theorem
7.13
, once we check the validity of the remaining assumptions:
\chi>0
imply both (
7.3
) and
(C_{3})
, (
7.51
) imply
(C_{1}),(C_{2})
, and (
7.53
) is equivalent to
(C_{4})
. On the other hand, the function

\bar{\beta}(t)=c(1+t)^{-\chi-1},\qquad\text{with }\quad c<K_{\infty}

satisfies
(\beta_{1})
and
(\beta_{2})
. To conclude, note that (
7.44
) is equivalent to

\mu\leq\max\left\{\chi+1,\chi-\frac{\alpha}{2}\right\}=\chi-\frac{\alpha}{2}.

∎

Remark 7.15
.

In the hypotheses of Theorem
7.14
, set
v(r)=\mathrm{vol}(\partial B_{r}(\cal O))
. Using the divergence theorem and coarea formula we deduce

v^{\prime}(r)=\int_{\partial B_{r}(\cal O)}\Delta r\geq-C_{1}r^{\chi-\mu}v(r)

where we used (
7.45
) with
\alpha/2=\chi-\mu
. A further integration gives

\begin{array}[]{ll}\text{if }\,\mu<\chi+1,&\qquad v(r)\geq C_{1}e^{-C_{2}r^{\chi+1-\mu}}\\[5.69046pt]
\text{if }\,\mu=\chi+1,&\qquad v(r)\geq C_{1}r^{-C_{2}}.\end{array}

for some constants
C_{1},C_{2}>1
. Assume that
\mathrm{vol}(M)<\infty
. Integrating the above on
(r,\infty)
, taking logarithms and recalling that

\mathrm{vol}(M)-\mathrm{vol}(B_{r}(\mathcal{O}))=\int_{r}^{\infty}v(s)\mathrm{d}s

we deduce that

\begin{array}[]{ll}\text{if }\,\mu<\chi+1,&\displaystyle\qquad\limsup_{r\rightarrow\infty}\frac{-\log\big(\mathrm{vol}(M)-\mathrm{vol}(B_{r}(\cal O))\big)}{r^{1+\chi-\mu}}<\infty;\\[14.22636pt]
\text{if }\,\mu=\chi+1,&\displaystyle\qquad\limsup_{r\rightarrow\infty}\frac{-\log\big(\mathrm{vol}(M)-\mathrm{vol}(B_{r}(\cal O))\big)}{\log r}<\infty.\end{array}

(7.56)

It is interesting to compare (
7.56
) with conditions (
5.6
). In view of Theorem
5.4
, one might wonder whether the
(\mathrm{CSP})
could be proved under a volume growth assumption like (
7.56
). Indeed, the problem seems to be quite hard, and one of the main reasons lies in the fact that a manifold satisfies
(\mathrm{CSP})
if and only if each of its ends does. This forces (
7.56
) to be satisfied on
each
end, otherwise an end with big volume would be sufficient for (
7.56
) to hold independently of the behaviour of the others. However, an approach via integral estimates like in Theorem
5.4
, loosely speaking, seems unable to distinguish among different ends, and thus, at least, it needs to be complemented by new techniques.

Remark 7.16
.

Both
(\mathrm{FMP})
and
(\mathrm{CSP})
can be considered for more general inequalities, including the prototype ones

\Delta_{p}u=u^{\omega}\pm|\nabla u|^{q}

for some
\omega,q>0
. In fact, the Keller-Osserman condition changes according to whether
q\geq p-1
or
q<p-1
, see
[
125
]
and
[
51
]
for a detailed account. In particular, in
[
51
]
the authors propose suitable Keller-Osserman conditions for the case when the terms
u^{\omega}
and
|\nabla u|^{q}
strongly interact. The sharpness of these conditions for general nonlinearities in
u
and
|\nabla u|
is, to our knowledge, still an open problem.

7.2.2
A second ODE lemma: locating the support

Proposition
7.8
guarantees the existence of
R_{1}>R
such that the supersolution
w
in (
7.87
) vanishes outside of
B_{R_{1}}
. However, the proof gives loose indication on the distance between
R_{1}
and
R
. Although this further information is not needed in the results that we present here, nevertheless we feel worth to underline that the construction of
w
can be modified in such a way to locate
R_{1}
, say to have
R_{1}=2R
. More importantly, this new method, that works under a set of assumptions which is skew with respect to that in Proposition
7.8
, allows for weights
b(x)
that may oscillate between two different polynomial type decays. In the sequel, we need

(C_{2})^{\prime}

there exist constants
d_{1}>0
and
c_{1}>0
such that

\begin{array}[]{ll}\varphi^{\prime}(st)\leq d_{1}\varphi^{\prime}(s)\varphi^{\prime}(t)&\quad\text{for each }\,s,t\in(0,1];\\[8.5359pt]
\displaystyle l(s)l(t)\leq c_{1}l(st)&\quad\text{for each }\,s,t\in(0,1];\end{array}

instead of the weaker
(C_{2})
(cf. Lemma
7.34
). On the other hand, we will not need
(C_{3})
. Regarding
\beta
, we assume that
\beta\equiv\bar{\beta}
, that
\beta
vanishes at infinity, and a further condition
(\beta_{3})
, namely we require

(\beta_{1})^{\prime}

0<\beta\in C^{1}([r_{0},\infty))
,
\quad\beta^{\prime}\leq 0
for
t\geq r_{0}
,
\quad\beta(t)\rightarrow 0
as
t\rightarrow\infty
;

(\beta_{2})^{\prime}

there exists a constant
c_{\beta}\geq 1
such that

\frac{-\beta^{\prime}(t)}{K^{-1}(\beta(t))}\leq c_{\beta}\beta(t)\qquad\text{for each }\,t\in[r_{0},\infty).

(\beta_{3})

There exists a constant
\hat{c}_{\beta}>0
such that

\limsup_{t\rightarrow\infty}\frac{-t\beta^{\prime}(t)}{\beta(t)}\geq\hat{c}_{\beta}.

Remark 7.17
.

Up to choosing
r_{0}
large enough, by
(\beta_{1})^{\prime}
we can assume
\beta(t)\in[0,K_{\infty})
, thus
(\beta_{2})^{\prime}
is meaningful.

Example 7.18
.

Referring to Example
7.5
,
\varphi
and
l
satisfy
(C_{1}),(C_{2})^{\prime}
for each
\chi\geq 0
, while
(C_{4})
is met for
\omega\leq\chi
. If further
\beta(t)=(1+t)^{-\mu}
,
(\beta_{1})^{\prime}
and
(\beta_{3})
require
\mu>0
to be both satisfied, and
(\beta_{2})^{\prime}
needs
\mu\leq\chi+1
.

We are ready to state our second main ODE result, to be compared to Proposition
7.8
. Its delicate proof originates from the paper
[
121
]
, later refined in
[
120
]
,
[
132
]
and
[
136
]
, and to help readability we postpone it to the end of this section.

Proposition 7.19
.

Let
\varphi,f,l
satisfy (
7.5
) and (
7.3
), and assume the validity of
(C_{1}),(C_{2})^{\prime},(C_{4})
and
(\beta_{1})^{\prime},(\beta_{2})^{\prime},(\beta_{3})
, for some
r_{0}>0
. Having fixed a non-negative
\theta\in C([r_{0},\infty))
, suppose that

\displaystyle\limsup_{R\rightarrow\infty}K\left(\frac{1}{RK^{-1}(\beta(2R))}\right)R\theta(R)<\infty.

(7.57)

Then, there exists a diverging sequence
\{R_{j}\}
such that the following holds: if

\frac{1}{K^{-1}\circ F}\in L^{1}(0^{+}),

(
\mathrm{KO}_{0}
)

then for each
\epsilon\in(0,\xi_{0})
, there exist
\lambda\in(0,\eta_{0})
and, for each
R\in\{R_{j}\}
, a function
z
with the following properties:

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}z\in C^{1}([R,\infty)),\quad\text{and $C^{2}$ except possibly at $2R$}\\[5.69046pt]
0\leq z\leq\lambda,\qquad z(R)=\lambda,\qquad z\equiv 0\quad\text{on }\,[2R,\infty),\\[5.69046pt]
z^{\prime}<0\quad\text{on }\,[R,2R),\qquad|z^{\prime}|\leq\epsilon\quad\text{on }\,[R,\infty),\\[5.69046pt]
\big(\varphi(z^{\prime})\big)^{\prime}-\theta(t)\varphi(z^{\prime})\leq\epsilon\beta(t)f(z)l(|z^{\prime}|)\qquad{\rm on}\,[R,\infty)\end{array}\right.

(7.58)

Remark 7.20
.

The two
\limsup
in
(\beta_{3})
, (
7.57
) could be simultaneously replaced by
\liminf
. Indeed, the sequence
\{R_{j}\}
is just required to satisfy

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\displaystyle R_{1}\geq 2r_{0}\\[5.69046pt]
\displaystyle\frac{-R_{j}\beta^{\prime}(R_{j})}{\beta(R_{j})}\geq\frac{\hat{c}_{\beta}}{2}\\[11.38092pt]
\displaystyle K\left(\frac{1}{R_{j}K^{-1}(\beta(2R_{j}))}\right)R_{j}\theta(R_{j})\leq B_{2},\end{array}\right.

(7.59)

for some
B_{2}>0
. Observe that, in the “double liminf” case, the vanishing of
\beta
is automatic by integrating
(\beta_{3})
, hence
(\beta_{1})^{\prime}
coincides with
(\beta_{1})
.

As a direct corollary, we have the following result, whose proof follows verbatim that of Theorem
7.13
replacing, in the argument, Proposition
7.8
with Proposition
7.19
.

Theorem 7.21
.

Let
(M,\langle\,,\,\rangle)
be a manifold possessing a pole
\mathcal{O}
, and let
r(x)=\mathrm{dist}(x,\mathcal{O})
. Suppose that (
7.43
) holds, consider
0<b\in C(M)
and let
\beta\in C^{1}([r_{0},\infty))
such that

b(x)\geq\beta\big(r(x)\big)\qquad\text{ for }\,r(x)\geq r_{0}.

Let
\varphi,f,l
satisfy (
7.2
), (
7.3
) and (
7.5
), and assume the validity of
(C_{1}),(C_{2})^{\prime},(C_{4})
and
(\beta_{1})^{\prime},(\beta_{2})^{\prime},(\beta_{3})
. Suppose that

\displaystyle\limsup_{R\rightarrow\infty}K\left(\frac{1}{RK^{-1}(\beta(2R))}\right)R^{1+\frac{\alpha}{2}}<\infty.

Then,

\eqref{KO_zero}\qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad\text{$(\mathrm{CSP})$ holds for \eqref{csp_problem}.}

If moreover

l\in\mathrm{Lip}_{\mathrm{loc}}\big((0,\xi_{0})\big),\qquad f(0)l(0)=0,

then

\eqref{KO_zero}\qquad\Longleftrightarrow\qquad\text{$(\mathrm{CSP})$ holds for \eqref{csp_problem}.}

Specified to power-like
\varphi,f,l
, Theorem
7.21
has the next corollary for general weights
b
, where we used Remark
7.20
.

Corollary 7.22
.

Let
M
be a complete manifold with a pole
\mathcal{O}
and satisfying (
7.43
). Suppose that
\varphi,f,l
satisfy (
7.2
), (
7.3
), (
7.5
) and
l\in\mathrm{Lip}_{\mathrm{loc}}((0,\xi_{0}))
. Moreover, assume that for some
p,\chi,\omega\in\mathbb{R}
satisfying

p>1,\qquad 0<\chi\leq p-1,\qquad\omega>0,

it holds

\varphi^{\prime}(t)\asymp t^{p-2},\qquad l(t)\asymp t^{p-1-\chi},\qquad f(t)\asymp t^{\omega}

in a right neighbourhood of
t=0
. Suppose that there exist
r_{0}>0
and
0<\beta\in C^{1}([r_{0},\infty))
satisfying
(\beta_{1})
and

\begin{array}[]{l}\displaystyle-\beta^{\prime}(t)\leq B\big[\beta(t)\big]^{\frac{\chi+2}{\chi+1}}\qquad\text{on }\,[r_{0},\infty);\\[8.5359pt]
\displaystyle\liminf_{t\to\infty}\frac{-t\beta^{\prime}(t)}{\beta(t)}>0,\qquad\displaystyle\liminf_{t\rightarrow\infty}\frac{t^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-\chi}}{\beta(t)}<\infty,\end{array}

(7.60)

for some constant
B>0
. If
0<b\in C(M)
satisfies

b(x)\geq\beta(r(x))\qquad{\rm for}\ r(x)\geq r_{0},

then

\text{$(\mathrm{CSP})$ holds for \eqref{csp_problem}}\qquad\Longleftrightarrow\qquad\omega<\chi.

Remark 7.23
.

A careful analysis of (
7.60
) shows that the above corollary allows for bounds
\beta
that oscillate between the polynomial decays
t^{-1-\chi}
and
t^{\alpha/2-\chi}
.

7.2.3
Non-empty cut-locus: the
p
-Laplacian case

With the help of Section
2
we now remove the pole condition in the particular case of the
p
-Laplace operator, exploiting the fake distance function
\varrho
. Let
\Omega
be an end of
M
, and we consider a solution
u
of

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\Delta_{p}u\geq b(x)f(u)|\nabla u|^{p-1-\chi}\qquad\text{on }\,\Omega,\\[5.69046pt]
\displaystyle u\geq 0,\qquad\lim_{x\in\Omega,\,x\rightarrow\infty}u(x)=0.\end{array}\right.

(7.61)

Where
0\leq\chi\leq p-1
. Since
\varphi(t)=t^{p-1}
and
l(t)=t^{p-1-\chi}
, the function
K
in (
7.4
) automatically satisfies
(C_{1})
and
(C_{2})^{\prime}
. Eventually, we assume
(C_{4})
, that in the present case can be written as follows:

(C_{4})

there exists
c_{F}\geq 1
such that

c_{F}f(t)\geq F(t)^{\frac{\chi}{\chi+1}}\qquad\text{for }\,t\in[0,\eta_{0}).

Let
(M^{m},\langle\,,\,\rangle)
be a complete Riemannian manifold satisfying,

\mathrm{Ric}(\nabla r,\nabla r)\geq-(m-1)\kappa^{2}\qquad\text{on }\,\mathcal{D}_{o},

(7.62)

for some origin
o
and constant
\kappa>0
. Suppose that
\Delta_{p}
is non-parabolic on
M
, and define the fake distance
\varrho
as in (
2.7
) associated to the hyperbolic space of curvature
-\kappa^{2}
(that is,
g(r)=\kappa^{-1}\sinh(\kappa r)
).
To be able to radialize with respect to
\varrho
, we shall assume

b(x)\geq\beta\big(\varrho(x)\big)\qquad\text{on }\,M,

(7.63)

for some function
\beta
matching the necessary assumptions to apply Proposition
7.8
. In our case of interest, we can restrict to non-increasing
\beta
and to
\bar{\beta}=\beta^{\gamma}
, for some
\gamma\geq 1
. Then,
(\beta_{1})
and
(\beta_{2})
amount to the requests

(\beta_{1})

0<\beta\in C^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{+}_{0})
,
\beta^{\prime}\leq 0
on
\mathbb{R}^{+}
.

(\beta_{2})

For some
\gamma\geq 1
,

-\beta^{\prime}(t)\leq c_{\beta}\beta(t)^{\frac{\chi+1+\gamma}{\chi+1}}\qquad\text{on }\,[1,+\infty).

The prototype example is given by the choice
\beta(t)=(1+t)^{-\mu}
with
\mu\in[0,\chi+1]
.

Remark 7.24
.

Condition (
7.63
) is expressed in terms of level sets of the Green kernel, and it would be desirable to obtain a corresponding decay of
b
in terms of the distance function
r
. However, finding good geometric conditions to estimate
\varrho
from
below
by
r
at infinity is a delicate issue, especially when
\mathrm{Ric}
is negative somewhere. A particular, relevant case is when
\mathrm{Ric}\geq 0
and
M
has maximal volume growth, for which, in case
p=2
, we have that
\rho\asymp r
. A further discussion appears at the end of the section.

Our main result is the following

Theorem 7.25
.

Let
(M^{m},\langle\,,\,\rangle)
be a complete Riemannian manifold such that, for some origin
o
,

\mathrm{Ric}(\nabla r,\nabla r)\geq-(m-1)\kappa^{2}\qquad\text{on }\,\mathcal{D}_{o},

(7.64)

for some constant
\kappa>0
. Let
p\in(1,\infty)
; suppose that
\Delta_{p}
is non-parabolic on
M
and that the minimal positive Green kernel
\mathcal{G}_{p}(x,o)
with pole at
o
satisfies

\mathcal{G}_{p}(x,o)\rightarrow 0\quad\text{as $x$ diverges.}

(7.65)

Let
f
satisfy

f\in C(\mathbb{R}),\qquad f>0\quad\text{and $C$-increasing on }\,(0,\eta_{0})

(7.66)

and, for
\chi\in(0,p-1]
, assume
(C_{4})
. Let
0<b\in C(M)
satisfy (
7.63
), for some
\beta
matching
(\beta_{1}),(\beta_{2})
above. If, either

(i)

\chi=p-1
and
f(0)=0
, or

(ii)

\chi\in(0,p-1)
and
p\in(1,2]
,

then

\text{$(\mathrm{CSP})$ holds for \eqref{CSP_plaplacian}}\qquad\Longleftrightarrow\qquad F^{-\frac{1}{\chi+1}}\in L^{1}(0^{+}).

(7.67)

Remark 7.26
.

In view of Corollary
2.6
, Theorem
2.7
and Examples
2.8
,
2.9
and
2.10
, the vanishing of
\mathcal{G}
is granted provided either one of the next conditions holds:

(i)

p\in(1,\infty)
and
\mathrm{Ric}\geq 0
on
M
;

(ii)

p\in(1,\infty)
and
M
supports the Sobolev inequality (
2.20
);

(iii)

p\in(1,m)
and
M
is minimally immersed into a Cartan-Hadamard manifold;

(iv)

p\in(1,m)
,

\mathrm{Ric}\geq-(m-1)\kappa^{2}\langle\,,\,\rangle,\qquad\inf_{x\in M}\mathrm{vol}\big(B_{1}(x)\big)>0

and
M
supports the Poincaré inequality (
2.27
);

(v)

p\in(1,m)
,
M
is roughly isometric to
\mathbb{R}^{m}
, and

\mathrm{Ric}\geq-(m-1)\kappa^{2}\langle\,,\,\rangle,\qquad\mathrm{inj}(M)>0.

Proof.

Because of (
7.65
), the fake distance
\varrho
defined in (
2.7
) and associated to the hyperbolic space of curvature
-\kappa^{2}
is proper. Furthermore, by (
2.12
) and Proposition
2.3
,

|\nabla\varrho|\leq C_{\varrho},\quad\Delta_{p}\varrho\geq 0\qquad\text{on }\,\{\varrho\geq 1\},

(7.68)

for some constant
C_{\varrho}
. For
s>0
, write
D_{s}
to denote the fake ball
\{\varrho<s\}
, and define
\Omega_{s}=\Omega\cap D_{s}
.

We first prove the implication
\eqref{KO_zero}\Rightarrow(\mathrm{CSP})
. Since (
7.25
) is automatically met for
\theta(t)\equiv 0
, for each fixed
\varepsilon>0
Proposition
7.8
guarantees the existence of
\lambda\in(0,\eta_{0})
sufficiently small and
r_{0}>1
such that, for each
R>r_{0}
, we can find
R_{1}>R
and a solution
w
of

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}w\in C^{1}([R,\infty))\quad\text{and $C^{2}$ except possibly at $R_{1}$;}\\[5.69046pt]
w\geq 0,\quad\text{on }\,[R,\infty),\qquad w(R)=\lambda,\qquad w\equiv 0\quad\text{on }\,[R_{1},\infty),\\[8.5359pt]
w^{\prime}<0\quad\text{on }\,[R,R_{1}),\qquad|w^{\prime}|\leq\varepsilon\quad\text{on }\,[R,\infty),\\[5.69046pt]
\displaystyle\big(|w^{\prime}|^{p-2}w^{\prime})\big)^{\prime}\leq\varepsilon\beta(r)f(w)|w^{\prime}|^{p-1-\chi}\qquad\text{on }\,[R,\infty),\end{array}\right.

(7.69)

Choose
r_{0}
such that
u<\lambda
on
\Omega_{r_{0}}
, and
R>r_{0}
. Since
R_{1}
depends continuously on
\varepsilon,R
, up to changing them a little bit we can assume that
R_{1}
is a regular value of
\varrho
. Therefore,
\partial\Omega_{R_{1}}
is smooth (we recall that the Green kernel
\mathcal{G}_{p}
, being
p
-harmonic outside of
o
, is smooth where
\nabla\mathcal{G}
does not vanish, and therefore so is
\varrho
).
The value of
\varepsilon
will be specified later, depending just on
C_{\varrho}
in (
7.68
) and on the
C
-increasing constant. Define
\bar{w}(x)=w(\varrho(x))
. If we combine (
2.13
),
w^{\prime}\leq 0
and (
2.12
), by (
7.68
) we deduce

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\begin{array}[]{lcl}\Delta_{p}\bar{w}&=&\displaystyle\left[\big(|w^{\prime}|^{p-2}w^{\prime}\big)^{\prime}+\frac{v_{g}^{\prime}}{v_{g}}|w^{\prime}|^{p-2}w^{\prime}\right](\varrho)|\nabla\varrho|^{p}\leq\big(|w^{\prime}|^{p-2}w^{\prime}\big)^{\prime}|\nabla\varrho|^{p}\\[11.38092pt]
&\leq&\displaystyle\varepsilon\beta(\varrho)f(\bar{w})|w^{\prime}(\varrho)|^{p-1-\chi}|\nabla\varrho|^{p}\qquad\text{on }\,M\backslash D_{R},\end{array}\\[22.76228pt]
\bar{w}\in C^{1}(M\backslash D_{R})\quad\text{and is in fact $C^{2}$ except possibly on $\partial D_{R_{1}}$;}\\[5.69046pt]
\bar{w}\geq 0,\quad\text{on }\,M\backslash D_{R},\qquad\bar{w}\equiv 0\quad\text{on }\,M\backslash D_{R_{1}},\\[8.5359pt]
\bar{w}=\lambda\quad\text{on }\,\partial D_{R},\qquad|\nabla\bar{w}|\leq\varepsilon C_{\varrho}\quad\text{on }\,D_{R_{1}}\backslash D_{R}.\\[5.69046pt]
\end{array}\right.

(7.70)

To prove
(\mathrm{CSP})
we show, as before, that
u\leq\bar{w}
on
\Omega_{R}
: we proceed by contradiction, assuming
u>\bar{w}
somewhere, and we look at the set

\Gamma=\big\{u-\bar{w}=c\big\}\Subset\Omega_{R},\qquad\text{with}\quad c=\max_{\Omega_{R}}(u-\bar{w})>0.

We will then apply a comparison theorem on an upper level set

U_{\delta}=\{u-\bar{w}>\delta\}\cap\Omega_{R}

for
\delta
close enough to
c
. To this aim, in Theorem
7.13
we obtained
\Delta_{p}u\geq\Delta_{p}\bar{w}
on
U_{\delta}
, crucially using
|\nabla\bar{w}|>0
on
\Gamma
. However, now
\bar{w}
is radial with respect to the fake distance
\varrho
, which differently from
r
may possess stationary points. This forces us to use a different argument when
\chi<p-1
, that is, in case
(ii)
. On the other hand, in case
(i)
the gradient term disappears and the argument goes straightforwardly: first choose
\varepsilon
so that
\varepsilon CC_{\varrho}^{p}\leq 1
, then observe that
0\leq u,\bar{w}\leq\lambda<\eta_{0}
on
\Omega_{R}
. Therefore, using that
f
is
C
-increasing, on
U_{\delta}
we have

\Delta_{p}u\geq b(x)f(u)\geq\frac{1}{C}\beta(\varrho)f(\bar{w})\geq\frac{1}{C\varepsilon C_{\varrho}^{p}}\varepsilon\beta(\varrho)f(\bar{w})|\nabla\varrho|^{p}\geq\varepsilon\beta(\varrho)f(\bar{w})|\nabla\varrho|^{p}\geq\Delta_{p}\bar{w},

and by comparison we conclude
u\leq\bar{w}+\delta
on
U_{\delta}
, contradiction.

Case
(ii)
is more delicate. Although we cannot guarantee that
|\nabla\bar{w}|>0
on
\Gamma
, nevertheless, as a first step we still claim that

\Gamma\Subset D_{R_{1}}.

(7.71)

Indeed, if by contradiction there exists
x\in\Gamma\cap\mathrm{Int}(\Omega_{R_{1}})
, let
V
be the connected component of
\Omega_{R_{1}}
containing
x
. By the second in (
7.68
) and
R_{1}\geq 1
, we deduce that
V
is necessarily unbounded by the maximum principle, being a component of the upper level set
\{\varrho\geq R_{1}\}
of a
p
-subharmonic function. Arguing as in the proof of Theorem
7.13
, case
(i)
we deduce
u\equiv c
on
V
, which contradicts the vanishing of
u
at infinity. Therefore,
u<c
on
\mathrm{Int}(\Omega_{R_{1}})
, and since
\partial\Omega_{R_{1}}
is smooth we can then apply the boundary maximum principle as in
(ii)
of Theorem
7.13
to get
u<c
also on
\partial\Omega_{R_{1}}
. This proves (
7.71
). As a consequence, from (
7.69
) the inequality
|w^{\prime}(\varrho)|>0
holds on
\Gamma
. Coupling with
|\nabla u|=|\nabla\bar{w}|\leq\varepsilon C_{\varrho}
on
\Gamma
, if
\varepsilon C_{\varrho}<1/2
we can choose
\delta_{0}
close enough to
c
in such a way that

|w^{\prime}(\varrho)|>0,\qquad|\nabla u|+|\nabla\bar{w}|<1\qquad\text{on }\,\overline{U}_{\delta_{0}}.

(7.72)

Note that
\delta_{0}
depends on
\varepsilon
. We come back to the differential inequality for
\bar{w}
, which on
U_{\delta_{0}}
implies

\displaystyle\Delta_{p}\bar{w}\leq\varepsilon\beta(\varrho)f(\bar{w})|w^{\prime}(\varrho)|^{p-1-\chi}|\nabla\varrho|^{p}=\varepsilon\beta(\varrho)|w^{\prime}(\varrho)|^{-1-\chi}f(\bar{w})|\nabla\bar{w}|^{p}.

(7.73)

For
\delta\in(\delta_{0},c)
, we consider the open sets

E_{\delta}=U_{\delta}\cap\big\{|\nabla u|<|w^{\prime}(\varrho)|\big\},\qquad\hat{E}_{\delta}=U_{\delta}\cap\big\{|\nabla u|>|w^{\prime}(\varrho)|/2\big\}.

On
E_{\delta}
, using the
C
-increasing property of
f
and
u>\bar{w}
, whenever
\varepsilon\leq C^{-1}
we deduce that
u
is a weak solution of

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\displaystyle\Delta_{p}u&\geq&\displaystyle\beta(\varrho)f(u)|\nabla u|^{p}|\nabla u|^{-1-\chi}\geq\frac{\beta(\varrho)}{C}f(\bar{w})|\nabla u|^{p}|w^{\prime}(\varrho)|^{-1-\chi}\\[8.5359pt]
&\geq&\displaystyle\varepsilon\beta(\varrho)|w^{\prime}(\varrho)|^{-1-\chi}f(\bar{w})|\nabla u|^{p}.\end{array}

(7.74)

On the other hand, on
\Gamma\cap\hat{E}_{\delta}
we have
|\nabla u|=|\nabla\bar{w}|=|w^{\prime}(\varrho)||\nabla\varrho|
, hence
|\nabla\varrho|>1/2
. By continuity, we can therefore choose
\delta
sufficiently close to
c
so that

\frac{|\nabla u|}{|w^{\prime}(\varrho)|}\leq 2|\nabla\varrho|\leq 2C_{\varrho}\qquad\text{on }\,\hat{E}_{\delta}.

As a consequence, if
\varepsilon\leq[C(2C_{\varrho})^{\chi+1}]^{-1}
, on
\hat{E}_{\delta}
the function
u
weakly solves

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\displaystyle\Delta_{p}u&\geq&\displaystyle\beta(\varrho)f(u)|\nabla u|^{p}|\nabla u|^{-1-\chi}\geq\frac{\beta(\varrho)}{C}f(\bar{w})|\nabla u|^{p}|w^{\prime}(\varrho)|^{-1-\chi}\frac{1}{(2C_{\varrho})^{\chi+1}}\\[8.5359pt]
&\geq&\displaystyle\varepsilon\beta(\varrho)|w^{\prime}(\varrho)|^{-1-\chi}f(\bar{w})|\nabla u|^{p}.\end{array}

(7.75)

Putting together (
7.73
), (
7.74
) and (
7.75
), for
\varepsilon
small enough depending only on
C,C_{\varrho},\chi
and setting
\bar{w}_{\delta}=\bar{w}+\delta
, the following inequalities hold on
U_{\delta}
:

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\displaystyle\Delta_{p}u\geq\displaystyle\Big[\varepsilon\beta(\varrho)|w^{\prime}|^{-1-\chi}f(\bar{w})\Big]|\nabla u|^{p};\\[8.5359pt]
\Delta_{p}\bar{w}_{\delta}\leq\displaystyle\displaystyle\Big[\varepsilon\beta(\varrho)|w^{\prime}|^{-1-\chi}f(\bar{w})\Big]|\nabla\bar{w}_{\delta}|^{p},\\[8.5359pt]
u=\bar{w}_{\delta}\qquad\text{on }\,\partial U_{\delta}.\end{array}\right.

(7.76)

To conclude we observe that, since
p\leq 2
, the
p
-Laplacian is non-degenerate elliptic. We claim that we can apply the comparison Theorem 5.3 in
[
7
]
(the manifold version of
[
123
, Thm. 3.5.1]
) with the choice

B(x,z,\xi)=\varepsilon\beta(\varrho)|w^{\prime}(\varrho)|^{-1-\chi}f(\bar{w})|\xi|^{p}

to deduce
u\leq\bar{w}_{\delta}
on
U_{\delta}
, a contradiction. To ensure the applicability of the above theorem, we shall check that
B
is
regular
, in the sense specified in
[
7
]
: for each compact set
K\Subset\mathbb{R}\times TU_{\delta}
, there exists a constant
L_{K}>0
such that

\big|B(x,z,\xi)-B(x,z,\eta)\big|\leq L_{K}|\xi-\eta|\qquad\forall\,(x,z,\xi),(x,z,\eta)\in K.

Let
A
be such that
|\xi|+|\eta|\leq A
for
(x,z,\xi)\in K
. From

\left||\xi|^{p}-|\eta|^{p}\right|=\left|\int_{|\eta|}^{|\xi|}pt^{p-1}\mathrm{d}t\right|\leq pA^{p-1}\left|\int_{|\eta|}^{|\xi|}\mathrm{d}t\right|=pA^{p-1}\big||\xi|-|\eta|\big|\leq pA^{p-1}|\xi-\eta|,

we obtain

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\displaystyle|B(x,z,\xi)-B(x,z,\eta)|&\leq&\displaystyle\varepsilon\|\beta(\varrho)w^{\prime}(\varrho)^{-1-\chi}f(\bar{w})\|_{\infty}\big||\xi|^{p}-|\eta|^{p}\big|\\[8.5359pt]
&\leq&\bar{C}\varepsilon|\xi-\eta|,\end{array}

for some
\bar{C}>0
, as claimed. This concludes the proof that
u\leq\bar{w}
.

We next show that
(\mathrm{CSP})\Rightarrow\eqref{KO_zero}
. Suppose the failure of (
KO0
). Both cases
(i)
and
(ii)
imply
f(0)l(0)=0
since
l(t)=t^{p-1-\chi}
; thus, having fixed
r_{0}>0
and
\xi\in(0,\xi_{0})
, we can apply Theorem
7.3
to deduce the existence of
\eta
small enough and of a solution
u_{1}\in\mathrm{Lip}(M\backslash B_{r_{0}})
, (
B_{r_{0}}
centered at some fixed origin
o
) of

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\Delta_{p}u_{1}\geq b(x)f(u_{1})|\nabla u_{1}|^{p-1-\chi}\qquad\text{weakly on }\,M\backslash B_{r_{0}},\\[5.69046pt]
0<u_{1}\leq\eta\quad\text{on }\,M\backslash B_{r_{0}},\\[5.69046pt]
u_{1}=\eta\quad\text{on }\,\partial B_{r_{0}},\qquad u_{1}(x)\rightarrow 0\quad\text{as }\,r(x)\rightarrow\infty,\\[5.69046pt]
|\nabla u_{1}|<\xi\qquad\text{on }\,M\backslash B_{r_{0}}.\end{array}\right.

Set
u_{2}(x)=\mathcal{G}_{p}(x,o)
. Up to decreasing
\eta
, we can suppose that
u_{1}\leq u_{2}
on
\partial B_{r_{0}}
, and thus, by comparison (being
\Delta_{p}u_{1}\geq 0
) we deduce
u_{1}\leq u_{2}
on
M\backslash B_{r_{0}}
. Since
u_{2}
trivially solves
(P_{\leq})
, by the subsolution-supersolution method (cf.
[
82
, Thm. 4.4]
) there exists a solution
u
of

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\Delta_{p}u=b(x)f(u)|\nabla u|^{p-1-\chi}&\quad\text{on }\,M\backslash B_{r_{0}}\\[5.69046pt]
u_{1}\leq u\leq u_{2}&\quad\text{on }\,M\backslash B_{r_{0}}\end{array}\right.

The regularity theorem in
[
145
, Thm. 1]
ensures that
u\in C^{1,\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(M\backslash B_{r_{0}})
. Since, by assumption,
u_{2}
vanishes at infinity,
u
shows the failure of
(\mathrm{CSP})
, concluding our proof.
∎

To conclude this section, we specialize to manifolds with non-negative Ricci tensor and we restrict to the linear case
p=2
. If
\mathrm{Ric}\geq 0
, by
[
88
, Thm. 5.2]

M
is non-parabolic if and only if
r/\mathrm{vol}(B_{r})\in L^{1}(\infty)
(that forces
m\geq 3
by Bishop-Gromov comparison), and more precisely the Green kernel
\mathcal{G}_{2}
of
\Delta
satisfies

C^{-1}\int_{2r(x)}^{\infty}\frac{s\,\mathrm{d}s}{\mathrm{vol}(B_{s})}\leq\mathcal{G}_{2}(x,o)\leq C\int_{2r(x)}^{\infty}\frac{s\,\mathrm{d}s}{\mathrm{vol}(B_{s})}\qquad\forall\,x\in M\backslash\{o\},

(7.77)

for some constant
C>0
. Note that, by Theorem
2.5
, the same holds for the
p
-Laplacian but
x
is restricted to lie in
\partial M(r)
.

If we define
\varrho
in (
2.7
) associated to the Euclidean space, from (
7.77
) we readily have

\varrho(x)\asymp h(x)=\left[\int_{2r(x)}^{\infty}\frac{s\,\mathrm{d}s}{\mathrm{vol}(B_{s})}\right]^{\frac{1}{2-m}}\qquad\text{ for }\,r(x)\geq 1.

(7.78)

In particular, if we suppose that
M
has maximal volume growth

\mathrm{vol}(B_{r})\geq cr^{m}\qquad\forall\,r>0,

(7.79)

for some
c>0
, then
\varrho\asymp r
. Condition (
7.63
) can be stated in terms of the more manageable
h(x)
and
r(x)
, giving the following result.

Theorem 7.27
.

Let
(M^{m},\langle\,,\,\rangle)
be a complete Riemannian manifold with
\mathrm{Ric}\geq 0
and dimension
m\geq 3
. Fix an origin
o
, suppose that
r/\mathrm{vol}(B_{r})\in L^{1}(\infty)
and define

h(x)=\left[\int_{2r(x)}^{\infty}\frac{s\,\mathrm{d}s}{\mathrm{vol}(B_{s})}\right]^{\frac{1}{2-m}}.

Fix
\chi\in(0,1]
,
\mu\in[0,\chi+1]
and let
f
satisfy

f\in C(\mathbb{R}),\qquad f\ \text{ is positive and $C$-increasing on }\,(0,\eta_{0}),

for some
\eta_{0}>0
. Assume
(C_{4})
and, if
\chi=1
, assume also that
f(0)=0
. Then,
(\mathrm{CSP})
holds for solutions of

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\Delta u\geq\big(1+h(x)\big)^{-\mu}f(u)|\nabla u|^{1-\chi}\qquad\text{on }\,\Omega\,\text{ end of $M$;}\\[5.69046pt]
\displaystyle u\geq 0,\qquad\lim_{x\in\Omega,\,x\rightarrow\infty}u(x)=0.\end{array}\right.

(7.80)

if and only if

F^{-\frac{1}{\chi+1}}\in L^{1}(0^{+}).

(7.81)

Proof.

Let
\varrho
be the fake distance associated to the Euclidean space model. From (
7.78
),

b(x)=\big(1+h(x)\big)^{-\mu}\geq C\big(1+\varrho(x)\big)^{-\mu},

for some constant
C>0
, and
\varrho
is proper. To apply Theorem
7.25
, we just need to ensure
|\nabla\varrho|\leq C_{\varrho}
(Proposition
2.3
deals only with the case where
\mathrm{Ric}
has a negative lower bound). In the present setting, the sharp bound
|\nabla\varrho|\leq 1
has been shown in
[
32
]
.
∎

The above theorem could be extended to cover the
p
-Laplace operator, simply replacing
\chi\in(0,1]
with
\chi\in(0,p-1]
, provided the validity of the following conditions:

(i)

the inequalities (
2.19
) in Theorem
2.5
hold for each
x\in\partial B_{r}
and not only for
x\in\partial M(r)
. This is, to our knowledge, still an open problem;

(ii)

estimate
|\nabla\varrho|\leq C_{\varrho}
holds when the reference model is the Euclidean space. In
[
94
]
, the authors claim that the stronger inequality
|\nabla\varrho|\leq 1
is true for each
p\in(1,m)
.

7.2.4
A further fake distance and the Feller property

When
l
is constant and
\Delta_{\varphi}
is the Laplace-Beltrami operator, a different fake distance
\varsigma
recently constructed in
[
13
, Thm 2.1]
turns out to be effective to improve Theorem
7.25
:

Theorem 7.28

(
[
13
]
)
.

Let
(M,\langle\,,\,\rangle)
be a complete manifold of dimension
m\geq 2
satisfying

\mathrm{Ric}\geq-(m-1)\kappa^{2}\big(1+r^{2}\big)^{\alpha/2}\langle\,,\,\rangle\qquad\text{on }\,M,

(7.82)

for some
\kappa>0
and
\alpha\in[-2,2]
. Fix an origin
o
with associated distance
r(x)=\mathrm{dist}(x,o)
. Then, there exists constants
C_{1},C_{2}>0
depending on
m,\kappa,\alpha,o
and a function
\varsigma\in C^{\infty}(M)
such that

\begin{array}[]{ll}C_{1}^{-1}\displaystyle\big(1+r(x)\big)^{1+\frac{\alpha}{2}}\leq\varsigma(x)\leq C_{1}\displaystyle\big(1+r(x)\big)^{1+\frac{\alpha}{2}}&\quad\text{if }\,\alpha\in(-2,2],\\[8.5359pt]
C_{1}^{-1}\displaystyle\log\big(2+r(x)\big)\leq\varsigma(x)\leq C_{1}\log\big(2+r(x)\big)&\quad\text{if }\,\alpha=-2,\\[11.38092pt]
\max\Big\{|\nabla\varsigma|^{2},|\Delta\varsigma|\Big\}\leq C_{2}(1+r)^{\alpha}&\quad\text{on }\,M.\end{array}

(7.83)

The proof of the existence of
\varsigma
is delicate and inspired by the one in
[
138
]
, and we refer the reader to both references for details. Here, we show ho to use
\varsigma
to prove the compact support principle for solutions of

\Delta u\geq(1+r)^{-\mu}f(u)

under the only geometric requirement (
7.82
), for each
\alpha\in(-2,2]
and

\mu\leq 1-\frac{\alpha}{2},

(7.84)

provided that (

KO
0

) holds, i.e, if

\frac{1}{\sqrt{F(t)}}\in L^{1}(0^{+}).

(7.85)

The core is to construct the radial compactly supported supersolution
\bar{w}=w(\varsigma)
, for some
w
that we assume to be
C^{2}
, convex and strictly decreasing until it touches zero in a
C^{1}
way. Using (
7.83
) and
w^{\prime}<0
,
w^{\prime\prime}\geq 0
, for
\alpha>-2
we deduce

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\Delta\bar{w}&=&\displaystyle w^{\prime\prime}|\nabla\varsigma|^{2}+w^{\prime}\Delta\varsigma\\[5.69046pt]
&\leq&\displaystyle C_{2}(1+r)^{\alpha}\Big\{w^{\prime\prime}-w^{\prime}\Big\}\leq C_{3}\varsigma^{\alpha\left(1+\frac{\alpha}{2}\right)^{-1}}\Big\{w^{\prime\prime}-w^{\prime}\Big\},\end{array}

(7.86)

for some constant
C_{3}>0
. Now, let
f
satisfying (
7.66
),
f(0)=0
and

c_{F}f(t)\geq\sqrt{F(t)}\qquad\text{for }\,t\in[0,\eta_{0}),

for some constant
c_{F}>0
. We apply Proposition
7.8
with the choices

\varphi(t)=t,\ \ l(t)=1,\ \ \chi=1,\ \ \theta(t)=1,\ \ \beta(t)=t^{-(\mu+\alpha)\left(1+\frac{\alpha}{2}\right)^{-1}},\quad\bar{\beta}(t)=t^{-2}

to deduce the existence of
w
satisfying

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}w\in C^{1}([R,\infty))\quad\text{and $C^{2}$ except possibly at $R_{1}$;}\\[5.69046pt]
0\leq w\leq\lambda,\qquad w(R)=\lambda,\qquad w\equiv 0\quad\text{on }\,[R_{1},\infty),\\[5.69046pt]
w^{\prime}<0\quad\text{on }\,[R,R_{1}),\qquad|w^{\prime}|\leq\varepsilon\quad\text{on }\,[R,\infty),\\[5.69046pt]
w^{\prime\prime}-w^{\prime}\leq\varepsilon t^{-(\mu+\alpha)\left(1+\frac{\alpha}{2}\right)^{-1}}f(w)\qquad\text{on }\,[R,\infty).\end{array}\right.

(7.87)

In fact,
K(t)\asymp t^{2}
and the growth requirement (
7.25
) is equivalent to (
7.84
). Plugging into (
7.88
) and using again (
7.83
) we get

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\Delta\bar{w}&\leq&\displaystyle C_{3}\varepsilon\varsigma^{\alpha\left(1+\frac{\alpha}{2}\right)^{-1}}\varsigma^{-(\mu+\alpha)\left(1+\frac{\alpha}{2}\right)^{-1}}f(w)\\[11.38092pt]
&\leq&\displaystyle C_{4}\varepsilon(1+r)^{-\mu}f(\bar{w}),\end{array}

(7.88)

for some
C_{4}>0
, hence
\bar{w}
is the desired supersolution. The proof of
(\mathrm{CSP})
now proceeds verbatim as in Theorem
7.25
, case
(i)
, leading to the following

Theorem 7.29
.

Let
M
be a complete
m
-dimensional manifold satisfying (
7.82
), for some
\kappa>0
and
\alpha\in(-2,2]
. Let
f
satisfying (
7.66
),
f(0)=0
and

c_{F}f(t)\geq\sqrt{F(t)}\qquad\text{for }\,t\in[0,\eta_{0}),

for some constant
c_{F}>0
. Fix
\mu\leq 1-\frac{\alpha}{2}
. If

\frac{1}{\sqrt{F(t)}}\in L^{1}(0^{+}),

(7.89)

then
(\mathrm{CSP})
holds for solutions of

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\Delta u\geq\big(1+r(x)\big)^{-\mu}f(u)\qquad\text{on }\,\Omega\,\text{ end of $M$;}\\[5.69046pt]
\displaystyle u\geq 0,\qquad\lim_{x\in\Omega,\,x\rightarrow\infty}u(x)=0.\end{array}\right.

(7.90)

Remark 7.30
.

The same method directly applies to the
p
-Laplacian for each
p>1
, provided that the corresponding of Theorem
7.28
hold. This is likely to be the case, but the construction of
\varsigma
may reveal subtleties. In this respect, the gradient estimates in
[
153
]
should be useful.

We conclude this section by commenting on Theorem
7.29
. Analogously to the link between
(\mathrm{SMP}_{\infty})
and (
KO∞
), it seems to us that the function-theoretic property that might describe how geometry relates to
(\mathrm{CSP})
be the so called Feller property:

Definition 7.31
.

We say that the
Feller property
(shortly,
(\mathrm{FE})
) holds if, for every end
\Omega
of
M
and every
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
, the minimal positive solution
8
8

8

Given any fixed exhaustion
\{\Omega_{j}\}
of
\overline{\Omega}
by smooth, relatively compact open sets containing
\partial\Omega
,
h
is obtained as a limit of
h_{j}
solving
\Delta h_{j}=\lambda h_{j}
on
\Omega_{j}
,
h_{j}=1
on
\partial\Omega
and
h_{j}=0
on
\partial\Omega_{j}
. By comparison,
h
is independent of the chosen exhaustion.

h
of

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\Delta h=\lambda h&\quad\text{on }\,\Omega,\\[5.69046pt]
h=1&\quad\text{on }\,\partial\Omega.\end{array}\right.

satisfies
h(x)\rightarrow 0
as
x
diverges in
\Omega
.

Classically, the Feller property is introduced as the
C_{0}
conservation property for the heat flow, that is, the fact that the heat semigroup
P_{t}
preserves the space
C_{0}(M)
of functions on
M
that vanish at infinity:

if
u(x)\rightarrow 0
as
x
diverges, then, for each
t>0
,
(P_{t}u)(x)\rightarrow 0
as
x
diverges.

Its equivalence with Definition
7.31
is shown by R. Azencott, cf.
[
8
]
. Various authors investigated the geometric conditions needed to guarantee the Feller property, notably
[
156
,
44
,
87
,
74
,
41
]
and the recent
[
114
]
. The most general criteria for its validity are, to the best of our knowledge, the following two. For
G\in C(\mathbb{R}^{+}_{0})
, as usual let
g\in C^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+}_{0})
be the solution of Jacobi equation

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}g^{\prime\prime}=Gg=0\qquad\text{on }\,\mathbb{R}^{+}\\[5.69046pt]
g(0)=0,\quad g^{\prime}(0)=1,\end{array}\right.\qquad\text{and set}\qquad v_{g}(r)=\omega_{m-1}g(r)^{m-1}.

(7.91)

Theorem 7.32
.

Let
M
be a complete manifold of dimension
m\geq 2
, fix
o\in M
and let
r(x)=\mathrm{dist}(x,o)
. Then,
M
is Feller provided that one of the following properties holds:

(i)

[
114
, Thms. 3.4 and 5.9]

o
is a pole,

K_{\mathrm{rad}}(x)\leq-G\big(r(x)\big)\qquad\text{on }\,M\backslash\{o\},

for some
G\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{+}_{0})
, and setting
v_{g}
as in (
7.91
),

\text{either}\quad\frac{1}{v_{g}}\in L^{1}(\infty),\qquad\text{or}\quad\frac{1}{v_{g}}\not\in L^{1}(\infty),\ \ \frac{\int^{\infty}_{r}v_{g}}{v_{g}(r)}\not\in L^{1}(\infty),

(7.92)

where the last condition is intended to be trivially satisfied if
v_{g}\not\in L^{1}(\infty)
.

(ii)

[
74
,
75
]
the Ricci curvature satisfies

\mathrm{Ric}\geq-G(r)\langle\,,\,\rangle\qquad\text{on }\,M,

(7.93)

for some
G\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{+}_{0})
matching

G>0,\ \ G^{\prime}\geq 0\ \ \text{ on $\,\mathbb{R}^{+}_{0},\ $ and}\qquad\frac{1}{\sqrt{G}}\not\in L^{1}(\infty).

(7.94)

In view of
[
114
, Thm. 3.4]
,
(i)
and
(ii)
are sharp for the Feller property, and indeed (
7.92
) is both necessary and sufficient for the model manifold
M_{g}
to be Feller. Observe that the inequalities in
(ii)
coincide with those appearing in (
1.35
), (
1.36
) to guarantee the
(\mathrm{SMP}_{\infty})
, and that the limit polynomial threshold for both
(i)
and
(ii)
is
G(r)\asymp 1+r^{2}
, that is,
\alpha=2
. Setting
l\equiv 1
,
b\equiv 1
and restricting to the Laplace-Beltrami operator, cases
(i)
and
(ii)
match, respectively, with the geometric conditions (
7.43
) in Theorem
7.13
and (
7.82
) in Theorem
7.29
. In view of these remarks, we feel interesting to study the following

Problem 5
.

Investigate the validity of the implication

\text{\eqref{KO_0CSP}}\quad+\quad\text{$(\mathrm{FE})$}\quad\Longrightarrow\quad\text{$(\mathrm{CSP})$}

on a (complete) Riemannian manifold, possibly restricting to the inequality

\Delta_{p}u\geq f(u)|\nabla u|^{p-1-\chi}.

Could we obtain, for
(\mathrm{CSP})
, a result analogous to Theorem
8.6
below?

7.2.5
Proof of Proposition
7.19

We report the statement to help readability.

Proposition 7.33
.

Let
\varphi,f,l
satisfy (
7.5
) and (
7.3
), and assume the validity of
(C_{1}),(C_{2})^{\prime},(C_{4})
and
(\beta_{1})^{\prime},(\beta_{2})^{\prime},(\beta_{3})
, for some
r_{0}>0
. Having fixed a non-negative
\theta\in C([r_{0},\infty))
, suppose that

\displaystyle\limsup_{R\rightarrow\infty}K\left(\frac{1}{RK^{-1}(\beta(2R))}\right)R\theta(R)<\infty.

(7.95)

Then, there exists a diverging sequence
\{R_{j}\}
such that the following holds: if

\frac{1}{K^{-1}\circ F}\in L^{1}(0^{+}),

(
\mathrm{KO}_{0}
)

then for each
\epsilon\in(0,\xi_{0})
, there exist
\lambda\in(0,\eta_{0})
and, for each
R\in\{R_{j}\}
, a function
z
with the following properties:

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}z\in C^{1}([R,\infty)),\quad\text{and $C^{2}$ except possibly at $2R$}\\[5.69046pt]
0\leq z\leq\lambda,\qquad z(R)=\lambda,\qquad z\equiv 0\quad\text{on }\,[2R,\infty),\\[5.69046pt]
z^{\prime}<0\quad\text{on }\,[R,2R),\qquad|z^{\prime}|\leq\epsilon\quad\text{on }\,[R,\infty),\\[5.69046pt]
\big(\varphi(z^{\prime})\big)^{\prime}-\theta(t)\varphi(z^{\prime})\leq\epsilon\beta(t)f(z)l(|z^{\prime}|)\qquad{\rm on}\,[R,\infty)\end{array}\right.

(7.96)

We first need the next simple result, whose proof is by direct integration.

Lemma 7.34
.

If
(C_{1})
and
(C_{2})^{\prime}
hold, then
(C_{2})
holds, and also

(K_{4})

\varphi(st)\leq d_{1}K^{\prime}(t)l(t)\varphi(s)=d_{1}t\varphi^{\prime}(t)\varphi(s)\quad
for each
\,s,t\in(0,1]

Proof of Proposition
7.19
.

Because of
(\beta_{3})
and (
7.95
), we can choose a sequence
\{R_{j}\}
satisfying (
7.59
), for some
B_{2}>0
. Let
\lambda\in(0,\eta_{0})
to be specified later. Using (
KO0
), the quantity

C_{\lambda}=\int_{0}^{\lambda}\frac{\mathrm{d}s}{K^{-1}(F(s))}

is well-defined, increasing on
(0,\eta_{0})
and
C_{\lambda}\downarrow 0
as
\lambda\rightarrow 0^{+}
. For each fixed
j\in\mathbb{N}
, we set
R=R_{j}
and choose
T=T(R,\lambda)
small enough that

T\leq R,\qquad\int_{2R-T}^{2R}K^{-1}(\beta(s))\mathrm{d}s\leq C_{\lambda}.

(7.97)

We also set

D=D(\lambda,T,R)=\frac{C_{\lambda}}{\int_{2R-T}^{2R}K^{-1}(\beta(s))\mathrm{d}s},

(7.98)

and note that
D\geq 1
. Next, we implicitly define
\alpha:[0,TD]\rightarrow[0,\lambda]
by the formula

\int_{0}^{\alpha(s)}\frac{\mathrm{d}\tau}{K^{-1}(F(\tau))}=D\int_{2R-\frac{s}{D}}^{2R}K^{-1}\big(\beta(\tau)\big)\mathrm{d}\tau.

(7.99)

Then,
\alpha
is increasing and
\alpha(0)=0
,
\alpha(TD)=\lambda
. Hereafter, the subscript
s
denotes differentiation in the
s
variable. From

\alpha_{s}(s)=K^{-1}\big(F(\alpha(s))\big)K^{-1}\big(\beta(2R-s/D)\big)>0\qquad\text{on }\,(0,TD],

(7.100)

we deduce
\alpha_{s}(0)=0
and
0\leq\alpha(s)\leq\alpha(DT)=\lambda
. Using
K(0)=0
, we choose
\lambda
small enough that

K^{-1}\big(F(\alpha(s))\big)\leq 1\qquad\text{for }\,s\in[0,DT].

(7.101)

Furthermore, since
2R-s/D\geq 2R-T\geq R
in view of (
7.97
), by
(\beta_{1})^{\prime}
we can choose
R_{2}\geq R_{1}
large enough that

K^{-1}\big(\beta(2R-s/D)\big)\leq 1\qquad\text{for }\,s\in[0,DT],

(7.102)

whence

|\alpha_{s}(s)|\leq 1\qquad\text{for each}\ s\in[0,DT]

(7.103)

.

To simplify the writing set

\tilde{\beta}(s)=\beta(2R-s/D),\qquad\rho(s)=K^{-1}\big(F(\alpha(s))\big),\qquad\tau(s)=K^{-1}\big(\tilde{\beta}(s)\big),

(7.104)

and note that
(\beta_{2})^{\prime}
can be rewritten as

\frac{\tilde{\beta}_{s}(s)}{K^{-1}(\tilde{\beta}(s))}\leq\frac{c_{\beta}}{D}\tilde{\beta}(s)\leq c_{\beta}\tilde{\beta}(s),

(7.105)

the last inequality being a consequence of
D\geq 1
. Equation (
7.100
) becomes
\alpha_{s}=\rho\tau
and therefore

K(\alpha_{s})=K(\rho\tau).

Differentiating this latter and using, in order, (
7.104
),
(C_{1})
together with
(C_{2})^{\prime}
(and so
(C_{2})
by Lemma
7.34
),
(C_{1})
,
(\beta_{2})^{\prime}
,
(C_{4})
and (
7.100
) we obtain

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\displaystyle\big(K(\alpha_{s})\big)_{s}&=&\displaystyle K^{\prime}(\rho\tau)\left[\frac{f(\alpha)\alpha_{s}\tau}{K^{\prime}(\rho)}+\frac{\tilde{\beta}_{s}\rho}{K^{\prime}(\tau)}\right]\\[11.38092pt]
&\mathrel{{\mathop{\leq}\limits}}&\displaystyle k_{2}K^{\prime}(\rho)K^{\prime}(\tau)\left[\frac{f(\alpha)\alpha_{s}\tau}{K^{\prime}(\rho)}+\frac{\tilde{\beta}_{s}\rho}{K^{\prime}(\tau)}\right]\\[11.38092pt]
&=&\displaystyle k_{2}\left[f(\alpha)\alpha_{s}\tau K^{\prime}(\tau)+\tilde{\beta}_{s}\rho K^{\prime}(\rho)\right]\\[8.5359pt]
&\mathrel{{\mathop{\leq}\limits}}&\displaystyle k_{2}k_{1}\left[f(\alpha)\alpha_{s}K(\tau)+\tilde{\beta}_{s}K(\rho)\right]\\[8.5359pt]
&=&\displaystyle k_{2}k_{1}\left[f(\alpha)\alpha_{s}\tilde{\beta}+\tilde{\beta}_{s}F(\alpha)\right]\\[8.5359pt]
&\mathrel{{\mathop{\leq}\limits}}&\displaystyle k_{2}k_{1}\left[f(\alpha)\alpha_{s}\tilde{\beta}+c_{\beta}\tilde{\beta}K^{-1}(\tilde{\beta})F(\alpha)\right]\\[8.5359pt]
&\mathrel{{\mathop{\leq}\limits}}&\displaystyle k_{2}k_{1}\left[f(\alpha)\alpha_{s}\tilde{\beta}+c_{F}c_{\beta}\tilde{\beta}K^{-1}(\tilde{\beta})f(\alpha)K^{-1}(F(\alpha))\right]\\[8.5359pt]
&\mathrel{{\mathop{=}\limits}}&\displaystyle k_{2}k_{1}\big[1+c_{F}c_{\beta}\big]\tilde{\beta}f(\alpha)\alpha_{s}\qquad\text{for each }\,s\in(0,DT).\end{array}

(7.106)

Therefore, differentiating
K
we deduce

\frac{\alpha_{s}\varphi_{s}(\alpha_{s})}{l(\alpha_{s})}\alpha_{ss}=\big(K(\alpha_{s})\big)_{s}\leq k_{2}k_{1}\big[1+c_{F}c_{\beta}\big]\tilde{\beta}f(\alpha)\alpha_{s},

and since
\alpha_{s}>0
and
T\leq R
, by the monotonicity of
\tilde{\beta}
we obtain

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\displaystyle\big(\varphi(\alpha_{s})\big)_{s}&\leq&\displaystyle k_{2}k_{1}\big[1+c_{F}c_{\beta}\big]\tilde{\beta}f(\alpha)l(\alpha_{s})\\[8.5359pt]
&\leq&k_{2}k_{1}\big[1+c_{F}c_{\beta}\big]\tilde{\beta}\left(\frac{Rs}{T}\right)f(\alpha)l(\alpha_{s})\qquad\text{on }\,(0,DT).\end{array}

(7.107)

We also note that
\big(K(\alpha_{s})\big)_{s}\geq 0
follows from the first line in (
7.106
) and the fact that
\tilde{\beta}_{s}\geq 0
, hence
\alpha_{ss}\geq 0
. Integrating (
7.107
) on
(0,s]
,
s\in(0,DT]
and using
K^{-1}(0)=0
, the monotonicity of
\tilde{\beta}_{s}
and
\alpha_{s}
, (
7.5
) and

we get

\displaystyle\varphi\big(\alpha_{s}(s)\big)\leq\displaystyle k_{2}k_{1}\big[1+c_{F}c_{\beta}\big]C^{2}TD\tilde{\beta}\left(\frac{Rs}{T}\right)f\big(\alpha(s)\big)l\big(\alpha_{s}(s)\big)

(7.108)

Next, we define the function
z:[R,\infty)\rightarrow[0,\lambda)
by setting

z(t)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\alpha(s),\quad s=DT\left(2-\frac{t}{R}\right)&\quad\text{if }\,t\in[R,2R];\\[8.5359pt]
0&\quad\text{if }\,t>2R.\end{array}\right.

(7.109)

Then,
z\in C^{1}([R,\infty))
(actually,
C^{2}
with a possible exception at

) and
z
is non-increasing. Furthermore,

z(R)=\alpha(DT)=\lambda,\qquad z(2R)=z^{\prime}(2R)=0,\qquad z^{\prime}(t)=-\frac{DT}{R}\alpha_{s}(s).

(7.110)

We pause for a moment to estimate the quotient
DT/R
. By definition, and since
\beta
is decreasing,

\frac{DT}{R}=\frac{C_{\lambda}T}{R\int_{2R-T}^{2R}K^{-1}(\beta(\tau))\mathrm{d}\tau}\leq\frac{2C_{\lambda}}{2RK^{-1}(\beta(2R))}.

(7.111)

Using
(\beta_{3})
and recalling that
R=R_{j}
satisfy (
7.59
),

\frac{-2R\beta^{\prime}(2R)}{\beta(2R)}\geq\frac{\hat{c}_{\beta}}{2},

whence, applying
(\beta_{2})^{\prime}
,

\frac{1}{2RK^{-1}(\beta(2R))}\leq\frac{c_{\beta}\beta(2R)}{-2R\beta^{\prime}(2R)}\leq\frac{2c_{\beta}}{\hat{c}_{\beta}},

(7.112)

and inserting into (
7.111
),

\frac{DT}{R}\leq\frac{4c_{\beta}C_{\lambda}}{\hat{c}_{\beta}}.

(7.113)

Up to reducing
\lambda
further, we can guarantee that

, and consequently by (

) and (

)

|z^{\prime}|\leq\epsilon.

This shows the third relation in (
7.58
). Next, since
|\alpha_{s}|\leq 1
on
(0,DT)
, applying
(K_{4})
in Lemma
7.34
, (
7.108
),
(C_{2})^{\prime}
and since

\displaystyle\tilde{\beta}\left(\frac{R}{T}s\right)=\tilde{\beta}(2DR-Dt)=\beta(t)

we infer

\begin{array}[]{rcl}-\varphi\big(z^{\prime}(t)\big)&=&\varphi\big(-z^{\prime}(t)\big)=\varphi\left(\frac{DT}{R}\alpha_{s}(s)\right)\\[8.5359pt]
&\mathrel{{\mathop{\leq}\limits}}&d_{1}K^{\prime}\left(\frac{DT}{R}\right)l\left(\frac{DT}{R}\right)\varphi\big(\alpha_{s}(s)\big)\\[8.5359pt]
&\mathrel{{\mathop{\leq}\limits}}&d_{1}k_{1}k_{2}\left[1+c_{F}c_{\beta}\right]C^{2}DTK^{\prime}\left(\frac{DT}{R}\right)l\left(\frac{DT}{R}\right)\tilde{\beta}\left(\frac{Rs}{T}\right)f\big(\alpha(s)\big)l\big(\alpha_{s}(s)\big)\\[8.5359pt]
&\mathrel{{\mathop{\leq}\limits}}&c_{1}d_{1}k_{1}k_{2}\left[1+c_{F}c_{\beta}\right]C^{2}DTK^{\prime}\left(\frac{DT}{R}\right)\beta(t)f\big(\alpha(s)\big)l\big(\frac{DT}{R}\alpha_{s}(s)\big)\\[8.5359pt]
&=&c_{1}d_{1}k_{1}k_{2}\left[1+c_{F}c_{\beta}\right]C^{2}DTK^{\prime}\left(\frac{DT}{R}\right)\beta(t)f\big(z(t)\big)l\big(|z^{\prime}(t)|\big)\\[8.5359pt]
&\leq&c_{1}d_{1}k_{1}^{2}k_{2}\left[1+c_{F}c_{\beta}\right]C^{2}RK\left(\frac{DT}{R}\right)\beta(t)f\big(z(t)\big)l\big(|z^{\prime}(t)|\big).\end{array}

(7.114)

We next investigate
(\varphi(z^{\prime}))^{\prime}
. By definition, and because of
(C_{2})^{\prime}
,
\alpha_{ss}\geq 0
, (
7.107
) and
(C_{1})
, we obtain

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\big(\varphi(z^{\prime})\big)^{\prime}&=&\displaystyle\varphi^{\prime}\left(\frac{DT}{R}\alpha_{s}(s)\right)\frac{D^{2}T^{2}}{R^{2}}\alpha_{ss}(s)\mathrel{{\mathop{\leq}\limits}}d_{1}\varphi^{\prime}\left(\frac{DT}{R}\right)\varphi^{\prime}(\alpha_{s}(s))\frac{D^{2}T^{2}}{R^{2}}\alpha_{ss}(s)\\[8.5359pt]
&=&\displaystyle d_{1}\varphi^{\prime}\left(\frac{DT}{R}\right)\frac{D^{2}T^{2}}{R^{2}}\big(\varphi(\alpha_{s}(s))\big)_{s}\\[8.5359pt]
&\mathrel{{\mathop{\leq}\limits}}&\displaystyle k_{2}k_{1}\big[1+c_{F}c_{\beta}\big]d_{1}\varphi^{\prime}\left(\frac{DT}{R}\right)\frac{D^{2}T^{2}}{R^{2}}\beta(t)f\big(\alpha(s)\big)l\big(\alpha_{s}(s)\big)\\[8.5359pt]
&=&\displaystyle k_{2}k_{1}\big[1+c_{F}c_{\beta}\big]d_{1}\varphi^{\prime}\left(\frac{DT}{R}\right)\frac{D^{2}T^{2}}{R^{2}}\beta(t)f\big(z(t)\big)l\left(\frac{R}{DT}|z^{\prime}(t)|\right)\\[8.5359pt]
&=&\displaystyle k_{2}k_{1}\big[1+c_{F}c_{\beta}\big]d_{1}\frac{DT}{R}K^{\prime}\left(\frac{DT}{R}\right)l\left(\frac{DT}{R}\right)\beta(t)f\big(z(t)\big)l\left(\frac{R}{DT}|z^{\prime}(t)|\right)\\[8.5359pt]
&\mathrel{{\mathop{\leq}\limits}}&\displaystyle c_{1}k_{2}k_{1}^{2}\big[1+c_{F}c_{\beta}\big]d_{1}K\left(\frac{DT}{R}\right)\beta(t)f\big(z(t)\big)l\big(|z^{\prime}(t)|\big),\end{array}

(7.115)

Combining (
7.114
) and (
7.115
) and using
z^{\prime}\leq 0
we get on
t\in[R,2R]

\begin{array}[]{l}\displaystyle\big(\varphi(z^{\prime})\big)^{\prime}-\theta(t)\varphi(z^{\prime})=\big(\varphi(z^{\prime})\big)^{\prime}+\theta(t)\varphi(-z^{\prime})\\[8.5359pt]
\leq\displaystyle\displaystyle c_{1}d_{1}k_{1}^{2}k_{2}\big[1+c_{F}c_{\beta}\big]K\left(\frac{DT}{R}\right)\left[1+C^{2}R\theta(R)\right]\beta(t)f(z)l\big(|z^{\prime}|\big).\end{array}

(7.116)

Observe now that properties
(C_{1})
and
(C_{2})^{\prime}
(hence,
(C_{2})
by Lemma
7.34
) guarantee the validity of
(K_{1})
in Lemma
7.11
. Possibly reducing
\lambda
in such a way that

\max\left(\sqrt{C_{\lambda}},K\left(\frac{\sqrt{C_{\lambda}}4c_{\beta}}{\hat{c}_{\beta}}\right)\right)\leq 1

and using (
7.111
), we get

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\displaystyle K\left(\frac{DT}{R}\right)&\leq&\displaystyle K\left(\frac{C_{\lambda}}{RK^{-1}(\beta(2R))}\right)\\[17.07182pt]
&\mathrel{{\mathop{\leq}\limits}}&\displaystyle k_{1}k_{2}K(\sqrt{C_{\lambda}})K\left(\frac{\sqrt{C_{\lambda}}}{RK^{-1}(\beta(2R))}\right)\\[17.07182pt]
&\leq&\displaystyle k_{1}k_{2}K(\sqrt{C_{\lambda}})\min\left[K\left(\frac{1}{RK^{-1}(\beta(2R))}\right),1\right].\end{array}

Inserting into (
7.116
), using
C\geq 1
and (
7.95
),

\begin{array}[]{l}\displaystyle\big(\varphi(z^{\prime})\big)^{\prime}-\theta(t)\varphi(z^{\prime})\\[14.22636pt]
\leq\displaystyle c_{1}d_{1}k_{1}^{3}k_{2}^{2}\big[1+c_{F}c_{\beta}\big]C^{2}K\Big(\sqrt{C_{\lambda}}\Big)\left[1+K\left(\frac{1}{RK^{-1}(\beta(2R))}\right)R\theta(R)\right]\beta(t)f(z)l\big(|z^{\prime}|\big)\\[14.22636pt]
\leq\displaystyle(1+B_{2})c_{1}d_{1}k_{1}^{3}k_{2}^{2}\big[1+c_{F}c_{\beta}\big]C^{2}K\Big(\sqrt{C_{\lambda}}\Big)\beta(t)f(z)l\big(|z^{\prime}|\big)\quad{\rm for}\ t\geq R.\end{array}

(7.117)

With a possible smaller choice of
\lambda
, still independent of
R
, we can ensure that

(1+B_{2})c_{1}d_{1}k_{1}^{3}k_{2}^{2}\big[1+c_{F}c_{\beta}\big]C^{2}K(\sqrt{C_{\lambda}})\leq\epsilon,

concluding the proof.
∎

8
Keller-Osserman, a-priori estimates and
(\mathrm{SL})

In this section, we relate the Keller-Osserman condition

\frac{1}{K^{-1}\circ F}\in L^{1}(\infty)

(
\mathrm{KO}_{\infty}
)

to the strong Liouville property
(\mathrm{SL})
for solutions of
(P_{\geq})
. It is particularly interesting to see how geometry comes into play via the validity of the weak or the strong maximum principle for
(bl)^{-1}\Delta_{\varphi}
. Hereafter, we require

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\varphi\in C(\mathbb{R}^{+}_{0})\cap C^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{+}),\qquad\varphi(0)=0,\qquad\varphi^{\prime}>0\ \text{ on }\,\mathbb{R}^{+},\\[8.5359pt]
l\in C(\mathbb{R}^{+}_{0}),\qquad l>0\ \text{on }\,\mathbb{R}^{+},\\[8.5359pt]
f\in C(\mathbb{R}),\\[8.5359pt]
\text{$f>0\ \ $ and $C$-increasing on $(\bar{\eta}_{0},\infty)$, for some $\bar{\eta}_{0}\geq 0$,}\\[8.5359pt]
\end{array}\right.

(8.1)

and moreover

\frac{t\varphi^{\prime}(t)}{l(t)}\in L^{1}(0^{+})\backslash L^{1}(\infty).

(8.2)

Having defined
K
as in (
1.19
), by (
8.2
)
K
is a homeomorphism of
\mathbb{R}^{+}_{0}
onto itself, thus (

KO
∞

) is well defined with

F(t)=\int_{\bar{\eta}_{0}}^{t}f(s)\mathrm{d}s.

(8.3)

Note that, for the mean curvature operator, if
l\equiv 1
then
K
is not surjective on
\mathbb{R}^{+}
, that is, (
8.2
) does not hold. Indeed, for operators of mean curvature type one is able to guarantee property
(\mathrm{SL})

without
the need of the Keller-Osserman condition, at least in some instances. In this respect, the following result of Y. Naito and H. Usami
[
100
]
is illustrative
9
9

9

The statement reported here is slightly different from the original one in Theorems 1,2,3 of
[
100
]
. However, the two are equivalent in view of Lemma
3.6
. Moreover, their notion of solution needs the further condition
|\nabla u|^{-1}\varphi(|\nabla u|)\nabla u\in C^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{m})
, and
\Delta_{\varphi}u\geq f(u)
is meant in the pointwise sense.
:

Theorem 8.1

(
[
100
]
, Thms. 1,2,3)
.

Let
\varphi,f
satisfy (
8.1
) and

f(0)=0,\qquad f>0\quad\text{and non decreasing on }\,\mathbb{R}^{+}.

Consider a non-negative solution
u\in C^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{m})
of
\Delta_{\varphi}u\geq f(u)
on
\mathbb{R}^{m}
.

(i)

If
\varphi(\infty)<\infty
, then
u\equiv 0
on
\mathbb{R}^{m}
.

(ii)

If
\varphi(\infty)=\infty
, then the only non-negative solution is
u\equiv 0
if and only if (

KO
∞

) holds.

Remark 8.2
.

As observed in
[
100
]
, when
l\equiv 1
condition
\varphi(\infty)=\infty
implies
K_{\infty}=\infty
and thus (

KO
∞

) is meaningful. This follows from the next inequalities:

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\displaystyle K(t)+\int_{0}^{1}\varphi(s)\mathrm{d}s&=&\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}s\varphi^{\prime}(s)\mathrm{d}s+\int_{0}^{1}\varphi(s)\mathrm{d}s\\[11.38092pt]
&=&\displaystyle t\varphi(t)-\int_{1}^{t}\varphi(s)\mathrm{d}s\geq\varphi(t).\end{array}

In fact, in the Appendix of
[
100
]
it is also proved that if
t\varphi(t)\asymp t^{p}
as
t\rightarrow\infty
, for some
p>1
, then also
K(t)\asymp t^{p}
.

In the last subsection, we will discuss in detail the case of mean curvature type operators, for which we describe appropriate Keller-Osserman conditions (that are necessary in some cases!) for the validity of
(\mathrm{SL})
. First, we focus on those operators for which (
8.2
) holds, and begin with considering the implication
(\mathrm{SL})\Rightarrow\eqref{KO_2}
.

8.1
Necessity of (

KO
∞

) for the
(\mathrm{SL})
property

The main result of this section is Theorem
8.4
below: under the failure of (

KO
∞

), we exhibit a non-constant, non-negative solution
u\in C^{1}(M)
of
(P_{\geq})
on any complete manifold with a pole
o
and satisfying the mild curvature restriction

K_{\mathrm{rad}}(x)\leq-G\big(r(x)\big)\qquad\text{on }\,M\backslash\{o\},

(8.4)

for some
G\in C(\mathbb{R}^{+}_{0})
matching

t\int_{t}^{\infty}G_{-}(s)\mathrm{d}s\leq\frac{1}{4}\qquad\forall\,t\in\mathbb{R}^{+}

(8.5)

with
G_{-}=-\min\{G,0\}
.

Remark 8.3
.

Condition (
8.5
) is met, for instance, by any
G\in C(\mathbb{R}^{+}_{0})
satisfying
G(t)\geq-(4t^{2})^{-1}
. Therefore,

K_{\mathrm{rad}}(x)\leq\frac{1}{4r(x)^{2}}\qquad\text{on }\,M\backslash\{o\}

(8.6)

is sufficient for the validity of (
8.4
) and (
8.5
). Note that (
8.6
) includes both the Euclidean and the hyperbolic spaces, as well as models with a mild positive curvature that, at infinity, open like paraboloids.

It is worth to stress that (
8.4
) and (
8.5
) are only used to guarantee that the model to be compared to
M
is defined on the entire
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, see Remarks
8.3
and
8.5
, and seems somehow to be merely technical (although, we believe, challenging to remove). Loosely speaking, this would suggest that there is no geometric obstruction to ensure that (

KO
∞

) be necessary for
(\mathrm{SL})
.

Theorem 8.4
.

Let
M^{m}
be a complete Riemannian manifold with a pole
o\in M
, and assume (
8.4
) for some
G\in C(\mathbb{R}^{+}_{0})
enjoying (
8.5
). Let
\varphi,f,l
satisfy (
8.1
) and (
8.2
), and

f(0)=0,\qquad f>0\qquad\text{on }\,\mathbb{R}^{+}.

If

\frac{1}{K^{-1}\circ F}\not\in L^{1}(\infty),

(
\neg
KO
∞
)

Then, for each
b\in C(M)
,
b>0
on
M
there exists a non-constant, non-negative
u\in C^{1}(M)
satisfying
(P_{\geq})
, that is,

\Delta_{\varphi}u\geq b(x)f(u)l(|\nabla u|)\qquad\text{on }\,M.

In particular,
(\mathrm{SL})
does not hold on
M
.

Proof.

Let
g\in C^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+}_{0})
be the solution of

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}g^{\prime\prime}-Gg=0\quad\text{on }\,\mathbb{R}^{+},\\[5.69046pt]
g(0)>0,\quad g^{\prime}(0)=1.\end{array}\right.

(8.7)

Assumption (
8.5
) guarantees that
g>0
and
g^{\prime}>0
on
\mathbb{R}^{+}
, see
[
14
, Prop. 1.21]
. Let
\wp(r)=\mathrm{vol}(\mathbb{S}^{m-1})g(r)^{m-1}
be the volume growth of spheres of the model
M_{g}
. Define
a\in C(\mathbb{R}^{+}_{0})
and
\bar{l}\in C(\mathbb{R}^{+}_{0})
in such a way that

\begin{array}[]{l}\displaystyle b(x)\leq a\big(r(x)\big)\qquad\forall\,x\in M,\\[5.69046pt]
\displaystyle\bar{l}\geq l\quad\text{on }\,\mathbb{R}^{+}_{0},\qquad\bar{l}(0)>0,\qquad\bar{l}=l\quad\text{on }\,[1,\infty).\end{array}

Then, all of the assumptions in Proposition
3.13
are satisfied with
\bar{l}
replacing
l
, and there exists a non-constant function
w\in C^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{+}_{0})
solving

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\big[\wp\varphi(w^{\prime})\big]^{\prime}=a\wp f(w)\bar{l}(|w^{\prime}|)\qquad\text{on }\,\mathbb{R}^{+},\\[5.69046pt]
w^{\prime}(0)=0,\quad w\geq 0,\quad w^{\prime}\geq 0\quad\text{on }\,\mathbb{R}^{+},\\[5.69046pt]
\end{array}\right.

Set
u(x)=w\big(r(x)\big)
. By the Laplacian comparison theorem from below (i.e. taking traces in (
9.7
) of Theorem
9.2
), and using that
\bar{l}\geq l
,
w^{\prime}\geq 0
and
\varphi(w^{\prime})\in C^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{+}_{0})
, we have

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\Delta_{\varphi}u&=&\displaystyle\big[\varphi(w^{\prime})\big]^{\prime}+\varphi(w^{\prime})\Delta r\geq\big[\varphi(w^{\prime})\big]^{\prime}+\varphi(w^{\prime})\frac{\wp^{\prime}}{\wp}=\wp^{-1}\big[\wp\varphi(w^{\prime})\big]^{\prime}\\[8.5359pt]
&=&af(w)\bar{l}(|w^{\prime}|)\geq b(x)f(u)l(|\nabla u|).\end{array}

(8.8)

Since
o
is a pole and
w^{\prime}(0)=0
,
u\in C^{1}(M)
and provides the desired solution.
∎

Remark 8.5
.

Evidently, (
8.5
) can be replaced by the only requirement that the solution
g
of the Jacobi equation (
8.7
) is positive and non-decreasing on
\mathbb{R}^{+}
.

The use of the mixed Dirichlet-Neumann problem to prove Theorem
8.4
is inspired by the very recent
[
21
]
: in Theorem 1.1 therein, the authors prove existence under (

¬

KO∞
) in the setting of the Heisenberg group, for each
l\in C(\mathbb{R}^{+}_{0})
with
l>0
on
\mathbb{R}^{+}_{0}
. In particular, no
C
-monotonicity of
l
is needed. To the best of our knowledge, in the literature the existence of entire solutions of
(P_{\geq})
under the failure of (

KO
∞

) has been shown just in few further special cases, see for instance
[
95
, p. 694-696]
,
[
89
, Thm. 1.3]
,
[
57
, Cor. 1.1]
. Differently from
[
21
]
, in the constructions in
[
95
,
89
,
57
]
the radial function
u
is defined implicitly by a direct use of (

KO
∞

), in a way analogous to that in Proposition
7.8
. This method needs various structural assumptions on
\varphi,b,l
, that considerably restrict the range of the operators. On the contrary, the use of the Dirichlet-Neumann problem allows to make a clean, simpler proof of the existence of solutions of
(P_{\geq})
and, at the same time, to remove the unnecessary conditions on
\varphi,f,l
: in particular, neither we assume a controlled growth of
b
nor the
C
-monotonicity (increasing or decreasing) of
l
. However, the presence of a pole, intimately related to the use of the comparison theorem from below, is unavoidable for our method to work, as well as for those in the above references. It would be interesting to investigate the following

Question
.

Can one prove the necessity of (

KO
∞

) for property
(\mathrm{SL})
on a general complete manifold, at least for some classes of
\varphi
and
l
?

Note that, in the
p
-Laplacian case, a direct use of the fake distance
\varrho
as in the proof of Theorem
1.42
is not enough to conclude. Indeed, from (
2.13
) and taking into account (
8.8
), for
u
to solve
(P_{\geq})
we need a
global lower bound
for
|\nabla\varrho|
. Despite the fact that lower bounds for
|\nabla\varrho|
seem very difficult to achieve, their existence coupled with the properness of
\varrho
would still force, by Morse theory, topological restrictions on
M
.

8.2
Sufficiency of (

KO
∞

) for the
(\mathrm{SL})
property

The investigation of the sufficiency of (

KO
∞

) for
(\mathrm{SL})
in a manifold setting began with the pioneering
[
28
,
156
]
, for the prototype semilinear example
\Delta u\geq f(u)
. There, geometry is taken into account via a constant lower bound on the Ricci tensor of
M
. The Liouville theorems therein proved to be remarkably effective in a wealth of different geometric problems. Among them, we stress a striking proof of the generalized Schwarz Lemma for maps between Kahler manifolds in
[
154
]
, and the Bernstein theorem for maximal hypersurfaces in Minkovski space in
[
29
]
. Since then, various authors studied possible useful generalizations, notably
[
99
]
for the inequality

\Delta u\geq\varphi(u,|\nabla u|).

The topic has first been considered from a general perspective in
[
110
]
, that also contains a detailed account of the previous literature, and later more specifically in
[
95
]
for quasilinear equations including
(P_{\geq})
with non-constant
l
. As usual, the geometric requirements range from a control on the Ricci to a growth estimate for the volume of geodesic balls. In the next subsections, we will describe improvements of the results therein, as well as new theorems, and discuss their sharpness. Of particular interest for us is the case of mean curvature type operators, for which interesting specific phenomena appear. Typically, but not exclusively, we will consider a gradient nonlinearity of the type

l(t)\asymp\frac{\varphi(t)}{t^{\chi}},

that, for the mean curvature operator, vanishes both as
t\rightarrow 0
and as
t\rightarrow\infty
when
\chi\in(0,1)
. When
l
is allowed to vanish both at
t=0
and at
t=\infty
we cannot rely on the existing literature, because all of the results that we know require a
C
-monotonicity of
l
, either increasing (cf.
[
95
]
) or decreasing (cf.
[
21
]
).

We begin with the following result that considers homogeneous operators and a power-like gradient dependence. In this case, we can give a very simple proof of the next implication:

(\mathrm{SMP}_{\infty})\quad+\quad\eqref{KO}\ \ \Longrightarrow\ \ {(\mathrm{SL})}.

(8.9)

The argument naturally splits into two steps. First, the combination of
(\mathrm{SMP}_{\infty})
and (

KO
∞

) guarantees that each solution of
(P_{\geq})
is, in fact, bounded from above; in the second step we are left to prove the validity of
(\mathrm{L})
.

Theorem 8.6
.

Let
M
be a Riemannian manifold. Fix
p>1
,
\chi\in(-1,p-1]
, and let
f\in C(\mathbb{R})
satisfying

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}f>0\quad\text{on }\,(\bar{\eta}_{0},\infty)\\[5.69046pt]
F(t)^{\frac{\chi}{\chi+1}}\leq c_{F}f(t),\qquad\text{on $(\bar{\eta}_{0},\infty)$, for some constant $c_{F}>0$.}\end{array}\right.

(8.10)

Let
u\in C^{1}(M)
solve
\Delta_{p}u\geq f(u)|\nabla u|^{p-1-\chi}
on
M
. If

1.

l^{-1}\Delta_{p}
satisfies
(\mathrm{SMP}_{\infty})
with
l(t)=t^{p-1-\chi}
, and

2.

the Keller-Osserman condition (

KO
∞

) holds, that is,

F(t)^{-\frac{1}{\chi+1}}\in L^{1}(\infty),

then

u^{*}=\sup_{M}u<\infty\qquad\text{and}\qquad f(u^{*})\leq 0.

(8.11)

Remark 8.7
.

Note that the second in (
8.10
) is the equivalent, at infinity, of condition
(C_{4})
, repeatedly used in the proof of the compact support principle. It is easy to see that the condition is, for general
f
, unrelated to (

KO
∞

).

Proof.

In our assumptions, the function
K
defined in (
1.19
) satisfies
K(t)\asymp t^{\chi+1}
as
t\rightarrow\infty
, thus
K_{\infty}=\infty
and (

KO
∞

) is meaningful. Let
g\in C^{2}(\mathbb{R})
be such that

g^{\prime}>0\quad\text{on }\,\mathbb{R},\qquad g(t)=\int_{\bar{\eta}_{0}}^{t}\frac{\mathrm{d}s}{K^{-1}(F(s))}\qquad\text{for }\,t\geq\bar{\eta}_{0}+1.

Suppose by contradiction that
u^{*}=\infty
, so that
\Omega_{\bar{\eta}_{0}+1}=\{x\in M:u(x)>\bar{\eta}_{0}+1\}
is non-empty. Set
h(x)=g(u(x))
. Then,
h\in C^{1}(M)
and
h^{*}<\infty
because of (

KO
∞

). Computations show that, on
\Omega_{\bar{\eta}_{0}+1}
,

\displaystyle\displaystyle\nabla h

\displaystyle=\displaystyle g^{\prime}(u)\nabla u=\frac{\nabla u}{K^{-1}(F(u))}

(8.12)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\Delta_{p}h

\displaystyle=\displaystyle(p-1)(g^{\prime})^{p-2}g^{\prime\prime}|\nabla u|^{p}+(g^{\prime})^{p-1}\Delta_{p}u

\displaystyle\geq\left[-|\nabla h|^{p}K^{-1}(F(u))^{p-1-\chi}+|\nabla u|^{p-1-\chi}\right]\frac{f(u)}{K^{-1}(F(u))^{p-1}}

\displaystyle=\left[1-|\nabla h|^{\chi+1}\right]\frac{f(u)|\nabla h|^{p-1-\chi}}{K^{-1}(F(u))^{\chi}}

in the weak sense.
Next, by (
8.10
),

\frac{f(u)}{K^{-1}(F(u))^{\chi}}\geq\hat{c}_{F}>0\qquad\text{on }\,(\bar{\eta}_{0},\infty),

(8.13)

for some constant
\hat{c}_{F}>0
. Then, for each
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
on the open, non-empty set

\Omega_{\eta,\varepsilon}=\big\{x\in M\ :\ h(x)>\eta\ {\rm and}\ |\nabla h(x)|<\varepsilon\big\}

the function
h
solves

\Delta_{p}h\geq\hat{c}_{F}(1-\varepsilon^{p})|\nabla h|^{p-1-\chi},

contradicting the validity of
(\mathrm{SMP}_{\infty})
for
l^{-1}\Delta_{p}
. Therefore,
u^{*}<\infty
and, since
(\mathrm{SMP}_{\infty})
is in force, we can apply Proposition
5.3
to deduce that
f(u^{*})\leq 0
. This concludes the proof.
∎

Corollary 8.8
.

In the assumptions of Theorem
8.6
,
(\mathrm{SL})
holds for each
f>0
on
\mathbb{R}^{+}
satisfying (
8.10
).

Remark 8.9
.

Theorem
8.6
should be compared with
[
110
, Thm. 1.31]
, that improves on previous results of Cheng and Yau
[
28
]
and Motomiya
[
99
]
. In
[
110
]
, the authors consider solutions of

\Delta u\geq\varphi(u,|\nabla u|)\qquad\text{on }\,M

under suitable assumptions on
\varphi
that are skew with those in Theorem
8.6
, and infer the bound
f(u^{*})\leq 0
under the validity of
(\mathrm{SMP}_{\infty})
for the Laplace-Beltrami operator
\Delta
.

Remark 8.10
.

We stress that Theorem
8.6
also holds for
l(t)=t^{p-1-\chi}
when
\chi\in(-1,0)
. However, this range is not included in Theorem
5.4
and, indeed, currently we do not know which geometric conditions ensure
(\mathrm{SMP}_{\infty})
or even
(\mathrm{WMP}_{\infty})
for
l^{-1}\Delta_{p}
and negative
\chi
. For further related comments, we refer to Remark
8.42
below.

As a prototype example of applicability of the above theorem, we give a quick proof of the following classical result. The first part of Theorem
8.11
below is due to R. Osserman who introduced (

KO
∞

), as we have mentioned in the introduction, to prove this result; the second is a restatement of the classical Schwarz lemma for complex analysis.

Theorem 8.11

(
[
104
]
)
.

Let
(M,g)
be a complete, non-compact, simply connected Riemann surface whose sectional curvature satisfies
K(x)\leq-1
. Then,
M
is conformally equivalent to the Poincaré disk
(\mathbb{D},g_{\mathbb{H}})
via some conformal diffeomorphism
\varphi:\mathbb{D}\rightarrow M
satisfying
\varphi^{*}g\leq g_{\mathbb{H}}
.

Proof.

We recall that, if
g=e^{2u}h
is a conformal deformation of a metric
h
on a surface
M
, then
u
turns out to satisfy the Yamabe equation

\Delta u=-K_{g}e^{2u}+K_{h},

where
K_{g}
and
K_{h}
are the Gaussian curvatures of
g
and
h
, and
\Delta
is the Laplace-Beltrami operator of
h
. By the Riemann-Köbe uniformization theorem,
M
is conformal either to the Euclidean plane
\mathbb{R}^{2}
with its flat metric
g_{\mathbb{R}}
, or to the Poincaré disk
(\mathbb{D},g_{\mathbb{H}})
with the hyperbolic metric
g_{\mathbb{H}}
of, say, sectional curvature
-1
. Both on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
and on
\mathbb{D}
, the operator
\Delta
satisfies
(\mathrm{SMP}_{\infty})
because of Theorem
6.5
. Suppose by contradiction that
g=e^{2u}g_{\mathbb{R}}
. Then,
u
satisfies

\Delta u=-K_{g}(x)e^{2u}\geq e^{2u}\qquad\text{on }\,\mathbb{R}^{2}.

(8.14)

We therefore apply Theorem
8.6
with
f(t)=e^{2t}
to deduce that
u
is bounded above and
f(u^{*})\leq 0
, that is clearly impossible. Hence,
M
is conformally the Poincaré disk and setting
g=e^{2u}g_{\mathbb{H}}
, from
K_{g_{\mathbb{H}}}=-1
and
K_{g}\leq-1
the function
u
satisfies

\Delta u=-K_{g}e^{2u}-1\geq e^{2u}-1.

(8.15)

Setting
f(t)=e^{2t}-1
,
f
still satisfies (
KO∞
) and (
8.10
). Furthermore, on the Poincaré disk
\Delta
satisfies
(\mathrm{SMP}_{\infty})
, and again by Theorem
8.6
we get
f(u^{*})\leq 0
, that is
u^{*}\leq 0
. Therefore,
g=e^{2u}g_{\mathbb{H}}\leq g_{\mathbb{H}}
.
∎

We pause for a moment to comment on the necessity to require
(\mathrm{SMP}_{\infty})
in Theorem
8.6
. The validity of
(\mathrm{L})
for
f>0
on
\mathbb{R}^{+}
is granted under the sole assumption
(\mathrm{WMP}_{\infty})
by Proposition
5.3
. Thus, one might wonder whether the implication

(\mathrm{SMP}_{\infty})\quad+\quad\eqref{KO}\ \ \Longrightarrow\ \ (\mathrm{SL})

for
f>0
on
\mathbb{R}^{+}
satisfying (
8.10
), could be improved to

(\mathrm{WMP}_{\infty})\quad+\quad\eqref{KO}\ \ \Longrightarrow\ \ (\mathrm{SL}).

This amounts to showing that the combination
(\mathrm{WMP}_{\infty})+\eqref{KO}
guarantees global
L^{\infty}
-estimates for solutions of
\Delta_{p}u\geq f(u)
. This is generally false, as the following example shows.

Example 8.12
.

Consider the punctured Euclidean space
\mathbb{R}^{m}\backslash\{0\}
with its flat metric. Then, it is easy to see that
\Delta
satisfies
(\mathrm{WMP}_{\infty})
. Indeed, define a function
w\in C^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{m}\backslash\{0\})
as follows:

\begin{array}[]{ll}w(x)=-\log|x|+|x|^{2}&\quad\text{if }\,m=2,\\[5.69046pt]
w(x)=|x|^{2-m}+|x|^{2}&\quad\text{if }\,m\geq 3.\end{array}

then
w
is an exhaustion on
\mathbb{R}^{m}\backslash\{0\}
, i.e., it has relatively compact sublevel sets, and
\Delta w=2m\leq w
outside a compact set. The function
w
is therefore a good Khasminskii potential, whose existence implies
(\mathrm{WMP}_{\infty})
, see for instance
[
63
]
and
[
110
, Prop. 3.2]
. In fact, the existence of such a
w
is equivalent to
(\mathrm{WMP}_{\infty})
, cf.
[
96
,
93
]
for details. Now, consider

\sigma\in\left(1,\frac{m+2}{m}\right),\quad\beta=\frac{2}{\sigma-1},\quad u(x)=|x|^{-\beta}.

Then, a computation shows that
