\Delta u=\beta(\beta-m)u^{\sigma}.

Hence,
\Delta u\geq cu^{\sigma}
for some constant
c>0
in our range on
\sigma
. On the other hand, it is easy to check that the function
f(t)=ct^{\sigma}
satisfies
f>0
on
\mathbb{R}^{+}
together with (
8.10
) and (

KO
∞

). Therefore,
(\mathrm{WMP}_{\infty})
is not enough to conclude
(\mathrm{SL})
even for functions matching (

KO
∞

).

Remark 8.13
.

Example
8.12
is on a geodesically incomplete manifold. As suggested in the Introduction, it would be very interesting to find an analogous phenomenon for a complete manifold. It is likely that the technique in
[
20
]
to construct a complete manifold such that
\Delta
satisfies
(\mathrm{WMP}_{\infty})
, but not
(\mathrm{SMP}_{\infty})
, be useful to produce a complete example.

8.3
Ricci curvature and
(\mathrm{SL})

When the operator is not of
p
-Laplacian type, the straightforward method described in Theorem
8.6
does not work, and we cannot directly infer implication (
8.9
). Nevertheless, in what follows we will describe how to obtain a sharp result for a much larger class of operators including various of geometric interest. The geometric assumption is given in term of a control on the Ricci curvature at infinity, the same as the one in Theorem
6.5
to guarantee the validity of the
(\mathrm{SMP}_{\infty})
. Similarly, the approach is inspired by the Phrágmen-Lindelöff method. To construct the relevant supersolutions, we need some mild conditions relating
\varphi
and
l
. We assume (
8.1
) and (
8.2
), and furthermore that

l
is
C
-increasing on
\mathbb{R}^{+}_{0}
.

(8.16)

Moreover, we require the existence of
\chi_{1},\chi_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
such that

\begin{array}[]{lll}(\chi_{1})&\quad\displaystyle t\mapsto\frac{\varphi^{\prime}(t)}{l(t)}t^{1-\chi_{1}}&\qquad\text{is $C$-increasing on $\mathbb{R}^{+}$,}\\[11.38092pt]
(\chi_{2})&\quad\displaystyle t\mapsto\frac{\varphi(t)}{l(t)}t^{-\chi_{2}}&\qquad\text{is $C$-increasing on $\mathbb{R}^{+}$.}\end{array}

Concerning
\beta
and
\bar{\beta}
, we require

(\beta\bar{\beta})

\beta\in C([r_{0},\infty))
,
\quad\bar{\beta}\in C^{1}([r_{0},\infty))
,

\bar{\beta}^{\prime}\leq 0,\qquad\bar{\beta}\not\in L^{1}(\infty).

Example 8.14
.

If
\varphi(t)=t^{p-1}
and
l(t)\asymp t^{p-1-\chi}
, then
(\chi_{1}),(\chi_{2})
are both satisfied provided that

\chi_{j}\leq\chi\quad\text{for each }\,j\in\{1,2\}.

If
\varphi(t)=t/\sqrt{1+t^{2}}
is the mean curvature operator, and
l(t)\asymp\varphi(t)/t^{\chi}
for some
\chi\in\mathbb{R}
, then
(\chi_{1}),(\chi_{2})
hold provided that

\chi_{1}\leq\chi-2,\qquad\chi_{2}\leq\chi.

Finally, if
\varphi(t)=te^{t^{2}}
is the operator of exponentially harmonic functions and
l(t)\asymp t^{q}
,
(\chi_{1})
and
(\chi_{2})
hold whenever

\max\{\chi_{1},\chi_{2}\}\leq 1-q.

We shall first deduce some useful properties from the validity of
(\chi_{1})
and
(\chi_{2})
.

Lemma 8.15
.

Assume that
\varphi
and
l
satisfy (
8.1
). Then,
(\chi_{1})
with
\chi_{1}>-1
implies (
8.2
). Moreover,
(\chi_{2})
with
\chi_{2}\geq 0
implies

t^{-\chi_{2}}\frac{\varphi(t)}{l(t)}\in L^{\infty}\big((0,1)\big).

(8.17)

In particular,
\varphi(t)/l(t)\rightarrow 0
as
t\rightarrow 0
whenever
\chi_{2}>0
.

Proof.

Assume
(\chi_{1})
. By definition, there exists
C\geq 1
such that

0<s^{1-\chi_{1}}\frac{\varphi^{\prime}(st)}{l(st)}\leq C\frac{\varphi^{\prime}(t)}{l(t)}\quad\forall t\in\mathbb{R}^{+}\,\,s\in(0,1],

or, equivalently,

s^{1-\chi_{1}}\frac{\varphi^{\prime}(st)}{l(st)}\geq C^{-1}\frac{\varphi^{\prime}(t)}{l(t)}\quad\forall t\in\mathbb{R}^{+}\,\,s\in[1,\infty).

(8.18)

Setting
t=1
, if
\chi_{1}>-1
we deduce that
\frac{s\varphi^{\prime}(s)}{l(s)}\in L^{1}(0+)\setminus L^{1}(\infty)
, that is, (
8.2
). In an similar way, if
(\chi_{2})
holds then

\frac{\varphi(st)}{l(st)}s^{-\chi_{2}}\leq C\frac{\varphi(t)}{l(t)}\quad\forall t\in\mathbb{R}^{+}\,\,s\in(0,1],

and (
8.17
) follows by setting
t=1
.
∎

Lemma 8.16
.

Assume that
\varphi
and
l
satisfy (
8.1
), and let
F
be a positive function
defined on
(\bar{\eta}_{0},\infty)
. If
(\chi_{1})
holds with
\chi_{1}>-1
, then there
exists a constant
B\geq 1
such that, for every
\sigma\leq 1
,

\frac{\sigma^{\frac{1}{\chi_{1}+1}}}{K^{-1}(\sigma F(t))}\leq\frac{B}{K^{-1}(F(t))}\quad\text{ on }(\bar{\eta}_{0},\infty).

(8.19)

Proof.

According to Lemma
8.15
,
(\chi_{1})
with
\chi_{1}>-1
implies (
8.2
), so
K^{-1}
is well defined on
\mathbb{R}^{+}_{0}
. Changing variables in the definition of
K
, and using (
8.18
) above,
for every
\lambda\geq 1
and
t\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
we have

\begin{split}K(\lambda t)&=\int_{0}^{\lambda t}s\frac{\varphi^{\prime}(s)}{l(s)}\mathrm{d}s=\lambda^{2}\int_{0}^{t}s\frac{\varphi^{\prime}(\lambda s)}{l(\lambda s)}\mathrm{d}s\\
&\geq C^{-1}\lambda^{\chi_{1}+1}\int_{0}^{t}s\frac{\varphi^{\prime}(s)}{l(s)}\mathrm{d}s=C^{-1}\lambda^{\chi_{1}+1}K(t),\end{split}

where
C\geq 1
is the constant in
(\chi_{1})
. Applying
K^{-1}
to both sides of the above inequality, and setting

t=K^{-1}(\sigma F(s))
we deduce

\lambda K^{-1}(\sigma F(s))\geq K^{-1}(\lambda^{\chi_{1}+1}\sigma C^{-1}F(s)),

whence, setting
\lambda=(C/\sigma)^{1/(\chi_{1}+1)}\geq 1
, the required conclusion follows with
B=C^{1/(\chi_{1}+1)}
.
∎

We are ready to construct blowing-up supersolutions that remain close to the constant
\bar{\eta}_{0}
in (
8.1
) on an arbitrarily fixed annulus. The construction is an improvement of the one in
[
95
, Prop. 3.4]
, and at the same a simplification of it. We recall that
F
is defined as in (
8.3
).

Proposition 8.17
.

Assume (
8.1
) and (
8.16
). Suppose further
(\chi_{1})
and
(\chi_{2})
with

\chi_{1}>0,\qquad\chi_{2}>0,

and let
\beta,\bar{\beta}
satisfy
(\beta\bar{\beta})
. Fix
\theta\in C([r_{0},\infty))
with the property that

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\displaystyle\frac{\bar{\beta}(r)^{\chi_{1}+1}}{\beta(r)}\in L^{\infty}([r_{0},\infty)),\\[14.22636pt]
\displaystyle\frac{\theta(r)\bar{\beta}(r)^{\chi_{2}}}{\beta(r)}\in L^{\infty}\big([r_{0},\infty)\big).\end{array}\right.

(8.20)

If (

KO
∞

) holds, then for each
\varepsilon>0
,
0<\delta<\lambda
and
r_{1}>r_{0}
, there exist
R_{1}>r_{1}
and a
C^{1}

function
w:[r_{0},R_{1})\to[\bar{\eta}_{0}+\delta,\infty)
solving

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\displaystyle\big(\varphi(w^{\prime})\big)^{\prime}+\theta(r)\varphi(w^{\prime})\leq\varepsilon\beta(r)f(w)l(w^{\prime})\qquad\text{on }\,[r_{0},R_{1})\\[8.5359pt]
\displaystyle w^{\prime}>0\ \ \text{ on }\,[r_{0},R_{1}),\qquad w(r)\rightarrow+\infty\ \ \text{ as }r\to R_{1}^{-}\\[8.5359pt]
\displaystyle\bar{\eta}_{0}+\delta\leq w\leq\bar{\eta}_{0}+\lambda\qquad\text{on }\,[r_{0},r_{1}].\end{array}\right.

(8.21)

Proof.

Note first of all that (

KO
∞

) is meaningful because, by Lemma
8.15
,
(\chi_{1})
with
\chi_{1}>-1
implies (
8.2
). Since
\bar{\beta}
is bounded on
[r_{0},\infty)
, by rescaling we can assume that
\bar{\beta}\leq 1
. For a given
\sigma\in(0,1]
to be specified later, set

C_{\sigma}=\int_{\bar{\eta}_{0}+\delta}^{\infty}\frac{\mathrm{d}s}{K^{-1}(\sigma F(s))},

(8.22)

which is well defined in view of (

KO
∞

), (
8.16
) and Lemma
3.6
. Since
\bar{\beta}\not\in L^{1}(\infty)
, there exists
R_{\sigma}>r_{0}
such that

C_{\sigma}=\int_{r_{0}}^{R_{\sigma}}\bar{\beta}(s)\mathrm{d}s.

We note that, by monotone convergence,
C_{\sigma}\rightarrow\infty
as
\sigma\rightarrow 0+,
and we can therefore choose
\sigma>0
small enough that
R_{\sigma}>r_{1}
. We let
w:[r_{0},R_{\sigma})\rightarrow[\bar{\eta}_{0}+\delta,\infty)
be
implicitly defined by the equation

\int_{r}^{R_{\sigma}}\bar{\beta}(s)\mathrm{d}s=\int_{w(r)}^{\infty}\frac{\mathrm{d}s}{K^{-1}(\sigma F(s))},

(8.23)

so that, by definition,

w(r_{0})=\bar{\eta}_{0}+\delta,\qquad w(r)\rightarrow+\infty\text{ as }\,r\rightarrow R_{\sigma}^{-}.

Differentiating (
8.23
) yields

w^{\prime}(r)=\bar{\beta}(r)K^{-1}\big(\sigma F(w(r))\big),

(8.24)

so that
w^{\prime}>0
on
[r_{0},R_{\sigma})
, and

\sigma F(w)=K(w^{\prime}/\bar{\beta}).

Differentiating once more, using the definition of
K
and (
8.24
), we obtain

\sigma f(w)w^{\prime}=K^{\prime}(w^{\prime}/\bar{\beta})(w^{\prime}/\bar{\beta})^{\prime}=\frac{w^{\prime}}{\bar{\beta}}\frac{\varphi^{\prime}(w^{\prime}/\bar{\beta})}{l(w^{\prime}/\bar{\beta})}\Bigl(\frac{w^{\prime}}{\bar{\beta}}\Bigr)^{\prime},

(8.25)

that is,

\varphi^{\prime}\left(\frac{w^{\prime}}{\bar{\beta}}\right)\left(\frac{w^{\prime}}{\bar{\beta}}\right)^{\prime}=\sigma\bar{\beta}f(w)l\left(\frac{w^{\prime}}{\bar{\beta}}\right)\qquad\text{on }\,[r_{0},R_{\sigma}).

(8.26)

Since
f>0
on
(\bar{\eta}_{0},\infty)
and
w^{\prime}>0
, we infer that
w^{\prime}/\bar{\beta}
is non-decreasing. Moreover, from
\bar{\beta}^{\prime}\leq 0
we deduce

\Bigl(\frac{w^{\prime}}{\bar{\beta}}\Bigr)^{\prime}=\frac{w^{\prime\prime}}{\bar{\beta}}-\frac{w^{\prime}\bar{\beta}^{\prime}}{\bar{\beta}^{2}}\geq\frac{w^{\prime\prime}}{\bar{\beta}}.

Inserting this into (
8.26
), using
\bar{\beta}\leq 1
and
(\chi_{1})
(in the form of (
8.18
)), and rearranging we obtain

\varphi^{\prime}(w^{\prime})w^{\prime\prime}\leq\left\{C\sigma\frac{\bar{\beta}^{\chi_{1}+1}}{\beta}\right\}\beta f(w)l(w^{\prime})\qquad\text{on }\,[r_{0},R_{\sigma}).

(8.27)

We next integrate (
8.26
) on
[r_{0},r]
, and we use (
8.16
) coupled with the monotonicity of both
w
and
w/\bar{\beta}
to deduce

\varphi\left(\frac{w^{\prime}}{\bar{\beta}}\right)\leq\varphi\left(\frac{w^{\prime}}{\bar{\beta}}\right)(r_{0})+C^{2}\sigma f(w)l\left(\frac{w^{\prime}}{\bar{\beta}}\right)\int_{r_{0}}^{r}\bar{\beta}\mathrm{d}s,

(8.28)

and thus using
(\chi_{2})
,
\bar{\beta}\leq 1
and
w(r_{0})=\bar{\eta}_{0}+\delta
we get

\begin{split}\frac{\varphi(w^{\prime})}{l(w^{\prime})}&\mathrel{{\mathop{\leq}\limits}}C^{2}\bar{\beta}^{\chi_{2}}\frac{\varphi(w^{\prime}/\bar{\beta})}{l(w^{\prime}/\bar{\beta})}\\
&\leq C^{2}\bar{\beta}^{\chi_{2}}\Bigl[\frac{\varphi(w^{\prime}/\bar{\beta})(r_{0})}{l(w^{\prime}/\bar{\beta})}+C^{2}\sigma f(w)\int_{r_{0}}^{r}\bar{\beta}\mathrm{d}s\Bigr]\\
&\mathrel{{\mathop{\leq}\limits}}C^{2}\frac{\bar{\beta}^{\chi_{2}}}{\beta}\Bigl[C^{2}\frac{\varphi(w^{\prime}/\bar{\beta})(r_{0})}{f(\bar{\eta}_{0}+\delta)l(w^{\prime}/\bar{\beta})(r_{0})}+C^{2}\sigma\int_{r_{0}}^{r}\bar{\beta}\mathrm{d}s\Bigr]\beta f(w)\\
&\mathrel{{\mathop{\leq}\limits}}C^{4}\frac{\bar{\beta}^{\chi_{2}}}{\beta}\Bigl[\frac{\varphi(w^{\prime}/\bar{\beta})(r_{0})}{f(\bar{\eta}_{0}+\delta)l(w^{\prime}/\bar{\beta})(r_{0})}+\sigma\int_{\bar{\eta}_{0}+\delta}^{\infty}\frac{\mathrm{d}s}{K^{-1}(\sigma F(s))}\Bigr]\beta f(w).\end{split}

(8.29)

Next, we use (
8.24
) with
r=r_{0}
and the fact that, by Lemma
8.16
,

\sigma\int_{\bar{\eta}_{0}+\delta}^{\infty}\frac{\mathrm{d}s}{K^{-1}(\sigma F(s))}\leq B\sigma^{\frac{\chi_{1}}{\chi_{1}+1}}\int_{\bar{\eta}_{0}+\delta}^{\infty}\frac{\mathrm{d}s}{K^{-1}(F(s))}

to obtain

\begin{split}\varphi(w^{\prime})&\leq C_{1}\frac{\bar{\beta}^{\chi_{2}}}{\beta}\Bigl[\frac{\varphi(K^{-1}(\sigma F(\bar{\eta}_{0}+\delta)))}{f(\bar{\eta}_{0}+\delta)l(K^{-1}(\sigma F(\bar{\eta}_{0}+\delta)))}+\sigma^{\frac{\chi_{1}}{\chi_{1}+1}}\int_{\bar{\eta}_{0}+\delta}^{\infty}\frac{\mathrm{d}s}{K^{-1}(F(s))}\Bigr]\beta f(w)l(w^{\prime})\\
&=C_{1}\hat{C}_{\sigma}\frac{\bar{\beta}^{\chi_{2}}}{\beta}\beta f(w)l(w^{\prime})\end{split}

(8.30)

for some
C_{1}>0
, and where we set

\hat{C}_{\sigma}=\frac{\varphi(K^{-1}(\sigma F(\bar{\eta}_{0}+\delta)))}{f(\bar{\eta}_{0}+\delta)l(K^{-1}(\sigma F(\bar{\eta}_{0}+\delta)))}+\sigma^{\frac{\chi_{1}}{\chi_{1}+1}}\int_{\bar{\eta}_{0}+\delta}^{\infty}\frac{\mathrm{d}s}{K^{-1}(F(s))}.

(8.31)

Putting together (
8.27
) and (
8.30
), and using (
8.20
), we obtain

\begin{array}[]{l}\displaystyle\big(\varphi(w^{\prime})\big)^{\prime}+\theta(r)\varphi(w^{\prime})\\[8.5359pt]
\quad\leq\displaystyle\left\{C\sigma\left\|\frac{\bar{\beta}^{\chi_{1}+1}}{\beta}\right\|_{L^{\infty}([r_{0},\infty))}+C_{1}\hat{C}_{\sigma}\left\|\frac{\theta\bar{\beta}^{\chi_{2}}}{\beta}\right\|_{L^{\infty}([r_{0},\infty))}\right\}\beta f(w)l(w^{\prime}).\end{array}

(8.32)

Because of
(\chi_{2})
with
\chi_{2}>0
and Lemma
8.15
,
\hat{C}_{\sigma}\rightarrow 0
as
\sigma\rightarrow 0
, and we can choose
\sigma
small enough such that the differential inequality in (
8.21
) is satisfied. To prove the last condition in (
8.21
), simply observe that by (
8.23
)

\int_{r_{0}}^{r_{1}}\bar{\beta}(s)\mathrm{d}s=\int_{\bar{\eta}_{0}+\delta}^{w(r_{1})}\frac{\mathrm{d}s}{K^{-1}(\sigma F(s))},

thus
w(r_{1})\rightarrow\bar{\eta}_{0}+\delta
as
\sigma\rightarrow 0
. Since
w
is increasing, it is enough to choose
\sigma
in such a way that

\int_{r_{0}}^{r_{1}}\bar{\beta}(s)\mathrm{d}s<\int_{\bar{\eta}_{0}+\delta}^{\bar{\eta}_{0}+\lambda}\frac{\mathrm{d}s}{K^{-1}(\sigma F(s))}.

∎

Remark 8.18
.

If we weaken our assumptions on
\chi_{1},\chi_{2}
to

\chi_{1}\geq 0,\quad\chi_{2}\geq 0\quad\text{and}\quad\chi_{1}\chi_{2}=0,

assume further

\limsup_{t\rightarrow+\infty}f(t)=+\infty.

(8.33)

Then, one can still guarantee the existence of a
divergent
sequence
\delta_{j}\rightarrow\infty
such that, for each
\delta\in\{\delta_{j}\}
and
\lambda>\delta
, there exists
w
solving (
8.21
). Indeed, all is needed to conclude the proof is that
\hat{C}_{\sigma}
can be made arbitrarily small for suitable
\sigma
and
\delta
. First, using
(\chi_{2})
and Lemma
8.15
, choose
\delta_{j}
so that

\frac{1}{f(\bar{\eta}_{0}+\delta_{j})}\left\|\frac{\varphi}{l}\right\|_{L^{\infty}([0,1])}+\int_{\bar{\eta}_{0}+\delta_{j}}^{\infty}\frac{\mathrm{d}s}{K^{-1}(F(s))}

is small enough, and then choose
\sigma
in such a way that
K^{-1}(\sigma F(\bar{\eta}_{0}+\delta))\leq 1
. The coupling of these two conditions guarantee the smallness of
\hat{C}_{\sigma}
.

Remark 8.19
.

It is interesting to compare Proposition
8.17
with the corresponding Proposition
7.8
for the compact support principle. Although their underlying idea is the same, the two constructions are
not specular
, neither are the conditions on
\varphi,f,l
. The reason is that, while the monotonicity of the supersolution changes, the weight
\bar{\beta}
is still decreasing. To grasp the core of the technical problem, we invite the interested reader to try to prove Proposition
7.8
by following the estimates in Proposition
8.17
, suitably replacing
(\chi_{1})
and
(\chi_{2})
in a neighbourhood of zero.

We can now investigate the validity of
(\mathrm{SL})
.

Theorem 8.20
.

Let
M^{m}
be a complete Riemannian manifold of dimension
m\geq 2
such that, for some origin
o\in M
, the distance function
r(x)
from
o
satisfies

\mathrm{Ric}(\nabla r,\nabla r)\geq-(m-1)\kappa^{2}\big(1+r^{2}\big)^{\alpha/2}\qquad\text{on }\,\mathcal{D}_{o},

(8.34)

for some
\kappa\geq 0
and
\alpha\geq-2
. Let
\varphi,f,l
meet (
8.1
), (
8.16
), and assume
(\chi_{1})
and
(\chi_{2})
with

\chi_{1}>0,\qquad\chi_{2}>0.

(8.35)

Consider
0<b\in C(M)
such that

b(x)\geq C_{1}\big(1+r(x)\big)^{-\mu}\qquad\text{on }\,M,

for some constants
C_{1}>0
,
\mu\in\mathbb{R}
satisfying

\mu\leq\min\left\{\chi_{1}+1,\chi_{2}-\frac{\alpha}{2}\right\}.

(8.36)

If (

KO
∞

) holds, then any non-constant solution
u\in C^{1}(M)
of
(P_{\geq})
is bounded above and
f(u^{*})\leq 0
. In particular, if
f>0
on
\mathbb{R}^{+}
then
(\mathrm{SL})
holds for
C^{1}
solutions.

Proof.

We first prove that
u
is bounded above. By contradiction, let
u^{*}=\infty
and consider a geodesic ball
B_{r_{0}}
centered at
o
, with
r_{0}>0
. Fix
0<\delta<\lambda
and
\bar{x}\not\in B_{r_{0}}
enjoying

u_{0}^{*}=\max_{B_{r_{0}}}u\leq\bar{\eta}_{0}+\delta,\qquad u(\bar{x})>\bar{\eta}_{0}+\lambda,

(8.37)

and choose
r_{1}
in such a way that
\bar{x}\in B_{r_{1}}
. From (
8.34
) and the Laplacian comparison theorem,

\Delta r\leq Ar^{\alpha/2}\qquad\text{weakly on }\,M\backslash B_{\frac{r_{0}}{2}},

(8.38)

for some constant
A=A(r_{0},\alpha,\kappa,m)>0
. Applying Proposition
8.17
with

\theta(r)=Ar^{\alpha/2},\qquad\bar{\beta}(r)=(1+r)^{-1},\qquad\beta(r)=C_{1}(1+r)^{-\mu},\qquad\varepsilon=\frac{1}{2C}

we deduce the existence of
w
satisfying (
8.21
) (note that (
8.36
) guarantees (
8.20
)). Setting
\bar{w}(x)=w(r(x))
and taking into account
w^{\prime}>0
, (
8.38
) and
b\geq\beta(r)
,
\bar{w}
solves

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\Delta_{\varphi}\bar{w}\leq\frac{1}{2C}b(x)f(\bar{w})l(w^{\prime}(r))\qquad\text{on }\,B_{R_{1}}\backslash B_{r_{0}}\\[8.5359pt]
\displaystyle w^{\prime}(r)>0\ \ \text{ on }\,B_{R_{1}}\backslash B_{r_{0}},\qquad\bar{w}\rightarrow+\infty\ \ \text{ as }x\rightarrow\partial B_{R_{1}}\\[8.5359pt]
\displaystyle\bar{\eta}_{0}+\delta\leq\bar{w}\leq\bar{\eta}_{0}+\lambda\qquad\text{on }\,B_{r_{1}}\backslash B_{r_{0}}.\end{array}\right.

(8.39)

We compare
u
and
\bar{w}
on
B_{R_{1}}\backslash B_{r_{0}}
. By construction,
u\leq\bar{w}
on
\partial B_{r_{0}}
and
u-\bar{w}\rightarrow-\infty
approaching
\partial B_{R_{1}}
. On the other hand,

u(\bar{x})>\bar{\eta}_{0}+\lambda\geq\bar{w}(\bar{x}),

and thus
c=\max\{u-\bar{w}\}
is positive and attained on some compact set
\Gamma=\{u-\bar{w}=c\}\Subset B_{R_{1}}\backslash B_{r_{0}}
. For
\eta\in(0,c)
, consider
U_{\eta}=\{u-\bar{w}>\eta\}\Subset B_{R_{1}}\backslash B_{r_{0}}
. If
x\in\Gamma\backslash\mathrm{cut}(o)
, then
\bar{w}\in C^{1}
around
x
and therefore

\nabla u(x)=\nabla\bar{w}(x)=w^{\prime}(r(x)).

The same relation also holds if
x\in\mathrm{cut}(o)
by using Calabi’s trick (see the proof of Theorem
6.5
). Since both
\nabla u
and
w^{\prime}(r)
are continuous, for
\eta
close enough to
c
the inequality
|\nabla u|\geq w^{\prime}(r)/2
holds on
U_{\eta}
. From

\Delta_{\varphi}u\geq b(x)f(u)l(|\nabla u|)\geq\frac{1}{2C}b(x)f(\bar{w})l\big(w^{\prime}(r)\big)\geq\Delta_{\varphi}\bar{w}=\Delta_{\varphi}(\bar{w}+\eta),

we deduce by comparison that
u\leq\bar{w}+\eta
on
U_{\eta}
, contradiction.

It remains to prove that
f(u^{*})\leq 0
. If
f(u^{*})>K>0
, then choose an upper level set
\Omega_{\eta}
with
\eta<u^{*}
in such a way that
f(u)>K
on
\Omega_{\eta}
, and a continuous function
\bar{f}\leq f
with

\begin{array}[]{l}\displaystyle\bar{f}(\eta)=0,\qquad\bar{f}\ \text{ is positive and $C$-increasing on $(\eta,\infty)$},\\[5.69046pt]
\bar{f}\leq f\quad\text{on }\,(\eta,u^{*}),\qquad\bar{f}=f\quad\text{on }\,\big(\max\{\bar{\eta}_{0},u^{*}\},\infty).\end{array}

Then,
\bar{u}=\max\{u,\eta\}
satisfies

\Delta_{\varphi}\bar{u}\geq b(x)\bar{f}(\bar{u})l(|\nabla\bar{u}|)\qquad\text{on }\,M

(observe that
\bar{f}(\eta)l(0)=0
). Let
\bar{u}_{0}^{*}=\sup_{B_{r_{0}}}\bar{u}
, and note that
\bar{u}_{0}^{*}<\bar{u}^{*}
in view of the finite maximum principle applied to
\bar{u}^{*}-\bar{u}
. Fix
\lambda>0
such that
\bar{u}_{0}^{*}+2\lambda<\bar{u}^{*}-2\lambda
, set
\delta=\lambda/2
, let
\bar{x}
satisfying
\bar{u}(\bar{x})>\bar{u}^{*}-\lambda
and choose
r_{1}
big enough that
\bar{x}\in B_{r_{1}}
. With our choices,
\bar{x}
belongs to the relatively compact set
\{\bar{u}>\bar{w}\}
and, consequently, the desired contradiction is achieved by proceeding verbatim as in the case
u^{*}=\infty
, with
\bar{u}
replacing
u
,
\bar{u}_{0}^{*}
replacing
u_{0}^{*}
and with the same function
w
.
∎

Once the bound
u^{*}<\infty
is shown, an alternative way to conclude
f(u^{*})\leq 0
in Theorem
8.20
is to use Theorem
1.19
to ensure the validity of
(\mathrm{WMP}_{\infty})
. However, we should require the extra condition (
1.45
), that is avoided in the above argument. Nevertheless, this second approach is needed to deal with the relevant, borderline case when either
\chi_{1}
or
\chi_{2}
vanishes, that is considered in the next

Theorem 8.21
.

In the assumptions of Theorem
8.20
, suppose that (
8.35
) is replaced by

\chi_{1}\geq 0,\qquad\chi_{2}\geq 0,\qquad\chi_{1}\chi_{2}=0

and the validity of

\limsup_{t\rightarrow+\infty}f(t)=+\infty.

(8.40)

Assume that

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\displaystyle l(t)\geq C_{1}\frac{\varphi(t)}{t^{\chi_{2}}}&\qquad\text{on $\mathbb{R}^{+}$, for some $C_{1}>0$},\\[8.5359pt]
\displaystyle\varphi(t)\leq C_{2}t^{p-1}&\qquad\text{on $[0,1]$, for some }\,C_{2}>0,\ p>1,\\[8.5359pt]
\displaystyle\varphi(t)\leq\bar{C}_{2}t^{\bar{p}-1}&\qquad\text{on $[1,\infty)$, for some }\,\bar{C}_{2}>0,\ \bar{p}>1,\end{array}\right.

(8.41)

and that, besides (
8.36
), one of the following conditions is met:

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\alpha\geq-2,\quad\chi_{2}>0,\quad\text{or}\\[5.69046pt]
\alpha\geq-2,\quad\chi_{2}=0,\quad\mu<-\frac{\alpha}{2},\quad\text{or }\\[5.69046pt]
\alpha>-2,\quad\chi_{2}=0,\quad\mu=-\frac{\alpha}{2},\quad V_{\infty}=0,\quad\text{or }\\[5.69046pt]
\alpha=-2,\quad\chi_{2}=0,\quad\mu=-\frac{\alpha}{2},\quad V_{\infty}\leq p,\end{array}\right.

(8.42)

where

V_{\infty}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\displaystyle\liminf_{r\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\log\mathrm{vol}(B_{r})}{r^{1+\alpha/2}}&\quad\text{if }\,\alpha\geq-2,\\[11.38092pt]
\displaystyle\liminf_{r\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\log\mathrm{vol}(B_{r})}{\log r}&\quad\text{if }\,\alpha=-2.\end{array}\right.

Then, if (

KO
∞

) holds, any non-constant solution
u\in C^{1}(M)
of
(P_{\geq})
on
M
is bounded above and
f(u^{*})\leq 0
. In particular, if
f>0
on
\mathbb{R}^{+}
,
(\mathrm{SL})
holds for
C^{1}
solutions.

Proof.

Because of Remark
8.18
, for
\delta
large and suitably chosen we can still produce a solution
w
of (
8.21
), that gives rise to
\bar{w}
solving (
8.39
). Following the proof of Theorem
8.20
we obtain
u^{*}<\infty
. To conclude, we observe that we are in the position to apply Theorem
1.19
with the choice
\chi=\chi_{2}
: in this respect, note that the first requirement in (
1.44
) corresponds to the first in (
8.41
). The conclusion
f(u^{*})\leq 0
follows from the validity of
(\mathrm{WMP}_{\infty})
and Proposition
1.16
.
∎

Remark 8.22
.

By the Bishop-Gromov theorem (Theorem
9.7
and the subsequent remarks in the Appendix), (
8.34
) implies
V_{\infty}<\infty
and in particular, if
\alpha=-2
,

V_{\infty}\leq(m-1)\bar{\kappa}+1\qquad\text{with}\qquad\bar{\kappa}=\frac{1+\sqrt{1+4\kappa^{2}}}{2}.

(8.43)

Therefore, condition
V_{\infty}\leq p
in the last of (
8.42
) is implied by
\bar{\kappa}\leq\frac{p-1}{m-1}
.

Remark 8.23
.

One could alternatively use Theorem
1.14
to conclude
f(u^{*})\leq 0
. Doing so, on the one hand (
8.41
) would weaken to (
1.39
), requiring
\varphi,l
only on
[0,1]
, but on the other hand the conclusion in the case
(\chi_{2}=)\ \chi=0
in (
1.41
) is only possible under the Euclidean type behaviour
\alpha=-2
.

We conclude this section with some comments on Theorem
8.20
. We begin with the following corollary for the
p
-Laplace operator, a slight improvement of
[
95
, Cor. A1]
.

Corollary 8.24
.

Let
M^{m}
be complete and satisfying

\mathrm{Ric}(\nabla r,\nabla r)\geq-(m-1)\kappa^{2}\big(1+r^{2}\big)^{\alpha/2}\qquad\text{on }\,\mathcal{D}_{o},

for some
\kappa\geq 0
,
\alpha\geq-2
and some origin
o
. Fix
p>1
and
\chi\in(0,p-1]
. Consider
0<b\in C(M)
and
f\in C(\mathbb{R})
such that

\begin{array}[]{l}\displaystyle b(x)\geq C_{1}\big(1+r(x)\big)^{-\mu}\qquad\text{on }\,M,\\[8.5359pt]
f(0)=0,\qquad f>0\ \text{ and $C$-increasing on }\,\mathbb{R}^{+}.\end{array}

for some constants
C,C_{1}>0
and

\mu\leq\chi-\frac{\alpha}{2}.

If the Keller-Osserman condition

F(t)^{-\frac{1}{\chi+1}}\in L^{1}(\infty)

is met, then
(\mathrm{SL})
holds for
C^{1}
solutions of

\Delta_{p}u\geq b(x)f(u)|\nabla u|^{p-1-\chi}.

The same conclusion holds if
\chi=0
, provided that
M
satisfies one of the next further conditions: either

\begin{array}[]{ll}\alpha>-2,&\qquad\displaystyle\liminf_{r\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\log\mathrm{vol}(B_{r})}{r^{1+\alpha/2}}=0,\qquad\text{or }\\[11.38092pt]
\alpha=-2,&\displaystyle\qquad\bar{\kappa}\leq\frac{p-1}{m-1},\end{array}

with
\bar{\kappa}
as in (
8.43
).

Proof.

It is a direct application of Theorems
8.20
,
8.21
and Remark
8.22
. If
\chi=0
, note that (
8.40
) follows from
F^{-\frac{1}{\chi+1}}\in L^{1}(\infty)
.
∎

Moving to consider the mean curvature operator, a substantial problem arises: in view of (
8.16
),
l
is bounded from below in a neighbourhood of infinity, and thus
K_{\infty}<\infty
and (
KO∞
) is meaningless. To overcome the problem and be able to include inequalities of the type

\mathrm{div}\left(\frac{\nabla u}{\sqrt{1+|\nabla u|^{2}}}\right)\geq b(x)f(u)|\nabla u|^{q},

(8.44)

taking into account that the mean curvature operator satisfies

t\varphi^{\prime}(t)\leq C\varphi(t)\qquad\text{on $\mathbb{R}^{+}$}

(8.45)

for some
C>0
, the authors in
[
95
, Sect. 4]
propose to replace
t\varphi^{\prime}(t)
with
\varphi(t)
in the definition of
K
in (
1.19
). In this way, the corresponding (
KO∞
) makes sense for some classes of
C
-increasing
l
. As we shall see in a moment, this seemingly “rough” replacement allows indeed to obtain a sharp result, but in the course of the proof in
[
95
]
the authors lose optimality in some inequalities, and consequently their main result (Corollary A2 therein) is not sharp. We now describe how to achieve the optimal range of parameters. Clearly, the bulk is to get an analogue of Proposition
8.17
for mean curvature type operators and
not requiring that
l
be
C
-increasing.
Note that the
C
-monotonicity of
l
is essential to obtain inequality (
8.28
). We restrict to consider the relevant case of operators satisfying (
8.45
) and gradient terms
l
of the type

l(t)=\frac{\varphi(t)}{t^{\chi}},

for
\chi>0
small enough to make
l
continuous at
t=0
. Observe that
\varphi
may vanish both at
t=0
and at infinity. Following the idea in
[
95
]
, we set

K(t)=\int_{0}^{t}\frac{\varphi(s)}{l(s)}\asymp t^{\chi+1}

and the Keller-Osserman condition becomes
F^{-\frac{1}{\chi+1}}\in L^{1}(\infty)
, with
F
as in (
8.3
).

Proposition 8.25
.

Let
\varphi
satisfy (
8.1
) and (
8.45
). Fix
\chi>0
,
f\in C(\mathbb{R})
satisfying

f>0\ \ \ \text{ and $C$-increasing on $(\bar{\eta}_{0},\infty)$, for some $\bar{\eta}_{0}>0$,}

and
\beta,\bar{\beta}
satisfying
(\beta\bar{\beta})
. Let
\theta\in C([r_{0},\infty))
with the property that

\displaystyle\frac{\max\{\bar{\beta}(r),\theta(r)\}\cdot\bar{\beta}(r)^{\chi}}{\beta(r)}\in L^{\infty}([r_{0},\infty)).

(8.46)

If the Keller-Osserman condition

F^{-\frac{1}{\chi+1}}\in L^{1}(\infty)

holds, then for each
\varepsilon>0
,
0<\delta<\lambda
and
r_{1}>r_{0}
, there exist
R_{1}>r_{1}
and a
C^{1}

function
w:[r_{0},R_{1})\to[\bar{\eta}_{0}+\delta,\infty)
solving

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\displaystyle\big(\varphi(w^{\prime})\big)^{\prime}+\theta(r)\varphi(w^{\prime})\leq\varepsilon\beta(r)f(w)\frac{\varphi(w^{\prime})}{[w^{\prime}]^{\chi}}\qquad\text{on }\,[r_{0},R_{1})\\[8.5359pt]
\displaystyle w^{\prime}>0\ \ \text{ on }\,[r_{0},R_{1}),\qquad w(r)\rightarrow+\infty\ \ \text{ as }r\to R_{1}^{-}\\[8.5359pt]
\displaystyle\bar{\eta}_{0}+\delta\leq w\leq\bar{\eta}_{0}+\lambda\qquad\text{on }\,[r_{0},r_{1}].\end{array}\right.

(8.47)

Proof.

We proceed as in Proposition (
8.17
), so we skip some of the details and just concentrate on the main differences. For
\sigma\in(0,1]
to be specified later, set

C_{\sigma}=\int_{\bar{\eta}_{0}+\delta}^{\infty}[\sigma F(s)]^{-\frac{1}{\chi+1}}\mathrm{d}s,

(8.48)

and since
\bar{\beta}\not\in L^{1}(\infty)
, pick
R_{\sigma}>r_{0}
such that

C_{\sigma}=\int_{r_{0}}^{R_{\sigma}}\bar{\beta}(s)\mathrm{d}s.

We can choose
\sigma>0
small enough that
R_{\sigma}>r_{1}
. We let
w:[r_{0},R_{\sigma})\rightarrow[\bar{\eta}_{0}+\delta,\infty)
be
implicitly defined by the equation

\int_{r}^{R_{\sigma}}\bar{\beta}(s)\mathrm{d}s=\int_{w(r)}^{\infty}[\sigma F(s)]^{-\frac{1}{\chi+1}}\mathrm{d}s.

(8.49)

Differentiating and rearranging,

\sigma F(w)=[w^{\prime}/\bar{\beta}]^{\chi+1}.

Set
l(t)=\varphi(t)/t^{\chi}
. A second differentiation gives

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\displaystyle\sigma f(w)w^{\prime}&=&\displaystyle(\chi+1)(w^{\prime}/\bar{\beta})^{\chi}(w^{\prime}/\bar{\beta})^{\prime}=(\chi+1)\frac{\varphi(w^{\prime})}{l(w^{\prime})}\bar{\beta}^{-\chi}(w^{\prime}/\bar{\beta})^{\prime}\\[8.5359pt]
&\geq&\displaystyle(\chi+1)\frac{\varphi(w^{\prime})}{l(w^{\prime})}w^{\prime\prime}\bar{\beta}^{-\chi-1},\end{array}

(8.50)

where we used that
w^{\prime}/\beta
is increasing by the first equality in (
8.50
), and
\bar{\beta}^{\prime}\leq 0
by
(\beta\bar{\beta})
. We next use (
8.45
) and simplify to deduce

\varphi^{\prime}(w^{\prime})w^{\prime\prime}\leq\left\{c_{1}\sigma\frac{\bar{\beta}^{\chi+1}}{\beta}\right\}\beta f(w)l(w^{\prime}),

(8.51)

for some constant
c_{1}>0
independent of
\sigma
. On the other hand, from the first equality in (
8.50
) we deduce

\sigma f(w)\bar{\beta}=(\chi+1)(w^{\prime}/\bar{\beta})^{\chi-1}(w^{\prime}/\bar{\beta})^{\prime},

thus integrating on
[r_{0},r]
and using the
C
-monotonicity of
f
we get

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\displaystyle\frac{\chi+1}{\chi}(w^{\prime}/\bar{\beta})^{\chi}&=&\displaystyle\frac{\chi+1}{\chi}(w^{\prime}/\bar{\beta})(r_{0})^{\chi}+\sigma\int_{r_{0}}^{r}f(w)\bar{\beta}\\[11.38092pt]
&\leq&\displaystyle\frac{\chi+1}{\chi}(w^{\prime}/\bar{\beta})(r_{0})^{\chi}+C\sigma f(w(r))\int_{r_{0}}^{r}\bar{\beta}.\end{array}

Therefore, from
\bar{\beta}\leq 1
(we can always assumed it, up to rescaling) we get

\frac{\varphi(w^{\prime})}{l(w^{\prime})}=[w^{\prime}]^{\chi}\leq\bar{\beta}^{\chi}(w^{\prime}/\bar{\beta})(r_{0})^{\chi}+c_{2}\sigma\bar{\beta}^{\chi}f(w)\int_{r_{0}}^{r}\bar{\beta},

for some constant
c_{2}>0
independent of
\sigma
. Rearranging, by (
8.49
) and the
C
-monotonicity of
f
we deduce

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\displaystyle\varphi(w^{\prime})&\leq&\displaystyle\frac{\bar{\beta}^{\chi}}{\beta}\left\{C\frac{(w^{\prime}/\bar{\beta})(r_{0})^{\chi}}{f(\bar{\eta}_{0}+\delta)}+c_{2}\sigma\int_{r_{0}}^{r}\bar{\beta}\right\}\beta f(w)l(w^{\prime})\\[14.22636pt]
&\leq&\displaystyle\frac{\bar{\beta}^{\chi}}{\beta}\left\{C\frac{(w^{\prime}/\bar{\beta})(r_{0})^{\chi}}{f(\bar{\eta}_{0}+\delta)}+c_{2}\sigma\int_{\bar{\eta}_{0}+\delta}^{\infty}[\sigma F(s)]^{-\frac{1}{\chi+1}}\mathrm{d}s\right\}\beta f(w)l(w^{\prime})\\[14.22636pt]
\end{array}

Coupling with (
8.51
) and using (
8.46
), we finally infer

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\displaystyle\varphi^{\prime}(w^{\prime})w^{\prime\prime}+\theta(r)\varphi(w^{\prime})&\leq&\displaystyle\left\{c_{1}\sigma\left\|\frac{\bar{\beta}^{\chi+1}}{\beta}\right\|_{L^{\infty}([r_{0},\infty))}+\left[C\frac{(w^{\prime}/\bar{\beta})(r_{0})^{\chi}}{f(\bar{\eta}_{0}+\delta)}\right.\right.\\[14.22636pt]
&&\displaystyle\left.\left.+c_{2}\sigma^{\frac{\chi}{\chi+1}}\int_{\bar{\eta}_{0}+\delta}^{\infty}[F(s)]^{-\frac{1}{\chi+1}}\mathrm{d}s\right]\left\|\frac{\theta\bar{\beta}^{\chi}}{\beta}\right\|_{L^{\infty}([r_{0},\infty))}\right\}\beta f(w)l(w^{\prime}).\end{array}

The desired conclusions now follow verbatim from the arguments in Proposition
8.17
.
∎

Remark 8.26
.

We point out that
l(t)=\varphi(t)/t^{\chi}
can be singular at
t=0
. Indeed, by construction
w^{\prime}>0
on
[r_{0},R_{1})
and the continuity of
l
at
t=0
is not needed.

Once Proposition
8.25
is established, we proceed as in Theorem
8.20
to obtain the following result, that also applies to mean curvature type operators. In particular, a direct application of the next result yields Theorem
1.28
in the Introduction.

Theorem 8.27
.

Let
M^{m}
be complete and satisfying

\mathrm{Ric}(\nabla r,\nabla r)\geq-(m-1)\kappa^{2}\big(1+r^{2}\big)^{\alpha/2}\qquad\text{on }\,\mathcal{D}_{o},

(8.52)

for some
\kappa\geq 0
,
\alpha\geq-2
and some origin
o
. Let
\varphi,l
meet (
8.1
) and

t\varphi^{\prime}(t)\leq C_{2}\varphi(t),\qquad l(t)\geq C_{1}\frac{\varphi(t)}{t^{\chi}}\qquad\text{on }\,\mathbb{R}^{+},

(8.53)

for some constants
C_{1},C_{2}>0
and
\chi>0
. Consider
0<b\in C(M)
such that

b(x)\geq C_{3}\big(1+r(x)\big)^{-\mu}\qquad\text{on }\,M,

for some constants
C_{3}>0
,
\mu\in\mathbb{R}
satisfying

\mu\leq\chi-\frac{\alpha}{2}.

(8.54)

Then, under the validity of the Keller-Osserman condition

F^{-\frac{1}{\chi+1}}\in L^{1}(\infty)

(8.55)

with
F
as in (
8.3
), any non-constant solution
u\in C^{1}(M)
of
(P_{\geq})
on
M
is bounded above and
f(u^{*})\leq 0
. In particular, if
f>0
on
\mathbb{R}^{+}
then
(\mathrm{SL})
holds for
C^{1}
-solutions.

Remark 8.28
.

The continuity of
l
at
t=0
forces an upper bound on
\chi
by (
8.53
). If we appropriately define solutions of
(P_{\geq})
when
l
has a singularity, it is likely that the upper bound on
\chi
be removable or, at least, weakened. We will not pursue this issue here, and leave it to the interested reader.

Remark 8.29
.

The above proof of Proposition
8.25
fails if
\chi=0
, and thus, in this borderline case the possible validity of an analogous of Remark
8.18
and of Theorem
8.27
is yet to be investigated.

8.4
Sharpness

We now conclude this section by discussing the sharpness of Theorem
8.27
. Consider the polynomial case
f(t)=t^{\omega}
, for some
\omega\geq 0
. Then, (
8.55
) becomes

\omega>\chi.

(8.56)

We are going to contradict
(\mathrm{SL})
under the failure of (
8.56
), on a suitable manifold and for
\varphi,l,b,\chi,\mu,\alpha
meeting all of the remaining requirements in Theorem
8.27
. Let
(M,\mathrm{d}s_{g}^{2})
be a model manifold as in Subsection
5.3
, and suppose further that
\varphi^{\prime}\geq 0
on
\mathbb{R}^{+}
. Note that, because of (
5.87
) and the asymptotic behaviour
\Delta r\sim(m-1)/r
as
r\rightarrow 0
,

\Delta r\geq c(1+r^{2})^{\alpha/4}\qquad\text{on }\,M,

(8.57)

for some constant
c>0
. For
\sigma>1
define the smooth function
u=w(r)=(1+r^{2})^{\sigma}
. A direct computation using
\alpha\geq-2
and
\varphi^{\prime}\geq 0
,
w^{\prime},w^{\prime\prime}\geq 0
gives

\Delta_{\varphi}u=\varphi^{\prime}(w^{\prime})w^{\prime\prime}+\varphi(w^{\prime})\Delta r\geq c(1+r^{2})^{\alpha/4}\varphi(w^{\prime}).

Therefore,
u
solves

\Delta_{\varphi}u\geq C\big(1+r(x)\big)^{-\mu}u^{\omega}\frac{\varphi(|\nabla u|)}{|\nabla u|^{\chi}}\qquad\text{on }\,M,

(8.58)

for some
C>0
, if and only if

2\sigma(\omega-\chi)\leq\frac{\alpha}{2}+\mu-\chi.

(8.59)

Since the right-hand side is non-positive because of (
8.54
), (
8.59
) is always satisfied for some
\sigma
large enough if and only if

\left\{\begin{array}[]{lll}\omega<\chi,&\quad\text{for each}&\mu\leq\chi-\frac{\alpha}{2},\quad\text{or }\\[8.5359pt]
\omega=\chi&\quad\text{and}&\mu=\chi-\frac{\alpha}{2},\end{array}\right.

(8.60)

that proves the sharpness of (
8.56
). Also, the last restriction in the second of (
8.60
) is optimal: in fact, in Euclidean setting
\alpha=-2
, if
\omega=\chi
and
\mu<\chi+1
then entire solutions of (
8.58
) are constant if they have polynomial growth, see
[
50
, Thm. 12]
and also Example 4 at p. 4402 therein.

Remark 8.30
.

Differently from Theorem
8.27
, the above counterexample also works if
\chi=0
and
\omega=0
.

8.5
Volume growth and
(\mathrm{SL})

In this section, we study property
(\mathrm{SL})
for solutions
u
of
(P_{\geq})
when the condition on the Ricci curvature is replaced by a volume growth requirement, in the particular case when
f(t)\asymp t^{\omega}
and

l(t)\asymp\frac{\varphi(t)}{t^{\chi}}.

In this setting, Theorem
8.27
and the subsequent remarks show that a sharp Keller-Osserman condition to guarantee the boundedness of
u
and
f(u^{*})\leq 0
is (
8.55
), that is,
\omega>\chi
. The condition is optimal also for the mean curvature operator. However, a quite interesting phenomenon happens in this case: we begin by commenting on the following Liouville theorem for solutions of
(P_{\geq})
, specific to mean curvature type operators and polynomial volume growths, where
no Keller-Osserman condition
is needed on
f
nor growth requirements are imposed on
u
. The result considers
(P_{\geq})
with a borderline gradient dependence
l(t)\geq C_{2}\varphi(t)
on
\mathbb{R}^{+}
. Its proof is inspired by the original one due to Tkachev in
[
144
]
for
b\equiv 1
,
l\equiv 1
, later extended in
[
141
]
.

Theorem 8.31
.

Let
M
be a complete Riemannian manifold, and consider

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\varphi\in C(\mathbb{R}^{+}_{0}),\qquad 0\leq\varphi\leq C_{1}\quad\text{on }\,\mathbb{R}^{+}_{0};\\[5.69046pt]
f\in C(\mathbb{R}),\qquad f\ \text{ non-decreasing on }\,\mathbb{R};\\[5.69046pt]
l\in C(\mathbb{R}^{+}_{0}),\qquad l(t)\geq C_{2}\varphi(t)\quad\text{on }\,\mathbb{R}^{+}_{0}.\end{array}\right.

for some constant
C_{1},C_{2}>0
. Fix
b\in C(M)
satisfying

b(x)\geq C\big(1+r(x)\big)^{-\mu}\qquad\text{on }\,M,

for some constants
C>0
,
\mu<1
. Let
u\in\mathrm{Lip}_{\mathrm{loc}}(M)
be a non-constant solution of

\Delta_{\varphi}u\geq b(x)f(u)l(|\nabla u|)\qquad\text{on }\,M.

(8.61)

If

\liminf_{r\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\log\mathrm{vol}(B_{r})}{\log r}<\infty

(8.62)

Then

f(u)\varphi(|\nabla u|)\leq 0\qquad\text{on }\,M.

In particular, if
\varphi>0
on
\mathbb{R}^{+}
, then
f(u)\leq 0
on
M
.

Furthermore, under the same assumptions, if
u\in\mathrm{Lip}_{\mathrm{loc}}(M)
is a non-constant solution of
(P_{=})
then

f(u)\varphi(|\nabla u|)\equiv 0\qquad\text{on }\,M,

and
f(u)\equiv 0
provided that
\varphi>0
on
\mathbb{R}^{+}
.

Proof.

Let
\{f_{k}\}
be a sequence of locally Lipschitz functions converging pointwise to
f
from below: for instance, one can choose

f_{k}(t)=\inf_{y\in[t-1,t+1]}\Big\{f(y)+k|t-y|\Big\}.

Since
f
is increasing, up to replacing
f_{k}
with
\bar{f}_{k}(t)=\sup_{(-\infty,t)}f_{k}
we can further suppose that
f_{k}
is increasing for each
k
. From (
8.61
) we deduce

\Delta_{\varphi}u\geq b(x)f_{k}(u)l(|\nabla u|)\qquad\text{on }\,M.

(8.63)

Fix a divergent sequence
\{R_{j}\}
such that
\{2R_{j}\}
realizes the liminf in (
8.62
), and let
d_{0},C
be positive constants such that

\mathrm{vol}\big(B_{2R_{j}}\big)\leq CR_{j}^{d_{0}}\qquad\text{for each }\,j.

(8.64)

Suppose that the set
U=\{x:f(u(x))>0\}
is non-empty, otherwise the thesis directly follows. We are going to prove that
f(u)\varphi(|\nabla u|)=0
on
U
. Fix a cut-off function
0\leq\psi\in\mathrm{Lip}_{c}(M)
whose support intersects
U
, and choose to test the weak formulation of (
8.63
) the function

\phi=\big(f_{k}(u)\big)_{+}^{\alpha-1}\psi,

with
(f_{k})_{+}=\max\{f_{k},0\}
the positive part of
f_{k}
, and with
\alpha
a fixed number satisfying

\alpha>\max\left\{4,d_{0},\frac{d_{0}-\mu}{1-\mu}\right\}.

Define the open set
U_{k}=\{x:f_{k}(u(x))>0\}
and note that
U_{k}\uparrow U
by the monotone convergence of
f_{k}
, thus
U_{k}\neq\emptyset
and
\phi\not\equiv 0
on
U_{k}
for large
k
. Using
l(t)\geq C_{2}\varphi(t)
, we obtain

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\displaystyle C_{2}\int_{U_{k}}b|f_{k}(u)|^{\alpha}\varphi(|\nabla u|)\psi&\leq&\displaystyle\int_{U_{k}}b|f_{k}(u)|^{\alpha}l(|\nabla u|)\psi\\[14.22636pt]
&\leq&\displaystyle\displaystyle-\int_{U_{k}}f_{k}(u)^{\alpha-1}\langle\frac{\varphi(|\nabla u|)}{|\nabla u|}\nabla u,\nabla\psi\rangle\\[14.22636pt]
&&\displaystyle-(\alpha-1)\int_{U_{k}}f_{k}(u)^{\alpha-2}f_{k}^{\prime}(u)\psi\varphi(|\nabla u|)|\nabla u|\\[14.22636pt]
&\leq&\displaystyle-\int_{U_{k}}f_{k}(u)^{\alpha-1}\langle\frac{\varphi(|\nabla u|)}{|\nabla u|}\nabla u,\nabla\psi\rangle,\end{array}

where we used
f_{k}^{\prime}\geq 0
,
\psi\geq 0
and
\varphi\geq 0
to get rid of the second integral in the right hand-side. Applying the Cauchy-Schwarz and Hölder inequalities, we thus get

\displaystyle\displaystyle C_{2}\int_{U_{k}}b|f_{k}(u)|^{\alpha}\varphi(|\nabla u|)\psi

\displaystyle\leq\displaystyle\int_{U_{k}}|f_{k}(u)|^{\alpha-1}\varphi(|\nabla u)|\nabla\psi|

\displaystyle\leq\displaystyle\left\{\int_{U_{k}}b|f_{k}(u)|^{\alpha}\varphi(|\nabla u|)\psi\right\}^{\frac{\alpha-1}{\alpha}}\left\{\int_{U_{k}}\varphi(|\nabla u|)b^{1-\alpha}\frac{|\nabla\psi|^{\alpha}}{\psi^{\alpha-1}}\right\}^{{1}/{\alpha}},

whence, rearranging and using the boundedness of
\varphi
,

\int_{U_{k}}b|f_{k}(u)|^{\alpha}\varphi(|\nabla u|)\psi\leq C_{3}\int_{U_{k}}b^{1-\alpha}\frac{|\nabla\psi|^{\alpha}}{\psi^{\alpha-1}}\leq C_{3}\int_{M}b^{1-\alpha}\frac{|\nabla\psi|^{\alpha}}{\psi^{\alpha-1}}

for some constant
C_{3}>0
depending on
\alpha
.
Let
\psi(x)=\psi_{j}(x)=\gamma(r(x)/R_{j})
, where
\gamma\in\mathrm{Lip}(\mathbb{R})
is such that

\gamma(t)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}1&\quad\text{on }\,[0,1]\\[5.69046pt]
(2-t)^{\alpha}&\quad\text{on }\,[1,2)\\[5.69046pt]
0&\quad\text{on }\,(2,\infty).\end{array}\right.

(8.65)

Note that
\psi_{j}\rightarrow 1
locally uniformly on
M
, and that
|\gamma^{\prime}|^{\alpha}/\gamma^{\alpha-1}=\alpha^{\alpha}
is bounded on
[1,2]
. Using our bounds on
b
, the coarea formula and integrating by parts, we deduce

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\displaystyle\int_{U_{k}}b|f_{k}(u)|^{\alpha}\varphi(|\nabla u|)\psi_{j}&\leq&\displaystyle\frac{C_{4}}{R_{j}^{\alpha}}\int_{R_{j}}^{2R_{j}}\mathrm{vol}(\partial B_{t})(1+t)^{\mu(\alpha-1)}\mathrm{d}t\\[14.22636pt]
&=&\displaystyle\frac{C_{4}}{R_{j}^{\alpha}}\left\{\left[\mathrm{vol}(B_{t})(1+t)^{\mu(\alpha-1)}\right]_{R_{j}}^{2R_{j}}\right.\\[14.22636pt]
&&\displaystyle\left.-\mu(\alpha-1)\int_{R_{j}}^{2R_{j}}\mathrm{vol}(B_{t})(1+t)^{\mu(\alpha-1)-1}\mathrm{d}t\right\}.\end{array}

(8.66)

for some constant
C_{4}>0
. From (
8.64
) we get

\begin{array}[]{l}\displaystyle\int_{U_{k}}b|f_{k}(u)|^{\alpha}\varphi(|\nabla u|)\psi_{j}\\[14.22636pt]
\qquad\leq\displaystyle\ \ \frac{C_{4}}{R_{j}^{\alpha}}\left\{C_{5}R_{j}^{d_{0}+\mu(\alpha-1)}-\mu(\alpha-1)\int_{R_{j}}^{2R_{j}}\mathrm{vol}(B_{t})(1+t)^{\mu(\alpha-1)-1}\mathrm{d}t\right\}.\end{array}

(8.67)

If
\mu\geq 0
we get rid of the integral in brackets, while if
\mu<0
we use inequality
\mathrm{vol}(B_{t})\leq\mathrm{vol}(B_{2R_{j}})
, integrate
(1+t)^{\mu(\alpha-1)-1}
and exploit (
8.64
). In both of the cases, from (
8.68
) we infer the existence of a constant
C_{6}>0
such that

\begin{array}[]{l}\displaystyle\int_{U_{k}}b|f_{k}(u)|^{\alpha}\varphi(|\nabla u|)\psi_{j}\leq C_{6}R_{j}^{d_{0}+\mu(\alpha-1)-\alpha},\end{array}

and letting
k\rightarrow\infty
we get

\begin{array}[]{l}\displaystyle\int_{U}b|f(u)|^{\alpha}\varphi(|\nabla u|)\psi_{j}\leq C_{6}R_{j}^{d_{0}+\mu(\alpha-1)-\alpha}.\end{array}

(8.68)

Because of our choice of
\alpha
, the exponent of
R_{j}
is negative. Letting
j\rightarrow\infty
and using
b>0
,
\psi_{j}\rightarrow 1
we deduce
f(u)\varphi(|\nabla u|)\equiv 0
on
U
, as claimed. Moreover, from
f(u)>0
on
U
we get
\varphi(|\nabla u|)=0
on
U
. Next, if
\varphi>0
on
\mathbb{R}^{+}
then
\nabla u=0
on
U
, that is,
u
is constant on connected components of
U
. We claim that this is impossible unless
U
is empty. Indeed, if
\partial U=\emptyset
we deduce that
u
must be globally constant, contradicting our assumption. On the other hand, if
\partial U\neq\emptyset
then by continuity
f(u)=0
on
\partial U
, and thus
f(u)=0
on the entire
U
, contradicting the very definition of
U
. In conclusion, if
\varphi>0
on
\mathbb{R}^{+}
then
U
is empty, that is,
f(u)\leq 0
on
M
.

If
u
solves
(P_{=})
and is non-constant, we apply the first part of Theorem
8.31
both to
u
and to
v=-u
, which solves

\Delta_{\varphi}v\geq b(x)\bar{f}(v)l(|\nabla v|)\qquad\text{with }\,\bar{f}(t)=-f(-t),

to deduce both
f(u)\varphi(|\nabla u|)\leq 0
and
\bar{f}(v)\varphi(|\nabla v|)\leq 0
on
M
. The conclusion follows since
\bar{f}(v)=-f(u)
.
∎

Remark 8.32
.

Since
\varphi
is bounded, choosing
l\equiv 1
in Theorem
8.31
we include solutions of

\Delta_{\varphi}u\geq b(x)f(u)\qquad\text{on }\,M.

(8.69)

However, a minor modification of the above proof shows that, in fact, if
u
solves (
8.69
) then the stronger
f(u)\leq 0
holds on
M
, regardless of the behaviour of
\varphi
. With the equality sign, (
8.69
) has been considered in
[
144
]
, see also
[
100
]
, while in
[
141
]
the author investigated more general equalities of the type

\mathrm{div}\mathbf{A}(x,u,\nabla u)=b(x)f(u),

where
\mathbf{A}(x,u,\nabla u)\leq Cr(x)^{\lambda}
, cf. also
[
38
,
49
,
50
]
.

It is instructive to compare Theorem
8.31
with Theorem
8.27
and Corollary
5.8
. First, we observe that if
f\leq 0
on
\mathbb{R}
the conclusion of Theorem
8.31
is straightworward. Otherwise, since
f
is increasing, there exists a constant
C>0
such that
f(t)\geq C>0
for
t>>1
. Hence, Theorem
8.31
considers the range

\omega=\chi=0,

that is not covered by Theorem
8.27
(cf. Remark
8.29
). The sharpness of
\mu<1
in Theorem
8.31
follows from the counterexample in Subsection
8.4
: otherwise, if
\mu=1
, we can choose
\alpha=-2
(hence,
M
of polynomial growth) and
\chi=\omega=0
(by Remark
8.30
) to produce a non-constant smooth solution of

\mathrm{div}\left(\frac{\nabla u}{\sqrt{1+|\nabla u|^{2}}}\right)\geq C\big(1+r(x)\big)^{-1}\frac{|\nabla u|}{\sqrt{1+|\nabla u|^{2}}}\qquad\text{on }\,M.

Also, Theorem
8.31
is specific to operators of mean curvature type, that is, those satisfying
\varphi\leq C_{1}
on
\mathbb{R}
. To see it, suppose that
\varphi
is unbounded, more precisely that

t\varphi^{\prime}(t)\geq c_{1}\varphi(t)\qquad\text{on }\,\mathbb{R}^{+},

(8.70)

for some constant
c_{1}>0
. Note that, by integration,
\varphi(t)\geq c_{2}t^{c_{1}}
for some positive
c_{2}
. For such
\varphi
, we are going to produce

(i)

a manifold
M
satisfying (
8.52
), for any chosen
\alpha\geq-2
(in particular, for
\alpha=-2
, geodesic balls in
M
grow polynomially), and

(ii)

for each
\mu\in\mathbb{R}
, a
\mathrm{Lip}_{\mathrm{loc}}
, non-negative unbounded solution
u
of

\Delta_{\varphi}u\geq C\big(1+r(x)\big)^{-\mu}\varphi(|\nabla u|)\qquad\text{on }\,M,

(8.71)

for some constant
C>0
. The combination of
(i)
and
(ii)
with
\alpha=-2
show the failure of Theorem
8.31
for operators satisfying (
8.70
). Consider the model manifold in Subsection
8.4
. For a smooth, radial function
u=w(r)
with
w
convex and strictly increasing, by (
8.57
) we compute

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\Delta_{\varphi}u&=&\displaystyle\varphi^{\prime}(w^{\prime})w^{\prime\prime}+\varphi(w^{\prime})\Delta r\geq\left[c_{1}\frac{w^{\prime\prime}}{w^{\prime}}+\Delta r\right]\varphi(w^{\prime})\\[11.38092pt]
&\geq&\displaystyle\left[c_{1}\frac{w^{\prime\prime}}{w^{\prime}}+c(1+r^{2})^{\alpha/4}\right]\varphi(w^{\prime}),\end{array}

for some constant
c>0
. Therefore, if we choose

w(r)=\int_{0}^{r}\exp\left\{(1+t^{2})^{\sigma}\right\}\mathrm{d}t,

then

\Delta_{\varphi}u\geq c_{3}(1+r^{2})^{\max\{\sigma-1,\alpha/4\}}\varphi(|\nabla u|),

and
u
solves (
8.71
) whenever
\sigma\geq 1-\mu/2
, as claimed.

In the next result, we show how the technique in Theorem
8.31
can be adapted to handle
(P_{\geq})
with a more general gradient term
l(|\nabla u|)
that is not necessary borderline, and with no bound on the decay of
b
. In this case, however, a slow volume growth is needed.

Theorem 8.33
.

Let
M
be a complete Riemannian manifold, consider

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\varphi\in C(\mathbb{R}^{+}_{0}),\qquad 0\leq\varphi\leq C_{1}\quad\text{on }\,\mathbb{R}^{+}_{0};\\[5.69046pt]
f\in C(\mathbb{R}),\qquad f\ \text{ non-decreasing on }\,\mathbb{R};\\[5.69046pt]
l\in C(\mathbb{R}^{+}_{0}),\qquad l\geq 0\quad\text{on }\,\mathbb{R}^{+};\\[5.69046pt]
b\in C(M),\qquad b>0\ \text{ on }\,M,\end{array}\right.

for some constant
C_{1}>0
. Let
u\in\mathrm{Lip}_{\mathrm{loc}}(M)
be a non-constant solution of

\Delta_{\varphi}u\geq b(x)f(u)l(|\nabla u|)\qquad\text{on }\,M.

(8.72)

If

\liminf_{r\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\mathrm{vol}(B_{r})}{r}=0,

(8.73)

then
f(u)l(|\nabla u|)\leq 0
on
M
. If
u
is non-constant and solves
(P_{=})
, then
f(u)l(|\nabla u|)\equiv 0
on
M
.

Proof.

We proceed as in the proof of Theorem
8.31
: let
\{f_{k}\}
a sequence of increasing, locally Lipschitz functions converging to
f
from below, and note that
u
solves

\Delta_{\varphi}u\geq b(x)f_{k}(u)l(|\nabla u|)\qquad\text{on }\,M.

(8.74)

For
\varepsilon>0
we define

\eta_{\varepsilon}(t)=\frac{\big(f_{k}(t)\big)_{+}}{\sqrt{\big(f_{k}(t)\big)_{+}^{2}+\varepsilon^{2}}}.

The monotonicity of
f_{k}
implies that
\eta_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}\geq 0
. Define
U_{k}=\{f_{k}(u)>0\}
and
U=\{f(u)>0\}
, and assume that
U\neq\emptyset
, otherwise the conclusion is immediate. Fix a cut-off function
\psi\in\mathrm{Lip}_{c}(M)
to be chosen later, insert

\phi=\eta_{\varepsilon}(u)\psi\in\mathrm{Lip}_{c}(M)

in the weak definition of (
8.74
) and apply Cauchy-Schwarz inequality to deduce

\displaystyle\int b\eta_{\varepsilon}(u)f_{k}(u)l(|\nabla u|)\psi\leq\displaystyle-\int\eta_{\varepsilon}(u)\langle\frac{\varphi(|\nabla u|)}{|\nabla u|}\nabla u,\nabla\psi\rangle\leq\displaystyle\int\eta_{\varepsilon}(u)\varphi(|\nabla u|)|\nabla\psi|.

Letting
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0
, using Lebesgue convergence theorem and the boundedness of
\varphi
we get

\displaystyle\int_{U_{k}}b\big(f_{k}(u)\big)_{+}l(|\nabla u|)\psi\leq\int_{U_{k}}\varphi(|\nabla u|)|\nabla\psi|\leq C_{1}\int_{M}|\nabla\psi|.

(8.75)

Fix a diverging sequence
\{R_{j}\}
such that
\{2R_{j}\}
realizes the liminf in (
8.73
), and define
\psi(x)=\psi_{j}(x)=\gamma(r(x)/R_{j})
, where
\gamma\in\mathrm{Lip}(\mathbb{R})
satisfies

\gamma=1\quad\text{on }\,[0,1),\qquad\gamma=0\quad\text{on }\,(2,\infty),\qquad\gamma(t)=2-t\quad\text{on }\,[1,2].

Evaluating (
8.75
) with
\psi=\psi_{j}
and letting
k\rightarrow\infty
we obtain

\displaystyle\int_{U}b\big(f(u)\big)_{+}l(|\nabla u|)\psi_{j}\leq\frac{C_{1}}{R_{j}}\mathrm{vol}\big(B_{2R_{j}}\big).

The conclusion follows by letting
j\rightarrow\infty
, and the case of equality is handled as in Theorem
8.31
.
∎

We next consider inequalities
(P_{\geq})
under the validity of the Keller-Osserman condition

F^{-\frac{1}{\chi+1}}\in L^{1}(\infty),

when just a volume growth upper bound is imposed on
M
. The main result of this section, Theorem
8.34
, improves on
[
109
,
110
]
(see also
[
119
]
, Thm. 1.3). Although the proof is still based on the delicate iteration argument in
[
109
,
110
]
, the presence of a nontrivial gradient term
l
calls for new estimates, inspired by recent work in
[
50
]
.

In this section we assume

\varphi(t)\leq Ct^{p-1}\qquad\text{for some }p>1,\ C>0\quad{\rm and}\ t\in\mathbb{R}^{+}.

(8.76)

Theorem 8.34
.

Let
M
be a complete Riemannian manifold, and consider
\varphi,b,f,l
meeting assumptions (
1.3
), (
1.5
) and (
8.76
), for some
p>1
. Assume that, for some
\mu,\chi,\omega\in\mathbb{R}
with

\chi\geq 0,\qquad\mu\leq\chi+1,\qquad\omega>\chi

(8.77)

the following inequalities are satisfied:

\begin{array}[]{ll}b(x)\geq C\big(1+r(x)\big)^{-\mu}&\quad\text{on }\,M,\\[5.69046pt]
f(t)\geq Ct^{\omega}&\quad\text{for }\,t\gg 1\\[5.69046pt]
\displaystyle l(t)\geq C\frac{\varphi(t)}{t^{\chi}}&\quad\text{on }\,\mathbb{R}^{+},\end{array}

(8.78)

for some constant
C>0
. Let
u\in\mathrm{Lip}_{\mathrm{loc}}(M)
be a non-constant solution of
(P_{\geq})
on
M
, and suppose that either

\begin{array}[]{lll}\mu<\chi+1&\text{and}&\displaystyle\qquad\liminf_{r\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\log\mathrm{vol}(B_{r})}{r^{\chi+1-\mu}}<\infty\quad\text{($=0$ if $\chi=0$)};\\[11.38092pt]
\text{or}\\[2.84544pt]
\mu=\chi+1&\text{and}&\displaystyle\qquad\liminf_{r\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\log\mathrm{vol}(B_{r})}{\log r}<\infty\quad\text{($\leq p$ if $\chi=0$)}.\end{array}

(8.79)

Then,
u
is bounded above and
f(u^{*})\leq 0
. In particular in case
f>0
on
\mathbb{R}^{+}
,
(\mathrm{SL})
holds.

Remark 8.35
.

In the Euclidean space
\mathbb{R}^{m}
, and when the third in (
8.78
) is replaced with the stronger
l(t)\geq Ct^{p-1-\chi}
, Liouville type results covering some of the cases in Theorem
8.34
have been obtained by various authors (in some instances, even for more general quasilinear operators). Among them, we stress Thm 1 in
[
50
]
, that considers the entire range (
8.77
). However, if
\mu=\chi+1
, the authors further need
p>m
independently of the value of
\chi
, a quite stronger requirement than the second in (
8.79
). Previous work in
[
53
]
considered the case
0<\chi\leq p-1
,
\omega>\chi
and
\mu<\chi+1
under the restriction
10
10

10

See Corollaries 1.3 and 1.4 in
[
53
]
; the bound
p\in(1,m)
is assumed at p.2904.

p\in(1,m)
, for operators close either to the
p
-Laplacian or to the mean curvature ones.

The existence of a Liouville theorem for
\mu=\chi+1
and
l(t)\equiv 1
was conjectured by Mitidieri-Pohozaev in
[
98
, Sect. 14 Ch. 1]
, and has previously been proved in
[
100
]
(for the
p
-Laplace operator) and
[
148
]
(for the mean curvature operator), in both cases on
\mathbb{R}^{m}
.

Theorem
8.34
is a consequence of Theorem
5.4
and of the next

Proposition 8.36
.

Let
M
be a complete Riemannian manifold, and let
\varphi
satisfy (
1.3
), and (
8.76
) with
p>1
. Fix
\mu,\omega,\chi\in\mathbb{R}
satisfying

\chi\geq 0,\qquad\mu\leq\chi+1,\qquad\omega>\chi,

(8.80)

and assume either one of the following requirements:

\begin{array}[]{lll}\mu<\chi+1&\text{and}&\displaystyle\qquad\liminf_{r\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\log\mathrm{vol}(B_{r})}{r^{\chi+1-\mu}}<\infty\\[5.69046pt]
\text{or}\\[5.69046pt]
\mu=\chi+1&\text{and}&\displaystyle\qquad\liminf_{r\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\log\mathrm{vol}(B_{r})}{\log r}<\infty\quad\text{($\leq p$ if $\chi=0$)}.\end{array}

(8.81)

If
u\in\mathrm{Lip}_{\mathrm{loc}}(M)

\Delta_{\varphi}u\geq K(1+r)^{-\mu}u^{\omega}\frac{\varphi(|\nabla u|)}{|\nabla u|^{\chi}}\qquad\text{on }\,\Omega_{\eta}=\{x\in M\ :\ u(x)>\eta\}\neq\emptyset,

(8.82)

for some
\eta>0
, then
u
is bounded above.

Remark 8.37
.

Although we require no upper bound on
\varphi(t)/t^{\chi}
in a neighbourhood of zero, the weak inequality (
8.82
) implicitly assumes the term
\varphi(|\nabla u|)/|\nabla u|^{\chi}
to be locally integrable on
\Omega_{\eta}
.

Proof.

Suppose by contradiction that
u^{*}=\infty
. Fix
\gamma>\eta
, and take
\lambda\in C^{1}(\mathbb{R})
such that

0\leq\lambda\leq 1,\quad\lambda^{\prime}\geq 0,\quad\lambda\equiv 0\,\text{ on }\,(-\infty,\gamma],\quad\lambda>0\,\text{ on }\,(\gamma,\infty).

Let
\psi\in C^{\infty}_{c}(M)
be a cut-off function, and let
\varsigma,\alpha>1
to be specified later. We plug the non-negative test function

\phi=\psi^{\varsigma}\lambda(u)u^{\alpha}\in\mathrm{Lip}_{c}(M)

in the weak definition of (
8.82
) to deduce, using
\lambda^{\prime}\geq 0
and (
8.78
),

\displaystyle\displaystyle K\int\psi^{\varsigma}\lambda\frac{u^{\alpha+\omega}}{(1+r)^{\mu}}\frac{\varphi(|\nabla u|)}{|\nabla u|^{\chi}}

\displaystyle\leq-\displaystyle\int\frac{\varphi(|\nabla u|)}{|\nabla u|}\langle\nabla u,\nabla(\psi^{\varsigma}\lambda u^{\alpha})\rangle

(8.83)

\displaystyle\leq\displaystyle\varsigma\int\psi^{\varsigma-1}\lambda u^{\alpha}\varphi(|\nabla u|)|\nabla\psi|

\displaystyle-\alpha\int\psi^{\varsigma}\lambda u^{\alpha-1}\varphi(|\nabla u|)|\nabla u|.

We divide the proof into several steps:

Step 1: basic growth estimates.

The following inequalities hold:

-

If
\mu<\chi+1
, then for each
q>0
there exists
\alpha_{q}>1
and a constant
C_{q}
depending on
p,q,\chi,\mu,\omega
such that, if
\alpha\geq\alpha_{q}
,

\int_{B_{R}\cap\Omega_{\gamma}}\lambda\frac{u^{\alpha+\omega}}{(1+r)^{\mu}}\frac{\varphi(|\nabla u|)}{|\nabla u|^{\chi}}\leq C_{q}\frac{\mathrm{vol}(B_{2R})}{R^{q}}.

(8.84)

-

If
\mu=\chi+1
, then there exists a constant
C
depending on
p,\chi,\mu,\omega
such that

\int_{B_{R}\cap\Omega_{\gamma}}\lambda\frac{u^{\alpha+\omega}}{(1+r)^{\mu}}\frac{\varphi(|\nabla u|)}{|\nabla u|^{\chi}}\leq C\frac{\mathrm{vol}(B_{2R})}{R^{p}}.

(8.85)

Proof of Step 1.

The argument is an adaptation of Lemma 2.2 in
[
50
]
, and rests on the use of the triple
Young inequality to the first term on the right-hand side of (
8.83
): we need to find
z_{1},z_{2},z_{3}>1
satisfying

\frac{1}{z_{1}}+\frac{1}{z_{2}}+\frac{1}{z_{3}}=1

(8.86)

and
\tau,\bar{C}>0
such that

\varsigma\psi^{\varsigma-1}\lambda u^{\alpha}\varphi(|\nabla u|)|\nabla\psi|=\mathcal{J}_{1}^{\frac{1}{z_{1}}}\mathcal{J}_{2}^{\frac{1}{z_{2}}}\mathcal{J}_{3}^{\frac{1}{z_{3}}},

(8.87)

with

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\mathcal{J}_{1}&=&\displaystyle\frac{K}{2}\psi^{\varsigma}\lambda\frac{u^{\alpha+\omega}}{(1+r)^{\mu}}\frac{\varphi(|\nabla u|)}{|\nabla u|^{\chi}}\\[14.22636pt]
\mathcal{J}_{2}&=&\displaystyle\alpha\psi^{\varsigma}\lambda u^{\alpha-1}\varphi(|\nabla u|)|\nabla u|\\[14.22636pt]
\mathcal{J}_{3}&=&\displaystyle\bar{C}(1+r)^{\tau}\left[\frac{\varphi(|\nabla u|)}{|\nabla u|^{p-1}}\right]|\nabla\psi|^{z_{3}}.\end{array}

(8.88)

considering powers of
u
,
|\nabla u|
,
r
and
\psi
, to obtain (
8.87
) we need the following balancing:

\begin{array}[]{rllrll}i)&\text{powers of $u$:}&\ \displaystyle\alpha=\frac{\alpha+\omega}{z_{1}}+\frac{\alpha-1}{z_{2}}&\quad ii)&\text{powers of $|\nabla u|$:}&\ \displaystyle 0=-\frac{\chi}{z_{1}}+\frac{1}{z_{2}}-\frac{p-1}{z_{3}}\\[14.22636pt]
iii)&\text{powers of $r$:}&\ \displaystyle 0=-\frac{\mu}{z_{1}}+\frac{\tau}{z_{3}}&\quad iv)&\text{powers of $\psi$:}&\ \displaystyle\varsigma-1=\frac{\varsigma}{z_{1}}+\frac{\varsigma}{z_{2}}.\end{array}

To find
z_{1},z_{2},z_{3}
note that, by (
8.86
), the equality for
|\nabla u|
can be rewritten as

p-1=\frac{p-1-\chi}{z_{1}}+\frac{p}{z_{2}}

Thus, solving the equations for
u,|\nabla u|
with respect to
z_{1}
and
z_{2}
, and then recovering
z_{3}
from (
8.86
), we get

\begin{array}[]{c}\displaystyle\frac{1}{z_{1}}=\frac{\alpha+p-1}{(\chi+1)(\alpha-1)+p(\omega+1)},\qquad\displaystyle\frac{1}{z_{2}}=\frac{\chi\alpha+(p-1)\omega}{(\chi+1)(\alpha-1)+p(\omega+1)},\\[14.22636pt]
\displaystyle\qquad\frac{1}{z_{3}}=\frac{\omega-\chi}{(\chi+1)(\alpha-1)+p(\omega+1)}\end{array}

(these are positive numbers less than
1
because of (
8.80
)), and from the last two equations,

\tau=\mu\frac{z_{3}}{z_{1}}=\mu\frac{\alpha+p-1}{\omega-\chi},\qquad\varsigma=z_{3}.

The constant
\bar{C}
is then uniquely determined by (
8.87
). Having found the right parameters, from the triple Young inequality

\mathcal{J}_{1}^{\frac{1}{z_{1}}}\mathcal{J}_{2}^{\frac{1}{z_{2}}}\mathcal{J}_{3}^{\frac{1}{z_{3}}}\leq\mathcal{J}_{1}+\mathcal{J}_{2}+\mathcal{J}_{3},

and plugging into (
8.87
) and (
8.88
) we deduce

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\displaystyle\varsigma\psi^{\varsigma-1}\lambda u^{\alpha}\varphi(|\nabla u|)|\nabla\psi|&\leq&\displaystyle\frac{K}{2}\psi^{\varsigma}\lambda\frac{u^{\alpha+\omega}}{(1+r)^{\mu}}\frac{\varphi(|\nabla u|)}{|\nabla u|^{\chi}}+\alpha\psi^{\varsigma}\lambda u^{\alpha-1}\varphi(|\nabla u|)|\nabla u|\\[14.22636pt]
&&+\displaystyle\bar{C}(1+r)^{\mu\frac{z_{3}}{z_{1}}}\left[\frac{\varphi(|\nabla u|)}{|\nabla u|^{p-1}}\right]^{z_{3}}|\nabla\psi|^{z_{3}}.\end{array}

Inserting into (
8.83
) and using (
8.76
) we get

\displaystyle\displaystyle\frac{K}{2}\int\psi^{\varsigma}\lambda\frac{u^{\alpha+\omega}}{(1+r)^{\mu}}\frac{\varphi(|\nabla u|)}{|\nabla u|^{\chi}}

\displaystyle\leq C_{1}\int(1+r)^{\mu\frac{z_{3}}{z_{1}}}|\nabla\psi|^{z_{3}}.

(8.89)

For large
R>1
, we choose
\psi\in C^{\infty}_{c}(M)
satisfying

0\leq\psi\leq 1,\quad\psi\equiv 1\ \ \text{ on }\,B_{R},\quad\psi\equiv 0\ \ \text{ on }\,M\backslash B_{2R},\quad|\nabla\psi|\leq\frac{C}{R},

(8.90)

for an absolute constant
C
. Using (
8.90
) and the fact that
\lambda=0
when
u\leq\gamma
, we obtain

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\displaystyle\frac{K}{2}\int_{B_{R}\cap\Omega_{\gamma}}\lambda\frac{u^{\alpha+\omega}}{(1+r)^{\mu}}\frac{\varphi(|\nabla u|)}{|\nabla u|^{\chi}}&\leq&\displaystyle\frac{K}{2}\int\psi^{\varsigma}\lambda\frac{u^{\alpha+\omega}}{(1+r)^{\mu}}\frac{\varphi(|\nabla u|)}{|\nabla u|^{\chi}}\\[14.22636pt]
&\leq&\displaystyle\frac{C_{2}}{R^{z_{3}}}\int_{B_{2R}}(1+r)^{\mu\frac{z_{3}}{z_{1}}}\\[14.22636pt]
&\leq&C_{3}R^{\mu\frac{z_{3}}{z_{1}}-z_{3}}\mathrm{vol}(B_{2R}).\end{array}

(8.91)

The exponent of
R
in (
8.91
) can be written as

\mu\frac{z_{3}}{z_{1}}-z_{3}=\frac{\alpha+p-1}{\omega-\chi}(\mu-\chi-1)-p.

(8.92)

We examine the two cases, according to whether
\mu<\chi+1
or
\mu=\chi+1
.

-

If
\mu<\chi+1
, then for any given
q>0
we can choose
\alpha_{q}
sufficiently large that, for
\alpha\geq\alpha_{q}
,

\mu\frac{z_{3}}{z_{1}}-z_{3}\leq-q.

Having fixed such
\alpha_{q}
, from (
8.91
) we get

\int_{B_{R}\cap\Omega_{\gamma}}\lambda\frac{u^{\alpha+\omega}}{(1+r)^{\mu}}\frac{\varphi(|\nabla u|)}{|\nabla u|^{\chi}}\leq\displaystyle\frac{C_{q}}{K}\frac{\mathrm{vol}(B_{2R})}{R^{q}},

(8.93)

and the thesis follows.

-

If
\mu=\chi+1
, then by (
8.92
) the exponent of
R
in (
8.91
) is
-p
independently of
\alpha
, and we obtain

\int_{B_{R}\cap\Omega_{\gamma}}\lambda\frac{u^{\alpha+\omega}}{(1+r)^{\mu}}\frac{\varphi(|\nabla u|)}{|\nabla u|^{\chi}}\leq\displaystyle\frac{C_{5}}{K}\frac{\mathrm{vol}(B_{2R})}{R^{p}}.

(8.94)

as claimed.

∎

Step 2: a preliminary inequality.

We consider again (
8.83
), but we are going to choose
\alpha,\varsigma>\chi+1
in a way different to the one in Step 1.

Case 1:
\chi>0
.

We use Young’s inequality with exponents
\chi+1
and
(\chi+1)/\chi
to remove the second term in the right-hand side of (
8.83
): for each
\varepsilon>0
, we get

\displaystyle\varsigma\int\psi^{\varsigma-1}\lambda u^{\alpha}\varphi(|\nabla u|)|\nabla\psi|\leq\frac{\varsigma}{(\chi+1)\,\varepsilon^{\chi+1}}

\displaystyle\int\psi^{\varsigma-\chi-1}\lambda u^{\alpha+\chi}\frac{\varphi(|\nabla u|)}{|\nabla u|^{\chi}}|\nabla\psi|^{\chi+1}

(8.95)

\displaystyle+\frac{\chi\varsigma\varepsilon^{\frac{\chi+1}{\chi}}}{\chi+1}\int\psi^{\varsigma}\lambda u^{\alpha-1}\varphi(|\nabla u|)|\nabla u|.

choosing
\varepsilon
such that

\frac{\chi\varsigma\varepsilon^{\frac{\chi+1}{\chi}}}{\chi+1}=\alpha,\quad\text{that is,}\quad\varepsilon=\left(\frac{\alpha(\chi+1)}{\chi\varsigma}\right)^{\frac{\chi}{\chi+1}},

and inserting into (
8.83
), we obtain

\displaystyle K\int\psi^{\varsigma}\lambda\frac{u^{\alpha+\omega}}{(1+r)^{\mu}}\frac{\varphi(|\nabla u|)}{|\nabla u|^{\chi}}\leq\displaystyle\frac{C_{1}\varsigma^{\chi+1}}{\alpha^{\chi}}\int\psi^{\varsigma-\chi-1}\lambda u^{\alpha+\chi}\frac{\varphi(|\nabla u|)}{|\nabla u|^{\chi}}|\nabla\psi|^{\chi+1}

(8.96)

for some constant
C_{1}=C_{1}(\chi)>0
.

Case 2:
\chi=0
.

In this case, (
8.96
) with
\chi=0
and
C_{1}=1
directly follows from (
8.83
), getting rid of the second term on the right-hand side.

Step 3: induction for
\mu<\chi+1
.

If
\mu<\chi+1
, the following inductive relation holds:

\int_{B_{R}\cap\Omega_{\gamma}}\lambda(1+r)^{-\mu}\frac{\varphi(|\nabla u|)}{|\nabla u|^{\chi}}\leq 2^{-BR^{\theta}}\left[\int_{B_{2R}\cap\Omega_{\gamma}}\lambda(1+r)^{-\mu}\frac{\varphi(|\nabla u|)}{|\nabla u|^{\chi}}\right],

(8.97)

where

B=\frac{C_{6}\gamma^{\omega-\chi}}{\omega-\chi},\qquad\theta=\chi+1-\mu>0,

(8.98)

and
C_{6}=C_{6}(K,\omega,\chi,\mu)
is a positive constant independent of
\gamma,R
.

Proof of Step 3.

Fix
\xi>1
close enough to
1
in order to satisfy

\omega-\chi-(\chi+1)\left(1-\frac{1}{\xi}\right)>0

(8.99)

and, for
R\geq 2
choose a cut-off function
\psi\in\mathrm{Lip}_{c}(B_{2R})
such that

0\leq\psi\leq 1,\qquad\psi\equiv 1\ \text{ on }B_{R},\qquad|\nabla\psi|\leq\frac{C}{R}\psi^{1/\xi},

(8.100)

for some
C=C(\xi)
. Note that this is possible since
\xi>1
(for instance, one can take the cut-off in (
8.90
), call it
\psi_{0}
, and consider
\psi=\psi_{0}^{\xi/(\xi-1)}
).

Choose
\alpha
and
\varsigma
in order to satisfy

\varsigma=\alpha+\omega,\qquad\alpha>\max\left\{\omega,\chi+1-\omega\right\}.

(8.101)

However, for the ease of notation we feel convenient to keep
\varsigma
and
\alpha
independent in the next computations.
By Step 2, inequality (
8.96
) holds for each
\chi\geq 0
. Using then (
8.100
), and since
\{\nabla\psi\neq 0\}\subset B_{2R}\backslash B_{R}
,
R\geq 2
, from (
8.96
) we deduce

\begin{array}[]{l}\displaystyle K\int\psi^{\varsigma}\lambda\frac{u^{\alpha+\omega}}{(1+r)^{\mu}}\frac{\varphi(|\nabla u|)}{|\nabla u|^{\chi}}\\[14.22636pt]
\qquad\qquad\displaystyle\leq\displaystyle\frac{C_{2}\varsigma^{\chi+1}}{\alpha^{\chi}R^{\chi+1}}\int_{\{\nabla\psi\neq 0\}}\psi^{\varsigma-(\chi+1)\left(1-\frac{1}{\xi}\right)}\lambda u^{\alpha+\chi}\frac{\varphi(|\nabla u|)}{|\nabla u|^{\chi}}\\[14.22636pt]
\qquad\qquad\displaystyle\leq\displaystyle\frac{C_{3}\varsigma^{\chi+1}}{\alpha^{\chi}R^{\chi+1-\mu}}\int\psi^{\varsigma-(\chi+1)\left(1-\frac{1}{\xi}\right)}\lambda\frac{u^{\alpha+\chi}}{(1+r)^{\mu}}\frac{\varphi(|\nabla u|)}{|\nabla u|^{\chi}}\end{array}

(8.102)

Since
\omega>\chi
, we can apply Hölder’s inequality to the RHS with exponents

q=\frac{\alpha+\omega}{\omega-\chi},\qquad q^{\prime}=\frac{\alpha+\omega}{\alpha+\chi}

(8.103)

and get

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\displaystyle\int\psi^{\varsigma-(\chi+1)\left(1-\frac{1}{\xi}\right)}\lambda\frac{u^{\alpha+\chi}}{(1+r)^{\mu}}\frac{\varphi(|\nabla u|)}{|\nabla u|^{\chi}}&\leq&\displaystyle\left(\int\psi^{\varsigma}\lambda\frac{u^{q^{\prime}(\alpha+\chi)}}{(1+r)^{\mu}}\frac{\varphi(|\nabla u|)}{|\nabla u|^{\chi}}\right)^{1/q^{\prime}}\\[14.22636pt]
&&\displaystyle\cdot\left(\int\psi^{\varsigma-(\chi+1)\,q(1-1/{\xi})}\lambda(1+r)^{-\mu}\frac{\varphi(|\nabla u|)}{|\nabla u|^{\chi}}\right)^{1/q}\end{array}

Inserting into (
8.102
) we obtain

\begin{array}[]{l}\displaystyle\displaystyle\int\psi^{\varsigma}\lambda\frac{u^{\alpha+\omega}}{(1+r)^{\mu}}\frac{\varphi(|\nabla u|)}{|\nabla u|^{\chi}}\\[14.22636pt]
\qquad\qquad\displaystyle\leq\displaystyle\left(\frac{C_{4}\varsigma^{\chi+1}}{\alpha^{\chi}R^{\chi+1-\mu}}\right)^{q}\int\psi^{\varsigma-(\chi+1)q\left(1-\frac{1}{\xi}\right)}\lambda(1+r)^{-\mu}\frac{\varphi(|\nabla u|)}{|\nabla u|^{\chi}}\end{array}

(8.104)

for some
C_{4}(\chi,\mu,K)
. Now, by (
8.99
), (
8.101
) and (
8.103
),

\varsigma-(\chi+1)\,q\left(1-\frac{1}{\xi}\right)=\frac{\alpha+\omega}{\omega-\chi}\left[\omega-\chi-(\chi+1)\left(1-\frac{1}{\xi}\right)\right]>0,

hence the term with
\psi
on the right-hand side of (
8.104
) can be estimated with one on
B_{2R}
. Together with condition
\alpha>\omega
in (
8.101
), this gives

\begin{array}[]{l}\displaystyle\displaystyle\int\psi^{\varsigma}\lambda\frac{u^{\alpha+\omega}}{(1+r)^{\mu}}\frac{\varphi(|\nabla u|)}{|\nabla u|^{\chi}}\\[14.22636pt]
\qquad\qquad\displaystyle\leq\displaystyle\left(\frac{C_{5}\alpha}{R^{\chi+1-\mu}}\right)^{q}\int_{B_{2R}\cap\Omega_{\gamma}}\lambda(1+r)^{-\mu}\frac{\varphi(|\nabla u|)}{|\nabla u|^{\chi}}\end{array}

(8.105)

Now, as
u\geq\gamma
on the domain where
\lambda(u)
is positive and not zero, using again the properties of
\psi
and the definition of
q
we finally infer

\begin{array}[]{l}\displaystyle\displaystyle\int_{B_{R}}\lambda(1+r)^{-\mu}\frac{\varphi(|\nabla u|)}{|\nabla u|^{\chi}}\\[14.22636pt]
\qquad\qquad\displaystyle\leq\displaystyle\left(\frac{C_{5}\alpha}{R^{\chi+1-\mu}\gamma^{\omega-\chi}}\right)^{\frac{\alpha+\omega}{\omega-\chi}}\int_{B_{2R}}\lambda(1+r)^{-\mu}\frac{\varphi(|\nabla u|)}{|\nabla u|^{\chi}}\end{array}

(8.106)

Choose
\alpha
in such a way that

\frac{C_{5}\alpha}{R^{\chi+1-\mu}\gamma^{\omega-\chi}}=\frac{1}{2},

that is,

\alpha=\alpha(R)=\frac{\gamma^{\omega-\chi}}{2C_{5}}R^{\chi+1-\mu}=C_{6}\gamma^{\omega-\chi}R^{\chi+1-\mu}

Since
\chi+1-\mu>0
by assumption, if
R
is big enough then
\alpha
satisfies (
8.101
). Then, setting

\mathcal{H}(R)=\int_{B_{R}}\lambda(1+r)^{-\mu}\frac{\varphi(|\nabla u|)}{|\nabla u|^{\chi}},

we have

\mathcal{H}(R)\leq\displaystyle 2^{-\frac{\alpha+\omega}{\omega-\chi}}\mathcal{H}(2R)\leq 2^{-\frac{\alpha}{\omega-\chi}}\mathcal{H}(2R)=2^{-BR^{\theta}}\mathcal{H}(2R),

(8.107)

where

B=\frac{C_{6}\gamma^{\omega-\chi}}{\omega-\chi},\qquad\theta=\chi+1-\mu>0.\\

(8.108)

∎

Step 4: iteration and conclusion for
\mu<\chi+1
.

Fix
R_{0}
big and
\bar{R}>2R_{0}
such that
u
is not constant on
\Omega_{\gamma}\cap B_{\bar{R}}
. Then,
\mathcal{H}(\bar{R})>0
. Consider
R_{j}=2^{j}\bar{R}
, and let
k
be the integer satisfying
R_{k}<R\leq R_{k+1}
. Iterating (
8.107
)
k
-times and taking the logarithm, we get

\displaystyle\log\mathcal{H}(\bar{R})\leq\displaystyle-\left(\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}\bar{R}^{\theta}2^{j\theta}\right)B\log 2+\log\mathcal{H}(R_{k})\leq-\left(\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}R_{k}^{\theta}2^{(j-k)\theta}\right)B\log 2+\log\mathcal{H}(R).

Now,

\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}R_{k}^{\theta}2^{(j-k)\theta}=\frac{R_{k+1}^{\theta}}{2^{\theta}}\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}2^{(j-k)\theta}=\frac{R_{k+1}^{\theta}}{2^{\theta}}\left(\frac{2^{-\theta}-2^{-(k+1)\theta}}{1-2^{-\theta}}\right)\geq R^{\theta}C_{\theta},

for some constant
C_{\theta}>0
, thus

\log\mathcal{H}(\bar{R})\leq-R^{\theta}BC_{\theta}\log 2+\log\mathcal{H}(R),

(8.109)

or in other words,

\frac{\log\mathcal{H}(R)}{R^{\theta}}\geq\frac{\log\mathcal{H}(\bar{R})}{R^{\theta}}+BC_{\theta}\log 2.

(8.110)

By Step 1, for fixed
q=2
there exists
\alpha_{2}
such that, for
\alpha\geq\alpha_{2}
,

\mathcal{H}(R)\leq\frac{1}{\gamma^{\alpha+\omega}}\int_{B_{r}\cap\Omega_{\gamma}}\lambda\frac{u^{\alpha+\omega}}{(1+r)^{\mu}}\frac{\varphi(|\nabla u|)}{|\nabla u|^{\chi}}\leq\frac{C_{q}}{\gamma^{\alpha+\omega}}\frac{\mathrm{vol}(B_{2R})}{R^{2}}.

(8.111)

Now, choosing
\bar{R}
large enough that
\alpha(\bar{R})\geq\alpha_{2}
, plugging (
8.111
) into (
8.109
), letting
R\rightarrow\infty
and using the definition of
B
, because of (
8.81
) we get

\frac{C_{6}C_{\theta}\log 2}{\omega-\chi}\gamma^{\omega-\chi}\leq\liminf_{R\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\log\mathrm{vol}(B_{2R})}{R^{\theta}}<\infty.

However, the assumption
\omega>\chi
leads to a contradiction provided that
\gamma
is chosen to be large enough. Therefore,
u^{*}<\infty
, concluding the proof.

Step 5: conclusion for
\mu=\chi+1
,
\chi=0
.

In this case, let
\{R_{j}\}
be a divergent sequence such that
\{2R_{j}\}
satisfies the liminf condition in (
8.81
). Inserting into (
8.85
), using that
\lambda
is supported on
\{u\geq\gamma\}
and letting
k\rightarrow\infty
we deduce

\gamma^{\alpha+\omega}\int_{\Omega_{\gamma}}\frac{\lambda}{(1+r)^{\mu}}\frac{\varphi(|\nabla u|)}{|\nabla u|^{\chi}}\leq\int_{\Omega_{\gamma}}\lambda\frac{u^{\alpha+\omega}}{(1+r)^{\mu}}\frac{\varphi(|\nabla u|)}{|\nabla u|^{\chi}}\leq\displaystyle C\lim_{j\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\mathrm{vol}(B_{2R_{j}})}{R_{j}^{p}}=C_{1}.

Without loss of generality, we can suppose
\gamma>1
. The above inequality is then contradicted if
\alpha
is large enough. Therefore,
u^{*}<\infty
, as claimed.

Step 6: iteration and conclusion for
\mu=\chi+1
,
\chi>0
.

We begin again with (
8.96
), but we fix
\varsigma=\chi+2
. Choosing as
\psi
a cut-off satisfying

\psi\equiv 1\quad\text{on }\,B_{R},\qquad\psi\equiv 0\quad\text{on }M\backslash B_{2R},\qquad|\nabla\psi|\leq\frac{2}{R},

we obtain, since
\mu=\chi+1
,

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{u}(R)\doteq\int_{B_{R}\cap\Omega_{\gamma}}\lambda\frac{u^{\alpha+\omega}}{(1+r)^{\mu}}\frac{\varphi(|\nabla u|)}{|\nabla u|^{\chi}}&\leq&\displaystyle\frac{C_{2}}{K\alpha^{\chi}R^{\chi+1}}\int_{B_{2R}\cap\Omega_{\gamma}}\lambda u^{\alpha+\chi}\frac{\varphi(|\nabla u|)}{|\nabla u|^{\chi}}\\[14.22636pt]
&\leq&\displaystyle\displaystyle\frac{C_{3}}{K\alpha^{\chi}\gamma^{\omega-\chi}}\int_{B_{2R}\cap\Omega_{\gamma}}\lambda\frac{u^{\alpha+\omega}}{(1+r)^{\mu}}\frac{\varphi(|\nabla u|)}{|\nabla u|^{\chi}},\end{array}

(8.112)

for some
C_{3}(\chi)
. Since
\chi>0
, for fixed
S>0
we can choose
\alpha
large enough to satisfy

\mathcal{H}_{u}(R)\leq 2^{-S}\mathcal{H}_{u}(2R).

Fix
R_{0}
big,
\bar{R}>2R_{0}
,
R_{i}=2^{i}\bar{R}
. For
R>\bar{R}
, let
k\in\mathbb{N}
be such that
R_{k}<R\leq R_{k+1}
. Iterating
k
-times and taking the logarithm we deduce

\log\mathcal{H}_{u}(\bar{R})\leq-kS\log 2+\log\mathcal{H}_{u}(R_{k})\leq-kS\log 2+\log\mathcal{H}_{u}(R)

dividing by
\log R
and using that
\log R\leq(k+1)\log 2+\log\bar{R}\leq 2k\log 2
for large enough
R
, we deduce the following inequality:

\displaystyle\frac{\log\mathcal{H}_{u}(\bar{R})}{\log R}\leq\displaystyle-\frac{Sk}{\log R}\log 2+\frac{\log\mathcal{H}_{u}(R)}{\log R}\leq-\frac{S}{2}+\frac{\log\mathcal{H}_{u}(R)}{\log R}.

(8.113)

Now, because of Step 1,
\mathcal{H}_{u}(R)\leq C_{\alpha}\mathrm{vol}(B_{R})/R^{p}
, where the constant
C_{\alpha}
depends on
\alpha
. If
\{R_{j}\}
is a sequence realizing the liminf in (
8.81
),

\limsup_{j\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\log\mathcal{H}_{u}(R_{j})}{\log R_{j}}\leq\lim_{j\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\log\mathrm{vol}(B_{R_{j}})}{\log R_{j}}-p\doteq C_{*}<\infty.

Inserting into (
8.113
) and letting
j\rightarrow\infty
we obtain

0\leq-\frac{S}{2}+C_{*},

that leads to a contradiction provided that
S
is chosen large enough. Note that this conclusion does not need
\gamma
to be large enough, in other words, we showed that
u
cannot be a non-constant solution of (
5.32
) on any upper level set
\Omega_{\gamma}
.
∎

We are now ready to prove Theorem
8.34
.

Proof (of Theorem
8.34
).

We first show that
u
is bounded above. If not, using (
8.78
) we deduce that, for
\eta>0
sufficiently large,
u
would be a non-constant solution of

\Delta_{\varphi}u\geq K(1+r)^{-\mu}u^{\omega}\frac{\varphi(|\nabla u|)}{|\nabla u|^{\chi}}\qquad\text{on }\,\Omega_{\eta}=\{x\in M\ :\ u(x)>\eta\}\neq\emptyset,

(8.114)

for some
K>0
, contradicting Proposition
8.36
. Next, we invoke Theorem
5.4
with
\sigma=0
to deduce that
f(u^{*})\leq 0
, concluding the proof. If
\mu=\chi+1
and
\chi>0
, we could argue
f(u^{*})\leq 0
directly from Step 6: indeed, if
f(u^{*})>0
, then by continuity
u
would be a non-constant solution of (
8.114
) on
\Omega_{\eta}
for a suitable
K>0
and
\eta
close enough to
u^{*}
. This is impossible by what we observed at the end of Step 6.
∎

8.5.1
Yamabe type equations

With the aid of Theorem
8.34
, we are able to improve on various geometric corollaries of
[
110
, Thm. 4.8]
. By a way of example, we consider the following conformal rigidity result for manifolds with negative scalar curvature, first investigated by M. Obata in
[
102
]
(in the compact case) and S.T. Yau in
[
155
]
. The geometric conditions in their main theorems have later been substantially weakened in
[
110
, Thm. 4.9]
, and our next corollary is a mild generalization of it.

Corollary 8.38
.

Let
(M,\langle\,,\,\rangle)
be a complete Riemannian manifold of dimension
m\geq 2
whose scalar curvature
R(x)
satisfies

R(x)\leq-C\big(1+r(x)\big)^{-\mu}\qquad\text{on }\,M,

for some constants
\mu\in\mathbb{R}
,
C\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
. If either

\begin{array}[]{lll}\mu<2&\text{and}&\displaystyle\qquad\liminf_{r\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\log\mathrm{vol}(B_{r})}{r^{2-\mu}}<\infty,\qquad\text{or}\\[11.38092pt]
\mu=2&\text{and}&\displaystyle\qquad\liminf_{r\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\log\mathrm{vol}(B_{r})}{\log r}<\infty,\end{array}

then any conformal diffeomorphism of
M
preserving
R
is an isometry.

Proof.

Let
T:(M,\langle\,,\,\rangle)\rightarrow(M,\langle\,,\,\rangle)
be a conformal diffeomorphism, and let
(\,,\,)=T^{*}\langle\,,\,\rangle=\lambda^{2}\langle\,,\,\rangle
be the conformally deformed metric, for
0<\lambda\in C^{\infty}(M)
. If
m\geq 3
, writing
\lambda=u^{\frac{2}{m-2}}
then it is well known that
u
solves

\Delta u=\frac{R}{c_{m}}u-\frac{\bar{R}}{c_{m}}u^{\frac{m+2}{m-2}}\qquad\text{on }\,M,

where
\bar{R}
is the scalar curvature of
(\,,\,)
,
\Delta
is the Laplacian of the background metric
\langle\,,\,\rangle
, and
c_{m}=\frac{4(m-1)}{m-2}
. On the other hand, if
m=2
, writing
\lambda=e^{u}
it holds

2\Delta u=R-\bar{R}e^{2u}\qquad\text{on }\,M.

Therefore, if
T
preserves the scalar curvature,

\Delta u=-R(x)f(u),\qquad\text{with}\qquad f(u)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\displaystyle\frac{1}{c_{m}}\left[u^{\frac{m+2}{m-2}}-u\right]&\quad\text{if }\,m\geq 3,\\[11.38092pt]
\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\left[e^{2u}-1\right]&\quad\text{if }\,m=2.\end{array}\right.

We now apply Theorem
8.34
with
b(x)=-R(x)
,
\varphi(t)=t
and
\chi=1
both to
u
and to
-u
to deduce that
u
is bounded and
f(u^{*})\leq 0\leq f(u_{*})
. Hence,
u\equiv 1
if
m\geq 3
, respectively
u\equiv 0
if
m=2
, and
T
is therefore an isometry.
∎

For many other applications to Geometry, we refer the reader to
[
110
,
3
]
. Next, we focus on the mean curvature operator.

8.5.2
The capillarity equation

As observed in the Introduction, global solutions
u:\mathbb{R}^{m}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
of the capillary equation

\mathrm{div}\left(\frac{\nabla u}{\sqrt{1+|\nabla u|^{2}}}\right)=\kappa(x)u

(8.115)

have been considered in
[
144
,
100
]
, with subsequent improvements in
[
141
,
49
]
. Combing their results,
u
must vanish identically provided that

\kappa(x)\geq C\big(1+r(x)\big)^{-\mu}

(8.116)

on
\mathbb{R}^{m}
, for some constants
C>0
and
\mu<2
. In fact, in
[
49
]
the authors investigated a more general class of equations including

\mathrm{div}\left(\frac{\nabla u}{\sqrt{1+|\nabla u|^{2}}}\right)=\kappa(x)|u|^{\omega-1}u

(8.117)

on
\mathbb{R}^{m}
, with
\omega>0
and
\kappa(x)
enjoying (
1.59
), see also Section 5 in
[
124
]
. Applying the Corollary at p. 4387 in
[
49
]
,
u\equiv 0
on
\mathbb{R}^{m}
whenever either

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\omega>1,\quad\mu\leq 2,\qquad\text{or}\\[8.5359pt]
\omega\in(0,1],\quad\mu<\omega+1.\end{array}\right.

(8.118)

The upper bound
\mu<2
is readily recovered for the capillarity equation (
\omega=1
). In a manifold setting, the case
\omega>1
and
\mu<2
was already considered in
[
110
, Thm. 4.8]
: with the aid of Theorem
8.34
, we can improve on it by describing the full range
\omega>0
. In particular, specifying the next theorem to the capillarity problem yields Theorem
8.39
in the Introduction.

Theorem 8.39
.

Suppose that
M
is complete, fix
\omega>0
and let
\kappa\in C(M)
satisfying

\kappa(x)\geq C\big(1+r(x)\big)^{-\mu}\qquad\text{on }\,M,

(8.119)

for some constants
C>0
and
\mu\in\mathbb{R}
. Then, the only solution of (
8.117
) on
M
is
u\equiv 0
whenever one of the following cases occur:

\begin{array}[]{rlll}(i)&\displaystyle\omega>1,&\quad\mu<2&\quad\text{and}\qquad\displaystyle\liminf_{r\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\log\mathrm{vol}B_{r}}{r^{2-\mu}}<\infty;\\[14.22636pt]
(ii)&\displaystyle\omega>1,&\quad\mu=2&\quad\text{and}\qquad\displaystyle\liminf_{r\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\log\mathrm{vol}B_{r}}{\log r}<\infty;\\[14.22636pt]
(iii)&\displaystyle\omega\in(0,1],&\quad\mu<\omega+1&\quad\text{and}\qquad\displaystyle\liminf_{r\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\log\mathrm{vol}B_{r}}{r^{\omega+1-\mu-\varepsilon}}<\infty,\end{array}

(8.120)

for some
\varepsilon>0
.

Remark 8.40
.

Case
(i)
is due to
[
110
, Thm. 4.8]
. From (
8.120
), we readily deduce (
8.118
) in the Euclidean setting.

Proof.

Clearly,
u\equiv 0
is the only constant solution. Suppose that (
8.117
) admits a non-constant solution, set
p=2
and define
\chi=1
if
\omega>1
, while
\chi=\omega-\varepsilon
if
\omega\in(0,1]
. Up to reducing
\varepsilon
, we can assume that
\chi\in(0,1]
. Therefore, the boundedness of
t^{\chi}/\sqrt{1+t^{2}}
on
\mathbb{R}
guarantees the existence of a constant
C_{1}>0
depending on
\chi
such that

\mathrm{div}\left(\frac{\nabla u}{\sqrt{1+|\nabla u|^{2}}}\right)=\kappa(x)|u|^{\omega-1}u\geq C_{1}\kappa(x)|u|^{\omega-1}u\frac{|\nabla u|^{1-\chi}}{\sqrt{1+|\nabla u|^{2}}}

on
M
. Since
\omega>\chi
, applying Theorem
8.34
with the choices
b(x)=C_{1}\kappa(x)
,
f(t)=|t|^{\omega-1}t
we deduce
u^{*}\leq 0
. To conclude we then apply Theorem
8.34
to
-u
to get
u\equiv 0
, contradiction.
∎

8.6
Other ranges of parameters

In our investigation of problem
(P_{\geq})
, we mostly assumed (
8.78
) in the parameter range

\chi\geq 0,\qquad\mu\leq\chi+1.

The main reason for this choice was the possibility to obtain maximum principles at infinity for the operator
(bl)^{-1}\Delta_{\varphi}
. However, in the recent literature some interesting results in Euclidean space give subtle hints to grasp how Geometry comes into play for other ranges of
\chi,\mu
. To our knowledge, the problem is still completely open in a manifold setting.

Remark 8.41

(
The range
\mu>\chi+1
)
.

This case is considered in
[
49
,
50
,
124
]
. In particular, we quote
[
50
, Thm. 3]
where the authors establish a Liouville theorem under the restriction

\omega>\max\{\chi,0\},\qquad\frac{\mu-\chi-1}{\omega-\chi}<\frac{p-m}{p-1},

(8.121)

see also Thm. 2 and Ex. 3 in
[
49
]
. Note that
\mu>\chi+1
may enjoy (
8.121
) only if
p>m
. Further results for large
\mu
can be found in Theorems 4, 8 and 12 in
[
50
]
, Thm. C in
[
49
]
, Thms. 1.3 and 5.3 in
[
124
]
.

Remark 8.42

(
The range
\chi<0
)
.

This corresponds to a gradient dependence
l
that is allowed to vanish with high order in
t=0
, and we quote
[
40
, Thm. 11.4]
. There, the conclusions of Theorem
8.34
are shown to hold when (
8.78
) holds with
l(t)\geq Ct^{p-1-\chi}
and
\omega=0
, provided that

\mu<1,\qquad-\left[\frac{1-\mu}{m-1}\right](p-1)\leq\chi<0.

(8.122)

Note that, as shown in Remark 11.8 of
[
40
]
, when
\mu=0
the value
\frac{1-\mu}{m-1}(p-1)
in (
8.122
) is sharp. A similar bound also appears in Thms. 2 and 7 in
[
50
]
