. Related interesting results, for possibly singular
b(x)
and still in the range
\chi<0
, are given in
[
86
]
.

9
Appendix: models and comparisons

Comparison theory in Riemannian geometry allows to deduce the behaviour of relevant geometric quantities on
M
from the knowledge of the corresponding ones on a simpler, rotationally symmetric model example, provided that the curvatures of
M
are controlled by those of the model. For
\lambda>0
, let
\mathbb{S}^{m-1}_{\lambda}=(\mathbb{S}^{m-1},(\,,\,)_{\lambda})
denote the round sphere of radius
1/\lambda
. Given
0<g\in C^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})
, a model
(M_{g,\lambda},\mathrm{d}s^{2}_{g,\lambda})
is topologically

M_{g,\lambda}=\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathbb{S}^{m-1}_{\lambda},

(9.1)

endowed with the rotationally symmetric, warped product metric which in coordinates
(r,\theta)\in\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathbb{S}^{m-1}_{\lambda}
writes

\mathrm{d}s_{g,\lambda}^{2}=\mathrm{d}r^{2}+g(r)^{2}(\,,\,)_{\lambda}.

When
\lambda=1
,
g(0)=0
and
g^{\prime}(0)=1
,
\mathrm{d}s_{g,\lambda}^{2}
extends to a
C^{2}
-metric on the completion of (
9.1
), which is topologically
\mathbb{R}^{m}
. Examples include

-

\mathbb{R}^{m}
, recovered for
g(r)=r
;

-

the hyperbolic space
\mathbb{H}^{m}_{\kappa}
of curvature
-\kappa^{2}
, for which
g(r)=\kappa^{-1}\sinh(\kappa r)
.

However, in the study of
(\mathrm{CSP})
we shall also use models whose function
g
extends smoothly to a positive value at
r=0
, that is, the completion
\overline{M}_{g,\lambda}
is a manifold with boundary. In both the cases
r
coincides with the distance to
\{r=0\}
, and a direct computation shows

K_{\mathrm{rad}}=-\frac{g^{\prime\prime}(r)}{g(r)},\qquad\nabla\mathrm{d}r=\frac{g^{\prime}(r)}{g(r)}\Big(\mathrm{d}s_{g,\lambda}^{2}-\mathrm{d}r\otimes\mathrm{d}r\Big),\qquad\Delta r=(m-1)\frac{g^{\prime}(r)}{g(r)}.

In particular, if
\overline{M}_{g,\lambda}
has a boundary, the second and third imply that second fundamental form
\operatorname{II}_{-\nabla r}
of
\partial\overline{M}_{g,\lambda}
and its (unnormalized) mean curvature
H_{-\nabla r}
in the direction
-\nabla r
satisfy

\operatorname{II}_{-\nabla r}=\frac{g^{\prime}(0)}{g(0)}\mathrm{d}s_{g,\lambda}^{2},\qquad H_{-\nabla r}=(m-1)\frac{g^{\prime}(0)}{g(0)}.

Although the comparison theorems are well-known, we prefer to write down explicitly the results with (sketchy) proofs, for the convenience of the reader. We derive our main estimates in the less standard case when the model has a boundary.

Let
(M^{m},\langle\,,\,\rangle)
be a complete manifold, and fix an origin
\mathcal{O}\subset M
. The set
\cal O
can be either a single point, or a open subset with smooth boundary, and here we focus on the second case. Denote with
D_{\cal O}
the maximal domain where the normal exponential map

\exp^{\perp}\ \ :\ \ \mathscr{D}_{\mathcal{O}}\subset T\mathcal{O}^{\perp}\longrightarrow\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{O}}\subset M\backslash\mathcal{O}

is the inverse of a chart (
T\mathcal{O}^{\perp}
is the subset of the normal bundle of
\partial\mathcal{O}
made of outwards pointing normal vectors). It is known that
\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{O}}
is open and that
r(x)=\mathrm{dist}(x,\cal O)
is smooth
11
11

11

Observe that, since
\partial\mathcal{O}
is a smooth hypersurface,
r
is smooth up to
\partial\mathcal{O}
.
on
\mathcal{D}_{\cal O}
. Its complementary
M\backslash(\mathcal{O}\cup D_{\mathcal{O}})
is a closed set of measure zero, called the cut-locus of
\mathcal{O}
and denoted with
\mathrm{cut}(\mathcal{O})
.

The starting point of comparison theory is the following construction: for each
x\in D_{\cal O}
, let
\gamma:[0,r(x)]\rightarrow M
be the unique unit speed, minimizing geodesic normal to
\cal O
, starting from
\partial\cal O
and ending at
x
. Let
R
denote tha curvature tensor of
M
, fix a parallel, orthonormal basis
\{\gamma^{\prime},E_{2},\ldots E_{m}\}
along
\gamma
and note that
\{E_{\alpha}(0)\}_{\alpha\geq 2}
span
T_{\gamma(0)}\partial\cal O
. Differentiating twice the identity
|\nabla r|^{2}=1
, using the Ricci commutation rules and contracting with respect to
\{E_{\alpha}\}
, it turns out that the matrix function

B:[0,r(x)]\rightarrow\mathrm{Sym}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{m-1}),\qquad B_{\alpha\beta}(t)=\nabla\mathrm{d}r\big(E_{\alpha}(t),E_{\beta}(t)\big)

solves the matrix Riccati equation

B^{\prime}+B^{2}+R_{\gamma}=0,\qquad\text{where}\qquad(R_{\gamma})_{\alpha\beta}(t)=R\big(\nabla r,E_{\alpha}(t),\nabla r,E_{\beta}(t)\big),

(9.2)

with initial condition

B(0)_{\alpha\beta}=\operatorname{II}_{-\nabla r}\big(E_{\alpha}(0),E_{\beta}(0)\big),

(9.3)

where
\operatorname{II}_{-\nabla r}
is the second fundamental form of
\partial\cal O
in the inward pointing direction
-\nabla r
. We recall the matrix Riccati comparison theorem, as stated in
[
47
]
(see also
[
14
, Thm. 1.14]
).

Theorem 9.1
.

Let
R_{1},R_{2}:[0,T]\rightarrow\mathrm{Sym}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{m-1})
be continuous, and let
B_{1},B_{2}:(0,T]\rightarrow\mathrm{Sym}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
solve

B_{1}^{\prime}+B_{1}^{2}+R_{1}\leq 0,\qquad B_{2}^{\prime}+B_{2}^{2}+R_{2}\geq 0\quad\text{on }\,(0,T],

with initial condition
(B_{1}-B_{2})^{\prime}(0^{+})\leq 0
. If
R_{1}\geq R_{2}
on
[0,T]
, then

B_{1}\leq B_{2}\qquad\text{on }\,(0,T],

and
\dim\ker(B_{2}-B_{1})
is non-increasing. In particular, if
B_{1}(t_{0})=B_{2}(t_{0})
for some
t_{0}
, then
B_{1}\equiv B_{2}
on
[0,t_{0}]
.

The Hessian comparison theorem is a direct corollary. We recall that the radial sectional curvature
K_{\mathrm{rad}}
of
M
is the sectional curvature restricted to
2
-planes containing
\nabla r
.

Theorem 9.2

(Hessian comparison from below)
.

Let
(M^{m},\langle\,,\,\rangle)
be a complete manifold, let
\cal O
,
D_{\cal O}
,
r
be as above and suppose that

\mathrm{K}_{\mathrm{rad}}(\pi_{x})\leq-G\big(r(x)\big)\qquad\forall\,x\in D_{\cal O},\ \pi_{x}\subseteq T_{x}M\ \text{ $2$-plane containing $\nabla r(x)$,}

(9.4)

for some
G\in C^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+}_{0})
. Fix
\underline{\lambda}\in\mathbb{R}
such that

\inf_{\partial\cal O}\operatorname{II}_{-\nabla r}\geq\underline{\lambda},

(9.5)

consider the solution
g
of

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}g^{\prime\prime}-Gg\leq 0\qquad\text{on }\,\mathbb{R}^{+}\\[5.69046pt]
g(0)=1,\quad g^{\prime}(0^{+})\leq\underline{\lambda},\end{array}\right.

(9.6)

and let
[0,R)
be maximal interval where
g>0
. Then,

\nabla\mathrm{d}r(x)\geq\frac{g^{\prime}(r(x))}{g(r(x))}\Big(\langle\,,\,\rangle-\mathrm{d}r\otimes\mathrm{d}r\Big)\qquad\text{for }\,x\in D_{\cal O}\cap B_{R}(\cal O).

(9.7)

Proof.

Let
x\in D_{\cal O}
and let
\gamma,B,R_{\gamma}
be as above. Clearly, by (
9.4
)
R_{\gamma}\geq G(r)I_{m-1}
and
B(0)\geq\underline{\lambda}I_{m-1}
. Since the function
\bar{B}=g^{\prime}/gI_{m-1}
solves

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\bar{B}^{\prime}+\bar{B}^{2}-G(t)\leq 0\qquad\text{on }\,(0,R),\\[5.69046pt]
\bar{B}(0^{+})\leq\underline{\lambda}I_{m-1},\end{array}\right.

(9.8)

by Riccati comparison we get
B\geq\bar{B}
on
[0,\min\{r(x),R\})
. In other words,
\nabla\mathrm{d}r\geq\frac{g^{\prime}(r)}{g(r)}\langle\,,\,\rangle
on
\nabla r^{\perp}
. Taking into account that
\nabla\mathrm{d}r(\nabla r,\cdot)=0
(differentiate
|\nabla r|^{2}=1
), the estimate (
9.7
) follows at once.
∎

The Laplacian comparison from below simply follows by taking traces in (
9.7
), and the Hessian comparison from above by reversing all the inequalities in (
9.4
), (
9.5
) (that is, assume
\sup_{\partial\cal O}\operatorname{II}_{-\nabla r}\leq\underline{\lambda}
), (
9.6
) and (
9.7
). As a matter of fact, for the Hessian comparison from above, one can also prove that
D_{\cal O}\subset B_{R}(\cal O)
and that (
9.7
) (with the reversed sign) holds on all of
M\backslash\cal O
in the support sense (Calabi sense, see
[
106
]
).

The Laplacian comparison from above, on the other hand, requires a milder curvature requirement and an initial estimate just involving the unnormalized mean curvature
H_{-\nabla r}
of
\partial\cal O
.

Theorem 9.3

(Laplacian comparison from above)
.

Let
(M^{m},\langle\,,\,\rangle)
be a complete manifold, let
\cal O
,
D_{\cal O}
,
r
be as above and suppose that

\mathrm{Ric}(\nabla r,\nabla r)(x)\geq-(m-1)G\big(r(x)\big)\qquad\forall\,x\in D_{\cal O},

(9.9)

for some
G\in C^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+}_{0})
. Fix
\overline{\lambda}\in\mathbb{R}
such that

\sup_{\partial\cal O}H_{-\nabla r}\leq(m-1)\overline{\lambda},

(9.10)

consider the solution
g
of

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}g^{\prime\prime}-Gg\geq 0\qquad\text{on }\,\mathbb{R}^{+}\\[5.69046pt]
g(0)=1,\quad g^{\prime}(0^{+})\geq\overline{\lambda},\end{array}\right.

(9.11)

and let
[0,R)
be maximal interval where
g>0
. Then,
D_{\cal O}\subset B_{R}(\cal O)
and

\Delta r(x)\leq(m-1)\frac{g^{\prime}(r(x))}{g(r(x))}

(9.12)

holds pointwise on
D_{\cal O}
and weakly on
M\backslash\mathcal{O}
.

Proof.

Taking traces in (
9.2
) and applying Newton’s inequality
\mathrm{Tr}(B^{2})\geq\frac{(\mathrm{Tr}(B))^{2}}{m-1}
one deduces that the function

u(t)=\frac{\mathrm{Tr}B(t)}{m-1}=\frac{\Delta r(\gamma(t))}{m-1}

solves

u^{\prime}+u^{2}+\mathrm{Ric}(\gamma^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime})\leq 0,\qquad u(0)=\frac{1}{m-1}H_{-\nabla r}\big(\gamma(0)\big)\leq\overline{\lambda}.

On the other hand,
\bar{u}=g^{\prime}/g
satisfy

\bar{u}^{\prime}+\bar{u}^{2}-G\bar{u}\geq 0,\qquad\bar{u}(0^{+})\geq\overline{\lambda}.

Riccati comparison now applied to
B_{1}=uI_{m-1}
and
B_{2}=\bar{u}I_{m-1}
implies
u\leq\bar{u}
on
[0,\min\{R,r(x)\})
, whence (
9.12
) holds on
D_{\cal O}\cap B_{R}
. However,
\bar{u}\rightarrow-\infty
as
t\rightarrow R^{-}
, so necessarily
u
is unbounded from below as
t\rightarrow R^{-}
, which imples
r(x)<\bar{R}
. Hence,
D_{\cal O}\subset B_{R}
. The weak inequality can be proved as in
[
113
, Lem. 2.5]
(see also
[
14
, Thm. 1.19]
)
∎

Example 9.4
.

The initial condition satisfied by
g(r)
is crucial for the validity of the Hessian and Laplacian comparison theorems, as illustrated by the following example. Fix
\delta\geq 1
and consider the model
M_{\delta}
with metric

\mathrm{d}s_{\delta}^{2}=\mathrm{d}t^{2}+g_{\delta}(t)^{2}(\,,\,)_{1}\qquad\text{where}\quad\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}g_{\delta}\in C^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+}_{0})&g_{\delta}>0\quad\text{on }\,\mathbb{R}^{+}\\[5.69046pt]
g_{\delta}(t)=t&\text{if }\,t\leq 1/2\\[5.69046pt]
g_{\delta}(t)=\exp\{-t^{\delta}\}&\text{if }\,t\geq 1.\end{array}\right.

Define
\mathcal{O}=\{t<1\}
and
G_{\delta}=g_{\delta}^{\prime\prime}(t)/g_{\delta}(t)
. Note that
r=t-1
is the distance from
\cal O
, and that on
M\backslash\mathcal{O}
,

\begin{array}[]{l}\displaystyle\operatorname{II}_{-\nabla r}=-\delta\mathrm{d}s_{\delta}^{2},\qquad\Delta_{\delta}r=(m-1)\frac{g_{\delta}^{\prime}(1+r)}{g_{\delta}(1+r)}=-\delta(m-1)(1+r)^{\delta-1}\\[8.5359pt]
\mathrm{Ric}_{\delta}(\nabla_{\delta}r,\nabla_{\delta}r)=(m-1)K_{\mathrm{rad}}(r)\\[5.69046pt]
\qquad\qquad\qquad\quad=-(m-1)G_{\delta}(r)=-(m-1)\delta\big[-(\delta-1)(1+r)^{\delta-2}+\delta(1+r)^{2\delta-2}\big].\end{array}

Observe that
\mathrm{Ric}_{\delta}(\nabla_{\delta}r,\nabla_{\delta}r)
is a decreasing function of
\delta
, but also
\Delta_{\delta}r
is so. This is, however, not in contradiction with Theorem
9.3
. Indeed, to apply the latter with
M=M_{\delta}
and
G=G_{\bar{\delta}}
,
\delta\neq\bar{\delta}
, condition (
9.9
) would imply
\delta<\bar{\delta}
, while (
9.10
) gives
-\delta\leq\overline{\lambda}
. In this way, the function
g_{\bar{\delta}}
does not solve (
9.11
) because
g_{\bar{\delta}}^{\prime}(0)=-\bar{\delta}<\overline{\lambda}
.

For most manifolds the trace of inequality (
9.7
), that is,

\Delta r\geq(m-1)\frac{g^{\prime}(r)}{g(r)}

(9.13)

does not hold weakly on
M\backslash\mathcal{O}
even if
g^{\prime}(r)/g(r)
is well defined on
M\backslash\mathcal{O}
, that is, if
R=\infty
, since the singular part of the distribution
\Delta r
acts as a negative Radon measure concentrated on the cut-locus
\mathrm{cut}(\mathcal{O})
. More precisely, by
[
90
]
the distribution
\Delta r
is a Radon measure that can be written as

\Delta r=(\Delta r)_{AC}\mathrm{d}V-|\nabla^{+}r(x)-\nabla^{-}r(x)|\mathcal{H}^{m-1}\llcorner\mathrm{cut}(\mathcal{O}),

(9.14)

where
\mathcal{H}^{m-1}
is the
(m-1)
-dimensional Haurdorff measure, and

•

(\Delta r)_{AC}
coincides with the
L^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(M\backslash\mathcal{O})
function given by
\Delta r
outside
\mathrm{cut}(\mathcal{O})
;

•

|\nabla^{+}r(x)-\nabla^{-}r(x)|
is a function defined on the normal cut-locus, that is, the set of non-conjugate points
x\in\mathrm{cut}(\mathcal{O})
where exactly
2
minimizing geodesics meet, and
\nabla^{+}r(x)
and
\nabla^{-}r(x)
are the tangent vectors of the two
geodesics at
x
.

Remark 9.5
.

The result in
[
90
]
is stated for
\mathcal{O}
being a point, but the proof for smooth
\mathcal{O}
follows verbatim, see also
[
92
]
and Section 3.9 of
[
6
]
.

As a consequence of work of various authors (see the account in Section 1.1 of
[
14
]
), the complementary of the normal cut-locus has Hausdorff dimension at most
(m-2)
, and the normal cut-locus is dense in the set of non-conjugate (i.e., non-focal) cut-points. Therefore,
\Delta r
is an absolutely continuous measure if and only if
\mathrm{cut}(\mathcal{O})
consists only of conjugate points, and in this case (
9.13
) holds weakly on the whole of
M\backslash\mathcal{O}
. In the Introduction, and in particular in Theorem
1.34
, we claimed that (
1.65
) implies that
o
be a pole of
M
. We prove this statement in the next

Proposition 9.6
.

In the above notation, suppose that the negative part
(\Delta r)_{-}
of the measure
\Delta r
satisfies

(\Delta r)_{-}\in L^{\infty}_{\mathrm{loc}}(M\backslash\mathcal{O}).

(9.15)

Then,
\mathcal{O}
is a pole of
M
.

Proof.

Inequality (
9.12
) coming from the Laplacian comparison from above implies that the positive part
(\Delta r)_{+}\in L^{\infty}_{\mathrm{loc}}(M\backslash\mathcal{O})
. Because of (
9.15
),
\Delta r
is absolutely continuous and represented by a locally bounded function and thus, by (
9.14
) and the discussion above,
\mathrm{cut}(\mathcal{O})
has just conjugate points. On the other hand, if
x_{0}\in\mathrm{cut}(\mathcal{O})
is conjugate to
\mathcal{O}
and denoting with
g(r,\theta)
the determinant of
\langle\,,\,\rangle
in normal coordinates
(r,\theta)\in\mathscr{D}_{\mathcal{O}}\subset\mathbb{R}^{+}_{0}\times\partial\mathcal{O}
for
\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{O}}
, by the identity

\Delta r=\frac{1}{2}\partial_{r}\log g(r,\theta)

we see that
\Delta r(y)\rightarrow-\infty
as
y\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{O}}
,
y\rightarrow x
. Hence,
\Delta r
is not bounded in a neighbourhood of
x
, a contradiction which shows that
\mathrm{cut}(\mathcal{O})
is in fact empty, equivalently, that
\mathcal{O}
is a pole.
∎

The volume comparison theorems can be deduced by integration. For
R>0
, we define

\partial B_{R}(\mathcal{O})=\Big\{x\in M:r(x)=R\Big\},\qquad B_{R}(\mathcal{O})=\Big\{x\in M:r(x)\in(0,R)\Big\}.

Note that
\mathcal{O}\not\subseteq B_{R}(\mathcal{O})
, and thus
\mathrm{vol}(B_{R}(\mathcal{O}))\rightarrow 0
as
R\rightarrow 0
. Given
0<g\in C^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+}_{0})
we set

v_{g,\lambda}(t)=\mathrm{vol}(\mathbb{S}^{m-1}_{\lambda})g(t)^{m-1},\qquad\text{and}\qquad V_{g,\lambda}(t)=\int_{0}^{t}v_{g,\lambda}(s)\mathrm{d}s,

which are, respectively, the volume of a geodesic sphere
\{r=t\}
and ball
\{r<t\}
in
M_{g,\lambda}
. The proof of the next result follows verbatim the version in
[
113
, Thm. 2.14]
(see also
[
14
, Thm. 1.24]
).

Theorem 9.7

(Volume comparison)
.

Let
(M^{m},\langle\,,\,\rangle)
be a complete manifold,
\mathcal{O},D_{\mathcal{O}},r
be as above.

(1)

In the assumptions of Theorem
9.2
, the functions

\frac{\mathrm{vol}(\partial B_{r}(\cal O))}{v_{g,\lambda}(r)},\qquad\frac{\mathrm{vol}(B_{r}(\mathcal{O}))-\mathrm{vol}(B_{r_{0}}(\mathcal{O}))}{V_{g,\lambda}(r)-V_{g,\lambda}(r_{0})}

are non-decreasing in
r
provided that
r_{0}\leq r<R
and
B_{r}(\cal O)\subset D_{\cal O}
. In particular, there exists
C>0
such that for all such
r

\mathrm{vol}\big(\partial B_{r}(\mathcal{O})\big)\geq Cv_{g,\lambda}(r),\qquad\mathrm{vol}\big(B_{r}(\mathcal{O})\big)-\mathrm{vol}\big(B_{r_{0}}(\mathcal{O})\big)\geq C\big(V_{g,\lambda}(r)-V_{g,\lambda}(r_{0})\big).

(2)

In the assumptions of Theorem
9.3
, the functions

\frac{\mathrm{vol}(\partial B_{r}(\cal O))}{v_{g,\lambda}(r)},\qquad\frac{\mathrm{vol}(B_{r}(\mathcal{O}))-\mathrm{vol}(B_{r_{0}}(\mathcal{O}))}{V_{g,\lambda}(r)-V_{g,\lambda}(r_{0})}

are non-increasing in
r
for each
r\geq r_{0}
(a.e.
r
for the first one). In particular, there exists
C>0
such that for all such
r

\mathrm{vol}\big(\partial B_{r}(\mathcal{O})\big)\leq Cv_{g,\lambda}(r),\qquad\mathrm{vol}\big(B_{r}(\mathcal{O})\big)-\mathrm{vol}\big(B_{r_{0}}(\mathcal{O})\big)\leq C\big(V_{g,\lambda}(r)-V_{g,\lambda}(r_{0})\big).

The comparison theorem from above, in
(2)
, is due to Bishop-Gromov, see
[
113
, Sec.2]
for references. In the particular case

\mathrm{Ric}(\nabla r,\nabla r)\geq-(m-1)\kappa^{2}\big(1+r^{2}\big)^{\alpha/2},

(9.16)

for some
\kappa\geq 0
and
\alpha\geq-2
, and when
\mathcal{O}
is a pole, detailed computations of the asymptotic behaviour of a suitable solution
g
of

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}g^{\prime\prime}-\kappa^{2}(1+t^{2})^{\alpha/2}\geq 0\qquad\text{on }\,\mathbb{R}^{+},\\[5.69046pt]
g(0)=0,\qquad g^{\prime}(0)\geq 1\end{array}\right.

can be found in
[
113
, Prop. 2.1]
: more precisely, the above inequality admits a solution
g
with

g(r)\asymp\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\exp\left\{\frac{2\kappa}{2+\alpha}(1+r)^{1+\frac{\alpha}{2}}\right\}&\qquad\text{if }\,\alpha\geq 0\\[11.38092pt]
r^{-\frac{\alpha}{4}}\exp\left\{\frac{2\kappa}{2+\alpha}r^{1+\frac{\alpha}{2}}\right\}&\qquad\text{if }\,\alpha\in(-2,0)\\[11.38092pt]
r^{\bar{\kappa}},\quad\bar{\kappa}=\frac{1+\sqrt{1+4\kappa^{2}}}{2}&\qquad\text{if }\,\alpha=-2\end{array}\right.

(9.17)

as
r\rightarrow\infty
. In particular, setting
v_{g}(r)
as above, from

\log\int_{r_{0}}^{r}v_{g}\sim\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\frac{2\kappa(m-1)}{2+\alpha}r^{1+\frac{\alpha}{2}}&\quad\text{if }\,\alpha>-2;\\[5.69046pt]
\frac{2\kappa(m-1)}{2+\alpha}r^{1+\frac{\alpha}{2}}-\frac{\alpha(m-1)}{4}\log r&\quad\text{if }\,\alpha\in(-2,0);\\[5.69046pt]
\big[(m-1)\bar{\kappa}+1\big]\log r&\quad\text{if }\,\alpha=-2\end{array}\right.

(9.18)

and Theorem
9.7
we deduce

\begin{array}[]{ll}\displaystyle\limsup_{r\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\log\mathrm{vol}B_{r}}{r^{1+\alpha/2}}<\infty&\quad\text{if }\,\alpha>-2,\\[11.38092pt]
\displaystyle\limsup_{r\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\log\mathrm{vol}B_{r}}{\log r}\leq(m-1)\bar{\kappa}+1&\quad\text{if }\,\alpha=-2.\end{array}

(9.19)

We conclude by extending the examples in (
9.17
) to a larger class of solutions of (
9.6
) and (
9.11
), that enables to include more general initial conditions. When
\mathcal{O}
reduced to a point, further examples can be found in the appendix of
[
15
]
. The proof of the next lemma is by a direct computation.

Lemma 9.8
.

Let
G\in C^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{+})\cap C(\mathbb{R}^{+}_{0})
be non-negative, set

\theta_{*}=\inf_{\mathbb{R}^{+}}\frac{G^{\prime}}{2G^{3/2}},\qquad\theta^{*}=\sup_{\mathbb{R}^{+}}\frac{G^{\prime}}{2G^{3/2}},\qquad D_{\pm}(t)=\frac{1}{2}\left(-t\pm\sqrt{t^{2}+4}\right).

For constants
C>0
,
D\in\mathbb{R}
consider the function

g(t)=1+C\left\{\exp\left(D\int_{0}^{t}\sqrt{G(s)}\mathrm{d}s\right)-1\right\}.

(9.20)

Then, for a fixed
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
,

(1)

g
solves

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}g^{\prime\prime}-Gg\geq 0\qquad\text{on }\,\mathbb{R}^{+}\\[2.84544pt]
g(0)=1,\qquad g^{\prime}(0)\geq\lambda\end{array}\right.

provided that

C\geq 1,\qquad CD\sqrt{G(0)}\geq\lambda,\qquad D\in\big(-\infty,D_{-}(\theta^{*})\big]\ \cup\ \big[D_{+}(\theta_{*}),+\infty\big);

(2)

g
solves

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}g^{\prime\prime}-Gg\leq 0\qquad\text{on }\,\mathbb{R}^{+}\\[2.84544pt]
g(0)=1,\qquad g^{\prime}(0)\leq\lambda\end{array}\right.

provided that

C\in(0,1],\qquad CD\sqrt{G(0)}\leq\lambda,\qquad D\in\big[D_{-}(\theta_{*}),D_{+}(\theta^{*})\big].

In both
(1)
and
(2)
, if
\theta^{*}
or
\theta_{*}
are infinite then
D_{\pm}(\theta_{*}),D_{\pm}(\theta^{*})
are intended in the limit sense and shall be excluded from the range of
D
.
